Level V

1. A Country Called France

1Inside this book, one part tells where France is. Another part tells about the people. Another part tells about the land. Another part tells the history. Another part tells about celebrations. Another part tells about food. A closing follows those parts. A glossary ends the book. Where is France? No country in western Europe covers more land. Belgium is on the northeast side. Luxembourg is on the northeast side too. The east side touches Italy. The east side touches Germany. The east side touches Switzerland. The south side touches Spain. Northwest, across a channel of water, are the United Kingdom and Ireland. That water is the English Channel. Two sides of France meet the sea. Westward lies the Atlantic Ocean. The English Channel is also on the northwest. The Mediterranean Sea is on the southeast. Paris shows up on a map of the country. So do Dijon, Limoges, Bordeaux, Bayonne, Toulouse, Marseille, and Monaco. Corsica shows up as an island. The Bay of Biscay shows up on the west coast. The Gulf of Lion shows up on the south coast. Europe, Asia, and Africa show up on a wider map.

2Paris is France's capital. Its beauty is famous far and wide. Each year, millions visit the city. Famous landmarks bring them there. You can see the Eiffel Tower. Say Eiffel as EYE-ful. You can see Notre Dame Cathedral. Say Notre Dame as no-truh DAHM. You can see the art museum called the Louvre. Say Louvre as LOO-vruh. The city has a monument known as the Arc de Triomphe. Say that name as ARK du tree-OHMF. The monument rises over a main street, the Champs-Élysées. Say that street as SHAHM-zay-lee-ZAY. Paris holds a population above ten million. Business in France centers on this city. Culture in France centers on this city too. Fashion also looks to Paris as a world capital. Restaurants crowd the city, and hotels do too. Other French cities have them as well. Tourist visits to France pass eighty-two million. The country itself holds above sixty-six million people. Cities and towns hold most of the homes. Jobs are mostly in cities and towns too. Tourism leads every other service industry.

3Visitor numbers here are higher than in any other country. Hotels give work to people who greet visitors. Restaurants give that kind of work too. Museums give that kind of work too. Factory jobs are common for French workers. Cars are one product the factories turn out. Aircraft are another product. Clothing is another product. Chemicals come from other factories. Other factories process food. Mine work employs some people. They bring up coal and other resources. In the vineyards of France, workers gather the grapes. Making wine is the main use of those grapes. Farming takes more than half of French land. French farms produce wheat. They produce other grain as well. They produce sugar beets. They produce potatoes. Some farmers keep cattle, hogs, and sheep. Grape growing is the work of some farmers, and vineyards are where they do it. Most residents follow the Roman Catholic religion. Some residents are Protestant. Some residents are Muslim. Some residents are Jewish. French is the country's main language. Dialects change with the area where people live. Landforms in France are many. You will find hills, forests, and mountains.

4You will find rivers, lakes, and beaches. Most of the country is flat plains. Much of it is low rolling hills. High plateaus appear as well. Paris has the Seine River running through it. Say Seine as SEN. Smaller rivers run beside it. The Paris Basin is the low land these rivers drain. Beaches of great beauty are well known here. Many of them line the Mediterranean shore. A land map marks the Strait of Dover, the English Channel, the Seine River, and Paris. It also marks the Brittany Peninsula, the Loire Valley, the Paris Basin, and the Loire River. It also marks the Jura Mountains, the Bay of Biscay, Mont Blanc, and the Massif Central. It also marks the French Alps, the Ligurian Sea, the Cote d'Azur, and the Pyrenees Mountains. It also marks the Gulf of Lion, Corsica, and the Mediterranean Sea. Mont Blanc is found in the French Alps. Western Europe has no taller mountain. The ranges of mountains in France add up to five. The French Alps are a range many people recognize.

5The Pyrenees are a range many people recognize too. Say Pyrenees as PEER-uh-neez. A wider chain called the Alps includes the French portion. That wider chain crosses several countries. Switzerland shares a border with the French Alps. Italy shares a border with the French Alps. Spain shares a border with the Pyrenees. Southern France holds a large plateau. Old mountains rise there. Old volcanoes rise there. Those volcanoes are not active now. Deep gorges made by rivers cross the plateau. Islands are part of the country. Corsica is the biggest island. The island is south of the mainland, out in Mediterranean water. The past of this country goes back a long time. Experts think humans were here in very early days. The time they give is nearly two million years before the present. Gaul was an old name for the land. People used that name over two thousand years back. Celts was the word for the people then. Rome's empire took Gaul near the year 50 bc. Roman rule lasted close to five hundred years. Cave walls at Lascaux still show animal pictures from early humans.

6Say Lascaux as la-SKOH. The cave is in France. Near ad 400, Gaul passed from Roman hands to tribes out of Germany. Those tribes were known as the Franks. They called their land Francia. The modern name France grew from that older name. For a thousand years after, strong kings held power. Charlemagne was one French king. Say his name as SHAR-luh-mayn. In the year 800, the Holy Roman Empire came under his command. The empire held France. It held pieces of today's Belgium. It held pieces of today's Germany. It held pieces of today's Italy. His death came in 814. The empire broke up once he was gone. Control of French land caused many wars. Those wars ran between the year 1066 and the year 1453. Most of the fighting set France against England. In 1066, a band from Normandy entered England as invaders. Normandy is one region inside France. France and England began another long war in 1337. Fighting in that war lasted to 1453. People give that struggle the name Hundred Years War.

7Hundreds of years passed with kings in charge. Many kings gave the people a hard life. A rising of the people against their king began in 1789. The French Revolution began with that rising. By 1792, France had been made a republic. Unrest in the revolution went on until 1799. Later, the French forced out two other leaders. A new set of rulers followed each removal. The nation today is a republic. Its democracy is strong. Louis XIV ranks among the greatest kings of France. Say Louis as LEW-ee. He sat on the throne from 1643 until 1715. No European king has had a longer reign. Wars took up much of his time as king. Under him, the country's borders spread farther. He ordered a lavish palace and called it Versailles. Say Versailles as vehr-SEYE. Bastille Day parades often place veterans at the front. Those veterans once served in the nation's armed forces. People here mark many holidays. The French Revolution opened on July 14. That date now carries the name Bastille Day. On that 1789 day, Parisians poured into the Bastille.

8The building was a prison. The crowd took the building for themselves. They meant to let the prisoners out. Freedom is honored now with parades. Fireworks are part of the honor too. Mardi Gras is a holiday many people love. Say it as MAR-dee GRAH. Roman Catholics are the ones who keep this holiday. It falls about six weeks ahead of Easter. Huge carnivals fill a great many cities and towns. Parades roll through those carnivals. Floats roll through them too. Costumes cover the people in the streets. Dancing fills those same streets. France turns out more than five hundred kinds of cheese. Thin pancakes called crêpes are eaten often. Say crêpes as KRAYPS. Baguettes are eaten often too. The country is famous for its meals. The country is famous for styles of cooking. Good food matters a great deal to many French people. They treat it as one of life's major pieces. Rich dishes belong to one well-liked way of cooking. Cooks put butter in those dishes. Cooks put cream in those dishes. Cooks put eggs in those dishes.

9Cooks put other flavors in those dishes. Fresh bread of many kinds pleases people here. Most meals come with bread. Bakers often sell that bread as a long thin loaf called a baguette. Say baguette as ba-GETS. Cheese made in different corners of France is famous as well. Many corners offer a cheese that is theirs alone. Sidewalk cafes draw crowds in a lot of areas. France today is modern. Its story is a long one. Its story is full of interest. Powerful kings belong to that past. Empires belong to that past too. More travelers choose France today than choose any other country. They arrive from every region of the world. Beautiful cities are on their list. Villages are on their list. Castles are on their list. Rich foods are on their list. Gentle rolling hills carry vineyards. Farms cover those hills and the flat plains. Shops hold stacks of cheese. Shops hold stacks of fresh bread. If you came to France, which sight would you choose first? The population number is 66,259,012. Land covers 620,427 square kilometers.

10That area equals 239,548 square miles. The capital city is Paris. The language listed first is French. The religion listed first is Roman Catholic. The money in use is the euro. Say euro as YUR-oh. The Central Intelligence Agency gave these numbers in July 2014. The World Bank gave numbers as well. Glossary. A baguette is bread baked into a long thin loaf. Bakers in France usually make this loaf. A basin is a low place on the face of Earth. People also call that place a depression. Carnivals are festivals. A cathedral is a church built on a large scale. A dialect is one version of a language. One region may use its own version. One group of people may use its own version. Fashion means the clothes styles many people want right now. A gorge is a deep valley with a long run. Higher ground stands all around it. Tourism is work done for travelers. The travelers go somewhere in search of pleasure. A vineyard is land planted in grapes. Growers usually want those grapes for wine.

2. A Fish That Makes Electricity

1Venezuela was Alexander Humboldt's destination in 1800. He was a German naturalist. He went there to catch electric eels. He believed living electric eels could help his work. He wanted to solve mysteries about electricity. He knew he must not touch the eels with bare hands. Great pain was possible from their shocks. He asked some fishermen for help. South America was his exploration ground for five years. About thirty horses were led by the fishermen into a pond that was shallow. The eels rose up. Hundreds of shocks hit the horses. The eels grew tired and stopped shocking. Then the fishermen caught five eels. Humboldt learned a great deal about them. Not long ago, a scientist backed Humboldt's report. Self-protection was the reason the report gave for eels rising. Before that support, some scientists doubted the story. They thought Humboldt might have stretched the truth. A fish story sometimes grows that way.

2A picture was drawn from Humboldt's writing. It shows people fishing for eels with horses. It shows how powerful these eels are. Electric eels are long fish. Their bodies are gray-brown. Yellow or orange shows on their throats. These tube-shaped fish are closer to catfish than to other eels. Their group is called knifefish. Every knifefish can use electricity. An electric eel can grow 2 to 3 meters long. That length is 7 to 10 feet. It can weigh 20 to 22 kilograms. That weight is 44 to 48 pounds. An average adult human thigh is about the thickness of its body. The eel can be four times as long. Males and females match in color. They also match in size. The scientific name is Electrophorus electricus. The animal type is fish. The diet is fish, crustaceans, and amphibians. Air enters an electric eel by way of the mouth. Most other fish do not breathe that way.

3Electric eels do have gills. They mainly use the gills to breathe out. An eel lifts its mouth above the water. Special organs in the mouth take air in. Waste air leaves through the mouth. Waste air also leaves through the gills. An electric eel needs air every few minutes. It can drown if it is trapped underwater. Electric eels have fins, as most other fish do. They also have a backbone. Small fins sit on either side of the head. A long fin runs along the belly. That fin goes from the head to the tail. The backbone helps the eel move. The fins help it move too. To swim, the eel waves its belly fin in an S-shape. The swim is quite slow. The eel moves fast only during an attack. If the tail end of the body is cut off, the eel can regrow it. No fish on Earth is more electric than an electric eel.

4It uses bioelectricity to navigate. It uses bioelectricity to communicate. It uses bioelectricity to protect itself. It uses bioelectricity to hunt. It can stun prey with that power. It can kill prey with that power. Bioelectricity is electric current at work inside a body. Other knifefish use bioelectricity too. Some skates use it. Some rays use it. Some catfish use it. Those fish may navigate with it. They may communicate with it. They may protect themselves with it. None of them can zap as hard as an electric eel. Long ago, people in ancient Greece held a live electric ray to a forehead. People in ancient Rome did this too. They hoped the shock would cure a headache. No one should try that at home. A ray's shock can make a person feel numb. The numbness may be why the idea seemed to work.

5Other electric fish include the electric ray. They include the clearnose skate. They include the electric catfish. Three organs make an electric eel's shocks. The organs begin just behind the head. They fill two-thirds of the body. The body works like a battery. The head is the positive end. The tail is the negative end. Shocking power rises when the head curls in the direction of the tail. One electric organ is the main organ. Two other electric organs are smaller. Prey can receive a shock as high as 650 volts. The shock can kill a small animal. The shock can stun a large animal. A feeling of the shock can reach anything in the water out to 9 meters. That distance is 28 feet. A typical wall socket in the United States has far fewer volts. The eel has five times more. That power can be dangerous to people. An electric eel cannot feel its own shock.

6Scientists are not sure why. The reason might be the eel's size. The reason might be the path of the shock. The reason might be some body part that scientists do not yet understand. Scientists do know about special cells called electrocytes. Long rows are how these cells are arranged. Each cell makes a tiny shock. The tiny shocks happen at nearly the same moment. Together they make the eel's stunning attack. Before that attack, the eel must find its prey. Dark water makes the search hard. Cloudy water makes it hard too. Poor sight makes it hard too. Electric eels are most active at night. They use small shocks in place of their eyes. Some bats hunt by bouncing sound off objects. Some owls hunt that way too. Electric eels hunt in a similar way. The eel sends out small electric shocks. It sends about fifty shocks each second.

7The small shocks strike plants. They strike animals. They strike objects. They help the eel sense its surroundings. When prey is near, the eel sends a short group of stronger shocks. The group has two or three shocks. Those shocks make the prey twitch. The animal cannot stop moving. The eel then knows where the animal is. Next the eel sends its attack shock. The shock stuns the prey, or it kills the prey. The eel quickly sucks the prey in. The prey goes through the mouth and into the stomach. Small fish make up most of an electric eel's meals. They eat crustaceans. They eat amphibians. Young eels also eat worms. Young eels also eat insect larvae. In the rivers they usually occupy, electric eels sit at the top as predators. Those rivers move slowly. Scientists have found the eels in the Amazon River. They have found them in the Orinoco River.

8Both rivers are in South America. Freshwater swamps nearby are another home for the eels. They live in creeks. They live in ponds. They live in lakes. Some of this water might kill other fish. These eels can still live there because they take in air. They can also stay out of water for hours. Their skin must stay wet. A habitat map shows the Atlantic Ocean. It shows the Orinoco River. It shows the Amazon River. It shows South America. It shows the Pacific Ocean. Small ponds are the usual mating places for electric eels. A male builds a nest from bubbles. He makes the bubbles with saliva. A female lays eggs in the bubbles. The male guards the nest. His guard duty lasts until the young reach about 10 centimeters. That length is 4 inches. After that, the young must live on their own. Young eels have weaker shocks than adults.

9Other animals are more likely to eat the young. Crocodiles are one example. Black caimans are relatives of crocodiles. Black caimans are among the few animals that try to prey on these eels. Today's knowledge of electric eels goes beyond what Humboldt knew. There is still more to learn. Electrocytes are the model that scientists at Yale University hope to copy. They hope to build cells that act like batteries. Some medical devices hold batteries. A pacemaker is one example. A pacemaker keeps a heart beating. A battery in that device could hurt a person if it broke. Scientists want batteries that will not cause harm. They also want batteries that will not run out of power. Humboldt's comment on the electricity of fishes dates back over two hundred years. He said people were still far from knowing every secret of that electrical action. Which new secret might scientists learn from electric eels?

10Electric describes something charged with energy from moving charged particles. It can also mean powered by that energy. It can also mean producing that energy. An electric current is a movement of electric charges through matter. To mate is to come together to reproduce. The result is a baby. To navigate is to find the way over a long distance. It can also mean steering a course toward a destination. An organ is a body part with a specific job. Kidneys are organs. Lungs are organs. A shock is a sharp jolt. It happens when electric current touches a body or travels through it. To stun is to knock a living thing unconscious. It can also leave that living thing dazed. Nothing else eats a top predator, which stands highest in a food chain. No other animal eats it. The push that starts an electric current is measured in volts.

3. A Place That Honors Dr. King

1Each year, people visit a memorial in Washington, D.C. This memorial remembers Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. Visitors are people of all ages. Their eyes go to Dr. King's sculpture. The sculpture is large. Some of his famous words are part of the site. Those words inspire the people who come. Dr. King taught that the right deed should be done at any hour. A photo shows many people listening to him. He is giving a speech about equality. Who was Dr. King? Why do people remember him? Civil rights had a leader in Dr. King.

2He worked through his whole life for equal rights. He wanted those rights for all people. One family photo includes Martin Luther King Jr. as a boy. He stands at the front right. Georgia was the state of his childhood. He was a child in the 1930s. His childhood was in the South. He faced racism there. Unfair laws pressed on Black people during his youth. A photo shows men drinking at water fountains. The fountains are marked with the word Colored. Today, this word is considered rude. The word can be hurtful. Groups were kept apart by laws of the United States.

3Segregation separated Black people from white people. Segregated means they were kept separate. Some states did not use these laws. Enforcement was common in many southern states. In the South, Black people could not marry white people. Schools for white children were closed to Black children. Separate drinking fountains were required for Black people. White people used other fountains. A photo shows a bus in Dallas, Texas. Black riders sit at the back of the bus. City rules made Black people sit in the back. The front seats were saved for white people only.

4Railroad cars for white people were closed to Black people. Public restrooms were split between Black people and white people. The laws called this plan separate but equal. The quality was lower in many places set aside for Black people. They were not as clean. They were not kept as well as places for white people. A photo shows Dr. King speaking at a news conference. The year is 1965. The place is Selma, Alabama. He talks with people about unfair laws. He wanted those unfair laws to change. He wanted people of every race treated the same under the law.

5His dream was larger than a set of rules. He wanted Americans to come together as one people. He wanted them to live without racism. He gave many speeches. He spoke with many people about changing the laws. Dr. King said hate can never stop hate. He said only love can stop hate. He wanted peaceful ways to make changes. Peaceful ways are also called nonviolent ways. He said the world already had too much hate. He would not use violence to fight racism. Unfair laws drew protests from Dr.

6King and other leaders. They refused to follow those laws. One example was the sit-in. Many Black people sat at restaurant counters. The counters were for white customers only. The sitters refused to move. Some people were arrested. Dr. King was arrested. A photo shows Dr. King leading a march. The march was a civil rights march. It was in Boston, Massachusetts, in 1965. Many people joined Dr. King and his supporters in the march. Marching was another nonviolent way to protest. They protested racism. They protested the laws that kept people apart.

7They hoped to reach many listeners. They especially hoped to reach lawmakers. They wanted those leaders to see that the unfair laws should change. The marchers also went to Washington, D.C. After that march, Dr. King gave a famous speech. The speech is known as I Have a Dream. The Lincoln Memorial was the site in 1963. His powerful words reached many people. A later photo shows a handshake. A handshake joins Dr. King and President Lyndon Johnson. The year is 1964. President Johnson signed a bill. The bill changed the unfair laws. The work of Dr.

8King helped bring that change. The work of other people helped too. Segregation laws were rewritten by the government in 1964. The new laws are fairer. Race can no longer legally decide who gets better treatment. Rosa Parks showed courage in 1955. She was seated at the front of a city bus. She refused to move to a back seat. She was arrested. Anger spread to many people across the country. They were angry about the way she was treated. Unfair laws began to change with help from her courage. A photo shows two Black girls at a school.

9The school is in Baltimore, Maryland. White children once had that school to themselves. Shared schools are now open to Black children and white children. People can choose their own seats on buses. They can choose seats on trains. They can choose seats in restaurants. The same water fountains can be used by anyone. Any person may use the same restrooms. Race does not decide who may use them. Another photo shows Dr. King waving. He waves to a crowd in Washington, D.C. The moment is just before a famous speech.

10The speech is I Have a Dream. The year is 1963. Freedom for every person was his wish. Fair laws for every person were part of that wish. Getting along across every race was his dream. Today the laws are fairer than before. More work remains. Dr. King said a person may not manage great deeds. He said that person can still do little deeds with great care. People who visit his memorial remember him. They remember his dream. His dream was fair laws. His dream was free people. The visitors carry on his work.

4. A Ride Past Earth's Shapes

1A map of major landforms in the United States shows Canada and Mexico. Cities on it include Seattle, Boston, Saint Paul, and Detroit. Philadelphia, New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, Houston, and Monterrey are marked too. Mountain lands include the Cascade Range and the Rocky Mountains. The Sierra Nevada Range, the Coast Range, and the Appalachian Mountains are marked. Rivers include the Columbia and the Missouri. The Ohio, the Rio Grande, the Colorado, and the Mississippi are shown. The Mississippi River delta is marked as well. Lakes include Superior, Huron, Michigan, Ontario, and Erie. Great Salt Lake and Lake Okeechobee are shown. The Great Lakes appear as a group. Plateaus include the Columbia Plateau and the Colorado Plateau. The Ozark Plateau and the Edwards Plateau are shown too. Deserts include the Great Basin Desert and the Mojave Desert. The Sonoran Desert and the Chihuahuan Desert are marked. The Great Plains, the Central Lowlands, and the Central Valley are on the map. The Atlantic Coastal Plain and the Gulf Coastal Plain are shown. The Atlantic Ocean, the Pacific Ocean, and the Gulf of Mexico are marked. The Grand Canyon and the Badlands appear too. A key shows hills, mountains, plains, and plateaus.

2The parts of this book are the road trip, class time, getting ready, days on the road, one landform left, and a word list. This summer I get to take a long car trip. Boston is our start, and Seattle is the far end of the drive with my parents. Landforms are what my class is studying. Mom says amazing land shapes will appear along our route. My teacher, Mr. Lopez, compared Earth to a hard-boiled egg. Both are built in layers. Each has a shell that is thin and hard. The crust is split into pieces. It looks like an eggshell full of cracks. The mantle matches the egg white. The core is the center, just as the yolk is. A drawing of the inside shows the crust. It shows a solid inner core and the mantle. It also shows a liquid outer core. A puzzle map of the crust shows the Eurasian Plate and the Pacific Plate. It shows the North American Plate and the Indian Plate. The Pacific Plate is drawn more than once. The African Plate and the Indo-Australian Plate are there. The South American Plate and the Antarctic Plate are there too.

3Shifting of those crust pieces is one force that builds landforms. We also learned that huge rock pieces make up the crust. Tectonic plates is the name for those pieces. The plates rest on rock that is soft and nearly melted. Heat deep inside Earth makes the plates slide slowly. Underground, nothing shapes landforms more than these shifts. A United States highway map shows the interstate roads. Main east-west routes use even numbers, such as I-90, I-80, and I-70. Main north-south routes use odd numbers, such as I-5, I-15, and I-25. A diagram shows how a volcano near a coast can form. Heat in the mantle makes the mantle move. An ocean plate slides beneath a land plate. Heat from the sinking ocean plate helps magma form. That magma can feed a volcano. Mr. Lopez also said the surface of Earth never stays the same. Some changes take a long time. Other changes happen very fast. Both kinds can reshape a landform that is already there. Both kinds can make a new landform too. Now and then, a small spot high in the mantle grows hot enough to melt. The melt is called magma.

4This melted rock can push upward. It can burst out at the surface as lava. Lava cools and turns hard. It can pile up into a volcano. A volcano is one kind of mountain. The Grand Canyon was cut by water erosion and by wind. Weathering shaped a rock arch. Plates and magma change the planet under the ground. Other work happens on the surface at the same time. Weathering breaks rock apart and gives it shape. Erosion hauls away the loose bits, called sediments. Later it sets those bits down. Weathering and erosion work mainly through wind, water, and ice. Mr. Lopez gave us a list of landforms. I plan to carry the list on the trip. I will try to spot every item on it. The Boston part of the list names an island, a harbor, and a peninsula. Mom and I looked at islands in Boston Harbor. We begin at Castle Island, inside Boston Harbor. It is not a true island. Water does not wrap all the way around it. One side joins the mainland. That link makes it a peninsula. From there you can still see a lot of islands.

5Boston Harbor belongs to Massachusetts Bay. This stretch of water joins the Atlantic Ocean. The North American continent's east coast is where Boston stands. A continent is one incredibly huge mass of land. After we head west, the hills and valleys get bigger. It feels as if we are climbing. Dad says mountains will show up soon. I do not see any peaks that are tall. Dad says these mountains are very old. Erosion wore them down until they look like big hills. A mountain, a hill, and a plateau are also on my list. To me the Berkshires seem like big hills, not the mountains I picture. One map shows Canada, the Adirondack Mountains, and New York. It shows the Catskills and the Allegheny Plateau. Buffalo and Pennsylvania are marked. The key lists hills, mountains, plains, and plateaus. Across New York State, some land is low and full of hills. Other spots sit higher. Dad says the higher ground is part of one huge raised area. People call that area a plateau (pla-TOH). The high ground grew over a long time. Erosion dropped sediments there, and the pile rose. North America's biggest plateau is the one called the Colorado Plateau.

6Parts of it lie in Colorado, Utah, Arizona, and New Mexico. Another map shows Canada, Lake Michigan, and Wisconsin. Buffalo, New York, Boston, and Michigan are on it. Lake Erie, the Ohio River, and Chicago are marked. Pennsylvania, Ohio, Illinois, and Indiana are marked too. A lake is the next item I hope to check off. Lake Erie is far too wide for me to see the far shore. After we sleep for a night, we continue west. The land drops and grows much flatter. Lake Erie's south shore is the road we take. Lake Erie belongs to the Great Lakes. About fourteen thousand years ago, a glacier scooped out the Great Lakes. They form Earth's largest set of freshwater lakes. Mom says another Great Lake is coming soon. Its name is Lake Michigan. Chicago stands on that lake. A Madison map shows Lake Mendota and Lake Monona. Downtown sits on the strip of land between the lakes. That strip is an isthmus. Wisconsin's state capitol building stands on Madison's isthmus. We spend a night in Chicago. Then we return to the road. Once we enter Wisconsin, we pass Madison, the state capital.

7Downtown Madison rests on an isthmus (IS-muhs). An isthmus is a narrow band of land with water on both sides. I grin and mark it off the list. A gorge is next. We stop at the Wisconsin Dells. The Dells is a gorge, meaning a valley that is steep and narrow. The Wisconsin River cut it. Canyons line its sides. A canyon is like a gorge, yet it is not as steep and not as narrow. A river is on the list as well. Soon we should see the Mississippi River. Length puts it with the longest rivers on Earth. Glaciers did most of the carving of this stretch of the Mississippi. We have just crossed the Mississippi. The Great Plains will be our official setting very soon. In school I learned what a plain is. It is a broad, flat stretch of land. Trees are scarce there. The Great Plains began when two tectonic plates crashed. The plates locked together. Some of the Great Plains is flat. Some of it has hills. A map shows Montana, North Dakota, and the Mississippi River. It shows the Missouri River, South Dakota, and Minnesota. The Bighorn Mountains and Wisconsin are marked.

8Badlands National Park, Madison, and Wyoming are marked. Iowa, Nebraska, and Chicago appear too. I can check off a plain. Grass on the Great Plains once fed enormous herds. The herds were bison and horses. Rock shapes in Badlands National Park grew from sediments that were laid down. Wind erosion and water erosion carved them after that. The park holds many fossils. One skull is shown up close. It came from a mammal that lived between 38 million and 16 million years ago. We sleep in western Minnesota. The next morning we drive into South Dakota. Things stay fairly flat until Badlands National Park draws near. We hike inside the park. Amazing rock shapes rise around us. After one more night, we cross a corner of Wyoming. The Bighorn Mountains pass on our left. Snow sits on some of those peaks. Mom says still larger mountains lie ahead. We enter Montana and pass Billings. Then Dad tells us to get ready for a big sight. A moment later I whoop. A line of high peaks stands in the snow. Those peaks are the Rocky Mountains. The Rockies are a chief mountain range of the West.

9They rose when plates met. Two small tectonic plates under the Pacific Ocean slid beneath the North American Plate. A map shows Canada, Washington, and Seattle. Montana and the Missouri River are on it. Mount Rainier and North Dakota are marked. The Columbia River, Billings, and South Dakota are marked. Oregon, Idaho, and Wyoming are shown too. I check off a mountain range. Montana's Rocky Mountain peaks wear a cover of snow. A glacier and a volcano remain on my list. Glaciers cap Mount Rainier. A glacier is a large field of ice and packed snow. It lasts through the whole year. After Montana, Idaho, and Washington, I am excited to see water again. I am even more excited about Mount Rainier (ray-NEER). It is a huge volcano. We roll into Seattle. The city sits beside Puget Sound. Puget Sound is an arm, or an inlet, of the Pacific Ocean. Interstate 90 ends here. Our landform hunt is almost done. The last picture shows the Skagit River flowing toward Skagit Bay. It marks a river delta. Sediments piled up there over time. The pile made the river divide into many smaller branches. Those branches sit in the zone between the dotted lines.

10One landform is still unchecked. We drive one hour north to reach it. The place is the Skagit (SKA-jit) River delta. Sediments built this shape where the river enters Skagit Bay. This trip has shown me amazing landforms. I have learned a lot about how Earth's surface changes. I cannot wait to tell Mr. Lopez about my summer. A delta is a triangle-shaped patch of land. A river's mouth is where sediment piles up to make it. To deposit is to lay an amount of material, such as sediment, onto a surface. Erosion is nature's removal of rock or soil. Water, wind, or ice can carry it off. A gorge is a long, deep valley boxed in by higher ground. An isthmus is a thin strip of land joining two bigger landmasses. Landforms are natural shapes on Earth's surface, such as a valley, a plateau, a mountain, a plain, or a hill. A peninsula is a long arm of land with water on nearly every side. A plateau is a wide, raised sheet of flat land. Tectonic plates are the great rock sheets that form Earth's crust.

5. A Small Ball, a Club, and a Hole

1Golfers spend many hours on their swing. They try to make it better. Set golf next to other sports, and the aim can sound simple. The ball must end in a hole cut into the ground. The game is not simple at all. Patience is required for good play. They need balance. They need focus. They need lots of practice. Millions of people still love the sport. It is played in beautiful places. The land is green and rolling. Golf gives people a chance to learn. It gives them a chance to practice. It gives them a chance to improve. Hours of fun are the biggest gift of the game. It also offers hours of relaxation. A golf hole has several named parts. The fringe is longer grass around the green. The hole is marked with a flagstick. The putting green is the short grass by the hole. Players call it the green for short. A water hazard may be a stream. A water hazard may be a pond.

2The fairway leads toward the hole. A bunker is a shallow pit in the ground. Sand usually fills it. A bunker is usually filled with sand. The rough is an area with trees. It also has shrubs and long grass. The teeing ground is where the hole begins. A small ball is struck with clubs. The ball is hard. They try to get it into a hole. They want as few strokes as they can manage. A stroke is a swing. A very long trip is sometimes required of the ball. A short trip is enough at other times. A high path through the air is needed at still other times. A high ball can get over long grass. It can get over other things. Every one of these shots must be in a golfer's set of skills. Club heads have different shapes. The shape depends on the job of the club. Shorter shots call for irons. Long trips for the ball call for woods.

3Fairway metals are used for long distances too. A short shot that climbs high calls for a wedge. Sand can be escaped with a wedge. A putter makes the ball roll. A putter is used once the ball is on the green. A golf course is the place for the sport. The land of a course covers a wide area. The land is mowed with care. It is trimmed to mark the parts of the course. A course usually has nine sections. Some courses have eighteen sections. Each section is called a hole. The teeing ground opens every section. Golfers usually place the ball on a tee. A tee is a small peg. The fairway is the path of the first big hit. The fairway leads to the small hole. That small hole waits where the section ends. Putting green is the name of the area beside that small hole. People also call it the green. Several strokes are nearly always needed before the ball arrives.

4A hole in one sometimes rewards a lucky golfer. Golf uses the word hole in two ways. One meaning is the small hole that receives the ball. The other meaning is one section of the course. A course has nine or eighteen sections. Golfers also deal with hazards. A hazard is a part of the course they try to avoid. Extra strokes may be the cost of a ball in a hazard. Extra strokes are bad for a score. Sand inside a bunker makes one kind of hazard. Streams are another type of hazard. Ponds are another type. The rough can also cause problems. The rough is the area around each section. Trees usually grow there. Bushes grow there too. The grass is longer. Water nearly rings one hole in a picture. Rough borders that hole. A large bunker is there as well. Fewer hazards may appear on other holes. A golf game usually means playing eighteen holes. A nine-hole course is often played a second time.

5Par is the number given to each hole. A par of four sets a goal. Four strokes or fewer is the goal for that hole. The number depends on distance. Teeing ground to hole is the distance that sets the number. Most holes are par three. Others are par four. Others are par five. Par for the course is a total. It adds the par of every hole. The total may cover nine holes. It may cover eighteen holes. Golfers who play together compare totals for the whole course. They play in a small group. The win goes to the lowest score. A chart shows names for scores on a par five hole. A hole in one uses one stroke. An eagle is two strokes under par. On the chart, 5 minus 2 equals 3 shots. A birdie is one stroke under par. On the chart, 5 minus 1 equals 4 shots. A bogey is one stroke over par.

6On the chart, 5 plus 1 equals 6 shots. A double bogey is two strokes over par. On the chart, 5 plus 2 equals 7 shots. Golfers respect the concentration the game takes. They stand back when another person takes a swing. They stay quiet during that swing. A round of golf can take three to five hours. A round is eighteen holes. Fun players often travel the course by golf cart. Walking is how professional golfers reach the next hole. Caddies are helpers who carry the clubs. Spikes cover the bottoms of golf shoes. The spikes are for gripping the grass. Their clothing lets them move easily when they swing. One hand may be covered by a glove. The glove helps them grip the club. The glove helps them avoid blisters. Modern golf was born in Scotland. The 1400s may be when the game started. England likely received the game early in the 1600s. Soon after, it reached India. It reached Australia.

7It reached other British colonies. Many countries had the game by the late 1800s. Gear looks very different after many years of change. The first golf balls were made of wood. The first clubs were made of wood. A different ball came into use early in the 1600s. The ball was leather. Feathers filled it. In 1848, another ball was invented. Tree sap was the material. The sap was dried. The sap was heated. The sap was shaped. Players began using metal clubs with the new balls. Modern golf balls have rubber cores. Those balls first appeared in 1898. Dimples first showed up on balls early in the 1900s. A higher flight is one result of the dimples. A longer flight is another result. Pictures show old clubs. They show an old ball next to a modern ball. Because golf began in Scotland, it is most popular in certain countries. Those are countries where English is spoken. The game is played in more than 130 countries.

8No other country comes close to the United States in number of courses. Scotland leads in a different way. Compare the number of courses with the number of people. Scotland then has the most golf courses. Many places also have indoor digital simulators. People use them to improve their skills. Miniature golf is played indoors. Miniature golf is played outdoors too. A list gives the top ten countries by number of courses. The United States has 17,672. The United Kingdom has 2,752. Japan has 2,442. Canada has 2,300. Australia has 1,500. Germany has 684. France has 559. China has 500. Sweden has 480. South Africa has 450. World play passes sixty million people. Yearly play in the United States passes twenty million people. Programs exist for young people. The programs help them learn the game. The programs let them compete. Top men compete on a tour based in the United States. It is the Professional Golfers' Association Tour. People call it the PGA Tour.

9They also compete on the European Tour. They compete on several other tours. Top women from around the world compete on another tour. It is the Ladies Professional Golf Association Tour. That tour is based in the United States. People call it the LPGA Tour. Men have several top professional tournaments. Women have them too. These tournaments are called majors. Winning a major earns respect. It earns fame. It earns money. A living from tournaments comes to only a small group. They earn it by competing in tournaments. Wildlife can be a hazard at a competition. Baboons interrupted one event. South Africa hosted that 2008 Women's World Cup. History ranks Jack Nicklaus as the sport's top golfer. Important tournaments filled his years from 1961 to 2005. His wins passed one hundred competitions. He won eighteen majors. No woman in the sport's history ranks above Patty Berg. Her professional contests ran from 1940 to 1962. She won sixty tournaments. She won fifteen majors. Among golfers playing now, Tiger Woods may stand highest.

10No current golfer has a stronger PGA Tour record. Courses sit in many kinds of settings. Large parks of grass describe some courses. Tall trees grow in those parks. Some courses are narrow strips of land. The strips run along coastlines. Cactuses are common on desert courses. They often have palm trees. The most unusual place for this game is the Moon. The Moon trip of astronaut Alan Shepard was in 1971. A few golf balls flew from his club during that visit. A photo shows him swinging a club on the Moon. The club meets a ball. Few players will ever play golf on the Moon. Anyone who is interested can play here on Earth. A club is the first thing a new player needs. You also need a golf ball. Practice teaches young players an important lesson. Millions of golfers already know that lesson. The game feels as amazing as a trip beyond Earth.

6. A Song Contest for Europe

1In 1981 the contest was won by Bucks Fizz, a group from England. Their winning song was Making Your Mind Up. Dana won the contest in 1970. All Kinds of Everything was the song she performed. Across Europe, that sweet song rose to the top of the charts. My first look at this contest on television came in 1970. I watched with my family. Ireland is our family's country. Songs were chosen by judges who came from other countries. We wanted the song from Ireland to win. I still hold that first memory of the yearly show. The show taught me something about my home. I lived in a country on a larger continent. That continent is called Europe. I remember the night because Ireland won that year. Fans pose at the 2010 contest in Norway. No song competition in Europe is larger than this one. It is also the song competition that excites Europe the most.

2Pride in a home country's performance runs high. Contestants show their talent to neighbors in other countries. Audiences enjoy taking part in the vote. A 1963 photo shows contestants posed before the board of song entries. Until 2013, performance order had no set plan. Producers are the ones who choose the order now. Waterloo was performed in 1974 by ABBA, the pop group from Sweden. That contest was held in England. The contest started in 1956. It gave European countries a fun project to share. The Second World War was over when this project began. Switzerland was the site of the earliest contest. Seven was the number of European countries in that first show. The running time was longer than ninety minutes. Each country sent popular singers, and they sang live. Each country chooses its song in a national competition. Every song must be sung live. Every song must last three minutes or less. Early rules said the country's own language was required for its song.

3Sometimes the words were easy for many listeners to follow. Lulu won for England in 1969 with Boom, Bang-a-Bang. Massiel won for Spain in 1968 with La, La, La. The language rule changed in 1973. In 1974 the Swedish pop group ABBA sang Waterloo in English. That song won. A-Ba-Ni-Bi is one title that took the prize. The 1978 prize for that title went to Israel. Diggi-Loo Diggi-Ley is another winning title. Sweden won with it in 1984. In 2014 a prize for the oddest title could have gone to France. The song was Moustache, by the act Twin Twin. Its story is a man wishing for a moustache. Jedward are identical twins. They came in eighth in 2011. Their song was Lipstick. They wore jackets that paid tribute to Michael Jackson. The twins looked stylish. Every performer makes the trip to the country that is hosting.

4They compete with one another to reach the semifinals. The semifinals last two days. The Grand Final then includes the twenty-six acts that won. Votes come from the audience. The judges vote. Five experts from the music industry vote too. People at home can vote by telephone. They can vote by text message. They can vote through an app. People cannot vote for their own country. That rule helps keep the contest fair. In the Grand Final, each finalist performs one more time. Juries from all the countries taking part vote again. Audiences from those countries vote again too. When the voting ends, the presenters call on spokespeople. Each country has a spokesperson. On live television, those spokespeople announce the points. After that, presenters announce each country's full point total. They start with the lowest score. The highest score comes last. Excitement fills the fans as they wait for the winner's name. The winning song is sung once more.

5The winner receives a trophy. The winning country hosts the contest the next year. Ireland has won seven times. No country in the contest's history has had more success than Ireland. Staging the 1996 contest cost 3.5 million dollars. People thought it brought more than 9 million dollars to Ireland's economy. Norway has lost more often than any other country. Norway has also won three times. In 1969 four countries ended with the same number of points. All four had to be named winners. Later, tie-breaker rules were added. The rules were meant to stop another tie like that. Sixty years of the contest were marked in 2015. It is the longest-running yearly television music competition in history. Audiences love how grand and over-the-top the show can be. Fans can download a scorecard. They can mark scores at home while they watch. Gowns were what women wore in the early years. Seriousness marked those early performances.

6The Swiss singer Lys Assia took part. She won the first contest, in 1956. Her song was Refrain. She was also first to be called back for a closing song. Surprise made her lose the words. She filled the gap with la, la, la. Songs in this contest still like playful words that do not mean anything. Performers now wear dazzling costumes. The stage sets are often amazing. The performances are often amazing too. On many popular television talent shows, an unusual style may not reach the final. This contest accepts everyone. Some winners go on to successful careers. ABBA won the contest in 1974. Switzerland's entry in 1988 was Celine Dion. In 2014, audiences were amazed by Ukraine's Mariya Yaremchuk. Tick-Tock was her song. Behind her, a man ran inside a giant wheel. Cute lyrics got a showcase in 2012 from the Grannies from Buranovo. Attention can come from lyrics that are cute.

7Interest and winning do not always land on the same act. The Russian entry in 2012 was those same Grannies. They finished as the runner-up. Party for Everybody was the title. Audiences were amused by the Grannies. The cute lyrics said that everyone is happy. The words included dogs. The words included cats. Many cultures from all over Europe appear for the audiences. Entry is now open to more than forty countries. Worldwide, the watchers number at least two hundred million. Europeans vote to support the songs they like best. A 2006 study looked at how countries vote. Two colleges in London carried out the study. Neighbors tend to receive votes from countries that sit close by, the study found. People in Scandinavia support one another. People in Eastern Europe usually vote for one another. The United Kingdom and Ireland keep track of the points they give each other. A group of countries that vote together is called a bloc.

8Being in a bloc does not always bring a win. Norway belongs to the Scandinavian bloc. In this contest's history, no country has had worse luck than Norway. Norway has finished last a record eleven times. Norway has received nul points four times. Nul points means no points at all. Hard feelings between countries can grow from certain voting patterns. Azerbaijan, next door, put Armenia in last place in 2014. The lowest scores from Armenian voters went to Azerbaijan. Armenia's jury matched that low ranking. People thought a military conflict was the reason. That conflict ended in 1994. In 2015 each of the two countries again placed the other last. Twenty-seven countries have won since 1956. Some of those countries have won more than once. A winners' map shows lands by the Atlantic Ocean. The map also shows lands by the Mediterranean Sea. Austria has won. Azerbaijan has won. Belgium has won.

9Denmark has won. Estonia has won. Finland has won. France has won. Germany has won. Greece has won. Ireland has won. Israel has won. Italy has won. Latvia has won. Luxembourg has won. Monaco has won. The Netherlands has won. Norway has won. Portugal has won. Russia has won. Serbia has won. Spain has won. Sweden has won. Switzerland has won. Turkey has won. Ukraine has won. The United Kingdom has won. The former country of Yugoslavia has won. A group of public broadcasters runs the contest. The group is called the European Broadcasting Union. Its short name is EBU. Countries that belong to the EBU can enter. Some EBU members are not in Europe. The Soviet Union broke apart in 1991. After that, new countries from Eastern Europe joined. Some countries outside the EBU have still been invited. Morocco has been invited. Australia has been invited. Could countries grow closer because of this contest?

10Joy is at the center of the celebration. It celebrates different beliefs. It celebrates different customs. Every year the contest grows more popular. Every year it gains more fans. Friends gather to watch this unusual event. Families gather too. They cheer for the acts they like. No ending for the contest is in sight. Salvador Sobral won in 2017. Francesco Gabbani of Italy shared the stage that year with a gorilla that danced. An audience is a group gathered to see and hear a performance. A broadcaster sends out radio programs or television programs. A competition is a rivalry for something people want. A contestant takes part in a competition. A culture is the ideas and customs of a group of people. An expert knows a great deal about a subject. International means linked to two or more countries. Lyrics are the words of a song. A trophy is a prize given to a winner.

7. A Stone City in the High Andes

1A map of South America puts Machu Picchu in Peru. The Urubamba River runs nearby. Brazil, Bolivia, and Chile are lands around Peru. The Pacific Ocean lies west. The city still stands high in the mountains. The towering Andes of Peru hold it. Its height is 2,350 meters above the sea. That height is 7,710 feet. Visitors find a treasure there that cannot be priced. The city is ancient. It belonged to the Inca. The Inca were a people and an empire in these mountains until Spain conquered them. Mystery and a haunting feeling make this one of the world's remarkable places. Trees and vines of the forest hid it. Most of the world did not know it for more than four hundred years. A history professor named Hiram Bingham found it again in 1911. He sailed to South America with a hunch. An old Inca city, he thought, might sit somewhere in the Andes.

2A local farmer then told him of a city far up the mountains. The farmer said it had been left long before. The climb was steep, hard, and covered in growth. Amazement hit Bingham at the top. A ruin showed through tangled trees. Inca building at its finest was what he saw. Stone walls showed expert skill. Farm areas were irrigated, meaning water had been brought in for crops. Sacred temples stood there. Old paved roads stood there. These early ruins, he knew, could unlock the story of the Inca. That group flourished in South America in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries. He wanted to search the site and learn. Birth came to Hiram Bingham in Hawaii in 1875. Death came in 1956 in Washington, DC. The Inca are hard to study. Many experts think no written language was theirs. Careful guesses are still possible for archaeologists and historians.

3One guess says the Inca started as a small clan living in caves. Many groups shared the Andes. By 1400 CE, conquest of other local groups had begun. Pachacuti ruled as emperor from 1438 to 1471. His name sounds like pah-chah-KOO-tee. Some of the strongest years came under his lead. The city was most likely made for him. Bola stones, experts think, were thrown by Inca warriors at enemy legs. The aim was to trip those enemies and catch them. In the Inca language, his name means he who remakes the world. An empire is what he made of the Inca world. He was a fierce warrior. A strong army followed his command. Many neighboring kingdoms fell to that army. The empire moved past the edges of present-day Peru. Present-day Ecuador came inside it. About twelve million people belonged to the Inca Empire as time went on. A photo from 2016 shows archaeologists digging at the city.

4The reason for building Machu Picchu is unknown. Theories exist. A theory is a possible explanation. The theories do not all agree. Bingham's first idea was that Inca society was born here. He changed that idea later. His next idea was that this was the Inca's last refuge. A refuge is a safe place. He pictured Inca rulers fighting Spanish conquistadors here. Conquistador is a Spanish word for conqueror. Spain used these men to seize land in the New World. Their work came in the sixteenth century. Archaeologists now place that refuge in the jungle. Many kilometers separated it from Machu Picchu. Dozens of further theories have been offered. A prison was one scholar's idea. A trade hub was another. A retreat for women was another. A place for crowning kings was another. A station for trying new crops was another. A royal estate for Inca emperors is the idea many archaeologists hold today.

5Experts think the spot was chosen for a strategic reason, whatever the purpose was. Strategic means shaped by a careful plan. People moving in the valley below could be watched. People below could not tell a city was above them. Defense would have been easy even if the city had been seen. Natural resources were another gain of the spot. Those are useful things in nature, such as water, minerals, and forests. The Urubamba River runs far below. A spring fed by rain supplies the ridge. A stone base still marks where water leaves the ground. Spring water was gathered by a system built into the hillside. A canal took water to fountains. Drinking used those fountains. Bathing used them too. Flow at the fountains continues today. A granite quarry lies near the city. A quarry is a dig site for stone such as marble or slate.

6Heavy boulders did not have to come from far away. Building most likely started in the mid-1400s. Wooden wedges may have been a carving tool. Holes were drilled into rock. Wet wood wedges went into the holes. Builders then waited for a freeze. Ice split the rock. Perfect blocks are not explained by this method. Shaping huge granite blocks remains a mystery. Even modern tools make granite hard to carve. Many months may have gone into a single wall. Three walls also belong to the Temple of the Three Windows. Those windows are the largest known in Inca building. Centuries on a mountaintop have left the walls in remarkably fine shape. Tightly locked walls are an Inca mark, however the blocks were formed. Few spots on Earth show this kind of wall. Stones fit so well that no binder was needed. A knife today would not pass between them.

7A city plan marks the Royal Palace. It marks the Temple of the Sun. It marks the Main Temple. It marks the Temple of the Three Windows. It marks farming areas. It marks homes. It marks open space. Archaeologists think the 1450s were the building years. Rich and powerful Inca were the people it served. The ruler could relax in this royal spot. Important visitors could be entertained there. About 150 homes stood at Machu Picchu in all. Pachacuti's royal palace was his home. It may have been the grandest building of them all. Bingham saw a split into two areas. One area was urban. One area was agricultural. Main temples and other buildings fill the urban area. Some rooms housed the emperor. Other rooms housed workers and their families. The urban Temple of the Sun may have been a solar observatory, experts think. An observatory is a place for careful study of nature.

8Stars can be the subject. Weather can be the subject. Earthquakes can be the subject. Sunlight comes through a window on the winter solstice. A solstice is one of two yearly times. At those times the Sun stands farthest from the equator. Late June brings that winter solstice in the Southern Hemisphere. That sunlight lands on a big stone at the room's center. Terraces for crops make up the farm area. A terrace is a flat step cut into a hillside for growing food. Rain falls heavily at Machu Picchu. The Inca knew downhill water could strip soil away. Engineers sent rain off either side of the terraces. The water then entered the forest below. Short stone walls were the way they farmed steep slopes. Soil layers stayed in place because of those walls. The empire kept growing after the city was built and Pachacuti died.

9A new ruler, Huayna Capac, took power in 1493. His name sounds like WY-nah. The empire reached farther than ever under him. Death came to him in 1527. His two sons then fought over the next leadership. Atahualpa won that fight. His name sounds like ah-tah-WAHL-pah. Francisco Pizarro, a Spanish explorer, arrived in 1532. Taking over the empire was his aim. A surprise attack left many Inca dead. Atahualpa was held until a ransom could be paid. Pizarro is the explorer who put Spanish rule over the Inca. More than 22 metric tons of gold and silver were offered for Atahualpa's freedom. That mass is 24 tons. Pizarro still ordered the killing. Only a few more decades of life remained at Machu Picchu. Some historians blame years of fighting for the empire's decline. They also blame disease that Europeans brought. Trees and vines hid the city after the Inca left.

10Only a few farmers in the area knew it was there. Centuries passed that way. A hike of three or four days is how some tourists arrive. Trains bring other visitors. Peru's main draw for tourists today is this city. Each year, hundreds of thousands of people flock here. A World Heritage Site naming came in 1983. The naming body is UNESCO. Those letters stand for a United Nations group for education, science, and culture. A World Heritage Site has value for culture or for science. The United Nations recognizes it and protects it. Outstanding places from history are what this group tries to protect and preserve. A special cultural meaning for all humankind belongs to these sites. Money from the group also helps repair Machu Picchu and keep it sound. Learning about the Inca and the city still goes on. Mysteries remain at this amazing place.

8. Acadia, First Light in the East

1Mount Desert Island lies in Maine. Acadia National Park looks out on the Atlantic Ocean. The Sun comes up over Acadia's rocky coast. Sunrise reaches this park before it reaches most other places in the United States. A map shows why. Of the fifty states, Maine is the one that reaches farthest east. That eastern edge of Maine is where Acadia sits. It faces the Atlantic Ocean. The park covers less than 50,000 acres. That area is 202 square kilometers. Next to many national parks, Acadia is small. A national park is land the government owns and protects. The protection is for history or for nature. Even so, the separate pieces of Acadia make a beautiful park. In the United States, Acadia is among the national parks people visit most. Each year the visitors number more than two million.

2The park holds a beautiful coastline. It holds islands. It holds forests. It holds lakes. It holds mountains. It holds rocks. A small space holds a wide variety. Variety means a mix of different things. Water, rock, and bright fall color make a lovely combination. Mount Desert Island holds the largest section of the park. From the sea there, more than twenty mountains rise. Cadillac Mountain is the tallest. It reaches 1,530 feet. That height is 466 meters. The early sunrise makes it the most crowded mountain too. The amazing view adds to the crowd. The park has more than 40 miles of coastline. That length is 64 kilometers. The coast is young, so sandy beaches are rare. Waves move the sand that does exist. Waves also move rocks and other material.

3In 2010 President Barack Obama hiked Cadillac Mountain. His family hiked with him. A child studies a tide pool at Acadia. A sea star rests in one of the pools. A sea star is also called a starfish. When the tide is low, the ocean leaves pools of water behind. Sea stars live in the pools. Sea cucumbers live in them. Hot pink seaweed lives in them. Other living things live in them too. The pools do not stay forever. Tides make them, and tides come and go. A tide is the ocean's continual rise and fall. Gravity from the Moon helps produce the tides. Gravity from the Sun helps too. A rise and a fall of 12 feet can happen in Acadia's tide during one day. That change is 4 meters. Go while the water is falling if you want the best visit.

4More time for exploring is the result. Some beaches vanish completely once the water is high. The pools come back after the tide goes out. North America's smallest falcon is the American kestrel. It is the smallest falcon in Acadia too. Acadia is one of the country's best places to watch birds. People have seen 338 kinds of birds there. Every autumn, strong winds drive thousands of raptors south. A raptor is a bird that hunts small animals. Those hunts provide its food. These raptors migrate out of Canada. To migrate is to move from one region to another at the same time each year. They head for warmer places for the winter. Cadillac Mountain gives a close view as they pass overhead. Hawks fly over the peak.

5Kestrels fly over it too. A young peregrine at the park has just received a band. A wildlife biologist who works for the park put the band on. Bands on the legs mark individual birds. Biologists use them to learn what the birds do. Where the birds travel is part of what they learn. Several states have later reported birds that got bands at Acadia. They have been seen in Canada. Few animals on Earth are faster than peregrine falcons. Nesting on Mount Desert Island was once their habit. Speed did not protect them from people. Human activities killed many of the falcons. Acadia had no peregrine falcons by the 1960s. Work to bring them back started at the park in 1973. Every year since 1991, falcon chicks have hatched in the park.

6Baby foxes live in the park. The American mink lives there. The porcupine lives there. Other parts of Maine have many black bears. Those parts also have many moose. Acadia has only a few black bears. It has only a few moose. Most of its mammals are smaller. Foxes are among them. Mink are among them. Porcupines are among them. A mammal is warm-blooded and has a backbone. It has hair or fur. It nurses its young. Its babies are born live. Why are most of these mammals small? Acadia is not a large park. The small area limits how large the animals there can be. To limit means to keep something inside a set size or amount. It is surprising that a national park was created here at all. The land now called Maine was home to Native people, the Wabanaki.

7Their time on that land lasted 12,000 years. People of the Dawnland is what Wabanaki means. The Wabanaki formed when five different tribes joined. They built canoes from birch trees. They fished in the area now called Acadia. They hunted there. They gathered plants there. In the 1600s, settlers from France arrived. They took land from the Wabanaki. In the 1700s, British settlers started moving north. The French and the British fought often. The fighting killed many Wabanaki people. Diseases from European settlers killed many too. Other Wabanaki people were forced to leave. Settlers took over much of Maine. The Wabanaki stayed, but in smaller numbers. Statehood came to Maine in 1820. After that, rich people came to the rocky coast. They came from large cities in the East.

8The Wabanaki sold baskets they had made by hand. The baskets used sweetgrass and birch. The visitors bought land. They built fancy houses for the summer. They also bought the baskets. Five separate tribes had united as the Wabanaki. Those tribes, or nations, still live in northern Maine. They also live in parts of Canada. People who live near Acadia keep the traditions of their ancestors. They still build canoes. They still build baskets. They guide visitors who come to hunt. They sell shellfish that has been smoked. They teach at a yearly gathering. That gathering is called the Native American Festival and Basket Makers Market. Bark from local birch trees goes into the canoes. Wood from those trees goes into them too. A plan took shape among a few wealthy landowners near the start of the 1900s.

9They would buy land so it could be protected. George Dorr and other people began the work. They gave land so a park could be made. To donate is to give a gift to a cause. The park would preserve the coast and the woods. To preserve means to protect something for the future. By 1913 they had gained 5,000 acres. That area is 20 square kilometers. They turned the land over to the government. National park status came to that land in 1919. George Dorr lived by himself in a grand home on Mount Desert Island. When he died in 1944, his money was gone. He had spent it buying land and then giving the land away. The dream he held had come true, and Acadia was a national park.

10Growth by single pieces continued after he died. New gifts of land fit together like the squares of a quilt. Gifts of land around Acadia still arrive from owners. The park's first superintendent was Dorr. He kept that job for twenty-five years. Sand Beach is under the feet of two young visitors. They have finished a hike in the park. Acadia is where the sea and the mountains meet. People come to watch that meeting. People come to breathe air that tastes of salt. People come to see the Sun rise. After they greet the new day, more remains to see. More remains to do. Visitors can hike. Visitors can climb. Visitors can fish. A bicycle is another way to travel through the park. Take the chance if a visit ever becomes possible.

9. Across the Polynesian Ocean

1Jump rope is the game for Polynesian girls in a park. The park is in the city of Papeete. Papeete is on the island of Tahiti. A large ship was begun in Hawai'i in 1973 by a group of Polynesians. They named it Hōkūleʻa, said hoh-koo-LEH-ah. That name is the Hawaiian word for a bright star. Those islands lie under the path of that star. When finished, the ship was a canoe measuring 19 meters. That length is 62 feet. The canoe had two hulls. The builders copied the design of Polynesian ships from long ago as closely as they could. Ships of that kind had carried people out to settle islands. The islands lie all across the Pacific Ocean. No ship like it had been built for hundreds of years. Hōkūleʻa is a modern copy of an ancient Polynesian ship. Near the coast of Hawai'i, this canoe sails. Colonialism has changed the islands of Polynesia a great deal. Most Polynesian lands were under other countries by the early 1970s. Polynesian culture suffered because of that control. A culture is the ideas and customs of a certain group. Old beliefs of Pacific Islanders were pushed aside. Old practices were pushed aside too. The languages of their elders stayed unknown to many Polynesian children. They never learned the history of their elders. Sails on the boats gave way to engines. Old songs gave way to modern entertainment. It took the place of dances. It took the place of other customs. The people who created Hōkūleʻa wanted to study an old skill.

2They wanted to learn how sailors found their way. Navigation means steering a course toward a place you mean to reach. No land could be seen during crossings of huge distances. The creators asked Mau Piailug for help. His name is said pee-EYE-loog. He was from the Yap Islands. The Federated States of Micronesia is the home of those islands. Few people still knew the old methods, and Piailug was among them. He could use the stars to hold a boat on course. He could use clouds. He could use the patterns of waves. He could use other clues too. In 2006 a ceremony honored him. The honor recognized his teaching. Polynesian sailors of the next age group learned the old navigation from him. Hōkūleʻa departed Hawai'i on May 1, 1976. The trip to Tahiti covered 4,345 kilometers. That distance is 2,700 miles. The canoe had no engines. It had no other modern equipment. The voyage succeeded. About a month later the canoe and crew reached harbor. The harbor was Papeete Harbor in Tahiti. Papeete is said pah-peh-EH-teh. The crowd waiting there was larger than seventeen thousand. They cheered the travelers into port. This first voyage was not only a display. It proved a lesson from the old stories. Explorers from an earlier time really could make those crossings. The voyage was also a symbol. It helped wake up pride in the skills of Polynesian ancestors. Ancestors are family members from long ago.

3In 1784 an English artist made a sketch. The artist was John Webber. He drew Polynesian men rowing a canoe. The canoe had two hulls, in the old style. Pride has a strong basis for the people of Polynesia. Human history holds few explorers greater than their ancestors. The Pacific was their exploring ground from about 1500 BCE until 1400 CE. They crossed millions of square miles. A huge area filled up, island after island, as they settled. Credit for that work was withheld by others for a long time. Many people refused to believe the old travelers had the right technology. Technology means using scientific knowledge or tools to make or do something. Many people also refused to believe the travelers had the knowledge for such trips. The travelers left stories about what they had done. They did not leave written records of those achievements. Researchers had to rebuild the history in other ways. Archaeologists studied pottery. An archaeologist studies what remains of ancient cultures. They studied other old artifacts too. An artifact is an object people made or used long ago. The objects helped show links between groups that lived far apart. Experts compared the languages of different islands. The languages held clues about which people came from which place. More recently, researchers have used DNA testing. DNA is a code inside living things. The code carries genetic information. The long name for DNA is deoxyribonucleic acid. People's cells hold genes, and the tests study those genes. Where ancestors came from is clearer because of those clues.

4Those clues have helped open the history of the Polynesians. Together, the clues point to a first homeland. Southeast Asia was the first home of the Polynesians. For a time they lived in the islands now called Taiwan. They also lived in the Philippines. Both places are major groups of islands. Mainland China has these island groups off its coast. Around 3000 BCE, explorers began to sail toward the southeast. They often followed chains of islands. A chain like that is called an archipelago. They reached Fiji about three thousand years ago. Samoa was reached about three thousand years ago. Like many peoples in history, some Polynesians marked their skin. They used tools and ink to make tattoos. The marks stayed for life. The word tattoo began in Polynesia. James Cook carried that word to Europe. Colonizing Polynesia was pushed forward by Cook. A great leap by the explorers came about 1,100 years ago. They sailed into ocean that no person had visited yet. The leap came in two main periods of time. First they settled Tahiti and the islands around it. The years of that settling ran from 1025 to 1120. Later, during 1200 to 1400, voyaging canoes headed out in many directions. They went in many directions. They settled Hawai'i. They settled Rapa Nui, also called Easter Island. They settled New Zealand. Those three places are the corners of the Polynesian Triangle. A map shows this spread across the Pacific.

5The map also shows the United States, Japan, Mexico, and Australia around that ocean. On the map, Taiwan is marked 2500 BCE. The Philippines are marked 1500 BCE. The Melanesian Islands are marked 1300 BCE. Samoa is marked 800 BCE. Fiji is marked 900 BCE. Tonga is marked 900 BCE. The Cook Islands are marked 1025 CE. Tahiti is marked 1025 CE. The Marquesas Islands are marked 1100 CE. Hawai'i is marked 1200 CE. Rapa Nui is marked 1200 CE. New Zealand is marked 1400 CE. The ancestors moved south from Taiwan and the Philippines. They also moved east. The journeys took thousands of years. A traditional tattoo is given by one man to another on Savai'i. Savai'i is part of Independent Samoa. Hawai'i is part of Polynesia. New Zealand is part of it. Samoa is part of it. Tonga is part of it. Tahiti is part of it. Other Pacific islands belong to it too. These places are branches of one cultural tree. As groups split apart and spread, they carried their culture along. Some of the old connections lasted. Even so, life on each island grew in its own way. Life on each island changed in its own way. Settlers brought religious beliefs to the new islands. One belief is mana. Spirit, or life force, is the meaning of mana. Good is one form mana can take. Mana can be bad. Polynesians believe every living thing has this sacred energy. Sacred means holy, or tied to a god or a spiritual purpose.

6Their religions also include many gods. Stories about the forming of Earth are alike from one corner of the triangle to another. Stories about the forming of its creatures are alike too. The Maori of New Zealand believe in Rangi. Rangi is Father Sky. They believe in Papa. Papa is Mother Earth. Hawaiians know these gods by other names. The names are Ao and Po. Another figure appears often in the myths. His name is Maui. He is half god and half human. He is full of mischief. He is full of courage. He is full of cunning. One of the main islands of Hawai'i carries his name. Food was another way the culture spread. Voyagers packed plants into long, wide canoes. They packed animals too. They planned to start gardens and farms. The animals included dogs. They included chickens. They included pigs. Breadfruit was a crop they grew in each new home. Taro was another crop. Bananas were another crop. They were highly skilled at fishing. They knew other ways to meet their needs from the sea. Much of Polynesia shares one word. The word is moana. Moana means ocean. Rapa Nui is the island farthest east in Polynesia. Clues found by archaeologists tell about its first people. Over a thousand years had passed since the first people settled there. Fame for this far-off island rests mostly on the moai. The moai are giant statues. Carvers made them from volcanic stone. A moai can stand 10 meters tall.

7That height is 33 feet. A moai can weigh 75 metric tons. That weight is 83 tons. Nobody is sure why the islanders gave so much time to these huge monuments. Nobody is sure why they gave so much energy. Evidence suggests one possible reason. Families may have raised the statues to honor chiefs. Ancestors may have been the people those statues honored. On Rapa Nui, a moai stands tall. Members of the Maori people dance at a festival in New Zealand. The festival marks the new year. Huge distances kept Polynesian groups apart. Because of that, each group developed in a special way. Maori people made their home on New Zealand's two large islands. These people were known as fierce warriors. They built forts from earth and stone. A fort of that kind is called a pa. Their strong, expressive dance is usually called haka. Haka is a famous symbol of New Zealand. The way of life changed with the place. Where wood was easy to find, people built houses. They built villages. They built forts. Dress changed in hot places that were hotter than New Zealand. Building methods changed in those places too. Those hot, humid places near the equator are called tropical. Other groups made their own legends. They made their own customs. One of those customs is hula. Hula is a storytelling dance made by Hawaiians. For centuries, Polynesian people had much of the Pacific mostly to themselves. European ships began arriving at a time more than five hundred years before today.

8The culture was changed forever by that arrival. Many islands were seized by European powers. Britain seized New Zealand in 1788. People from Peru captured islanders on Rapa Nui.
