They forced those people into slavery. French forces took control of Tahiti in 1880. The islanders did not have weapons of steel. They did not have gunpowder. Colonizers from Europe defeated local communities with guns. They also brought new illnesses that could kill. The islanders had no immunity to those illnesses. They had no cure. Native Hawaiians were devastated by the new diseases. Deaths among them began in the late 1700s. Control of their islands lasted a long time, which was not true for some other island peoples. They kept that control until 1893. In 1893 the United States removed the queen. Her name was Liliʻuokalani. Hawai'i stayed a territory of the United States until 1959. In 1959 it became the fiftieth state. Today, Native Hawaiians are about 20 percent of the people in the state. Racism was built into colonialism. On their own islands, Polynesians were treated as second-class citizens. The land was taken by outsiders. Outsiders pushed traditions aside. In some places, the language was banned. Traditional religion was banned. Other customs were banned. Since the 1960s, respect for this culture has grown. Many of the islands are now popular with tourists. People visit for the natural beauty. People visit for the traditions. Around the world, the culture is recognized more than before. It is celebrated in surfing and in fashion.

9People of Polynesian heritage have also made a mark far from home. Heritage is a way of life, a tradition, or a trait passed down through generations. Daniel Akaka of Hawai'i was a United States senator. He was the first senator of Native Hawaiian heritage. Dwayne Johnson is a wrestler and an actor. People know him as The Rock. He is Samoan. Mike Iupati is a star in American football. He is Samoan too. Auli'i Cravalho is an actress. She is Hawaiian. She speaks the voice of the main character in the movie Moana. New Zealand is also called Aotearoa. About 4,612,000 people live there. About 600,000 of them are Maori. The Maori are the Polynesian people of New Zealand. Maori people today make up close to 14 percent of everyone there. Around the country, their culture can be seen. People celebrate it there. About 903,500 people live in Fiji. About 451,000 are native Fijians. More than three hundred islands make up Fiji. Native peoples there include both Polynesian and Melanesian roots. Melanesian names a different ethnic group. Papua New Guinea's people belong to that group. About 1,454,000 people live in Hawai'i. About 300,000 are Native Hawaiians. Hawai'i is the fiftieth state of the United States. Many honors go to its Polynesian past. More people now count Hawaiian ancestors than in earlier years. The population of Samoa is 195,418. About 181,000 are native Samoans. New Zealand ruled Samoa from 1914 to 1962. After that, Samoa became independent.

10American Samoa, nearby, is a territory of the United States. The population of Tahiti is 288,750. About 192,000 are native Tahitians. Tahiti belongs to the Society Islands. It also belongs to French Polynesia. It is still a territory of France. The population of Tonga is 107,667. Native Tongans number about 105,500. Polynesian and Melanesian roots are mixed in the Tongan people. Of Tonga's 169 islands, 36 have people living on them. Most of those people live on Tongatapu, the main island. In New Zealand, children at school use a hongi when they part. Hongi is said HONG-ee. The hongi is an old Maori gesture. The two people press their noses together. At the same time they press their foreheads together. Bringing their heritage back is work Polynesian peoples are doing now. Young people are learning the old arts again. They are learning rituals again. They are learning other parts of the culture again. In New Zealand, more people are speaking the Maori language. The number of speakers is rising. Recognition is growing for Polynesians on islands from Hawai'i out to Rapa Nui. People see them as brave explorers. People see that they have always been resourceful. Resourceful means able to find smart ways through difficulty. Hōkūleʻa still sails the Pacific. Wherever this canoe goes, it carries a bold spirit. It carries the seafaring legacy of these peoples. The central Pacific and the southern Pacific hold the islands called Polynesia. Those islands include Hawai'i, Samoa, and many more.

10. Amelia Earhart Takes to the Sky

1A photograph shows Amelia in 1937. The book opens with a start and with flying machines. Her youth in Kansas comes next. Then come the years she took to the air. Her final flight follows. The mystery of her fate comes after that. A word list ends the book. A small red plane bounced through the clouds. It was 12,000 feet, or 3,658 m, over the water. Icebergs rose out of the cold waves underneath. Huge black storm clouds waited in front of her. The year was 1932. Amelia Earhart rode in that airplane. Few pilots in the United States were more famous. An earlier Atlantic crossing had already made her the first woman to ride along in a plane. A man had been the one at the controls. Success now would make her the first woman at the controls for an Atlantic flight. She would also be the first person, woman or man, to make that ocean crossing by plane a second time. Success had to come first. Few people had managed the trip, and there was a clear reason. Danger filled the route. Planes of the 1930s were small. They did not carry many of the instruments pilots trust today.

2A pilot had to trust what she could see. She also had to sense how the plane felt. For hours at a stretch she had to hold the plane in tight control. A later photo, taken in Northern Ireland, shows Amelia next to the plane she had just flown. The black clouds were too wide to go around. Amelia aimed the little plane straight at them. Rain soon changed to ice. The controls froze solid. The plane dropped into a tailspin. It plummeted toward the water while Amelia fought for control. At last the warmer air under the clouds melted the ice. The spin ended when she brought the nose back up. Another photo shows reporters packed around her after that flight. Londonderry, in Northern Ireland, was the landing place many hours later. The goal was met. She had flown herself across the Atlantic Ocean. Fame was already hers before this journey. After it, she would become a legend. The memory that would last longest was her greatest failure. A flight meant to circle the whole world ended when she vanished. The first airplane flight came in 1903, in the year Amelia turned six. Wilbur and Orville Wright, two brothers, built a winged machine from wood.

3At Kitty Hawk, North Carolina, they got it into the air. A picture shows the Wright brothers on that first flight. In 1936, one of the earliest passenger planes began carrying people. Plenty of observers thought the brothers were foolish. Other people found the dream of flight exciting. The danger was real. Those early planes were fragile. They were slow, and they stayed low. Bad weather was too much for them. Crashes killed many pilots. By 1937, planes had grown much better. The first airlines had started to carry passengers. Most routes were short, and most stayed over land. Engineers kept designing stronger planes. Pilots such as Amelia learned safer ways to fly them. Crashes had not ended, though. People who knew the child Amelia had little reason to doubt her later successes. Her birth date was July 24, 1897. Her birthplace was Atchison, Kansas. Most of her days were spent at her grandparents' house, with cousins and friends. Missouri River banks were the children's climbing place. Amelia led the group into caves. Horseback riding was a joy for her. She invented games, and the children played them in her grandfather's barn.

4Their mother sewed bloomers because Amelia and her sister Muriel were so active. A dress kept a girl from running, riding, and climbing the way boys did. Bloomers did not. In those years, most girls were kept from being so active. Amelia refused to accept that limit. A childhood photo shows Amelia with her sister Muriel. The happy years ended when Amelia was eleven. Her parents moved Amelia and Muriel to Iowa. Her father failed to find work. The family shifted homes several more times. Amelia gained very few new friends. At twenty-one, she moved with her family to California. An air show one day left her dazzled. Planes looped and performed stunts. College was left unfinished after that. Flying was what she wanted. Her parents helped her take lessons in piloting. She saved until she could buy an airplane. Soon the young pilot was performing in air shows herself. A photo places Amelia next to Neta Snook, her first flight teacher. A plane stands beside them. Helping children was often part of Amelia's work. Between flights she moved to Boston and took a job at Denison House. Poor people could come there for help, and children most of all.

5She loved the work. She did it well. A name for herself was forming. Then a once-in-a-lifetime chance arrived. George Putnam, a publicist in New York, was hunting for a woman. He wanted the first woman who would ride in a plane across the Atlantic. The plan was full of danger. The year before, three women had died attempting that ocean crossing. Up to then, only men had crossed the Atlantic by airplane. Whoever tried it needed to be extremely tough. Danger and deep fatigue both waited. George believed Amelia Earhart was the right choice. She was eager to accept. Across her life, Amelia held many jobs. In that era, a job for a woman was unusual. She served as a nurse, a social worker, and a pilot. She also worked as a clothing designer. The Ninety-Nines began with her help, and this band of women pilots is still active. She was a magazine editor and a writer. She was an airline executive and a professor. A 1918 photo shows her in a nurse's uniform. During the 1928 crossing, Amelia sat only as a passenger. Useless is how she felt, so she likened herself to a sack of potatoes.

6Even so, nothing about her life stayed the same. World headlines carried news of that first Atlantic crossing by a woman. Afterward she was famous and constantly busy. Books about flying came from her pen. Speeches about flying filled her days. Denison House no longer had room on her schedule. Active women like her got clothes she designed, and luggage as well. She even helped start two airlines. Later she married George Putnam. Flying continued. She tested new aircraft on trips across the country. Contests drew her in. She set records on long-distance flights. Southampton, England, is where a photo catches her smile once the 1928 crossing was done. Another shows her facing a row of microphones during a public speech. By age thirty-nine she had completed every major flight except one. A fresh challenge was what she wanted. Flying around the world was the greatest test still open. A single pilot, a man, had circled the world several years earlier. His path had not followed the equator. That line is the longest route, and it is the hardest. Amelia chose that route. The question was whether she could finish it.

7By then, Amelia Earhart's flying skill was beyond doubt. On May 21, 1937, she left with Fred Noonan, her navigator. Their new silver plane flew from Oakland, California, to Miami, Florida. That hop was the opening leg of the around-the-world attempt. A photo shows Amelia with the plane before the long journey began. A map of the 1937 route marks Asia, Miami, and Africa. South America is on it. One line shows the finished portion. Another mark shows where the plane vanished. A further line shows the route that was never completed. Amelia and Fred made their first stop with the plane in Miami, Florida. The new plane had already crossed the full North American continent. Its first test was a success. Next they followed the equator route. George Putnam, her husband, had planned every stop with care. Stop after stop passed without trouble. The path led through South America and Africa. It led through India, Asia, and Australia. It seemed Amelia Earhart would again finish a historic flight with nothing going wrong. A later map marks Asia, India, Howland Island, and Hawaii. Lae and Australia are on it too. Indonesia was the setting of a photo of Fred Noonan with Amelia at a late stop.

8On June 29, 1937, they landed at Lae, New Guinea. Lae is a Pacific island just north of Australia. There they kept to the usual routine. Fuel went into the airplane. Maps were checked. Telegrams went back to the United States. The takeoff from Lae gave no sign of trouble to come. The Coast Guard cutter Itasca traded radio messages with Amelia's plane. As Howland Island drew near, those messages grew strange. After that tiny island, only Hawaii and then California remained. Offshore, the U.S. Coast Guard cutter Itasca stood by. Its job was to catch the plane's radio signals and help the crew find the island. The signals that arrived were patchy and odd. Amelia seemed unable to hear what the Itasca sent. She talked about a storm. The sky over the island, though, was clear. More than twenty hours after takeoff, her voice came on the radio. She reported that they were running north and south. After that, her voice was never heard again. What became of Amelia still haunts people. The Itasca started the search at once, and the U.S. Navy joined it. Navy officers thought a storm may have brought her down.

9They placed that storm northwest of Howland Island. Weeks of searching followed. No trace of the plane appeared. Amelia and Fred were not found either. A year of looking led her husband, George Putnam, to a conclusion. He decided she had died at sea. People still ask if that is the whole story. The U.S. Navy sent eight ships. One of those ships was the USS Lexington. Some people point to a scrap of airplane found on a nearby island. They believe the scrap came from Amelia's plane. Many explanations have been offered. One idea says Amelia and Fred lived through an emergency landing. A Japanese ship then took them aboard. A Japanese island held them as prisoners, and disease killed them later. Another idea says she set the plane down on a different island. She and Fred lived for a time on local foods. Disease or old age took them in the end. Plenty of people call those stories inventions. They think a simpler end is more likely. The plane came down in the water. Fierce waves, more than 10 feet or 3 m high, would have broken it apart fast. Amelia and Fred may have drowned before their life raft could be inflated.

10Her ending does not erase her place in history. The 20th century's greatest Americans include Amelia Earhart. She died while doing the thing she loved most. She proved that women did not have to accept limits. She proved that flight was open to anyone. She changed the world. She was a legend. One last photo shows her in the act she loved best, flying. Active women once wore bloomers, loose pants of an older cut gathered at the knee or the ankle. Disappeared means something ceased to be seen, or ceased to exist. Expeditions are journeys, or the groups that make those journeys. A legend is a famous person remembered for a special skill or a great deed. A navigator is the person who plots the course to a chosen destination. Patchy means spotty or uneven, not the same quality all the way through. A pilot is a person who flies a craft in the air or in space. A tailspin is a diving plane's rapid fall while it spins round and round. A telegram starts on a telegraph and arrives later as writing or print.

11. Animals That Travel Far

1In the fall, geese head south toward warmer climates. Some animals cross the sky for hundreds of miles. Others cross it for thousands of miles. Some thunder over wide plains. Some push through powerful ocean currents. The trips are a search for food. They are a search for water. They are a search for warmer weather. They are a search for a place to raise young. Every one of these trips is migration. Scientists use the word migration for these long animal journeys. Many sorts of animals migrate. Birds migrate. Bats migrate. Whales migrate. Sea turtles migrate.

2Large grazers migrate, including caribou and wildebeests. Salmon migrate. Some insects migrate. Pilot whales are one kind that migrates. Some animals leave at the same time each year. They follow one familiar path. Other animals move in a less regular way. Weather and other local conditions decide when they move. A few animals migrate in only one direction. Locusts leave when their numbers grow too large. They need a fresh place to feed. They remain in the new field until food there runs out. A swarm can cover that new feeding ground. Hungry bears feed on salmon that are on the move.

3For an animal, few journeys are more dangerous than migration. Many dangers wait along the route. Hungry predators are one danger. Strong winds are another. Ocean currents are a danger. Hunters are a danger. Fishers are a danger. The move still matters, because better living conditions help the animal survive. Food and water are the most common reason for a migration. One spectacular case happens in Africa. Each year, drought sends more than a million wildebeests on the move. Wildebeests are large animals that graze. Hot weather can dry up the grass in one place.

4It can dry up the water too. The herds then travel in huge numbers to find both. Zebras join these big mammals on the way. Gazelles join too. The joining animals number in the hundreds of thousands. Arctic terns cover 22,000 miles on their routes every year. The Arctic is where they nest. Antarctica is where the route ends. They pass the winter there. Other birds also fly astonishing distances for food. Warblers make such trips. Flycatchers make them. Storks, pelicans, and terns make them. Many other kinds of birds do too.

5The flights happen every year. Animals also migrate to find a place for eggs. They migrate to find a place to raise young. Salmon are fish that migrate in order to spawn. Trout do this too. Spawn means to lay eggs. The eggs are laid in freshwater streams. Freshwater eels do the reverse. They leave freshwater homes. Spawning happens after they reach the open sea. A female salmon may be seen on the trip toward those streams. Eels can switch from fresh water to salt water because of a kidney adaptation. Without that change in the kidneys, the switch would kill them.

6How do migrating animals find the way? Scientists are still learning the answer. Salmon are one example that uses smell. Smell leads them back to the stream of their birth. Indigo buntings fly at night. Stars guide other birds that fly at night. Birds that fly in daylight use the sun. Special landmarks help them too. A coastline can keep them on course. A mountain can keep them on course. Scientists keep studying more ways animals navigate. To navigate is to work out a route across a long distance.

7Some animals may sense the magnetic pull of Earth. The pull may guide them. Picture Earth as one giant magnet. The magnetic field behaves like a magnet inside the planet. Many bird species may find their way by sensing the magnetic north pole. They may sense the magnetic south pole too. Tiny bits of a magnetic material have been found in some bird brains. The material is called magnetite. Earth's poles may tug on those bits. The tug may steer the birds during migration. Monarch butterflies make one of the most interesting trips each year.

8Their wings are bright orange and black. They leave the northern parts of North America. They leave the eastern parts too. Mexico is one destination. The south of the United States is a second destination. Winter is spent on certain mountain trees. In spring the adults fly north again. They breed during that return. After the females lay eggs, the adults die. The eggs become young butterflies. Those young butterflies finish the trip home. Many animals of the sea migrate as well. Huge humpback whales are on the list. Lobsters are on the list. Sharks, sea turtles, and tuna are on the list.

9Many sea turtles follow ocean currents. The currents carry them from one ocean region to another. Scientists still cannot explain how the animals locate the exact beach. Eggs must be laid on that beach. It must be the beach where they hatched years earlier. Baby loggerhead turtles crawl to the sea after they hatch. The beach is the same one where their parents hatched. An adult loggerhead shows the animal those babies will become. Each year, millions of animals travel a long way. Some go for food. Some go for water.

10Some go for better weather. Some go to nest. Some go to raise their young. A few travel by themselves. Others move in huge flocks. Others move in herds. However they go, the trip is migration. A drought is a long dry spell, with little rain or with none. Migration means animals travel again and again between places. Navigate means finding a route across a great distance. Spawn means to lay eggs. Wildebeests are large African antelopes with an ox-like build. A long tail with a tuft is part of that build.

12. Art We See Everywhere

1Contents. The opening is listed at 5. Painters are listed at 6. Sculptors are listed at 8. Potters are listed at 10. Glassblowers are listed at 12. Fiber Artists are listed at 14. Quilters are listed at 15. Unusual Art is listed at 16. Where Art Is Found is listed at 19. The Word List follows those sections. The opening. Art of many kinds sits all around us. That art makes the world brighter. It also makes the world more interesting. Art is not a mystery. An artist can create almost anything. A curvy glass vase can be art. A painting can be art. A custom-built motorcycle can be art. An artist starts with an idea. Next the artist plans the size. The artist also plans the shape. Then the artist picks the materials. Some art brings a smile. It can make us think. It can simply look beautiful. Art can stir a wish to learn a new skill. We may want to make something ourselves. Alfred Quiroz painted a picture. The picture shows Quiroz himself. Painters. Painters use many different styles. Some paintings look like real objects. Other paintings do not stand for any object. A painting can focus on feelings. It can focus on thoughts. It can focus on moods. It can focus on hopes. It can focus on dreams.

2A painter may have a personal way with a brush. Another painter may smear color with a palette knife. A palette knife is a thin metal blade. Some painters splash paint. Some spray paint. Some drip paint. Some even use mud. The materials can differ. The techniques can differ. All of these people are still painters. A mural can cover a wall. A painter may choose a surface that is not a normal canvas. The shape of that surface may be the reason. The texture may be the reason. The size may be the reason. Glass sheets can receive paint. A painter might paint on dishes. Furniture can receive paint too. Many painters like to work big. They make murals as large as a wall. The paint goes directly on the wall. Outdoors, building sides become the place for murals. Waterproof colors help those murals last. Sculptors. Sculptors make art that has form. Their art also has depth. A sculpture can be small. One example is a carving small enough to hold. A sculpture can be majestic. The Statue of Liberty is one example. A sculptor may carve with a chisel. A sculptor may carve with a small axe. Solid materials can be carved. Wood is one material. Stone is another. Ice is another.

3Wax is another. A sculptor might weld metal pieces with a blowtorch. A sculptor might make soft sculptures. Those shapes come from strips joined together. The strips may be fabric. They may be leather. They may be colorful yarns. One picture shows a wood sculptor. One picture shows metal casting in bronze. A metal sculpture can be a huge statue. It can also be a tiny frog. Casting that sculpture takes several steps. The sculptor needs soft clay. The sculptor needs wet plaster. The sculptor needs a hot, melted metal. Bronze is one metal used this way. The first step is a clay model. Wet plaster paste goes over that model next. The thick plaster dries. It becomes a hard mold of the first shape. When the mold is ready, hot metal is poured in. The sculptor waits until the metal cools. After the piece cools, a careful hand can touch it and look it over. The check looks for cracks. Then the sculpture is polished so everyone can see it. Potters. Bowls, vases, and plates are called pottery. Artists called potters make them. Potters use soft clay. The clay is heavy and wet. They use different types of this clay. A lump of clay goes on a pottery wheel. The wheel looks like a small round table that spins.

4While the wheel spins, the potter pushes the clay. The potter pinches it. The potter pulls it. The lump becomes a shape. A push one way makes a bowl. A push another way makes a plate. One picture shows clay being formed into pottery. One picture shows clay pottery baking. A kiln is where the clay object is baked. A kiln is a very hot oven. Heat drives every bit of water from the clay. After the clay is baked dry, it is called ceramic. A potter may want the ceramic to be colorful. A potter may also want it to keep water out. Glaze is a special paint that can do both jobs. The glaze gives a smooth, shiny surface. Glassblowers. Glass art seems to glow. Light flows through the glass. The colors look extra bright. Glassblowers begin with melted glass that is still soft. They lift that hot glass. The tool is a blowpipe, a long metal tube with a hollow center. The tube is called a blowpipe. Air from the artist's breath goes down the pipe. A bubble forms in the glass blob. The artist can turn that bubble into nearly any form. The glass must stay soft and workable. Glassblowers return it to the fire often. While it is hot, they can twist it.

5They can pull it. They can stretch the glass until it matches the plan in mind. One picture shows a glassblower shaping a ball of glass. Hot glass is soft. Artists can form it in lots of different forms. Glassblowers must work very quickly. They must also be careful. Glass breaks easily. Soft glass is easy to work with. It is easy to shape. An artist can add swirling lines. An artist can add brightly colored dots. An artist can add bits of metal. An artist can put a handle on the side. Once the piece cools, it cannot be worked anymore. It can shatter into pieces. One picture shows a glass sculpture. Fiber Artists. Fiber art is made from thread. It is made from yarns. It is made from pieces of fabric. Some fiber art reaches the height of a room. It may hang from the ceiling. It may instead be as small as a placemat. A placemat sits on a table. Fiber artists love patterns. They love soft texture. They love color. One fiber artist is called a weaver. A weaver passes each yarn across the others, first above and then below. The work happens on a loom. A loom is a weaving machine. The loom keeps the threads even.

6It also keeps the design organized. Weavers make colorful rugs. They make blankets. They make mats. They make clothing. They make wall hangings. One picture shows a weaving loom. Patterned quilts are a popular kind of fiber art. Dolls are another popular kind. Quilters. Quilters cut small pieces of fabric. They arrange the pieces into a design pattern. Then they sew the pieces together. Quilting began long ago as a practical answer to a need. New fabric cost a great deal in those years. People saved money by using scraps. The scraps came from old clothes. The scraps became bed covers. The earliest quilts used plain patchwork. Later, those covers grew into art that looked beautiful. Talented quilters made them. They combined colors. They cut fabric into rich designs. Unusual Art. Some artists build art from objects they find. They use rusty junk. They use old car parts. They use wheels from toys. They use scrap wire. They use other everyday materials they find. Other artists may take a common object. A water fountain is one example. They place it in a museum display. They want us to look at it in a new way. They want us to see a design. They want us to see art. They do not want us to see only a fountain's job.

7One artist made a funny face. The face uses a wooden cutting board. It uses a circuit board. It uses telephone bells. It uses a telephone keypad. It uses springs. It uses wire. A sand castle can be real art. Sand artists build sculptures on the beach. They use rocks and sand. The following tide may wash the work away. Environmental artists often make artwork for a park. They may make artwork for a garden. The art becomes part of that place. Rocks may be part of the artwork. Graceful tree branches may be part of it. Grasses may be part of it. Other plants may be part of it. Some art is big enough to walk through. An installation of art takes a lot of space. It is often a whole room. It may even be a whole building turned into one artwork. Installation artists build environments. The rooms hold ideas the artist hopes you will feel. Objects may fill the spaces. Colors may fill them. Shapes may fill them. Sounds may fill them. Lights may fill them. Together they make a special atmosphere. Walk on in. One kind of installation turns an entire room into the artwork. Where Art Is Found. Humans spoke through art long before written language. People have found simple drawings cut into rock.

8Those drawings are over forty thousand years old. Caves in France hold drawings of animals. Caves in Spain hold drawings of animals too. Artists made those animal pictures more than thirteen thousand years ago. Scientists study the ruins of ancient cities. They find pottery there. They find wall murals. They find mosaics. They find little carved animals and little carved people. One picture shows an ancient cave drawing on the left. A Pueblo pot is on the right. Artists hope viewers will look at the finished work. Explore and discover. Visit an art museum. Visit an art gallery. Walk through an art fair. One picture shows an art museum. Art placed in public takes many forms. It also comes in many sizes. A mural of a lighthouse makes an interesting illusion. Sculptures of elephants make a park feel playful. Look around you. Somewhere in your own town you can find outdoor art. You might find a tall sculpture. You might find a mural painted across a big wall. Many towns set aside special areas. Artists display their work in those areas. Your town might have a sculpture park. Several art styles can be viewed there together. You could try making art of your own. Pick a kind of art and try it. Use soft colored chalk.

9Draw with it on a sidewalk. Make a painting that shows your pet. Make a frame for that picture. Use clay to make a sculpture. Snow can be the material. Sand can be the material. Bind small groups of sticks. Make them into an animal shape. Spray the shape with color. Glue pieces of shells onto a picture frame. Add stones. Add wood. Put a family photo in that frame. All of it is art. Artists use artwork to tell a story. They also use artwork to show how they feel. Art does not have to be serious. It does not have to be hard to do. Artwork can be interesting. It can be colorful. It can be funny. Sometimes the only goal is beauty. Seeing art and enjoying it makes life more fun. Look for art around you. Art is everywhere. Word List. Blowpipe is a noun on page 12. It means a long hollow metal tube. Air blown through it makes bubbles in melted glass. Kiln is a noun on page 11. It means a very hot oven. Clay bakes into ceramic inside it. Loom is a noun on page 14. It means a machine that turns yarn into cloth strips. Casting is a noun on page 9. It means filling a mold with hot metal, then waiting while the metal hardens.

10Mosaics is a noun on page 19. It means designs built from tiny colored pieces. The pieces may be paper, tile, or beads. Ceramic is a noun on page 11. It means clay that was baked until dry and now holds its form. Murals is a noun on page 7. It means big paintings made on walls or on buildings. Chisel is a noun on page 8. It means a sharp steel tool. People use it to shape stone, wood, or metal. Pottery wheel is a noun on page 10. It means a spinning wheel. People set clay on it and shape round forms. Found object art is a noun on page 16. It means art built from ordinary things the artist came across. Soft sculptures is a noun on page 8. It means sculptures built from cloth, yarn, or materials that feel soft. Glaze is a noun on page 11. It means a special paint that seals ceramics or decorates them. Weaver is a noun on page 14. It means an artist who makes fabric from yarn or thread. A loom is often the tool. Installation is a noun on page 18. It means a whole room or a whole building made into one work of art.

13. Australia, Country and Continent

1Commonwealth of Australia is the official name of this land. Oz is the name Australians use for it. Real life is the source, yet the place is so unusual it could seem like a fantasy story. No island on Earth is larger. No other country is a continent as well. The smallest continent is this one. The flattest continent is this one too. Beautiful beaches are here. Amazing wildlife is here. Fascinating is one word for the history. Troubling is another. Prisoners sent from Great Britain formed the first European colony. A great impact on the island's Indigenous peoples came from colonization. South of the equator, in Earth's southern half, is where Australia lies. Seasons there run opposite to seasons in places north of the equator. Summer in Australia is what arrives when North America has winter. Australian winter runs from June through August. Next you can learn about this country. You can learn about this continent. You can learn about this island, the land called Down Under. Melbourne, Australia, is the site of Tirhatuan Wetlands Conservation Reserve.

2Land area comes to 7,692,024 square kilometers. That equals 2,969,907 square miles. The west is bordered by the Indian Ocean. The south is bordered by it too. The northern edge meets the Pacific Ocean. The eastern edge meets it too. Three main areas divide Australia. The Western Plateau is one. The Central Lowlands are one. The Eastern Highlands are one. Over half the country is the Western Plateau. Flat desert and hot weather describe it. The world's oldest rocks have been found there by scientists. Dry grasslands fill the Central Lowlands. Heat lasts all year in the daytime. Nights can turn very cold. The Eastern Highlands include the Great Dividing Range. Rivers run in that range. Valleys lie there. The highest mountains in Australia stand there. Desert is one habitat. Grasslands are another. Rainforests are another. Heathlands are another. Coastal ground is the usual place for heathland. Low plants cover that ground. A tropical climate belongs to northern Australia. Mild weather is mostly what the southeast has. Mild weather is mostly what the southwest has too.

3Desert is mainly what fills the middle. Very few people live in most areas. Cities hold around ninety percent of Australians today. Coasts are where those cities mainly sit. Long distances separate most of the cities. The capital is Canberra. The largest city is Sydney. Its population is more than five million. The Outback is the name of the huge dry inland. People there are few. Enormous deserts fill the Outback. Mountainous regions are there too. Forever is how far the land seems to stretch. Years can pass with no rain. A few buildings are often the whole of a town. Far apart is how those towns sit. Stations, which are enormous ranches, are where some residents live and work. Mining is the work of other people. Oil production is the work of others. Species of plants and animals number over twenty thousand. Gum trees, also called eucalyptus, are the most common tree. Many Indigenous peoples lived in Australia thousands of years ago. About one million is a number some people suggest.

4A lower number is what others suggest. Forever is how the continent was changed once the British colonized it. Inhabitants now number more than 25 million. White Australians were around 91 percent in 2020. Asian Australians were around 7 percent. Indigenous peoples were under 3 percent. Languages spoken by different Indigenous groups number more than 250. Indigenous Australians are counted as two distinct groups. The mainland is home to Aboriginal peoples. Torres Strait Islanders are the other group. A stark halt came to Indigenous life in the seventeenth century, after fifty thousand years in Australia. Exploration by Europeans began. Colonizing began too. Overcrowded British prisons were a problem Britain hoped Australia could solve. England sent a settlement in 1788. Prisoners in that group numbered around 759. Men numbered 568. Women numbered 191. Soldiers numbered 200. Wives and children of the soldiers numbered 40. Tents were the homes. Native animals were eaten. Food sent from England was eaten too. Sydney is where a commander sets the British flag in place.

5More than 500 Indigenous groups lived in Australia before that colonization. A language of its own belonged to each group. A culture of its own belonged to each group. About 90 percent of the Indigenous population was killed between 1788 and 1798. Violent meetings with the British killed some. Diseases brought by the colonizers killed others. Lands were still fought for. Cultures were kept. Languages were kept. Traditions were kept. About 117,000 is how low the Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander population had fallen by 1901. Numbers from before colonization are about where it has climbed since then. Tens of thousands of children were taken by the government between 1910 and the 1970s. Aboriginal families lost children. Torres Strait Islander families lost children too. Adoption by white Australians followed. Indigenous cultures and traditions were kept away from the children by force. The Stolen Generation is the name for these children. Far is how far the impact has reached. Recovery from that terrible experience is still a struggle. The year 2008 brought members of the Stolen Generation together.

6An apology to Indigenous Australians came that year from the Australian Prime Minister. Wildlife felt the effects of Australia's history too. Wild areas shrank as cities grew. Some animals found habitats growing scarce. Food grew scarce too. A fitting home for some animals comes from the continent's isolation and harsh conditions. Nowhere else on Earth is where some of these animals live. Marsupials, such as kangaroos and wallabies, make up many of the mammals. Tiny babies are what these animals give birth to. Development continues only if the baby stays in a pouch on the mother's body. A pocket is what that pouch is like. Other continents lost most marsupials long ago. Some remain in South America. Many kinds of marsupials could thrive because Australia was isolated. Why that happened is not exactly clear to scientists. About four weeks is the age of one baby kangaroo, called a joey. Australia alone is where the platypus lives. Four legs are part of its body. Fur is part of it.

7Beady eyes are part of it. A beaver's tail is what its tail is like. A duck's bill is what its bill is like. Webbed feet are what it has. Back ankles of adult males carry venomous spurs. Eggs are laid by the females, even though a platypus is a mammal. The sea floor is where it hunts with its beak. Off the northeastern coast lies the Great Barrier Reef, one of the most extraordinary places. The world's largest coral reef system is what it is. Fish types there number over 1,500. Coral kinds number 2,000. Other sea creatures live there. Sea plants live there. Birds live there. A major threat is climate change. A major threat is fishing. A major threat is coastal construction. A major threat is pollutants. Saving the reef is work many people are doing. Algae that live on coral are cast out when reef water grows too warm from climate change. All white is the color the coral turns. Starving is what begins next. Death is likely for bleached coral.

8Survival is still possible. Mitchell Falls, also called Punamii-unpuu, is one Australian sight. Sunshine is the usual weather. The beach is a favorite for many Australians. Time on the water is a favorite too. Swimming is a typical activity. Surfing is one. Snorkeling is one. Scuba diving is one. Boating is one. Shark Bay is the largest bay. The western part of the island is where it lies. Wildlife there comes in an incredible array. Breathtaking gorges fill the Kimberley region in the west. Rocky shores are there. Waterfalls are there. Rivers are there. Beaches are there. Bondi Beach lies to the southeast of Sydney. It is among the famous beaches of the world. Three or four days on a train can cross the whole continent. Different habitats pass by the riders. Rocky peaks of the Flinders Ranges are among the breathtaking sights. Gorges there can be seen too. The Outback is where the Flinders Ranges stand. Disappointment is not what an Outback trip brings. Hiking is part of a visit.

9Wildlife is discovered. Wild water holes are for swimming. A single rock, called a monolith, is Uluru, and it is the world's largest. The Anangu peoples hold it as a sacred site. Alice Springs lies about two hundred miles to the east of it. The Red Centre is the name of that region. An important stop is Sydney. The harbor is enjoyed by guests. The Sydney Harbor Bridge is enjoyed too, and it is the world's largest steel arch bridge. Guests also enjoy the Sydney Opera House. Few modern buildings are known as widely. Millions of roof tiles cover the Opera House. Many unique paths of development came from Australia's isolated location. Natural beauty is a reason people celebrate the island. The world's biggest island is one name for it. People also know it as a country and a continent at the same time, the only one like that. No other place on Earth is quite like the land Down Under. Unique creatures are part of that. Incredible landscapes are part of it.

10Surfing the waves on the country's beautiful beaches is a pleasure for many Australians. Shockingly beautiful or delightful is what breathtaking means. Past events are what history means. Isolation means standing apart. The apartness can be from people or from things. Setting up rule by another country over a place is what colonized means. The visible features of a piece of land, seen together, are a landscape. One of Earth's main land divisions is a continent. The divisions are Africa and South America. They are Asia and Europe. They are North America, Australia, and Antarctica. Mammals whose young are born not yet ready for life on their own are marsupials. The mother's pouch is where that development goes on. A population is every member of a single species that lives in one area. Different enough to notice is what distinct means. Unusual or amazing is what extraordinary means. A new place where people go to live is a settlement. The natural homes of plants and of animals are habitats.

14. Barack Obama's Path to the Presidency

1High school is the setting of a photo of young Barack Obama. Contents. The opening is marked 4. Growing up is marked 5. Finding his way is marked 9. Becoming a politician is marked 14. The closing is marked 18. The word list is marked 19. Opening. This book tells the story of Barack Obama. He became the first Black president. Doubt in himself showed up at times during childhood. He often felt that he did not fit in. Other Black people, struggling inside their own communities, became part of what he studied. That learning inspired him to help people. A better world was what he hoped to build. Practices that had treated him as an outsider became the target of a growing passion. Devotion to a better world set him on a path. The path led him to become president. He served as president number 44 for the United States. A caption calls him the future president. Ann, his mother, appears with young Barry in a photo. Growing up. His full name was Barack Obama Jr. During childhood, people called him Barry. Birth came in 1961. Hawaii was the place. Ann Dunham, his mother, came from the state of Kansas. Barack Obama Sr., his father, came from Kenya. Kenya is a country in eastern Africa. The parents met while they were students. They married while they were students. The school was the University of Hawaii. The marriage did not last. By Barry's fourth birthday, his parents had divorced.

2Kenya was home again for his father. When Barry was six, his mother married again. Her new husband was from Indonesia. Southeast Asia is the region of Indonesia, a nation of islands. Hawaii was left behind when the family settled in Indonesia. A half-sister was born there. Four years of school in Indonesia followed for Barry. He learned the language of his new country. He learned its customs. He also saw daily life in low-income areas. Many Indonesian families struggled every day. They struggled just to have enough food. Barry sometimes felt strange so far from Hawaii. The community around him did not feel like his own. He wondered who he was. Belonging, and where it might be, stayed an open question. His parents were of different races. They came from different continents. Those facts made him wonder and question even more. A map of his beginnings marks Kansas. It marks the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean. It marks Hawaii, Kenya, and Indonesia. Lessons from his mother began during the Indonesia years. The lessons were about race and ethnicity. African enslavement was one subject she taught. That history took place in the United States. The civil rights movement entered his lessons too. In the 1960s, that movement brought an end to laws that kept people segregated in America. Ann taught him about important Black people. Politics was one of their fields. History, music, culture, and sports were the others.

3They helped improve the world. She wanted Barry to feel proud of his racial heritage. She also explained a harder truth. Skin color left some people feeling ashamed. Skin color alone led some people to call others wrong or ignorant. Religion could be the reason for that judgment too. Barry began to see a complication. Race and ethnicity were not simple. How can people feel pride when unfair judgment lands on the very things they prize? Martin Luther King Jr. was a civil rights leader in the United States. He inspired Barry. After four years in Indonesia, Ann made a decision. Barry would return to the United States for more schooling. In 1971 Barry was ten. Grandparents in Hawaii became his home again. A photo shows Barry and his grandparents. In Hawaii he again felt like an outsider. His class had only two Black students, and he was one of them. Classmates mocked the name they found unusual. He gained strength from books. Outsiders who still found a place filled those books. Those people had found a way in the world. Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. inspired him. Other leaders inspired him too. They believed in social change that stayed peaceful. A fact to know. His father's visit from Kenya came in the same year Barry came back to Hawaii. The visit lasted a month. Barry never saw his father again after that visit. A car accident killed his father in 1982.

4Cancer killed his mother in 1995. Finding his way. College in Los Angeles came after high school back in Hawaii. During that time he spoke out, along with other students. They spoke against the white government of South Africa. Black people suffered terrible treatment with that government's support. His African heritage became something Barry chose to embrace. Barack, the name from Kenya, was the one he began to use. Barack means blessed. He started to understand he did not have to choose. A white background was part of him. A Black background was part of him too. A photo shows Barack as a student at Columbia. He wanted to learn how laws work. He wanted to learn how government works. He wanted to help change injustices he saw. Columbia University became his school after a transfer in 1981. The university is in New York City. He graduated in 1983. His degree was in political science. By the end of college, he knew what he wanted. Communities of people were where he hoped to help. He wanted people involved in building better lives for themselves. Fair treatment, he believed, required changes in local laws. Chicago became his home in 1985, after a job opened there. He became director of an organization. Finding jobs was one way the group helped low-income neighborhoods. It helped them improve their lives. A fact to know. A short job at a big business came before the move to Chicago.

5The pay was good. He was not happy. He hungered to join work larger than his own life. He quit. He found work where he could help people. Later he said earning money is fine. A life aimed only at making a buck, he said, shows a poverty of ambition. A photo shows a Chicago neighborhood that he helped. A photo shows Chicago residents signing up to vote. Obama helped set up job training. Black workers who had lost jobs were the ones trained. The jobs vanished when factories closed. Asbestos drew public attention with his help. Asbestos is a dangerous material. Walls inside low-income housing held this material. A tutoring program was launched by his organization. College hopes shaped the young people this program served. New voters were signed up through a program he managed. Politics, he told people, was worth joining. Fairer laws could then be passed. Chicago work made that passion stronger. The passion was helping people in low-income areas. The work also showed him a limit. Lasting change required skill at changing laws. He believed unfair laws could be rewritten. Communities could then improve life for their people. He entered Harvard Law School in 1988. The school is near Boston. While there, he led a respected magazine. The magazine was the Harvard Law Review. No Black person had led that magazine before him. The year 1991 brought him a law degree. He graduated with honors.

6A fact to know. Before law school began in 1988, he traveled to Kenya. It was his first trip there. He met many relatives. He met his grandmother. Mama Sara Obama, grandmother from Kenya, stands with him in a photo. Daughters join Barack and Michelle Obama in a 2004 photo. Malia, said mah-LEE-uh, was 6 and stands on the right. Sasha was 3. Chicago welcomed him again once law school ended. He worked for a law company that summer. Another lawyer, Michelle Robinson, advised him. They became friends. Obama invited Robinson on a visit. The 1980s communities he had helped were the places they visited. Robinson was deeply moved by his vision of a better world. She saw the respect people felt for him. They married in 1992. Another photo shows the family campaigning in 2004. Barack was running to be a senator for the United States. Becoming a politician. Obama had become a lawyer in order to help people. He soon saw that lawyer's help moved very slowly. He decided politics could help faster. Changing laws would be the tool. The 1996 race aimed at a senate seat for the state of Illinois. He won. Tax laws were among the laws he helped rewrite in that senate job. Lower taxes became possible for working families whose incomes were low. He also helped change laws for children. The changes aimed at better education. They also aimed at better health care.

7The United States Senate race of 2004 included Barack Obama. An important Democratic Party meeting invited him to speak. The speech was hopeful. It said Americans could work together. A still greater country, he said, could grow if Americans worked as one. Success for the speech was large. His words inspired many people. A Senate seat came to him easily before that year ended. Many listeners of that speech pushed him toward a presidential run. No job in the United States is bigger than the presidency. The year they had in mind was 2008. Obama said change will not arrive if people wait for somebody else. It will not arrive if they wait for a later date. He said the listeners were the people they had been hoping for. He said they themselves were the change they wanted. The 2004 Democratic National Convention is where a photo catches him speaking. He told the crowd the night was a special honor for him. He said a life like his made a place on that stage unlikely. His father had been a student from another country. The father was born in Kenya and raised in a village that was small. As a boy, the father herded goats. School for the father was a shack, and tin covered its roof. His mother was born in Kansas. Kansas lay on a distant side of the world from Kenya. The parents shared a deep trust in what this nation might make possible.

8They gave him an African name, Barack. Barack means blessed. They believed a tolerant America would not let his name block success. They imagined the best schools for him, even though they were not rich. They believed a generous America does not require wealth before a person can reach full potential. He said he stood there grateful for a heritage of many parts. He said his parents' dreams lived on in his two daughters. He said his story belongs to the wider story of America. He said Earth offers no other country where a story like his could even happen. He spoke those words at the Democratic National Convention on July 27, 2004. Obama had to be convinced about running for president. He needed to believe it was best for him. He needed to believe it was best for his family. He thought his time as a U.S. senator was too short. He also knew a danger. Because he was Black, a run for president could be dangerous. He knew the run itself would change his life forever. It would change his family's life forever. A simple walk would draw a crowd. A haircut would draw a crowd. While he decided, his reputation grew. He had written two books. Letters of encouragement poured in from readers of those books. He kept giving speeches. He kept talking with people. More people felt inspired by his words. They noticed his clear thinking.

9They noticed his respect for all people. They noticed his belief in peaceful answers to big problems. A note says his two books are best sellers. Closing. In November of 2008, voters elected Barack Obama. Voters made him president number 44. No earlier president of the country had been Black. His wife Michelle moved into the White House. Their two young daughters moved in too. The White House is in Washington, D.C. He began the job of president on January 20, 2009. In October of that year he received the Nobel Peace Prize. The prize recognized his efforts. He was working to make the world better. He was working to make it more peaceful. A second term arrived when voters chose him again in 2012. In the nation's leading role, he enacted many changes. The changes helped Americans. They helped people worldwide. One important act helped millions of Americans get healthcare they could afford. He also made strides toward peace with other countries. Future leaders can draw inspiration from that legacy. That inspiration can last for generations. A photo shows him speaking to a huge crowd. The date was November 4, 2008, just after the election. Word list. Ashamed, an adjective on page 7, means embarrassed. Civil rights movement, a noun on page 7, names protests, boycotts, strikes, and further plans from the middle of the 1900s. The goal was to fight segregation and unfair treatment of Black people.

10Continents, a noun on page 6, means one of Earth's main landmasses. Africa is on the list, along with South America, Asia, and Europe. North America, Australia, and Antarctica are on it too. Degree, a noun on page 9, is a title a college or university gives after a student finishes a program of study. Ethnicity, a noun on page 7, means belonging to a people who share culture or nationality. Customs, traits, language, and a shared history can mark the group. Heritage, a noun on page 7, is a way of life, a tradition, or a trait passed from one generation to the next. Honors, a noun on page 12, are awards for outstanding achievement. Injustices, a noun on page 9, means treatment that is not fair, whether of one person or a whole group. Inspired, a verb on page 4, means stirred toward bravery or toward making something new. Local, an adjective on page 10, means limited to one specific place. Moved, a verb on page 13, means caused to feel strong emotion. Passion, a noun on page 4, means intense enthusiasm. Races, a noun on page 6, means human groups marked by skin color and features. Genetics can be the marker too. Reputation, a noun on page 17, is the general way others think of a person or a thing. Segregation, a noun on page 7, is a rule that keeps groups apart. Race is often the reason.

15. Beautiful Stones from the Ground

1Many people see gems as the most beautiful things Earth creates. They will pay thousands of dollars for that beauty. Even a small piece can cost that much. Gems decorate jewelry. The jewelry is among the most beautiful. It is also among the most valuable. Gems fall into three main groups. Crystals are the first group. Stones are the second group. Both groups are made of minerals. Minerals are natural materials that are not alive. Most rocks are made of minerals. Crystals are minerals that grow in tight shapes. They also grow in neat patterns. Stones are minerals mixed together. Their colors are beautiful. Their patterns are beautiful too. Strict shapes are not part of a stone. Organic gems are the third group. Living things make organic gems. Garnet is one crystal. Turquoise is one stone. A natural pearl is one organic gem. Every mineral is built from millions of tiny particles. Those particles are atoms. Most rocks hold atoms that sit in a jumble. In a crystal the atoms sit in a neat, orderly pattern. Flat sides appear on a crystal. Those sides are called faces. The faces make a shape. Some crystal shapes have the names cubic and tetragonal. Other shapes have the names hexagonal, trigonal, and monoclinic.

2A volcano can lift deep material toward the surface. Diamonds form far down in Earth's mantle. Eruptions carry the diamonds up. Magma and heat sit with layers of crust. Gravity presses down as well. Most crystals grow deep underground. The conditions have to be very special. Diamonds can form when layers of rock squeeze a mineral. The squeeze pushes the atoms into the smallest shape they can take. Sapphires can form in another way. A mineral inside Earth grows hot enough to melt. Slow cooling lets the atoms lock into a regular crystal pattern. Opals form in still another way. Minerals dissolve in water. The water evaporates very slowly. The mineral that remains becomes a crystal. Rock salt has another name, halite. Halite forms when salty seas dry up. A similar drying can be watched with table salt and water. Warm a cup of water, then stir in one tablespoon of salt. Keep stirring until the salt disappears into the water. Keep putting in small amounts of extra salt. Stop when the water cannot take any more salt. Set a clean toothpick in the cup. Leave the cup in a warm place in the sun. As the water dries, crystals grow on the toothpick. Crystals grow on the cup sides too.

3A magnifying glass shows the crystals more clearly. Notice the shape you see. Minerals inside stones do not line up in an orderly way. Other minerals may be mixed in. Stones often grow in layers. The layers leave streaks and lines. The name for those lines is grain. Pearls, amber, and coral are organic gems. Living things are their source. A pearl often starts with something trapped in an oyster shell. The trapped thing may be a tiny worm. It may be some other living thing. It may be a broken piece of shell. The oyster lays down coats over it. The coats are the same material used to build the shell. Some people think a grain of sand starts a pearl. That belief is wrong. Oysters are skilled at clearing sand from their shells. Jets of water shoot the sand out. Coral is built from skeletons. Millions of tiny sea animals leave those skeletons behind. Amber started as tree sap millions of years ago. Insects sometimes stuck in the sap. Spiders sometimes stuck in it too. Their fossils often remain inside the amber. Most gems made of minerals lie deep under the ground. Each gem is small. Each gem is rare. Hands still do much of the mining.

4Miners chip the rock. They cut into it while they search for stones. Opals can form when mineral-rich water dries out of underground cracks. A streak of crystal is left in the rock. A streak of crystal is called a vein. The finest opals on Earth come from veins under the ground. Those veins lie in the Australian desert. An opal mine can be a simple hole in the ground. A newly found gem is often very rough. The outside looks dull. The shape is bumpy. Cracks are common. Dark marks are common. Bubbles and other flaws are common too. Cutters shape the gem into a regular form. The form shows the best parts. It hides the flaws. An uncut diamond looks uneven. It also looks flawed. Most crystal gems receive flat surfaces called facets. Facets display the color. They display the pattern. They let the gem sparkle. A finished diamond throws back a lot of sparkle. Several cutting styles are used. Each style can be seen from the top and from the side. The style names are step, brilliant, carving, rose, and cabochon. Each style brings out a gem's best features. Diamonds often receive the brilliant style. Many facets in that style bounce light off the stone. Deep green emeralds often receive the step cut.

5Older gems often show the rose cut. The oldest style is only a smooth rounded polish. Opaque stones get that style most often. Opaque means you cannot see through the stone. Small shapes can be carved from jade. Coral can be carved that way too. Beauty is one reason gems cost so much. Rarity is another reason. Hardness helps set the value. Value also rests on four tests known as the four Cs. Cut is the first C. Carat weight is the second C. Color is the third C. Clarity is the fourth C. Cut is a judgment of how well the gem was shaped and polished. It can also judge beauty the gem already had in nature. Carat weight tells the size of the stone. A single carat is about the weight of one unpopped popcorn kernel. The weight word carat traces back to seeds of the carob tree. For centuries, jewelers used those seeds to weigh gems. The seeds grow inside pods. Seeds from different trees weigh almost the same. Seeds from different pods weigh almost the same too. A carat today is about equal to one carob seed. Color is another of the four Cs. Color is one cause of a gem's beauty.

6Ordinary rocks do not have the bright color of a gem. Gem color is pure. Gem color is strong. A purer, stronger color raises the value. One mineral can sometimes make gems of more than one color. Red rubies and blue sapphires hold the same mineral. Tiny bits of other chemicals create the different colors. Those bits mix with the main mineral. Clarity tells how free of flaws a gem is. Dark marks lower the value. Cracks lower it. Bubbles lower it. A cloudy gem is worth less than a clearer gem. Clarity often matters less than size. It often matters less than rarity. Emeralds often carry many flaws. A flawed emerald can still be worth more than a diamond with no flaws. Emeralds are that rare. An uncut emerald may show many cracks. Other flaws may show too. Hardness tells how pure the mineral in a gemstone is. It also tells how tightly the mineral is built. Friedrich Mohs was a geologist who made a hardness scale. He scratched one gem with a second gem. A gem can scratch only a softer gem. Nothing on Earth is harder than diamond. A diamond can scratch any other substance. Only a second diamond can scratch a diamond. The Mohs scale lines gems up from soft to hard.

7On this scale, fingernails score 2.5. A penknife measures 5.5. Number 1 on the scale is talc. Number 2 is gypsum. Number 3 is calcite. Number 4 is fluorite. Number 5 is apatite. Number 6 is orthoclase. Number 7 is quartz. Number 8 is topaz. Number 9 is emerald. Number 10 is diamond. Apatite sits in the middle of this hardness scale. A world chart lists diamond, sapphire, and turquoise. It also lists emerald, quartz, opal, pearl, ruby, and jade. The places named on the chart include Africa and North America. South America and Europe are named too. Asia, Australia, and the South Pacific are on the chart too. Nothing found in nature is harder than a diamond. A diamond is able to cut other materials. Flawed diamonds are often placed on tools. Saws get those diamonds. Drill tips get them too. The edge can cut metal. It can cut stone. A very rare diamond may be yellow. It may be red. It may be blue. Engagement rings often use diamonds. Fame surrounds the Hope diamond more than most other diamonds. It is not the biggest diamond. Its color is an unusual sky blue. The biggest diamond ever found came from South Africa.

8Its weight was over 3,100 carats. Its size matched a pineapple. Graphite, also called pencil lead, is the same mineral as diamond. Graphite atoms sit in loose layers. The loose layers make graphite very soft. A rub on paper knocks atoms loose. The loose atoms leave a pencil mark. Inside a diamond those same atoms are pressed tightly together. Only another diamond can scratch them away. Rubies and sapphires are the same mineral. A ruby is always red like blood, or else it is pink. Every other color of that mineral counts as a sapphire. A red ruby may be shown next to two sapphires. One sapphire is blue. The other sapphire is yellow. People first wore birthstones in the Middle Ages. Some people tied a birthday to certain stars. They also tied it to planets, flowers, and gems. The gem was thought to bring good luck. It was thought to bring protection. It was thought to bring wealth. Lists of birthstones are not the same in every place. One current list used in the United States runs through the year. January is garnet. February is amethyst. March is aquamarine. April is diamond. May is emerald. June is pearl. July is ruby. August is peridot. September is sapphire.

9October is opal. November is topaz. December is turquoise. Among all crystals, the quartz family appears most often. Quartz turns up in every part of the world. Its colors cover the whole rainbow. Opal is the quartz with the highest value. Quartz crystals are common. They can grow to a very large size. Emeralds are a different kind of crystal. A strong green color is what they are known for. Gem-quality emeralds are rare. They are usually small. The first emeralds were found in Egypt. That find was several thousand years ago. Colombia supplies most of the emeralds in the world today. Few stones look more beautiful than jade. Jade can be lavender. Jade can be white. Jade can be almost any shade of green. Green jade is the shade worth the most. Carvers shape jade into figurines. They carve larger jade pieces too. Black specks and black stripes often mark turquoise. Blue-green turquoise often shows pretty spots. Streaks run through it as well. Silver settings hold much of the turquoise found worldwide. A natural pearl is not a perfect circle. Natural pearls are very rare. An oyster needs two years or more to make a pearl. Nearly all the round pearls in jewelry shops come from pearl farms.

10Farmers place a round bead of shell inside an oyster. The oyster then builds a pearl that is perfectly round. The work can take six months. It can take several years. The oyster type sets the time. Natural pearls have an odd shape. Cultured pearls from the farms are round. A strip mine clears away a huge stretch of land. Lovely gems occur in many parts of the world. For centuries, people have killed to get them. People have died trying to get them. Digging gems with dynamite is often dangerous. Strip mining is often dangerous too. The work hurts people. The work hurts Earth. Scientists can grow gems in a lab. Lab gems can look like the finest gems from nature. The lab gems cost a thousand times less. Someday, made gems may meet the wish for gemstone beauty. They may do so while Earth and its people stay safer. Atoms are the tiny bits that build every substance. Crystals are minerals whose parts lock into a neat repeating pattern. Facets are the flat faces cut onto a gem. Grain is the streaky pattern left by mineral layers in a stone. Opaque describes a thing you cannot see through. Organic gems come from materials that living things produce.

16. Boats That Opened the World

1Contents. The opening is marked 4. The early years are marked 5. The Polynesians are marked 9. The Vikings are marked 11. The Age of Discovery is marked 13. The Chinese are marked 15. The later years are marked 17. The closing is marked 19. The word list is marked 20. Opening. Rivers, lakes, and seas were common places for people to settle. Long ago, people near large bodies of water felt curious. They wondered what lay past the horizon. They also thought water travel might be easier than land travel. Those thoughts led someone to build a simple boat. As boats and ships grew more practical, people could leave home and go farther. Each new kind of boat improved on the one before it. The newer boats were often safer. They carried more cargo. They felt more comfortable. For all but the last 150 years or so, goods mostly moved by boat. They also moved people from place to place. This book looks at boats used to explore the world. It also looks at how those boats changed as time passed. A map marks Egypt, Africa, and the Nile River. The early years. Egypt gives us the oldest record of travel by sea.

2More than 6,000 years have passed since Egyptians began water travel. They built boats by hollowing out tree trunks. Other cultures did the same when they needed to cross water. Those boats were sturdy. They could not carry very much. Later, bigger loads were what people hoped to shift along waterways. They wanted boats that could hold many people. They wanted room for food, for animals, and for goods to trade. Canoes cut from oak trees worked well on a lake. They worked well going down a river. They did not work as well for large loads. Picture a canoe on a lake with six sheep crowded around the rower. The space was simply too small. People have always felt driven to improve things. Long ago, that drive led them to seek boats that were bigger. They also wanted boats that were faster and safer. Better boats were needed to explore places far from home. One picture shows a dugout canoe. One picture shows a birchbark canoe. Builders next formed a frame out of wooden beams. Wooden planks then covered the frame. Bark from trees could cover it instead. Some cultures stretched animal skins over the frame. These boats were bigger.

3They were more streamlined. They could hold more cargo. Flat lengths of wood over a wooden frame were an Egyptian first. The result was a hull. A hull is a boat's outer shell. Some Egyptian boats reached 21.3 meters in length. That length is 70 feet. Egyptians were also first to use sails. Sails meant fewer people had to row. Some larger boats used rowers and sails together. A picture shows a Greek ship. Soon the Greeks built large boats driven by sails. Peoples living around the Mediterranean Sea followed that path. Two masts stood on sailing ships about 3,000 years ago. Each mast held a sail. A second mast made steering easier. It also made the ships much faster. Five hundred years after that, the Greeks built a ship with four sails. Some of the fastest ships of the time were made for battle. Some warships stacked rowers on three levels along each side. Larger ships let people travel to distant places and explore them. Exploration left the Mediterranean Sea behind in this period. Trade did too. The Polynesians. In the South Pacific, Pacific Island peoples built boats for the open ocean. Eastward travel by these people began over 3,500 years ago.

4They went well past the islands where they lived. They also explored waters off the Asian mainland. Their boat was an early form of today's catamaran. They joined two canoes with large wooden poles. A deck was built across those poles. Length usually fell between 15 and 18 meters. That length is 50 to 60 feet. The deck in the center held masts for sails. Several people could stay on that deck, with supplies, for a voyage that lasted a long time. A picture shows a Polynesian catamaran. These early boats made travel from island to island safer. Each new island they found became a place they settled. Near the opening of 1000 CE, almost every Pacific island they could reach had become home. Hawaii was one new home. New Zealand was another. Rapa Nui, also called Easter Island, was another. These sailors were highly skilled. They knew the ocean so well that wave size and wave shape told them where they were. Island hops let them cover great distances with ease. Sea travel became an ordinary way of life. A map of the Pacific Ocean marks Asia, the Hawaiian Islands, Easter Island, Australia, and New Zealand.

5Lines show the routes of Polynesian expansion. The Vikings. Another group started larger boats over 1,300 years ago. These people were the Vikings. Northern Europe was their home. They were known as warriors and conquerors. Travel over water was normal for them. In the year 700, no ocean boat outperformed theirs. A picture shows a Viking knarr. The trading boats were called knarrs, said kuh-NARS. Trading was their main use. A knarr could be as long as 16.5 meters and as wide as 4.5 meters. Those measures are 54 feet and 15 feet. A flat bottom let the knarr carry heavy loads. The boats were stable. They could cross the open ocean in safety. Vikings were willing to enter the unknown in order to conquer new lands. North America received its first European visitors in these sailors. They also built warships called longships. A longship looked the same at the front and at the back. Watchers often could not tell whether it was coming or going. That design let attackers change direction with ease. Speed for a longship could reach 15 knots. A knot is a unit for speed on water. A big longship might hold 200 warriors. Big ships, high speed, easy handling, and a fierce will to fight made the Vikings hard to beat.

6Ocean rule stayed with them for close to three centuries. A picture shows a Viking longship. The Age of Discovery. Until the 1400s, most sailors stayed close to home waters. The Age of Discovery changed that in a dramatic way. Every European country with sailing ships wanted new lands. The prize in that race was the Americas. Shipbuilders had to make ships that were faster and safer. In the 1400s and the 1500s, explorers used small, sturdy ships called caravels. Three masts carried the sails. Speed and easy steering came from this design. Square sails stood on the front mast and the middle mast. A sail shaped like a triangle stood on the back mast. Think about the people already living in the lands that were found. What did these voyages do to them? Too often, the result was tragic. Christopher Columbus led some of these trips. Hernan Cortes led others. Europeans like them often exploited the land they found. They often exploited the people too. Greed was usually the motive. Before long, ships also carried people across oceans to enslave them far from home.

7A picture shows a caravel. Christopher Columbus sailed three ships of this kind. Length for the biggest one was 27.4 meters. Width was 9.1 meters. Those measures are 90 feet and 30 feet. A modern ocean liner dwarfs a caravel. A modern cargo ship does too. Small as they were, Ferdinand Magellan set out with five of them. He meant to sail around the world. His crew numbered 260 sailors. Almost three years after the start, one ship finished the circle of the world. Eighteen of the original sailors finished with it. Magellan was not among the survivors. Death found him in the Philippine Islands. The Chinese. Explorers from Europe were crossing the world's seas. China, in a different region, had sent its own sailors onto the sea. They were skilled sailors. Zheng He was their most famous explorer. Sixty-two ships made up the fleet that traveled with him. European ships were much smaller than these. All three ships of Columbus could have rested on the deck of Zheng He's biggest ship. The huge ship was 121 meters long and 45.7 meters wide. Those measures are 400 feet and 150 feet. Support boats, smaller in size, numbered over one hundred.

8Those support boats traveled beside the main traders, and the traders numbered sixty-two. A map marks Asia, China, the Pacific Ocean, Africa, the Indian Ocean, and Australia. Chinese trips for exploration and trade are drawn as a line. A picture shows a Chinese junk. Trade was the reason these explorers reached Africa. War with Asian neighbors then pulled Chinese attention away from exploration. A fact to know. Christopher Columbus sailed with a whole crew of about 90 sailors. They filled three ships. One of his voyages used over 162 ships. He also had 30,000 sailors. The later years. As sea exploration went on, the Spanish led the way. By the 1500s, ship designs in Europe had improved. The newest ships were called galleons. Size, speed, and comfort were all greater. Length could reach 42.7 meters. That is 140 feet. Even then, Chinese ships were still bigger. Many galleons had living quarters that were nice and comfortable. A galleon could serve as a trading vessel. It could also serve as a warship. Size let a galleon carry many cannons. Those cannons made it dangerous in battle. A picture shows a galleon. British captain James Cook, in the late 1700s, carried out explorations among the greatest of that age.

9His first ship was Endeavour. A full circle of the world was Cook's trip aboard Endeavour. The ship was only 32 meters long, or 105 feet. Many other ships were larger than this one. Its hold was shallow. Fully loaded, the ship sat only 4.3 meters deep, or 14 feet. Cook was an intelligent explorer. He was a great sailor. He also knew how to keep a crew healthy. Limes and cabbages were among the foods he required them to eat. The foods kept them free from disease. Before Cook's time, as many as half the sailors on a long voyage might die. Scurvy was one cause. Other diseases were causes too. Cook knew his success depended on a healthy crew. A picture shows Endeavour. Closing. Most coasts of the world had been explored by sailors around the midpoint of the 1800s. They had mapped those coasts too. Homelands were left farther behind as people settled. Great distances called for better ships if people and supplies were to move. Clipper ships were fast. People crossed oceans on them within a few days. Some clipper ships carried as many as thirty-five sails. Within only a few years, the steam engine replaced wind power.

10The age of sailing ships ended. A picture shows a clipper ship. Designers of those early exploring ships deserve admiration. Their skill created those ships of discovery. Trips into unknown waters helped shape today's world. Word list. Caravels, a noun on page 13, names the small sail-driven ships of the 1400s and the 1500s. Catamaran, a noun on page 9, is a vessel under sail. Two hulls are joined by a deck. Galleons, a noun on page 17, are heavy Spanish sailing ships from the 15th century into the early 18th century. War or trade was their work. Hull, a noun on page 7, means the chief body of a ship that sails. Knarrs, a noun on page 11, are Viking ships used by merchants.
