Knots, a noun on page 12, are units of a ship's speed. One knot equals one nautical mile each hour. Scurvy, a noun on page 18, is a life-threatening illness common among early sailors. Too little vitamin C was the cause. Streamlined, an adjective on page 7, means shaped so movement through air or water is easy. Vessels, a noun on page 17, are ships or large boats.

17. Bright Beaks of the Cold Oceans

1A horned puffin comes down toward a rock. Alaska Maritime National Wildlife Refuge is the home of that rock. People also know puffins by a funny name. They call the birds clowns of the sea. See a puffin once. The sight stays with you. Even a person who has never seen one may know the shape. The body is plump and little. The feathers are black and white. The beak is huge. Color covers that beak. The look is funny. The look is unusual. That look is one reason people enjoy these birds. People like to watch them. People like to learn about them. Puffins are seabirds. A seabird spends most of life on the open ocean. Puffins are skillful swimmers. In the water, the wings act as flippers. The feet steer. A puffin can dive 100 feet or deeper.

2That depth is 30 m. It can stay under for about half a minute. Puffins fly well too. They walk with ease on rocks. They walk with ease on soil. In the air, the wings can flap 400 times a minute. Under water, swimming looks like flying. The Labrador Sea holds a huge colony of Atlantic puffins. It is among the biggest. The Labrador Sea is near Canada. The North Atlantic Ocean is one ocean these birds use. The North Pacific Ocean is their other ocean home. From the middle of spring through summer, they nest in groups. The nesting places are islands. The nesting places are also coastal cliffs. This time of year is the breeding season. The bright breeding look helps a bird find a mate.

3The beak is large and full of color. Areas of white feathers show. Extra feathers grow for decoration. The webbed feet turn a bright orange. After breeding, the birds remain on the ocean. Black, white, and gray are their main colors then. There are four types of puffins. Each type has marks that set it apart. The scientific name of the Atlantic puffin has a meaning. The meaning is little brother of the north. A grown Atlantic puffin stands 10 to 12.5 inches high. That height is 25 to 32 cm. Both shores of the North Atlantic Ocean have these birds. They are the smallest puffins. In breeding dress, they are the least fancy. Bright orange colors the tip of the beak. A blue-gray part sits on the beak. Yellow trim edges that part.

4The feet are orange. The face is white. The chest is white. The belly is white. Adult males and females of each type look the same. A male may be a bit bigger. Young puffins look like adults that are not in breeding dress. Pictures show breeding feathers and plainer feathers. A grown horned puffin is about 15 inches high on average. That height is 38 cm. The ocean home of horned puffins is the North Pacific. In the breeding season, the face is white. The chest is white. The belly is white. Those colors make them look like Atlantic puffins. The base of the beak is yellow. An Atlantic beak is blue-gray in that spot. A thin set of black feathers sits above each eye. People call those feathers eyelashes.

5The eyelashes make the bird look made up. A grown tufted puffin is about 15 inches high on average. That height is 38 cm. Both shores of the North Pacific Ocean have tufted puffins. Alaska is their northern limit. They live as far south as Japan. They also live as far south as central California. The chest is black. The belly is black. In winter, the body is black. The winter beak is large and red-orange. Breeding dress adds a white mask. An olive-yellow section shows on the beak. Orange rings circle the eyes. Long gold feathers grow above the eyes. Pictures show breeding feathers and plainer feathers. People know the other puffins better than rhinoceros auklets. They also look very different. For that reason, some people see them as close cousins.

6Those people do not call them true puffins. Adults average 13.8 to 15 inches tall. That height is 35 to 38 cm. Their homes follow North America's Pacific shore. Asia's shore near Japan is another home. In the breeding season, the bill is thin and yellow-orange. A short horn sits at the base of the bill. The back is dark brown. The chest is a lighter brown. The belly is a lighter brown. Each side of the head shows two narrow white feather lines. Late March or early April is the usual return from the ocean. Huge flocks are common while they breed. A group may hold several thousand birds. A bird usually keeps the same mate. The pair comes back to the same nesting site each year. Once back, each pair prepares a burrow.

7She produces only one egg. Some types dig burrows in soft soil. Other types nest in openings between rocks. Those rocks are on cliffs. A chick that has just hatched is called a puffling. Warming the egg is a job both parents share. Both parents feed the chick. Feeding lasts one to two months. It lasts until the chick can fly. A puffin becomes an adult at about five years of age. A puffin may live twenty years or more. Puffins can stack fish in the beak. An average stack holds ten fish. Puffins mainly eat small fish. In winter, fewer fish can be found. They may also eat squid. They may eat shrimp. They may eat crabs. They may eat other small animals. Adult puffins eat under the water.

8They also collect fish to carry back to the chicks. A beak can hold fifteen fish or more. A rough tongue holds the fish. Spines on the roof of the mouth hold them too. Some puffins have carried sixty fish or more. The hunt for puffins goes back hundreds of years. They wanted the meat. They wanted the eggs. They wanted the feathers. The hunt continues in some regions. Other human acts also threaten these birds. Fishers take vast amounts of the cold-water fish these birds need. Climate change makes ocean water warmer. Warmer water means less food for puffins. Chicks cannot swallow some fish that are common now. Those fish are too large. Fishing nets trap these birds. Pollution hurts them. Swallowing plastics hurts them. People have bothered many nesting areas. Other animals hunt puffins as well.

9Gulls hunt them. Eagles hunt them. Rats hunt them. Foxes hunt them. Near puffin homes, people try to solve the threats. They want these amazing seabirds to stay alive. A photo shows a tufted puffin waiting by the water. It may be waiting for fish. It may be looking at its own reflection. Early in the 1970s, a wildlife scientist came to a small island. His name was Dr. Stephen Kress. The island is Eastern Egg Rock. It lies off the coast of Maine. A century earlier, Atlantic puffins had nested on that island. Dr. Kress wondered if the birds could be brought back. He started Project Puffin. He found a way to return the birds to Eastern Egg Rock. The count in 2014 was 148 pairs raising young.

10The National Audubon Society sponsored Project Puffin. Bird safety is part of that group's work. It also works to protect other wildlife. It works to protect their habitats. An Atlantic puffin stands outside a den. Iceland's sea cliffs hold that den. Many months of each year are spent on the ocean. They are on land only during the breeding season. They are found mainly on cliffs. They are found mainly on islands. Those places are hard to reach. The trip is still worth the work. Choose a place that lets people look for wildlife. A boat tour may make puffins easier to see. On a boat, you can often get closer. Take binoculars with you. Take a camera too. You are sure to enjoy these wonderful birds. They are special birds.

18. Bullying Harms Every Person

1School is not always a place twelve-year-old Gardner likes to go. Safe is not always how he feels there. A sign on his back, inviting kicks or picking on, is how he says it feels. Lunch and recess bring about a dozen boys who tease him on the playground. Mean names are what they call him. Chasing is what they do. Pinecones are what they throw. Shoves have happened. Hits have happened. The ground is where he has been knocked. Bruises have been the result. A broken finger was the result once. The sixth grader says again that it feels like a kick-me or pick-on-me sign. Stupid is what the boys call him. Worth nothing is what they say he is. Gardner's classroom is another place for the name-calling. Whispers carry the names, or the words come while the teacher is busy. Focus grows hard, he says. Assignments from his teacher are sometimes missed. Second grade is how long this same group of boys has teased him and treated him badly. Frustrated is how the treatment makes him. Sad is how it makes him too. Sick of it is what he says he is. Unnoticed, under the radar, is how he would like to stay. Gardner's feelings are known by seven-year-old Lily. First grade at a new school is where she is. Friends have been hard to find. Almost every day, a ringleader says mean things. She heads a group of girls. She tells the others to run away. She tells them not to play with Lily. Bad is how Lily says it makes her feel. Mad is how she sometimes gets. Stop is what she wants to tell the girl.

2Keeping on is what the girl does anyway. Gardner and Lily may match a feeling you have had. Mean words from classmates, or from kids nearby, may have been aimed at you. Mean treatment may have been aimed at you too. Bullying is the name for that behavior. Cruel is what it is. Kids who say they are bullied number seven in every ten. Utah is where both Gardner and Lily live. Anywhere is where bullying can happen. Bullying shows up in so many places that the CDC calls it a major health danger for the whole United States. CDC stands for the Centers for Disease Control. Seeing bullying at school was reported by a large share of students in a 2010 study from the National Center for Education. Witnessing it was reported by more than 55 percent. About 71 percent of students said the bullying at school does not stop. Fear of bullies is why 15 percent of students said they stay away from school. Quitting a school, or changing schools, was reported by about one student in every ten, because the bullying kept happening. Study to study, the statistics differ. A big problem is what experts still agree bullying is. Basic facts come next. Physical pain caused on purpose by aggressive behavior is one face of bullying. Emotional pain is another face. Power over someone thought to be smaller or weaker is the aim. Feelings get hurt by all of us at times. An accident can be the cause. Purpose and repetition are what make bullying different. A long study of bullies has been done by many teachers and by doctors who study human feelings.

3Their findings come next. Any time people interact, bullying can happen. School is one place. Church is one. Neighborhoods are one. Home is one. Kids can be involved. Adults can be involved. Face to face is one way. Saying bad things about a person to others is another way. Body and strength used to hurt someone is physical bullying. Hitting happens. Shoving happens. Spitting happens. Kicking happens. Words used to hurt are verbal bullying. Mean things may be said. Names may be used to embarrass someone. Fun may be made of someone in front of others. Stories about people may be told. Lies may be spread. Shutting someone out of a group, with actions and with words, is social bullying. Friendships can be broken up. Blame for things in class can be set on other kids. Blame for things on the playground can be set on them too. A forced act, such as handing food to a bully, can be part of it. Handing money to a bully can be part of it. Cyberbullying uses technology to hurt. The tools include the Internet. They include email. They include text messages. They include other tools too. Large groups in many places can receive the messages, which makes this kind different. A real name can be left off the messages or the threats. Anonymous is the word for that. Hard to stop is what cyberbullying is. The sender is unknown. Kids on the playground of her school are drawn into talk by teacher Janet Moffat. Bullies are a subject Catherine DePino knows well.

4More than thirty years as a teacher and a school administrator in Philadelphia are behind her. A classroom changed by a bully is something she has seen firsthand. Books on bullying are something she has written. Worse is the direction she says the problem is taking. Far-away attacks are possible with computers. Text messages allow the same. Suffering they cause is less likely to be seen by kids. Feeling that suffering is less likely too, DePino says. Teachers can be pulled away from learning by bullies in a classroom. Other students can be pulled away too. A safe-space talk about bullying is also a chance bullies can give teachers. Kinds of bullying can be the focus of lessons that help. Effects on others can be the focus. Ways to stop can be the focus. Talking it out, with every party gathered, was the best path DePino found as a teacher. The bullied child is one party. The bully is one. The parents are one. Alone is something she says no one can do it. No simple reason explains why some people become bullies, says Professor Susan Limber. Emotions and behavior are the science she studies at Clemson University in South Carolina. Schools free of bullies are something she helps create. Common experiences can raise the chance someone becomes a bully, studies show, she says. One experience is growing up in a home with little love. A home with no rules can be one. A home with very harsh punishments can be one. Boys and girls can both bully, experts say. The ways often differ. Physical violence is what boys mostly use. Words are what girls more often use.

5Social bullying that shuts people out is what girls more often use. Alone is one way a bully can act. A group is another way. Power seems to be a need. Suffering caused to others is something they enjoy. More serious trouble later in life is more likely for bullies, studies show. Crimes can be that trouble. Drugs can be that trouble. Low self-esteem is an old assumption about bullies, Limber says. Something else is what research suggests. They can, so they do, is the reason for many kids who bully. Enjoyment is another reason. Stomachaches often come to kids who are bullied, research shows, Limber says. Headaches may come. Sleep may be hard. School may be a place they fear to go. The bus may be a ride they fear to take. Focus may be hard. Class assignments may be hard to finish. Sadness can fill a lot of time for kids who are targeted. A bad feeling about themselves may follow. Mean words that are not even true may start to be believed. People besides the target can be affected. Fear can grow in children who see bullying. Becoming a target themselves is the fear. Guilt can follow if they did not help a classmate. A bully's target can be who? Simple is the answer. Anyone can be the target. Different is the trait bullies look for, says SuEllen Fried. Three books about bullying have been written by this Kansas expert. Schools across the nation are where she teaches bullying prevention. Too tall or too short can make you a target, she says. Too heavy or too thin can do it.

6Too smart or too slow can do it. Too rich or too poor can do it. Hair of a different color can do it. Skin of a different color can do it. Glasses can do it. Freckles can do it. Braces can do it. Big ears can do it. A move from another school can do it. On and on is how the list goes. Bullying behaviors can be spotted with a short quiz. An unkind mistake is not the same as the cruel path bullies use to gain power. First, do you go after smaller kids, or mock people who seem different? Second, does teasing others, and watching them get upset, please you? Third, do you pass on rumors, or say cruel things when the person cannot hear? Fourth, do you leave some people out of your friend group on purpose? Fifth, do you like taking other people's things, or wrecking them? Sixth, do you want to be seen as the toughest kid around? Seventh, have you threatened to hurt someone, or punched, shoved, or hit another child? Help from a trusted adult, so you can change, is wise if many answers are yes. Courage is what telling the truth takes. A better friend begins with that first step. A person who thinks cruelty is cool can always find a trait to mock, Fried tells kids. Cool is not what it is. Two things should be remembered by a student who becomes a target. Fault is not yours. Help can be found. President Barack Obama speaks at the White House. The meeting is about how to prevent bullying.

7Worry about bullying's effects on all children is common among adults. Laws against bullying have been passed by many states. This problem was the subject of the first conference President Barack Obama held at the White House. Kids picked on in hallways or the school yard are a memory he said people share. Big ears and his name meant he was not immune, he said. Caring adults who can help are something young people in trouble need to know about, he said. Many people are required to stop bullying, say experts Fried and Limber. Teachers must join. Parents must join. Students must join. A place where everyone feels safe can be built together. Respect for everyone belongs in that place. Steps you can take are something they also name. Power belongs to kids, Fried said. Ways to make their situations better belong to kids too. Speaking up is the most important step for a target, experts agree. Adults must be told there is a problem. Scary is how it might feel. Worse trouble, if the bully is caught, is a fear some kids have. Help still comes best from speaking up. Physical safety matters too. Walking away is one choice. A loud stop can draw notice so others can help. At least two strategies are a good idea, Limber thinks. A short-term strategy should be one of them. Words or actions in the moment can stop the bullying or disrupt it. A long-term solution should be the other. Lessons or programs against bullying are something you might ask your school to include. Whether those programs work is a question many people still ask.

8Stopping bullying, and preventing it, is research that has just begun. Programs that continue over many months can reduce bullying, Limber says, and some proof exists. Ten elementary schools were studied in 2004, with a clear result. A drop of 28 percent came in social bullying. A drop of 37 percent came in physical bullying. Steps you can take are listed here. Stop is what you can tell a bully who attacks with words or with fists. A calm voice is worth trying. Humor is worth trying. A joke, or a laugh, can take power from the bully. Thank you, said after an unkind remark, may catch the speaker off guard. Walking away is right if speaking up seems too hard. Walking away is right if it is not safe. Next, find a grown person, or stand with several kids. Numbers bring safety. What you do can be changed. Another desk is something you can ask a teacher about. Playground time and lunch should be spent away from bullies. Adults should be nearby. A different route home from school can be chosen. Private is how online information should stay. Passwords should not be shared, or other people can enter your accounts. Care should guide the online friends you choose. Cyberbullying is blocked by those steps. If you watch one person bully a second person, answer with kindness. Friendship with the kid being picked on is something you can offer. Stop is what you can tell the bully if it is safe. An adult can be told what you saw. Respect is how others should be treated. Your words about another person should be thought over first.

9The same words said about you are a feeling you should imagine. A bold sign tells people to stop bullying. Gardner and Lily are each trying to end the bullying they face. Lily's mother was someone she talked with. The school counselor was someone she spoke with too. She felt uneasy about having the counselor speak to those girls. A tattletale is not what she wanted to be called. The problem is known by her teacher even so. It is known by the counselor. Help later is something Lily says she will seek from them if she needs it. Speaking up for herself is something she has learned. Hurt feelings caused by others' actions are something she is not afraid to name. Teachers have also been asked for help by Gardner. Attention to the problem has differed from teacher to teacher each year. Ignoring the bullies is what he tries on the playground. Avoiding them is what he tries. The opposite side of the schoolyard is where he plays. Adults are who he stays near. A walk away is what he tries whenever the bullies get too close. Teachers sat down with Gardner. The boys who bully him were there too. The playground was divided by school leaders after that. Distance between the boys and Gardner was the goal. Distance from the other students those boys bully was a goal too. His grandmother is who Gardner lives with. The school principal was someone she talked to. Lunch breaks for a month were spent inside by the group's ringleader, by the principal's order. Recess was spent inside too. A while is all the help lasted, Gardner says.

10Gardner says his focus stays on his own work in class. Attention or power for the bullies is not what he wants to give. Martial arts classes are something he is taking. Strength is built by the classes. Confidence is built too. More disciplined is how he says they make him feel. Proud of himself is how they make him feel. Less weak is how he feels. He tries to give each person fairness. That is the treatment he hopes to receive. Kindness is part of it. His grandmother is who taught him that. Respect for people is something their family talks about, he says. Ready or likely to attack or to argue is what aggressive means. The way a human or another animal acts in a certain situation is behavior. Mean or unkind, and a cause of suffering or pain, is what cruel means. An act that stops something from happening is prevention. A ringleader heads a group. The group makes trouble, or it pushes against authority. Respect for yourself, and confidence in your abilities, is self-esteem. Numbers that are collected, organized, and studied are statistics. Strategies are careful plans. A plan may solve a problem. A plan may reach a goal. Making fun of a person or another animal is teasing. Provoking them in a playful way can be teasing too. Dangers, or statements that harm is intended, are threats. A force that hurts or destroys is violence. Witnessing means you see an event as it happens. The event might be a crime. It might be an accident. It might be a ceremony.

19. Cars That Use Other Fuels

1Contents. How This Book Begins is listed at 4. Cars Powered by the Sun is listed at 6. Cars Powered by Hydrogen is listed at 12. Cars Powered by Batteries is listed at 16. Cars Powered by Ethanol is listed at 19. Looking Ahead is listed at 22. The Word List is listed at 23. The Word Finder is listed at 24. How This Book Begins. Cars and trucks fill the roads every day. Those trips burn huge amounts of gasoline. During 2023, burned gasoline totaled about 966 billion gallons. The United States by itself used over 137 billion gallons of gasoline. Each year, people consume a larger amount of gasoline. That heavy use of gasoline cannot continue forever. Only a limited supply of gasoline exists on Earth. The oil that is made into gasoline will run out someday. Some experts who study oil supplies share an estimate. Past the year 2050, the amount of oil produced will start to fall. Oil will be hard to find by the year 2125. Pause and think about the years ahead. A child living now might see oil shortages begin. That same child might have children later. Those children might live when oil is scarce. Running out of gasoline is only one problem. Cars and trucks release pollutants when they burn gasoline. Most scientists believe those pollutants hold extra heat near Earth's surface. That extra heat is the main cause of global warming. Global warming slowly heats the atmosphere of Earth. Weather patterns change because of this heating. Ice sheets and glaciers on Earth also melt because of it. The melting of those ice sheets and glaciers happens slowly. The outlook is not bright for drivers of gasoline-powered cars. Inventors hunt for different methods that could run cars and trucks. They hope to ease global warming, a problem people face now.

2They also hope to prepare for a future without oil. They are searching for alternative fuels. They are building cars that can run on those fuels. This book explains possible fuels of the future. It also explains vehicles that can use those fuels. Cars Powered by the Sun Most of the energy people use comes from the Sun. That star is the large one people see overhead. Coal holds energy that first came from the Sun. Oil holds energy that first came from the Sun too. Coal and oil formed from things that once were alive. Burning coal lets out energy that came from the Sun. Burning oil does the same thing. Burning gasoline made from oil does the same thing. That energy stayed stored in the fuels for a long time. Coal needed millions of years to form. Oil needed millions of years to form. After these fuels are used up, fresh supplies cannot form quickly. A person works underground in order to mine coal. Scientists found a way to turn radiant energy into electricity. The radiant energy comes straight from the Sun. Cities can be lit with that electricity. Cars can be powered with that electricity. Energy from the Sun is gathered by solar cells. Here is something worth knowing. Sunlight falls on just one square meter of land. One square meter equals 10.8 square feet. That patch can make enough electricity in a day for ten bulbs. Each of those bulbs is a 100-watt lightbulb. Solar cells make all of this possible. Calculators often show you what solar cells look like. Spacecraft get electricity from solar cells. Devices in places with no other electricity use them too. Unlike batteries, these cells are not used up. They keep giving electricity as long as light energy is there. The cells absorb that light energy. Set several solar cells side by side and you have a solar panel.

3Roofs of buildings often hold solar panels. Satellites can have solar panels attached. Space stations can have solar panels attached. Solar panels cover the outside of a solar-powered car. The panels make enough electricity for a special motor. That motor runs a solar car. Homes can be lit by this same kind of energy. A space station can get all its energy this way. No pollutants go into the air from this energy. Here is another fact. Silicon is the material inside the solar cells people meet most often. Silicon is one natural element found on Earth. Sand is made up of that same element. The space station gets its power from solar panels. Scientists are seeking low-cost ways to turn sunlight into electricity. You may wonder why solar cells do not run every car. Cost comes first when people ask about solar cells. Scientists still lack a low-cost way to build them. The cells would also need to make enough electricity. Sunlight itself is a free fuel. The cells that turn that free energy into electricity still cost money. That cost wipes out the savings from free sunlight. Try this math problem. The fuel for Car A is a mix. Ninety percent of the mix is nonrenewable gasoline. Ten percent of the mix is renewable ethanol. The tank in Car A holds 20 gallons of fuel. The fuel for Car B is all nonrenewable gasoline. Car B has a tank that holds 20 gallons too. How many gallons of nonrenewable gasoline does one full tank of Car A save next to Car B? Answer: 2 gallons. Energy output is a second problem. Newer solar cells make much more energy than the earliest models. They still fall short of the energy from gasoline burned in an engine. A typical solar-powered car reaches a top speed near 60 miles each hour.

4That speed equals 96.6 kilometers each hour. Hundreds of solar cells are needed to make enough electrical power for that speed. Driving at night brings another problem. Very cloudy days bring a problem too. Energy made during daylight must be stored. It is needed when sunlight is gone. A backup power supply is another possible fix. Car solar panels do not work during the night. They do not work on very cloudy days either. Many scientists are working as a large team on these problems. Each year, student teams from colleges hold a race. The colleges are in the United States and in Canada. The cars in the race are solar-powered. The race takes place in North America. Races like this one keep solar-cell technology moving forward. Cars Powered by Hydrogen Elements are simple substances. Everything on Earth is made from elements. Only 92 elements occur in nature. Those elements combine into thousands of other substances. Water is one of those substances. Plastic is another. Sugar is another. Hydrogen is the lightest of the 92 elements. It is also the most abundant of them. In pure form, hydrogen is a gas. A gas is a gaseous state of matter. People cannot see hydrogen. Hydrogen has no odor. Water is made from hydrogen plus one other element. Water is extremely abundant. Water covers close to 75 percent of the surface of Earth. Every living thing contains water. Plants contain water. Animals contain water. The remains of living things contain water too. Hydrogen reacts with other elements. It combines with them easily. New substances form in that combining. Energy is released at the same time. Scientists feel excited about that energy. They imagine a future fill-up for cars. The tanks would hold hydrogen gas. The tanks would not hold liquid gasoline.

5Earth does not hold hydrogen in a pure form. People still need a place to get hydrogen. Water is one of the clearest places to look. Someday a driver may fill a car with hydrogen gas. The driver would skip gasoline. Water contains two elements. The two elements are oxygen and hydrogen. Every element is made of tiny particles. The particles are called atoms. A chemical bond holds atoms of hydrogen and oxygen together. That joining makes water. Breaking the bonds gives pure hydrogen. Breaking the bonds also gives pure oxygen. The hydrogen can then serve as a fuel. Several methods can break the bond in water. The bond links hydrogen atoms with oxygen atoms. One method uses a charge of electrical energy. Another method uses extreme heat. Some other substances contain hydrogen as well. Methane is one example. Methane is a natural gas. Methane also contains oxygen. Methane also contains an element called carbon. Steam can supply heat that breaks bonds in methane. Those bonds join hydrogen atoms to atoms of carbon and oxygen. Pure hydrogen comes from that break too. Cars and trucks can run on pure hydrogen. People use pure hydrogen in one of two ways. One way burns it inside an engine. Gasoline cars burn gas in a similar kind of engine. The other way turns hydrogen into electricity. Vehicles can then run on that electricity. Some electric cars use hydrogen in fuel cells. The fuel cells produce the electricity. Fuel cells are a bit like batteries. One company built a concept car. That concept car runs on hydrogen. H O Here is a fact worth knowing. Some tiny living things that people cannot see make pure hydrogen. Those living things are called bacteria. Chemicals inside them take in energy from the Sun. They use the energy to split a bond in water.

6The bond is the one between hydrogen and oxygen. H A model of one water molecule shows a simple join. Two hydrogen atoms connect to one oxygen atom. Inside a fuel cell, hydrogen combines with oxygen. The joining of the two elements forms water. Energy comes out when the two elements join. The energy takes the form of electricity. All kinds of vehicles can run on that electricity. Any use of hydrogen still joins it with oxygen. That joining is what releases the energy. The only waste left behind is pure water. Water is not a pollutant. The environment is not harmed by water. Hydrogen looks like a strong alternative fuel for this reason. Plenty of water on Earth is a second reason. Much more research is still required. The fuel has to work well in real cars. Cars Powered by Batteries Scientists are building cars powered only by batteries. The batteries are rechargeable chemical batteries. Open the hood on one of these battery cars. An electric motor is what you find. Open the hood on a car that runs on gasoline. Hoses are part of what you find. The hoses carry water that cools an engine. Exhaust pipes are there too. The pipes carry waste products away. A thick bunch of wires sits in that front space. That car has no need to cool an engine. It produces no exhaust gases. No exhaust gases means no extra pollution of the air. One picture shows a normal gasoline engine. A battery car does not stop at a gas pump. Its driver does not fill a tank with gasoline. The driver plugs the car into an outlet. The outlet recharges the batteries. The driver pays an electric bill. The driver does not pay a fuel bill. One picture shows the hood area of a hybrid car.

7The hybrid uses both gasoline and electricity. A hybrid car has an electric motor. It also has a small gasoline engine. Each one can supply power that runs the car. Batteries run the electric motor. The motor can pull electricity out of the batteries. The motor can also send electricity back into the batteries. Nobody has to plug those batteries into an outlet to recharge them. While the motor is not driving the car, it puts energy back. One concept car could go farther than 62 miles. That distance is 100 kilometers. It used less than one gallon of fuel for the trip. One gallon equals 3.7 liters. Try this math problem. A gallon of gasoline costs $2.75. Connor drives an SUV. His SUV gets 12 miles from each gallon. He drives 120 miles every week. Sara drives a hybrid car. Her hybrid gets 40 miles from each gallon of gasoline. She drives 120 miles every week too. How much more money does Connor spend on gas each week than Sara? Answer: $19.25. One old advertisement shows an electric car. People could buy that car in 1910. Here is a fact from history. Vehicles that use alternative fuel are not new. Battery-powered vehicles were first produced in the 1830s. Steam-powered vehicles were produced in those years too. Gas-electric hybrids were produced alongside them. A poll taken in 1900 asked drivers what they preferred. Electric cars were the cars they preferred. Electric cars ran in a cleaner way than gasoline cars. They also ran in a safer way. Later, gasoline cars gained new technology. Builders could make them more cheaply than electric cars. Builders could also make them more cheaply than hybrids. By the 1920s, drivers picked gasoline cars. Many companies then stopped making alternative-fuel vehicles. Interest came back when gas grew scarce during the 1970s. A field of sugar cane can help make a fuel.

8A cornfield can help make that fuel too. Grain crops can be turned into ethanol. Cars Powered by Ethanol Ethanol is an alternative fuel. Its popularity is growing in lots of regions. People like it because it is a renewable resource. Coal cannot be renewed. Oil cannot be renewed. When the last of those fuels is gone, nothing brings them back. Plant material can be turned into ethanol. Animal material can be turned into ethanol. Most ethanol comes from grain crops. Corn is one of those crops. Barley is another. Sorghum is another. Potatoes can also be made into ethanol. Sugar cane can also be made into ethanol. Ground-up wood can be used as well. Other plant materials and animal materials can be used. People who want more ethanol can plant more crops. Corn and sugar cane are two crops they might plant. A crop can be ready within a matter of months. Workers then harvest the crop. The harvest is used to make ethanol. Ethanol made from biomass begins with a change. The plant matter is changed into sugar. The sugar is changed into alcohol after that. The change is a process called fermentation. One set of steps makes ethanol from corn. Ethanol Made from Corn 1. Workers grind the corn grain until it is a fine powder. 2. They mix that powder with water. A wet mash is the result. 3. A chemical is put into the mash. The mash turns into sugar. 4. Yeast goes into the sugar. Yeast are tiny living organisms. They start fermentation. 5. Fermentation turns the sugar into ethanol. Carbon dioxide gas forms at the same time. 6. Workers take the ethanol out of the mash. 7. They take out any water still left in the ethanol. 8. A little gasoline is mixed into the ethanol.

9The fuel is then ready to burn. 9. Grain left over from the mash becomes feed. Livestock eat that feed. Making Ethanol in the United States Vehicles with special engines can burn pure ethanol. Those engines are built for that fuel. Ethanol can also be mixed with gasoline first. A regular engine can then burn the mix. The mix means drivers use fewer fossil fuels. The mix also means less pollution. A word to know Ethanol combined with gasoline has a name. The name is gasohol. More places need to take up this kind of technology. Looking Ahead One idea should be clear by now. Oil for making gasoline will not last forever. People can try to stretch the oil supply they have now. They do that by conserving gasoline. One way is to drive cars that use fuel more efficiently. Another way is car-pooling. Another way is simply to drive less. Conservation alone is not enough. A plan is still needed for the day with no oil left for gasoline. Scientists are working hard on other methods that could run cars and trucks. Which alternative will people choose? Ethanol is one possible choice. Hydrogen is another choice. Solar energy is another choice. Chemical batteries are another choice. Economic factors will likely decide the winner. One factor is the price of building that new power supply. Another factor is what the driver finally pays for the alternative fuel. Which fuel do you expect your own car to use at age 70? Word List. Alternative fuels is a noun on page 5. It means fuels unlike the ones drivers use most today. Atoms is a noun on page 13. It means the tiniest units inside an element. Biomass is a noun on page 20. It means plant or animal material that lives now or lived lately.

10Bond is a noun on page 13. It means a link that holds atoms in a molecule. Ethanol is a noun on page 19. It means a fuel taken from crops. Corn is one crop. Sugar cane is another. Fermentation is a noun on page 20. It means a change that breaks a substance into a simpler form. Cheese and vinegar are made through this change. Fuel cells is a noun on page 14. It means devices that make electricity when fuel meets oxygen. Gaseous is an adjective on page 12. It means having the form of a gas. Hydrogen is a noun on page 12. It means one element listed on the periodic table. Pollutants is a noun on page 5. It means unwanted dirt in air, or loose debris on the ground. Radiant is an adjective on page 7. It means shining brightly. Renewable resource is a noun on page 19. It means a supply people can replace. It also means energy that use does not finish off. Solar cells is a noun on page 7. It means devices that turn light into electricity. Word Finder. Batteries are noted on pages 7 and 14. They are also noted on pages 16, 17, and 22. Ethanol is noted on pages 19 through 22. Fuel cells are noted on pages 14 and 15. Gasoline conservation is noted on pages 21 and 22. Gasoline consumption is noted on pages 4 and 5, and again on page 21. Hydrogen is noted on pages 12 through 15. Oil supply is noted on pages 4 through 6. It is also noted on pages 11, 18, and 22. Pollution is noted on pages 5 and 8. It is also noted on pages 16 and 21. Solar cells are noted on pages 7 through 11. Water is noted on pages 12 through 16.

20. Cities Beneath the Ground

1An introduction opens this book. Old cities come next. Turkey's Derinkuyu is the first old city. England comes after that, with cave homes at Nottingham. Newer cities follow the old ones. Australia's Coober Pedy is one newer place. SubTropolis is another. You find it in Missouri, inside the city of Kansas City, in the United States. Near the end, the book looks at underground building still to come. A word list finishes the book. An animal called the naked mole rat stays below the surface. Many animals share that way of living. You may already know a few of them. Ants are on that list. Moles are on that list. Groundhogs are on that list. Prairie dogs are on that list. People have lived below the surface too. That fact may be new to you. Some groups have kept this life for hundreds of years. Other groups have kept it for thousands of years. The practice still goes on in certain places. A visit shows why residents picked these dwellings. The dwellings are out of the ordinary. Safety from enemies was one reason. Safety from the weather was another reason. A home with a lower cost was another reason. A need for added living space was another reason. Places like these turn up in many parts of the world. Inside one cave house, the walls are carved stone. Reaching that home means going down four floors. The home stands in Derinkuyu, a city in Turkey.

2Old buried cities fill a region in the center of Turkey. Discoveries there pass two hundred. The cities have several levels each. Derinkuyu is one of the famous examples. Say the name as deh-rin-KOO-yoo. Workers cut the city from soft rock that came from volcanoes. Rock of this sort is easy to find nearby. Some caves were shaped by nature before any builder arrived. Wind and water both took part in that shaping. Other land shapes started the same way. Archaeologists still cannot name the year builders finished the city. No find tells them that exact year. Making Derinkuyu was amazing work in engineering. The distance from top to bottom is at least eleven stories. So far, only eight stories have been dug out and cleared. Thousands of shafts moved fresh air to the lowest floors. People could breathe that far down because the shafts were there. Scientists say the first builders knew engineering very well. A city on this scale required that knowledge. During 1963, a man who lived close by broke into the hidden rooms. He was cutting an opening in a wall. He hoped to enlarge the cave home he already had. The soft rock forced builders to make pillars extra strong. A weak pillar might have let a tunnel fall. Their strong pillars succeeded. Nobody has found a tunnel that fell. About twenty thousand people could fit inside. Room remained for their food.

3Room remained for their livestock. Doors cut from stone could close every way in or out. Someone on the inside could shut the doors. One door had a weight of 454 kilograms. That weight is the same as 1,000 lb. Each floor could be locked apart from the floors next to it. Clues say the whole place was for hiding when invaders came. People could hide there during those attacks, and so could their supplies. The buried city contained homes, stables, storage rooms, and a school. Fresh water was stored for everyone there. A tunnel joins this city to another one under the ground. The tunnel measures 9 kilometers. That measure equals 6 mi. Nottingham, in England, hides an old city of caves under its streets. Carvers cut that city from sandstone. The stone was soft, so simple hand tools could shape it. This city was not made like Derinkuyu. Builders here did not begin with caves nature had opened. They sliced rooms from rock that was solid. The number of caves is nearly five hundred. Every cave was cut by human hands. Southern Poland has a salt mine deep below the surface. People say no other salt mine is quite so unusual. The town beside the mine is Wieliczka. Say that town as vee-LEECH-ka. Settlers started the town in the thirteenth century. They came so they could dig salt close to home. Across later centuries, miners shaped statues in the mine.

4They shaped dozens of them. Rock salt was what they carved. Chapels were shaped there, and so was a huge cathedral. Digging for salt ended there in 1996. Nottingham's caves had residents a very long time ago. The record goes back to the eleventh century. People were still there into the middle of the 1800s, and maybe later. People used the caves as homes, as work rooms, and as cellars. Drury Hill names one neighborhood. Early on, that neighborhood was wealthy. Crowds overflowed it during the nineteenth century. Families squeezed into basements that held a single room. Some basements of that kind are still standing. Sickness spread fast in rooms packed with so many people. World War II ran from 1939 through 1945. Bombs struck England in those years. Residents hid from the air raids inside the caves. Nottingham Castle rises over the caves. Around the end of the 1960s, work started on shops above the caves. The plan was to fill every cave with concrete. Townspeople rejected the plan. A local history group then fought to keep the caves. Leaders named the caves an ancient monument. That step saved most of them. From 1978 on, visitors have been let inside. Coober Pedy is a newer Australian town with homes under the earth. A remote stretch of South Australia holds its mine. Great treasures of the Earth come out of that mine. No other spot supplies as many opals as this town does.

5The gems are unusual. Shifting colors are why people value them. Workers have taken opals here from 1915 to today. Summer heat is extreme. A hot day can reach 45°C. The same reading is 118°F. Miners escape the heat inside homes cut into hillsides. Those homes look like caves. The local name for them is dugouts. Paying to build a dugout comes to about the same sum as a house on top. Down below, the temperature does not rise and fall. Residents skip summer air-conditioning and winter heat. Subterranean shops, museums, and churches serve the town. Opals in photos of the town match stones from the local mines. Travelers come from a wide range of countries to look around. A working opal mine is open for tours. Guides tell how the area changed over the years. Guests can enter homes below the street. Guests can dig and hunt for opals. Guests can sleep in a hotel below the street. The Underground Motel takes guests through every season. You will find that motel in Coober Pedy, within Australia. Opal mines sit nearby. Julie Knight makes her home in the town. She describes daily life below the surface. To her, that life is better than life in normal housing. Windows show up only along the front rooms. The climate pushes people to use very few windows. Extra windows would let extra heat inside.

6Each room has a small vent for air. Air moves through the vent by natural circulation. Hot air climbs. Then the hot air exits. Do you know the story of the town's name? Words from Australian Aboriginal people formed the name Coober Pedy. The words are kupa piti. Together they stand for a white man in a hole. Only one room made up the earliest form of Faye's Historic Underground House. Later it grew into a complete house. A swimming pool became part of it. Through every season, the Celsius reading stays between 23 and 25. On the Fahrenheit scale, that is 73 to 77. There is no bill for heating or for cooling. An air shaft brings light into one room. The light arrives as a bright circle. Other rooms stay black until a lamp is switched on. Before the lamp, those rooms are pitch black. Early dugouts around this town were opened by hand. A pick was the tool. Some residents leave the pick scars on their walls. They want a reminder of hard digging in the first years. Other residents paint their rooms. White is the paint they choose most often. The pale color makes a room brighter and lighter. Almost no dust gathers indoors. A dugout blocks sound, which means it is soundproof. Street traffic cannot be heard. A neighbor's noise cannot be heard. Julie feels lucky in this home. The home is unique in her eyes.

7Character fills it. SubTropolis has paved roads under the earth. Those roads stretch 11.3 kilometers. The same length is 7 mi. A large truck rolls along the roads. This site belongs to the United States. Missouri is the state. Kansas City is the city. Two theme parks sit on the land above. Worlds of Fun is one park. Oceans of Fun is the other park. Under the parks lies a world unlike the one up top. People who run SubTropolis call it the biggest complex for business that exists under the ground. Limestone was mined here at the start. Businesses rent the huge hollow now. Some renters store goods in the space. Some manufacture goods there. Some print there. Some do other work there. Weather outdoors never reaches the inside. Rain, snow, ice, wind, and other rough weather do not trouble the firms. Staff never look up into open sky. White paint covers the interior anyway. That color leaves the space looking light. That color leaves the space looking bright. Temperatures below ground change only a few degrees as years pass. Firms here therefore burn less electricity. Their power need is about one-quarter the amount used by a firm on the surface. Humidity stays level. Moisture never collects into a problem. Some firms must have air that steady. Their machines demand it. Treasures can sit safely where the climate barely moves. SubTropolis keeps a great number of those treasures.

8Collectible stamps are there by the millions. The U.S. Postal Service owns the stamps. Original reels of film are stored beside them. Some reels are classic movies. Other reels are classic TV shows. Desert families in Tunisia once built downward to stay cool. Matmâta was one of their settlements. Other desert settlements did the same work. Step one was digging a wide hole at the center. Step two was cutting dwellings out along the sides of that pit. Few outsiders knew these places for a long while. Heavy floods during the 1960s changed that. After the floods, the settlements became known. At a later date, movie crews used the homes in Star Wars. The homes stood in for a dry desert planet on the set. Big shopping districts under city streets exist in many countries. A few examples come next. Hanoi, in Vietnam, holds Vincom Mega Mall Royal City. The place works as an entertainment complex. It also works as a mall below street level. The shop total passes six hundred. One area holds a water park. Another area holds an ice-skating rink. Another area holds game centers. Meals are sold on a food street. There is no food court. About two hundred restaurants face that street. Moscow, in Russia, holds Okhotny Ryad. The mall is buried and stands three stories high. A subway stop connects with it. Walls of that stop were built at ground level before anything else.

9The goal was to guard the buildings next door. Crews then brought the walls down below the street. Osaka, in Japan, holds the country's largest shopping district below ground. The district lies within the Umeda mall. Several subway stops join the mall. Hotels rise beside the stops. Banks rise beside the stops. Tall office buildings rise beside the stops. A giant red wheel for riders, a Ferris wheel, turns overhead. Nine stories make up the building beneath that wheel. Santiago, in Chile, holds SubCentro Las Condes. It is a shopping center under the street. The city's main subway reaches its final stop there. A fresh design, made not long ago, lets in added light. Glass replaced the old walls of the shops. The center now feels less closed in. Up above, a new plaza gives a park-like feel. A world view lists ten underground cities and shopping centers. First is Derinkuyu, which lies in Turkey. Second is the cave city at Nottingham, which lies in England. Third is Wieliczka, which lies in Poland. Fourth is Coober Pedy, which lies in Australia. Fifth is SubTropolis. That site lies in Kansas City. Kansas City lies in Missouri. Missouri lies in the United States. Sixth is Matmâta, which lies in Tunisia. Seventh is Vincom Mega Mall Royal City. That mall lies in Hanoi. Hanoi lies in Vietnam. Eighth is Okhotny Ryad. That mall lies in Moscow. Moscow lies in Russia. Ninth is the Umeda District.

10That district lies in Osaka. Osaka lies in Japan. Tenth is SubCentro Las Condes. That center lies in Santiago. Santiago lies in Chile. Future cities under the ground. Shopping zones below the street already appear in many lands. Large cities below the street are also taking shape in the present. In country after country, people are putting up subterranean houses. Those houses need less energy, their owners say. Looking after them takes less money, their owners say. Privacy is higher than in houses on the surface, their owners say. Skyscrapers still shape the cities we see. The cities seem to climb higher into the sky. A time may come when cities spread downward instead. Glossary. Archaeologists are scientists who study traces of ancient cultures. Circulation means air or water moving inside a system. A complex is nearby buildings gathered for one shared aim. Dwellings are the buildings or shelters people live in. Engineering is planning and making things with science plus math. Excavated means dug out, or brought into view. Humidity describes the moisture held by air. A mine is where minerals are removed. Digging is one way. Blasting is another way. A pillar is a post or column that gives support. Usual materials are stone or else wood or else metal. Preserved means kept unchanged and kept safe. Residents are the people or animals that stay in a place. Subterranean means located under the ground.

21. Cities Built on the Water

1Water cities appear in many parts of the world. One is Venice, in Italy. One is Amsterdam, in the Netherlands. One is Reine, in Norway. One is Singapore. One is Palm Jumeirah, in Dubai. One is Ganvié, in Benin. One is Fadiouth, in Senegal. One is the Uros Floating Village, in Peru. One is Mexcaltitán, in Mexico. One is Auckland, in New Zealand. One is Sausalito, in California. One is Manarola, in Italy. Millions of people travel to Venice each year from around the world. Canals and bridges made this city famous. Builders set it on more than a hundred islands. Its main waterway is the Grand Canal. No water city is better known. It is not the only one. A water city is a place built on water or beside it. Some of these cities are tiny. Some are huge. Every continent except Antarctica has them, near water or in it.

2Reasons for them differ. Each city has a story worth telling. Canals also made Amsterdam famous in Europe. It is the Netherlands' capital. It is that country's largest city. Below sea level is about one-third of the Netherlands. Sea level means how high the surface of the ocean sits. Heights and depths of other things are measured from it. Reclaimed land makes up one-sixth of the country. Reclaimed means brought back to a usable state. Water once covered that land. Amsterdam itself sits about 2 meters below sea level. That drop is 6.6 feet. People could build only after the water was pumped away. Dry land is kept by pumping stations. A moat ringed Amsterdam in the 1500s. The 1600s brought more people as the city grew. Swamps were drained, meaning the water was made to flow out.

3Three new canals were built around downtown. More than 1,200 bridges cross the canals. Another 161 canals have been added since those early ones. Total canal length is over 100 kilometers. That is 62 miles. Almost 100 small islands are cut out by the canals. More than 1,200 bridges tie the islands together. For centuries, moving through the city meant using the canals. Transportation is the act of moving people or goods from place to place. Smaller boats are the main users now. Character in Amsterdam still depends on these canals. Reine, Norway, lies far to the north. This tiny fishing village sits more than 100 kilometers above the Arctic Circle. That distance is 62 miles. Its nickname is The Most Beautiful Place in the World. The coast of one Lofoten Island is its setting. Fishing is offered. Kayaking is offered.

4Skiing is offered. Whale watching is offered. Hiking is offered. Beautiful beaches are offered. Views of the northern lights here can be amazing. Busy ports do not get much busier than Singapore. It is the only island city-state on Earth. That phrase means one island is both a city and a free country. About sixty-three islands make it up. Goods must come from elsewhere. Natural resources of its own are few. Size today is far beyond the size before the 1960s. Land once underwater has been reclaimed, as in Amsterdam. Pumping was not Singapore's method. Rocks and sand were dumped into the sea to add land. A project that began in 2016 will copy Amsterdam's pumping ways. More land area is the goal. The United Arab Emirates is the country of the modern city of Dubai. People shorten that country name to UAE.

5Palm Islands is one of its biggest builds. Three human-made islands take the shape of palm trees. Work goes on along the coast. Huge piles of sand and rock form the islands. Hundreds of miles of new coastline are being added. Thousands of homes are being added. The year 2001 opened the construction. Late 2016 saw the first island finished. Palm Jumeirah ranks among the largest human-made islands anywhere. A curved seawall guards it. Strong waves meet that wall. Storms meet it too. Africa's Venice is a nickname sometimes given to Ganvié. The name sounds like GAWN-vye. No stilt village in Africa is larger. A stilt is a post holding a structure above ground or water. Lake Nokoué, in Benin, has the village in its middle. The lake name sounds like NOHK-we. About twenty thousand people live there. About three thousand buildings stand there.

6Land holds only the school and a cemetery. At least three hundred years have passed since the village began. A powerful tribe called the Fon was capturing people then. Members of other tribes were sold into slavery. Fon religion blocked pursuit of anyone living on the water. To pursue can mean to chase in order to catch someone. It can also mean to chase in order to attack. The word can also mean following a plan until a goal is reached. The Tofinu, another tribe, fled to the lake. Capture was what they hoped to escape. The stilt village was their build. Their way of life still continues. We survived is what the village name means. Fishing is how most villagers earn a living. Underwater fences let them keep and breed fish. Chickens are kept too. Goats are kept too.

7Patches of grass growing in the water hold those animals. Long, narrow canoes carry people. Fadiouth, in Senegal, is an island of clamshells. The name sounds like fad-YOOT. Shell Island is another name. Clams have been eaten there for over three hundred years. Shells have been reused. Tree roots and reeds pin the shells in place. A reed is a tall plant in the grass family, with slender leaves, growing in water or wetlands. Shells form the buildings. Shells form the streets. Safety on the water was also the Uru choice, as it was for the Tofinu. Lake Titicaca became their refuge over five hundred years ago. Other people had taken their land. The floating islands number about seventy today. About two thousand Uru live on them. Reeds are the building stuff. Thickness runs from 1.2 to 2.4 meters. That is 4 to 8 feet.

8The bottom of an island breaks down. Fresh reeds have to go on top all the time. Canoes are also made from the reeds. Homes are made from them. Furniture is made from them. Reed canoes are their transportation. Mexcaltitán rests on a long sandbar off Mexico's coast. Large parts of Mesoamerica were ruled by the Aztec people. The fourteenth through the sixteenth centuries were their era. A story says their start was an island near Mexico's Pacific coast. Gods, the story says, told them to seek a new home. Two hundred years of searching followed. A city was founded. Mexico City is the modern name of that city. Some historians link the Aztec start to Mexcaltitán. The name sounds like mes-kahl-tee-TAHN. This small human-made island matches the place of an Aztec island on a map from 1579. Small canoes of the early people match too.

9Canoes used there today are almost the same. About eight hundred people live in the town. Mexican Venice is a name sometimes used. Summer is the rainy season. Boats then become the only street travel. Children play water soccer. The field is underwater. Flood season sends locals boating in the streets. Swimming in the streets happens too. Auckland is the largest city in New Zealand. Its population is 1.5 million. The North Island is its setting, in the South Pacific Ocean. Two large harbors flank it. An isthmus is the land it stands on. An isthmus is a narrow land strip joining two bigger land areas. The narrowest point is under 2 kilometers wide. That width is 1.24 miles. City of Sails is the nickname. About one-sixth of residents own a boat. A resident is someone who lives in a place.

10Thousands of beaches line the city. Swimming is one water activity. Surfing is another. Fishing is another. Diving is another. Kayaking is another. Horseback riding on the beach is another. Other water cities exist beyond the ones already told. Italy holds a tiny fishing town of bright-colored homes. Steep cliffs carry those homes. Manarola is that town. Northern California holds a floating neighborhood of houseboats. Sausalito is that place. Months aboard aircraft carriers are a home for some military people. Water does not mean the same thing to every person. For people who live in water cities, it may mean fun. It may mean work. It may mean safety. It may mean a tie to nature. It may mean a whole way of life. These cities prove a wide claim. A home can be made almost anywhere.

22. Clara Barton Builds a Way to Help

1Clara Barton stands at the center of the picture. The women beside her are the first nurses who graduated from Blockley Hospital in Philadelphia. Her childhood opens this book. Her early jobs come next. The Civil War follows those jobs. She then creates the American Red Cross. The Red Cross of today closes the story. Virginia's summer air was suffocating. Guns made a cracking sound. Cannons made a booming sound. A softer noise lay beneath all of that. Fear was stronger in the softer noise. Moans came from wounded men. Cries for help came from dying men. The battlefield was full of chaos. Those cries got no reply. Smoke opened on a sight that was hard to believe. The figure was a small woman. A modest dress covered her. Bandages loaded her arms. A Union commander stepped into her path. The wounded soldiers were still ahead of her. His shout said a woman did not belong in that place. The picture is a field hospital from the Civil War. City Point, Virginia, is the place. The woman met the officer eye to eye. A letter went from her hand into his. The signature was Surgeon General William A. Hammond. He stood as the top doctor of the nation. That letter gave Clara Barton her permission.

2Battlefields were places she could enter. Sick soldiers were people she could tend. Wounded soldiers were people she could tend too. Her name on the page made the commander stare. His eyes went wide. A bow showed his apology. Not one word came from her. Her steps led toward the injured soldiers. A famous name already followed her. People knew her as Angel of the Battlefield. Clara was the youngest child at home. December 25, 1821, was her birthday. Oxford, Massachusetts, was her birthplace. Teenagers already filled the older ranks of the family. Four siblings shared that house. Deep love for the little girl came from them. They taught her how to read. They taught her how to ride a horse. Work on the family farm came easily and fast. A quick mind was hers. Shyness was hers as well. Boarding school, her parents hoped, might draw her out of that shell. For Clara, school was traumatic. Strangers made speaking a terror. Illness followed that fear. Home was where her parents took her, and they did it quickly. Clara Barton appears in a picture from 1850. Wealth belonged to the family. Oxford knew the family well. The town is small. It sits in central Massachusetts.

3Eleven was Clara's age when life changed for good. David was the brother she loved best. A barn was going up, and he was helping. The roof was where he fell. His injuries were grave. More than two years passed at his bedside. She stayed until his recovery was complete. Purpose filled that care. The feeling never left her memory. Work was what grown Clara wanted. The 1800s closed many jobs to women. A career in teaching was one of the few they could hold. Her first classroom was a one-room schoolhouse. Oxford, Massachusetts, was the town. Bordentown, New Jersey, was the place of a later job. Free public schools did not exist in New Jersey then. Money was required, because the schools were private. Poor students, in Clara's view, suffered an injustice. A free school was her answer. The door was open to any child. Hundreds of students arrived in a short time. More teachers were people she hired. A woman in charge did not suit the New Jersey school board. A man was hired to be principal. The insult to Clara went deep. Leaving teaching felt like her only path. A later picture shows an office in Washington, D.C. Families came there to search for wounded soldiers who were missing.

4Patients wait in a larger picture. The year is 1864. The place is a Union Army hospital. Washington, D.C., became Clara's new home. The city is the capital of the nation. The U.S. Patent Office gave her a job. The work suited her skill. Promotion came in a short time. An important government job in a woman's hands worried some leaders. A man was put in her place. A search for other work had to wait. The nation stood ready to break in two. The year 1861 brought the Civil War. The Union was the northern side. The Confederacy was the southern side. The middle of the conflict held Washington, D.C. The city filled with wounded men. Help was Clara's immediate choice. Wounded Civil War soldiers wait in the picture. The year is 1862. Savage Station, Virginia, is the place. Hospitals needed supplies. Clara began to organize them. Some soldiers were too badly hurt to travel to a hospital. Care had to travel to those men instead. A letter from Clara went to the government. She asked to be allowed onto the battlefield. Putting a woman at the front lines was a new idea. Giving up was not her choice. A letter came at last.

5It sent her straight into the fight. Disease, in the Civil War, killed more soldiers than battle did. Tools in a doctor's hands were very few. Techniques were very few as well. Some surgery was possible. An arm could be removed. A leg could be removed. Basic first aid was work for doctors and for nurses. Wounds could be stitched. Bandages could be wrapped. Germs were poorly understood. Disease moved fast. Infection moved fast. Nurse training was something Clara never had. The skills known in that time were skills she learned quickly. Makeshift hospitals stood near the fighting. The name for them was field hospitals. That work became her calling. Gunfire did not stop her care for injured soldiers. Helpers were scarce. Supplies were scarcer still. Sleep was something she gave up. Candlelight was how she tended the wounded at night. Her sleeve was cut by a bullet. The shot only grazed her arm. Her post was a place she would not leave. Confederate soldiers received her care too. The North was her home side. Slavery, she believed with strength, was wrong. Troops gave her a name of their own. That name was Angel of the Battlefield. A map of the United States in 1862 marks a few of her stops.

6Pennsylvania appears. Maryland appears. Washington, D.C., appears. Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina appear. The Atlantic Ocean is on the map. Union states are marked. Confederate states are marked. Culpepper, Virginia, was the site of the Battle of Cedar Mountain on August 9, 1862. Fairfax, Virginia, was the site of the Second Battle of Bull Run from August 28 to August 30, 1862. Chantilly, Virginia, was the site of the Battle of Chantilly on September 1, 1862. Harper's Ferry, Maryland, was the site of the Battle of South Mountain on September 1, 1862. Antietam, Maryland, was the site of the Battle of Antietam on September 17, 1862. Fredericksburg, Virginia, was the site of the Battle of Fredericksburg from December 11 to December 15, 1862. Clara Barton helped soldiers in many Civil War fights. The map names only some of them. Exhaustion followed the war. Rest was her doctor's advice. Europe was where she went. Fame had already reached that side of the ocean. Leaders in Switzerland asked to meet her. Their new group was the International Red Cross. Wounded people in wartime received its care. Displaced people received its care too. The side a person was on did not matter. Germany, Switzerland, France, Austria, and Italy appear on a map.

7Geneva is marked there. Switzerland is small. It is a country in Europe. High Alps are its setting. France, Germany, Italy, and Austria all share that mountain range. Geneva grew into a meeting city. Nations gathered there to make agreements. Sixteen nations came together in the Geneva Convention of 1863. Rules for war were what they set. Care for prisoners was one rule. Care for wounded soldiers was another. Out of that meeting came the International Red Cross. The Franco-Prussian War had medical teams and civilian teams. Those teams were organized. They were well equipped. Wounded soldiers were in their care. Clara felt the idea like a spark. Volunteers gained her at once. France and Germany were where she served in the Franco-Prussian War. She saw teams with plenty of supplies. She saw teams with plenty of staff. She saw teams with strong organization. Teams of that kind had been only a dream in the Civil War. A promise formed in her. A Red Cross like that one would start after she came home to the United States. The year 1881 was when she founded the American Red Cross. President was the role she took. Peace was the state of the United States then.

8Idleness was not the state of the American Red Cross. Food was stored before trouble arrived. Tents were stored. Clothing, money, and medicine were stored too. An emergency would not force a scramble for supplies. Disaster zones were where she led volunteers. Michigan wildfires had taken people's homes. Flooding along the Mississippi River had done the same. Those people received her help. The Johnstown tragedy lived on in poems. It lived on in books. It lived on in songs. May of 1889 was the time. A dam near Johnstown, Pennsylvania, broke with no warning. Water came as a wall and carried the town off. The dead numbered over 2,200. A disaster that bad was rare in the United States. Wreckage did not keep Clara away. Red Cross workers went in with her. Five months was the length of her stay in Johnstown. Survivors got help rebuilding health. They got help rebuilding lives. Admiration for the American Red Cross ran deep across the whole nation. For years the group followed whatever plan Clara chose. Her own money paid the costs. Records were few. A charter, meaning a detailed contract, bound the Red Cross to the government. Renewal of that charter required a more official system. Her old habits were habits she would not drop.

9The president's role ended when she resigned in 1904. A picture from 1911 shows Clara Barton. Eighty-one was her age. Finished was not how she felt. The National First Aid Association of America was the group she started next. A first aid kit in a single box became popular through that group. An early kit appears in a picture. Ninety was the age when she finally slowed. April 12, 1912, was the day she died. Glen Echo, Maryland, was the home. Friends were with her. Hot meals leave an American Red Cross van in the picture. The work is disaster relief. Moore, Oklahoma, is the town. May 2013 was the month of a tornado there. Modern humanitarian aid began with Clara's work in the American Red Cross. She widened the group's role. Natural disasters joined war as reasons to respond. A hurricane often draws the Red Cross before any other group. An earthquake can do the same. A flood can do the same. Field hospitals go up for the hurt. They also serve the sick. Donations are gathered. Food is handed out. Clothing is handed out. Tents, clean water, and medicine are handed out too. Health, as Clara Barton saw it, is something every person deserves.

10Dignity is something every person deserves. Her years were many. Her days were full. She showed care for others at the time of greatest need. Her path is now followed by 97 million people. They are volunteers, members, and staff. The nations where they serve number 187. A few words from the story have set meanings. Authorized means someone gave permission or an official yes. Founded means a new group was created. A battlefield is the ground where a conflict is fought. Humanitarian describes a person or a group that helps by easing pain and suffering. Careers are jobs held over a long time, often with room to rise. Purposeful means full of meaning, or important. Survivors are people who live through a threat to life. The Civil War was fought from 1861 to 1865. It set the Northern states of the United States against the Southern states. Tend means to look after someone or something with special attention. A disaster is a sudden event that brings terrible harm and loss. Volunteers choose to help or to serve, and nobody pays them. Field hospitals are temporary spots for medical care. They stand near a fight or near some other emergency. The people inside are wounded or sick.

23. Courts That Explain the Law

1Topics in this book come in a set order. The rule of law comes first. The Constitution and the courts come next. How the courts are organized comes after that. Three famous cases show the courts at work. Checks and balances follow. Lady Justice comes next. Meanings of key words come last. Appeals courthouses in the United States set out three seats. Three judges use those seats while they consider a case. In 1951, Black parents sued the school district of Topeka, Kansas. The suit was for their children. Linda Brown, a third grader, was one of those children. Schools there kept children apart by race. A Black child could not share a classroom with a white child. The case name is Brown v. Board of Education. Topeka is the city in that case. The dispute rose until it reached the nation's highest court. That court is the highest in the country. The vote in 1954 ended school segregation that the law had allowed, and the ruling covered the whole country. Equal rights for Black Americans gained an important victory. The case shows the judicial branch doing its work. Courts and the legal system are under the watch of this branch.

2It explains the laws that steer government and society. It also enforces those laws. Among the children in the photograph, Linda Brown is the one at the center. One picture catches George Washington in mid speech. The gathering is the 1787 convention that shaped the Constitution. American leaders adopted a constitution in 1788. Laws and principles in it were meant to govern a young country. Time has altered the system. People still use it. Three branches, or parts, are how federal power is split. The president leads the executive branch. Most government business and departments sit there. The legislative branch is made of the Senate and the House of Representatives. Making laws is its job. Courts and the legal system sit under the judicial branch. Sometimes the three branches cooperate. Sometimes they compromise. At other times they stand against one another. Articles one through three of the Constitution are what divide national power into those branches. One of the branches is judicial. Seven sections make up the Constitution. One section sets out what the judicial branch does. It also lists the kinds of cases federal courts may hear. Cases for the national government are included. Cases against that government are included.

3Cases between states are included. Cases about breaking the Constitution are included. Cases about breaking federal laws are included. Law falls into two areas, criminal and civil. Criminal law covers theft. It also covers assault. It also covers damage to property. A guilty finding in criminal court can mean prison. Civil law covers a person, a business, or an organization that feels harmed by someone else. Money may be the end of a civil case, as in a settlement. Prison is not a risk in a civil case. A victim who cannot find justice in criminal court can turn to civil court. Most cases, whether criminal or civil, are settled with no trial at all. Federal courts are what the judicial branch is made of. District courts are part of the set. Courts of appeals are part of it. The Supreme Court is part of it. One judge, or several, manages each court. A district court is where most federal cases begin. Eighty-nine district courts are spread across the country. Each state has at least one. Five more district courts sit in territories under national control. Some cases go to a jury. Citizens in that group hear the case and give a judgment.

4Other cases are bench trials. In those, the judge fills both roles, judge and jury. A case may climb from lower federal courts up to the Supreme Court. A state case may also arrive there if it concerns a federal law. On the federal side of the path, the count of district courts shown is 94. Above them are 13 courts of appeals. The Supreme Court stands at the top. On the state side, a local trial court can send a case to a state appeals court. From there it can reach the highest appeals court in that state. The national Supreme Court may be the step after that. Judge Neomi Rao, of a court of appeals, is shown being sworn in. A losing side may dislike the final decision. After a ruling, lawyers for that side may ask an appeals court to look again. Thirteen appeals courts serve the country. Their job is not to run the trial a second time. Three judges, sitting together, go over the district court's work. They ask whether the case was handled in a proper way. If the review supports the earlier result, they may uphold the ruling.

5Washington, D.C., is home to the Supreme Court. It works as the top appeals court. Thousands of people each year ask this court to hear an appeal. Very few requests are accepted. Justices is the name for the judges on this court. Each accepted case gets careful study. The justices also join the arguments. Lawyers for both sides speak. Questions come from the justices. A later vote names the winning side. The hardest legal cases often land here. A decision can shape the national government. It can shape American society too. About 7,500 requests to hear a case arrive in a year. Fewer than 150 of them are usually heard. Those two numbers, 7,500 and 150, make the share 1/50. Nine justices serve today. The chief justice is one of the nine. Several important justices have sat on this high court. John Jay served from 1789 to 1795. He was the first to hold the chief justice post. John Marshall served from 1801 to 1835. No chief justice has served longer. Early American law took shape with his help. William Howard Taft served from 1921 to 1930. He had already been president, and no other person has held both offices.

6From 1953 until 1969, Earl Warren was the one in charge. Under him the court widened civil rights. It also widened federal power. Thurgood Marshall served from 1967 to 1991.
