He was the first Black justice. Sandra Day O'Connor served from 1981 to 2006. She was the first woman on this highest court. From 1993 until 2020, the court included Ruth Bader Ginsburg. Her decisions backed rights for workers. They also backed gender equality. Sonia Sotomayor began in 2009 and still serves. She is the first Hispanic justice. Television shows are the only view most Americans get of the judicial system. Real cases are rarely that dramatic. A trial never happens in most of them. Years may pass before some are finished. Importance does not depend on excitement. Three landmark cases of the Supreme Court show why. The year 1965 brought a lawsuit from parents. Three teenagers were their children. The district they sued was in Iowa. The case is known as Tinker versus the Des Moines schools. The district's longer name is Independent Community School District. Black armbands at school had led to suspension. The bands protested the nation's role in the Vietnam War. Students said the suspension broke their right of free speech.

7The year 1969 brought a win for the students. Speech at school is protected, the court said, as long as it does not cause disruption. Mary Beth Tinker wears an armband against the war. Her brother Paul wears one too. Arrest came for Clarence Gideon in 1961. The charge was breaking into a pool hall in Florida and stealing from it. A lawyer was beyond his means. The court refused his request for one. A guilty verdict followed. Prison time was set at five years. A letter from him went to the Supreme Court. His point was that a lawyer's help is promised by the Constitution to anyone a court charges with a crime. The court agreed with him. A new trial was held. The new result was not guilty. After that, every court in the country had to supply a lawyer to a person who could not pay. Massachusetts, joined by other states, made a request in 2003. They wanted a federal agency to set rules for certain gases. The agency is the Environmental Protection Agency. Global warming is tied to these gases. The agency said no. Massachusetts sued. The state said the Clean Air Act required that regulation.

8Congress passed that pollution law in 1963. The agency answered that the law did not cover these gases. Justices sided with the states, 5 to 4. Regulation of the gases then became a duty. Checks and balances are another gift of the judicial branch inside the federal government. During the 1780s, the people who wrote the Constitution held a fear. One person, or one group, might try to seize control. Checks and balances among the three branches were the guard they built. Each branch has tools to limit the other two. Choosing federal judges is the main check that the executive and legislative branches hold over the courts. Choosing the judges is a duty of the president, who leads the executive branch. The Senate must then confirm each nominee. That body is part of the legislative branch. A presidential choice can be blocked by the Senate. In that way the legislative branch checks both of the other branches. The year 2009 was when President Obama named Sonia Sotomayor to the Supreme Court. Judge Thomas Porteous, of a district court, faced an impeachment trial in 2010. Payoffs were among the charges. Lying under oath was among them too.

9After confirmation, a federal judge serves for life. Nobody in the other two branches can fire that judge. This limit stops leaders from removing judges over politics. The Senate can still impeach a judge for illegal behavior. Dishonest behavior can also bring impeachment. That is a further check on the courts. Impeachment has reached only fifteen federal judges. One of them sat on the Supreme Court. In the end, eight of the judges were found guilty and removed. Judicial review is one more power of the courts. Laws from the legislative branch can be reviewed. Actions by the executive branch can be reviewed. A law or action that violates the Constitution can be struck down. Roman mythology and Roman art are the source of Lady Justice. She is one symbol of the judicial branch. Scales are in her hands, and so is a sword. A blindfold covers her eyes. Weighing facts is one value she stands for. Listening for truth without prejudice is another. Power to protect, when protection is needed, is a third. The writers of the Constitution knew a strong justice system matters to a healthy society. Conflicts can be handled in peaceful ways. Basic rights of all Americans get protection.

10Power of the executive branch is limited by the courts. Power of the legislative branch is limited too. Perfection is not a claim this system can make. Equal treatment is not always what the law itself supports. The principles behind the judicial branch rest on solid ground. Fair use of those principles can produce true justice. Federal, an adjective, concerns national power that is divided with states or with regions. Legal, an adjective, means permitted by law. Regulate, a verb, means to hold amount, rate, speed, or behavior to a level that works or that follows rules. Interprets, a verb, means explains or grasps the meaning of something. Resolve, a verb, means to decide a dispute or to find a way through a problem. Judicial, an adjective, concerns the branch that checks laws against the Constitution. A ruling, a noun, is the official decision a judge gives in court. A landmark, a noun, is a major event used to point out a new stage. Violations, a noun, are acts that break laws or ignore rules. A lawsuit, a noun, is the path a court uses to settle a dispute between people or groups.

24. Creatures Newly Found

1A map marks where people found new animals. It shows Europe, Asia, and North America. It shows the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean. It shows Africa, the Indian Ocean, South America, and Australia. Find number 1 is the lesula monkey in the Democratic Republic of Congo. Find number 2 is the Caquetá titi monkey in Colombia. Find number 3 is the Matang narrow-mouthed frog in Borneo. Find number 4 names two animals in New Guinea. They are the long-nosed tree frog and the blossom bat. Find number 5 is Goodman's mouse lemur in Madagascar. Find number 6 is Sazima's tarantula in Brazil. Find number 7 is the troll-haired planthopper in Brazil's Amazon Rainforest. Find number 8 is the Gorgon's head starfish in the Atlantic Ocean. Find number 9 is the walking bamboo shark in Indonesia. Find number 10 is the olinguito in Ecuador and Colombia. Find number 11 is the Cambodian tailorbird in Cambodia. The book opens with a short start.

2New monkeys come next. Odd frogs follow. Furry animals come after the frogs. Then come animals that have no backbone. Ocean animals follow them. Next are animals that hid in plain sight. A look ahead comes after that. A word list ends the book. The Bonaire banded box jellyfish has the scientific name Tamoya ohboya. People picked that name in a contest. The winner said most viewers would blurt Oh boy. A search for a brand-new animal species could start in a remote corner of the globe. A microscope is another place to look. It can show how one species differs from another. A yearly count of animal species that are new passes 15,000. Beside the 1.5 million species already on record, that yearly count is only 1 percent. Species around the globe are also vanishing at record rates. That is why a surprise new species matters so much. In 2007, forests in the Democratic Republic of the Congo yielded a new monkey.

3The lesula has large eyes. It is shy. It is quiet too. Someone was raising that first lesula as a pet when scientists met it. Amazon jungle is the home of the Caquetá titi monkey. About twenty other titi species live there with it. A bushy red beard makes the Caquetá stand out. Other titi monkeys have a white bar on the forehead. The Caquetá does not. Its babies purr the way cats do. Today the living number for this monkey is only about 250. That low number means the species is endangered. Pitcher plants are where the Matang narrow-mouthed frog lays eggs. The tadpoles grow in liquid inside the plant. A pea matches this frog for size. People found it in 2010. The place was the Southeast Asian island called Borneo. Before 2010, these frogs had already met scientists' eyes. They thought the frogs were young of a different species.

4Then the scientists heard the frogs calling. Only an adult frog makes a call. The calls meant these tiny frogs were already full-grown. People also call the long-nosed tree frog the spike-nosed tree frog. A herpetologist found it in 2010. His search took place in New Guinea, in the Foja Mountains. People use the name Lost World for this hard-to-reach area. This frog is known for a long nose unlike any other. A calling male lifts his nose until it looks like a spike. When the call ends, the nose falls. Nobody knows the reason. The frog's other name is the Pinocchio frog. The herpetologist spotted it on a rice bag at his campsite. Nectar is the food of the blossom bat. That feeding also pollinates the flower. The flower can then make seeds for new plants. The blossom bat was another find on the 2010 Foja Mountains trip. Bats are mammals. A nickname for it is hummingbird of the bat world.

5Its long tongue drinks nectar from rainforest tree flowers. A 2005 trip went to a different island, Madagascar. Researchers there found Goodman's mouse lemurs. The tiny lemurs are not much bigger than mice. At night they jump around in the trees. Empty nests made by birds sometimes serve as their beds by day. Lemurs live only in Madagascar. Sazima's tarantulas are found only in Brazil's tabletop mountains. Those mountains have a climate unlike the land around them. Scientists estimate that vertebrates are just 3 percent of all species. A vertebrate is an animal with a backbone. The other 97 percent of known animal species are invertebrates. An invertebrate is an animal with no backbone. Sazima's tarantula has a special kind of beauty. Dark blue color makes the body look nearly lit from within. Very little land can support this arachnid. Only Brazil's high peaks hold it. Planthoppers pass through stages of development, as most insects do.

6One young planthopper is only beginning to grow wings. The troll-haired planthopper was found in 2013. The place was the rainforests of South America. The insect may not look handsome. It does have striking hair. That wild hair is not hair at all. It is made of waxy secretions from the belly. An attack makes that hair snap away. The planthopper can then jump away to safety. Scientists think that is how the hair helps. They are not sure. Greek myth gives this starfish the name Gorgon's head, after the Gorgons. Snakes by the hundred took the place of hair on those heads. Scientists found this starfish in 2010. It was in the Atlantic Ocean. The spot was about half a mile, or 800 m, under the surface. The Gorgon's head is a species of basket star. Five curly arms branch away from its body. All five arms together may carry five thousand tips.

7Drifting food in the water is what those tips locate. The arms also let it walk along the ocean floor. The animal uses those arms to protect itself as well. Coloring set the walking bamboo shark apart as a new species. Its colors are not the same as other bamboo sharks. Earth's oceans are so huge that large parts are still unexplored. Ocean size is not why this shark stayed hidden until 2013. Like most sharks, it is no threat to humans. Indonesia's coast is where this shark stays. A color pattern unlike other bamboo sharks showed scientists it was new. People think its length is about 30 inches, or 80 cm. It pushes with its fins along the ocean floor while it looks for food. The wiggling movement makes the shark look as if it is walking. Olinguitos have smaller and rounder faces than olingos. Their tails are shorter too. People had mistaken them for olingos.

8In 2013, researchers found out the old label was wrong. Olinguitos (oh-lin-GEE-tohs) carried a mistaken species label for over a hundred years. These mammals look like teddy bears. They leap through trees at night. No raccoon relative is smaller than the olinguito. Ecuador and Colombia are the countries where it lives. Thirty-five years had passed in the Americas since a species of this type was last found. People may meet an unidentified species for years. They often think it is a familiar species. This happens a lot when two species match on the outside. Scientists call those cryptic species. The split shows up only when scientists study the genetic code. DNA technology is being used more and more. As a result, news of separate new species keeps increasing. Dense brush hides the Cambodian tailorbird, so people rarely see it. A new bird species is almost as rare as a new mammal. The year 2009 brought researchers to this tailorbird close to Phnom Penh.

9Tests proved the bird stood apart as its own species. Scientists studied its DNA and its feathers. They also studied its pretty song. All tailorbirds warble. No two species have quite the same sound. Its song is the mark that separates this tailorbird from every other kind. From 2000 to 2009, new species were counted by type. Insects totaled 88,598. Plants totaled 23,604. Arachnids totaled 12,751. Fungi totaled 11,984. Crustaceans totaled 7,070. Mollusks totaled 5,949. Bacteria totaled 4,417. Fish totaled 3,587. All other kinds totaled 18,351. The counts come from a decade review by the International Institute for Species Exploration. New species still turn up, and the finds still surprise people. Experts agree that most species are not discovered yet. The animals we have found also bring problems into view. Many species have tiny populations. Human activity leaves many species with small habitats. Those habitats are still shrinking. Even so, each new discovery is a cause for hope.

10A find can renew the push to save a habitat. It can also renew the push to restore one. That work can save the animals living there. It can save the ones we know. It can also save ones we do not know yet. These words are used in the book. DNA is the code that carries genetic information in a living thing. Endangered means at risk of dying out completely. A habitat is the natural setting where a plant or an animal lives. A herpetologist studies reptiles and amphibians. A population counts every member of the species living in a set area. Remote means distant or cut off. Scientists are people who study one science field or more than one. Secretions are substances, usually liquids, that a plant or an animal makes and lets out. Living things that match in body and can have young form a species.

25. Daniel Inouye, Soldier and Senator

1Topics in this book come in a set order. A morning that changed a life comes first. Growing up in Hawai'i comes next. Fighting in Europe follows. A return home comes after that. Long years in Congress follow. The last years and the legacy come next. Meanings of key words come last. April 10, 1962, brought Representative Inouye to the White House for a meeting. President John F. Kennedy was the person he met. The strike date was December 7, 1941. Japanese planes hit the naval base known as Pearl Harbor. Antiaircraft shells exploded and ships burned, and the smoke filled the sky. That Sunday in Honolulu, Hawai'i, started out like any other. Daniel Inouye (ee-NOH-way) was seventeen. He was a Japanese American student. He was getting dressed for church. Music from a local radio station played while he put on a necktie. The music stopped without warning. The disc jockey started to yell. Japanese planes, he said, were bombing the naval base at Pearl Harbor nearby. Daniel guessed the station might be acting out a radio drama. He went outside with his father. They wanted to know if an attack was truly under way. Their eyes turned toward Pearl Harbor. Dark puffs from exploding shells filled the sky. Three Japanese planes passed overhead at that moment. The scene was not a drama. The attack was real. As a boy, Daniel dreamed of a career as a doctor.

2High school included volunteer work as a Red Cross aide. That training came back to him. He hurried away to help people who were wounded. America remembers that date as a dark day. Sixteen ships were destroyed or damaged in the surprise attack. More than 2,400 people were killed, and many of them were civilians. A day later, Congress declared war on Japan. The act was official. World War II now included the United States. War with Germany and Italy, the allies of Japan, followed soon after. Service to the United States in that war was what Daniel Inouye wanted. The chance would come quickly. In later years he would rank among those who served the longest in the Senate. Respect for him would be just as deep. Serving the country was already his hope while he was young. Movement from Japan to Hawai'i by farmers began in 1868. Sugar cane fields were the work that drew them. In the year 1920, 43 percent of the population in Hawai'i was of Japanese descent. Japanese Americans today form about 16 percent of residents in Hawai'i. Many more residents are part Japanese. Birth came for Daniel Ken Inouye on September 7, 1924, in Honolulu, Hawai'i. Hawai'i at that time was an American territory. Statehood had not come yet. Both parents were Japanese Americans. A move from Japan brought his father to Hawai'i. His mother had been born in Hawai'i.

3Four children were in the family, and Daniel was the oldest. Education, his parents believed, mattered deeply for every child. Land under the control of the United States military, but not a state, is an American territory. Sixty years passed as a territory before Hawai'i gained statehood. Before the territory, Hawai'i was an independent monarchy. Island rule rested with the Indigenous people. Business in Hawai'i was set up by European and American corporations. Those corporations plotted with sugar planters. Their aim was to overthrow Queen Liliuokalani of Hawai'i. She gave up power in a peaceful way so violence would not follow. The year 1898 was when the United States annexed the islands, meaning it took control. More than half a century later, statehood was granted by the U.S. Congress. Statehood arrived in 1959, and Hawai'i became state number fifty. Nisei (NEE-say) is the name for Japanese Americans of the first generation born in the United States. Daniel was one of them. After Pearl Harbor was attacked, many Nisei wanted to enter the military. The services turned them away. Discrimination against Asian Americans in the United States already had a long history. After the attack, life grew even harder for Japanese Americans. People mocked them. People labeled them enemy aliens. The year 1942 brought letters from many Nisei to President Franklin D. Roosevelt. They asked for a place in the war effort.

4Early 1943 finally opened the U.S. Army to Nisei, but only in all-Nisei units. Young Japanese Americans in Hawai'i had waited for this opening. Joy filled them. Sidewalks jammed as they rushed toward enlistment. Inouye sprinted so his name would be among the earliest on the list. Nearly three thousand Nisei in Hawai'i had volunteered by the end of March. Treatment of Japanese Americans in Hawai'i during World War II was not the same as treatment in many western states. Four states saw this removal: California, Oregon, Washington, and Arizona. Nearly 120,000 Japanese Americans had to abandon homes and businesses. Remote camps held them as prisoners for most of the war. Everything was lost for many of them. Inouye and the other Nisei from Hawai'i joined the 442nd Regimental Combat Team. Training took place at Camp Shelby in Mississippi. Inside a year, Inouye became a sergeant. He also became a leader of a platoon. For its size and its length of service, the 442nd Regimental Combat Team earned more decorations than any other unit in U.S. military history. Seven Distinguished Unit Citations went to the 442nd. Twenty-one Medals of Honor went to it. Other awards brought the total to some 18,000. The price of those awards was high. About 14,000 men served in the 442nd. Death or a missing report came for more than 800. Wounds hit more than 4,000 others, and Inouye was among the wounded.

5A photograph from World War II shows the 442nd in France. Color guards stand at attention beside color bearers. Italy was the first combat for the 442nd. France was the next posting, in 1944. A very dangerous mission waited there. German lines had trapped 275 soldiers from the United States. Saving them was the regiment's job. Inouye and the soldiers with him found this Lost Battalion. More than two hundred of those men were brought to safety. The price was high. Some three thousand Nisei took part. Wounds, death, or disappearance hit roughly one thousand of them. Soon after that bloody mission, Inouye became a second lieutenant. His age was twenty. Fall of 1944 nearly cost him his life in France. A bullet hit his chest. Two silver dollars sat in his shirt pocket, and they turned the bullet aside. Early 1945 sent the 442nd back to Italy. German soldiers held a ridge, and that ridge was the aim of the attack. Three German machine guns opened fire on Inouye and his platoon. A shot hit him in the stomach. He still led the unit forward through heavy fire. Two of the machine guns were destroyed by him. Hand grenades were one weapon he used. His submachine gun was the other. At the third gun, a rifle grenade was fired by one German soldier. The blast shattered the right arm of Inouye.

6With his left arm he sent a hand grenade flying, and it fell inside the German spot. That grenade destroyed the position. He kept leading the attack on the ridge. A shot to the leg then dropped him. Leadership in combat came by example. Courage and determination were plain to see. Friendly soldiers carried him to a field hospital. Doctors amputated the right arm above the elbow. Bravery in that action earned him the Distinguished Service Cross. Recovery from the wounds took nearly two years. The place was a military hospital in Michigan. The year 1947 was when he left the army. His rank was captain. He was a wounded veteran. Being a Japanese American still brought him unfair treatment. A barbershop in San Francisco turned him away. Ribbons and medals covered that Army uniform. The shop owner then spoke in a rude way. Japanese customers, the owner said, were people he would not serve. Hawai'i was where Inouye went next. Study at the University of Hawai'i led him to Margaret Awamura, known as Maggie. On the second date, he asked her without warning to marry him. Her answer was yes. The wedding was in 1949. The marriage lasted fifty-eight years. One child was born, Daniel Ken Inouye Jr., in 1964. The degree in law came in 1952. The campus was George Washington University, located in Washington, D.C. Home in Hawai'i called him back.

7The city of Honolulu gave him work as its lawyer. His next step was politics, inside the Democratic Party. The Territorial House of Representatives elected him in 1954. The Territorial Senate elected him in 1958. Government officials faced Senate investigations into possible misdeeds. Senator Inouye led that work in an important way. The year 1959 made Hawai'i the fiftieth state of the nation. Voters sent Inouye into the House of Representatives. No Japanese American had served in Congress before him. The year 1962 sent him to the U.S. Senate. During the 1960s he strongly supported the civil rights program of President Lyndon Johnson. One aim was to stop discrimination. The listed grounds were race and color. Religion, gender, and national origin were listed too. Voters returned him to the Senate eight times. Many roles filled his long career. He chaired several important committees. He put hard work into programs meant to aid Hawai'i. Native Americans also had a strong supporter in Inouye. Through the Smithsonian Institution, he helped bring about Museum of the American Indian. The doors opened in 2004. The city was Washington, D.C. The year 2000 upgraded his Distinguished Service Cross to the Medal of Honor. That medal is the highest military award in the nation. Twenty other Asian American veterans received the Medal of Honor as well. Fourteen of those awards came after the veterans had died. June 21, 2000, was the ceremony date.

8President Bill Clinton presented the Medal of Honor. Daniel Inouye received it. In the course of World War II, the Medal of Honor reached only two Asian Americans. The Distinguished Service Cross, the second highest medal, went to 104. Those medals were studied again during the 1990s. The study showed an injustice. An unfair denial had blocked the Medal of Honor for twenty-one Asian Americans. Inouye was among that group. Margaret, his wife, died in March 2006. May 2008 was when he married Irene Hirano. She led the museum whose name is Japanese American National Museum. That museum stands in Los Angeles. The year 2010 brought election to a ninth term in the Senate. Hard work continued even as age advanced. Breathing difficulties sent him to a hospital, and he died there on December 17, 2012. Aloha was his last word. In Hawaiian, that word means goodbye. The year 2010 also brought them to a funeral. In 2010 they went to a funeral for a friend. The friend was Senator Ted Stevens from Alaska. Both Senator Inouye and Irene Hirano were there. The year 2013 brought him the Presidential Medal of Freedom. That honor is the highest civilian award in the nation. The same year saw the founding of the Daniel K. Inouye Institute in Hawai'i. His life story is told by the Institute. His papers are held there. Educational programs get its support.

9Community programs get its support too. People remember him as someone who was heroic in war. They also remember a statesman. Asian Americans in politics had a path he opened. He labored so every person in the United States could have a fairer life. Determination and optimism still have a model in him. Key dates mark the life of Daniel Inouye. The year 1924 brought his birth in Honolulu, Hawai'i, on September 7. The year 1942 brought graduation from high school. The year 1943 brought enlistment in the U.S. Army. Italy in 1945 was where combat cost him his right arm. The year 1947 brought discharge from the army at the rank of captain. The year 1949 brought marriage to Margaret Awamura, known as Maggie. The year 1950 brought graduation from the University of Hawai'i. The year 1952 brought his law studies to a degree. George Washington University Law School was the place. Membership in the Territorial House of Representatives began in 1954. That house served Hawai'i. In 1959 he became the first person from the new State of Hawai'i to sit in the House of Representatives. The year 1962 brought election to the U.S. Senate. The year 1964 brought the birth of his son, Daniel K. Inouye Jr. The year 1973 put him on the Senate committee that investigated the Watergate scandal. The year 2000 brought the Medal of Honor.

10The year 2006 brought the death of his wife, Maggie. The year 2008 brought marriage to Irene Hirano. The year 2010 brought election to a ninth consecutive Senate term. The year 2012 brought his death on December 17, at the age of eighty-eight. Amputated, a verb, means cut off. A platoon, a noun, is a soldier group made from sections and also from squads. At least two of those smaller units form it. A lieutenant often has the lead. Committees, a noun, are groups that consider a course of action or decide on one. Regimental, an adjective, has to do with a large military unit. Discrimination, a noun, means treating someone, or a whole group, in an unfair way because of gender, race, age, religion, or another difference. The Senate, a noun, is one lawmaking house of the U.S. Congress, and each state has two members in it. A similar body can exist in a state. Grenades, a noun, are small exploding weapons. The House of Representatives, a noun, is the lower house in a lawmaking branch of government. A sergeant, a noun, is a military rank above a corporal and below a lieutenant. A statesman, a noun, is a political leader who has earned respect. A lieutenant, a noun, is an officer rank above a sergeant and below a captain. A veteran, a noun, is someone who has served in the armed forces.

26. Days of Life Aboard a Space Station

1A picture shows astronaut Robert L. Curbeam Jr. He smiles for the camera aboard the station. He has just finished a successful space walk. The parts of this book follow in order. First comes weightless living. Then comes the race to space. Then come space stations. Then comes an astronaut's life. Then comes sleeping. Then comes eating. Then comes using the toilet. Then comes bathing and teeth cleaning. Then comes free time. Then comes exercising. Then comes the return to Earth. A glossary comes last. One view shows a space shuttle joined to the International Space Station. You wake just as the Sun is rising. It takes a moment to untangle from the sleeping bag. The space around you is small. You open the door. You grab a handle and flip yourself over. A gentle push sends you flying. You fly from one end of the room to the other. Another crew member floats by. He hands you some coffee. He says good morning in Russian. You thank him. You yawn. You are tired because you have not slept much.

2Your stomach is not used to the lack of gravity yet. You are incredibly excited. You are about to begin your first day of work. The work is on the International Space Station. Without gravity, even a simple activity turns difficult. You almost have to learn every one of those activities again. You will not head back to Earth for another six months. You may as well get started. The push to reach space took off in the 1950s and the 1960s. The first person in space was cosmonaut Yuri Gagarin. He was from Russia. Russia was then part of the Soviet Union. He orbited Earth one time. He landed safely after 108 minutes. The date was April 12, 1961. In 1968, United States astronauts flew on Apollo 8. They successfully orbited the Moon. The crew of Apollo 11 set foot on the Moon's surface on July 20, 1969. Space programs created vehicles for shorter stays. People could stay in space for days. They could stay for weeks. Scientists were also building a structure for a longer stay.

3In it, people could actually live for months. They could live for years. A picture shows the Soyuz spacecraft. Soyuz craft were first created in the 1960s. They are still used to take people to the International Space Station. They also bring people back. The first space station went up in 1971, sent by the Soviet Union. Its time in space lasted 175 days. Skylab was launched by the United States in 1973. Skylab supported three missions. It was abandoned in 1974. Mir (MEER) was a space station the Soviet Union sent up in 1986. No earlier space station had lasted more than a few years. It remained in orbit for fifteen years. That was three times longer than planned. Visitors included scientists from many different countries. A picture shows a space shuttle docked with Mir. A note tells about 438 days in space. A Russian scientist spent 438 days in space. The years were 1994 and 1995. During his stay, he lived on Mir. He orbited Earth 7,075 times.

4His record still stands. Work on a newer space station started while Mir was still up. The new station was larger. The United States worked on it. Russia worked on it. Japan worked on it. Canada worked on it. Other countries worked on it too. The first section of the International Space Station reached orbit in 1998. The short name is the station. More sections have been added over the years. More sections are planned for the future. As of 2012, the station was as long as a football field. It weighed almost 1 million pounds (453,592.3 kg). Over the years, more than 240 people have visited the station. They came from nineteen countries. A stay of about three to six months is common for many astronauts. Staying there can be very difficult. Scientists have found ways to make living in space easier. A picture shows an astronaut practicing a repair. The practice uses an underwater model of the station. Practice for a space walk can take place underwater. School fills many years before training for astronauts can start.

5The physical training includes being weightless. They become weightless while flying in an airplane. Astronauts also spend time in a space suit underwater. The time can be up to seven hours. They work on models of space vehicles. The work is in a huge pool. A note explains being sick in space. Some people feel ill when they first become weightless. The station comes with special bags. A bag has a cloth side that can be used to clean up. An extra sealable bag is attached. That extra bag is for easy disposal. The station has six small sleep pods. Each pod holds a sleeping bag with armholes. Each bag is tied to the wall. The tie keeps the astronaut from floating away. The station's orbit changes what the crew sees. Astronauts on board see sixteen sunrises every day. They also see sixteen sunsets every day. People are used to sleeping at night. They are used to being awake during the day. The many sunrises can cause sleep problems. A shades-down period is part of the station day.

6During this time, it is dark. It is also quiet. The quiet dark lets the astronauts get the sleep they need. A picture shows an astronaut getting ready to sleep on the station. Another picture shows a meal. An astronaut has eggs and sausage on a tortilla. Coffee sits with the meal. Astronauts eat many foods that most people enjoy. Chicken is one of those foods. Fruits are another. Peanut butter is another. Brownies are another. The station has no refrigerator. All food has to be packed so it will not spoil. An oven, though, is part of the station. Some meals need no extra preparation. Other food must have water added. Spaghetti is one food that needs water. Astronauts use liquid salt. They use liquid pepper. Regular salt would float away. Regular pepper would float away too. Floating grains could damage the station equipment. Once the food is prepared, another challenge remains. Astronauts still have to get used to swallowing. Astronaut Piers Sellers described the feeling. He said it was like eating while he lay on one side.

7Using the bathroom can be tricky. Toilet use on the station calls for special training. Each toilet on the station is built in two parts. For liquid waste, astronauts use a tube. The tube has a nozzle. For solid waste, an astronaut must stay in place. Foot straps help. Handles help too. The toilet takes the waste away from the opening. It also takes waste from the tube. The waste goes to a place where it is stored. A picture shows the tube hanging on the wall. That tube is for liquid waste. Solid waste is handled by the base on the floor. The station has two toilets. A picture shows an astronaut washing her hair on the station. Bathing starts with a cloth. Astronauts use water and special soap on the cloth. They wash their bodies that way. They wash their hair with shampoo. The shampoo uses no water. Teeth get a special toothpaste. The toothpaste can be swallowed when they are finished. Free time on the station can be spent in many ways.

8Astronauts might play cards. They might read. They might write. Other choices exist too. Time to talk with loved ones matters to the crew. Astronauts are able to make calls. They can use video chats. Sometimes astronauts answer questions from students. The students are all over the world. Sometimes astronauts give video tours of the station. Many just look out the windows. Many take photos. Who can blame them? The view is pretty amazing. A picture shows an astronaut playing guitar on the station. During his last visit, he even made a music video. Another picture shows an astronaut running. The run is on a treadmill on the station. On Earth, exercise works bones and muscles against gravity. That work makes bones and muscles stronger. The lack of gravity in space makes moving things very easy. Because of this ease, bones weaken over time. Muscles weaken over time too. Every day, their exercise has to last at least two hours. A daily workout is not enough to prevent all loss.

9Their bones are weaker when they return to Earth. Their muscles are weaker too. A picture shows a Japanese astronaut after the return to Earth. The year is 2021. The astronaut gives a thumbs-up. The ride back was a crew capsule. Return to Earth does not finish the job. A six-month stay on the station has a cost. It takes six weeks of exercise to regain strength. An astronaut's bones need more time. Bones may need a full year before they are strong again. Astronauts have to work hard. The time spent living in space is worth it. The time spent working there is worth it too. Physical health of the astronauts is always tracked by scientists. They also keep track of mental health. The information they gather has already helped astronauts. Those astronauts live healthier lives. They live happier lives. Future trips to space might succeed because of this information. It might instead mean the failure of that travel. A glossary gives word meanings. Astronauts is a noun. Astronauts are people trained to travel and work in space.

10Cosmonaut is a noun. A cosmonaut is a person trained to travel and work in space. It is a Russian word for astronaut. Gravity is a noun. It is the natural force that tends to pull objects toward each other. One example is objects pulled toward the center of Earth. Launched is a verb. It means someone used force to start something moving. Missions is a noun. A mission is a flight of a spacecraft or an aircraft. The purpose is to complete a special assignment. A nozzle is a noun. It is a part at the end of a hose, pipe, or tube. It directs and controls a stream. The stream may be liquid. It may be gas. Orbited is a verb. It means something revolved around another object. A space station is a noun. It is a spacecraft in which people can live for a long time. It is used for scientific experiments and research. Training is a noun. It is the teaching of a specific skill or behavior.

27. Departments That Help Run the Nation

1Topics in this book come in a set order. An introduction comes first. Rules for a new nation come next. The cabinet follows. After that come the rising needs of a country that was getting bigger. A closing section follows. Meanings of key words come last. Washington, D.C., holds the Pentagon, one of the largest office buildings in the world. A picture shows the founding fathers drafting the Declaration of Independence. Building a house means hiring workers for special jobs. Walls are the work of carpenters. Roofs are the work of roofers. Pipes are placed by plumbers. Wires are put in by electricians. Each of these workers is an expert at the job. Working systems for the whole home are their goal. About 2.5 million people lived in the United States when it became a new nation. A house needs working systems, and the new country needed them too. Citizens needed those systems to work well. The founders knew this need. They designed a way for experts to build systems and run them. Help for the president in running the country was part of that design.

2In 1788, leaders in America approved a constitution written for their new country. Ideas and rules in that document were meant to guide government of the young country. Three branches, or parts, are how the Constitution splits the government. Congress is the legislative branch. The U.S. Senate sits in it. The House of Representatives sits in it too. Lawmaking is the work of Congress. Speaking for the citizens is also its work. Courts form the judicial branch. The president leads the executive branch. Daily work of governing the country is handled in that branch. Making laws is the legislative job, done by the Senate and the House of Representatives. Carrying out laws is the executive job, done by the president, the vice president, and the cabinet. Judges decide the ways laws may be used, and also the ways they should be used. The Supreme Court does this work, along with other federal courts. The founders gave long thought to an elected president. The nation would rest on that chief executive. One person alone faced a huge job. George Washington saw this even before he was president.

3Washington said a single person could not finish every large task the country demanded. Heads of state through history often kept advisors beside them. Founders of the country knew that pattern. The Constitution took the idea up. Expert advisors called secretaries could head executive departments. Those advisors form the cabinet that serves the president. Their help keeps the systems of the country working well for citizens. Section 2 in Article II lets the president name cabinet members. No law forces the president to do so. Three executive departments were in place by 1788. Congress had set them up. Congress created the Department of Foreign Affairs. It also created the Department of War. It also created the Treasury Department. Creation of the Office of Attorney General came in 1789. Office began for George Washington in 1789, and picking the leaders was his task. He sent each choice to the Senate so it could give approval. The men he picked were the most qualified he knew. Different regions of the country were their homes. Their views were not the same. From left to right, the first cabinet showed George Washington.

4Henry Knox stood for War. Alexander Hamilton stood for the Treasury. Thomas Jefferson stood for State. Edmund Randolph stood as Attorney General. February 1793 brought the first meeting of the cabinet. Arguments were common between Thomas Jefferson, who handled foreign affairs, and Alexander Hamilton, who handled the treasury. A strong central government was what Hamilton wanted, and Washington agreed. Rights of the states were what Jefferson believed in strongly. Resignation came from Jefferson in December 1793. Washington learned a lesson from that clash. He said he would not again pick cabinet members who disagreed about how to govern. Later choices mostly went to people who would support the president's position. Later presidents kept this habit, along with several others. One habit says a cabinet member has a single boss, the president. Approval must come from the Senate. Even so, the member answers only to the president. After approval, Congress cannot dismiss a department secretary. A request to leave can come only from the president. A last habit says cabinet members hold no power of their own. Presidential authority is not shared with them.

5Advice is allowed. The directions of the president must be followed. Presidential cabinets grew as the nation grew. Those first departments and offices did not stay the same, and their size increased. More experts were appointed so presidents could run the country. Specific duties belong to each department. The Department of Foreign Affairs took a new name, the Department of State. Building good relationships with other countries, and keeping them, is its main job. Ambassadors work inside those countries. Diplomats work there too. In those countries they act for the United States. Washington, D.C., holds the main offices of the State Department. More than 190 countries hold over 300 embassies of this department. State Department offices fill Harry S. Truman Federal Building. Defense offices use the Pentagon. Active duty there includes 1.8 million people. The year 1947 brought a vote in Congress to unify four services. They were the U.S. Army, the Navy, the Marines, and the Air Force. One department would hold them all. That department is the Department of Defense. The Pentagon in the capital is the headquarters site. Command of the armed forces as commander in chief belongs to the president.

6Protecting the nation with military forces is the management job of the Defense Department. No other cabinet department is larger. Service personnel number over 1.3 million. Civilian employees number 742,000. Soldiers were housed in the Treasury Department building during the Civil War. Among departmental buildings in Washington, it is the oldest. Regulation of the nation's banks belongs to the Department of the Treasury. Taxes are collected by it. Paper money is printed by it. Coins are minted by it. Most tasks given in 1789 continue inside the same department. Some duties have moved. Business matters now belong to the Department of Commerce. Kentucky holds much of the gold reserve of the United States, at Fort Knox. The Treasury Department includes Fort Knox. By 1870 the Attorney General's office had grown into an entire department. That department is the Department of Justice. Anything legal that touches the executive side of the federal government is handled in this department. Obedience to federal laws is its charge. The Federal Bureau of Investigation is part of this department. U.S. marshals are part of it.

7U.S. attorneys are part of it. Federal prisons are managed by it too. Like the other cabinet departments, Justice sits in the executive branch. It does not sit in the judicial branch. A local police force is a fair comparison. Justice applies the law. Courts belong to the judiciary. Federal courts exist to test whether applied laws are fair. Every law must follow the Constitution. Work on the Justice building finished in 1935. The building is named for Robert F. Kennedy. Digital maps came much later than the Department of the Interior. Mapping of land features has been a task of the U.S. Geological Survey from 1849 onward. Interior began as its own department in 1849. People often call it the housekeeper of the nation. Natural resources of the country are its main duty. Programs for various groups of Indigenous peoples are also in its charge. Fish and Wildlife Service, a United States agency, belongs to this department. The National Park Service belongs to it too. Ocean law and sea law are the Coast Guard's enforcement work. That service is a branch of the Armed Forces of the United States.

8Control covers both waters of the United States and international waters. The Treasury Department managed the Coast Guard at first. The year 1967 moved it into the Department of Transportation. Control now sits with the Department of Homeland Security. New York Harbor is on the route of a Coast Guard patrol boat. The year 2002 created the Department of Homeland Security. Keeping citizens safe within national borders is the mission. Among cabinet departments, it is the third largest. Staff size is close to 250,000. The U.S. Coast Guard belongs to this department. The Secret Service belongs to it too. Residents get protection from these agencies and from others in the department. Disasters of every kind are part of that work. Harm of every kind is part of it too. As of 2018, ten further cabinet departments also help run the government. The Department of Energy is one. The Department of Transportation is one. The Department of Labor is one. Secretaries from every department meet the president on a regular schedule. Meetings with the president also include the person who leads the Environmental Protection Agency.

9Each one shares news from inside the department. Together they discuss which actions to take. Four departments were the start of the cabinet. Growth since then has been large. Workers today number over four million. Changing times have been met with the help of that growth. Changing needs of the country have been met too. The United States can function because of this help. Importance of the cabinet has not faded. Experts in every department still serve the president. They still serve the citizens. Help with running the country is not their only role. If something happens to the president, cabinet members stand in the line of who takes office. As of 2018, the immediate line of presidential succession starts with the president. The vice president is next. The Speaker of the House is next. Next in the line is the Senate's President Pro Tempore. After that Senate officer, the heads of departments take their places. The Secretary of State is the first of those heads. The Secretary of the Treasury comes after that. The Secretary of Defense follows, and the line goes on.

10Advisors, a noun, are people who give suggestions or guidance on one subject. Agencies, a noun, are parts of a government, a company, or an organization that supply particular services. Approved, a verb, means officially accepted. Executive, an adjective, concerns the branch leading a country and putting its laws into action. Experts, a noun, are people with a great deal of knowledge on a subject. Federal, an adjective, concerns a central government sharing its power among states and regions that stay separate. Function, a verb, means to work in the way that is expected. Regulates, a verb, means sets limits on amount, rate, speed, or behavior so something works properly or follows laws and rules. Representatives, a noun, are people picked to speak, to vote, or to act for others. Security, a noun, means being safe from danger, from loss, or from threat. The Senate, a noun, is one lawmaking house of the U.S. Congress, and each state has two members in it. A similar body can exist in a state. Unify, a verb, means to join separate things into one whole.

28. Empty Towns and the Reasons People Left

1Kayakoy is a town in Turkey. A war of Greece against Turkey left the town empty. The war ended in 1922. Greece lost the war. Greek residents of Kayakoy were forced to leave. They had to go because of their religion. Bannack, in Montana, is a state park now. It is also a National Historic Landmark. John White's gold find came in summer 1862. He found it by Grasshopper Creek. Southern Montana is the modern name of that creek's region. Word of the find traveled fast. Hundreds of prospectors showed up inside a few months. They hoped to strike it rich at Grasshopper Creek. About three thousand residents filled the town by the following year. The town was known as Bannack. Over the next few years, miners found a huge amount of gold. The gold was worth millions of dollars. The town shifted when gold grew scarce. Bannack became a town with no people.

2Empty towns appear in many parts of the world. A map shows them in North America and South America. It shows them in Europe and Africa. It shows them in Asia and Australia. A ghost town is a human settlement where people no longer live. Each empty town has a story to tell. A threat is a common reason residents go. They also leave when their work disappears. Empty buildings are what visitors walk through. They try to picture the earlier lives in those rooms. The map names Centralia, in Pennsylvania. It names Kayakoy, in Turkey. It names Okuma, in Japan. It names Bannack, in Montana. It names Kolmanskop, in Namibia. It names Pripyat, in Ukraine. It names Grytviken, on South Georgia. It names St. Kilda, in Scotland. It names Plymouth, on Montserrat. It names Ordos, in China. It names Craco, in Italy.

3It names Villa Epecuen, in Argentina. A used-up resource empties many towns, and Bannack is one example. A railroad worker's sparkly find came in 1908. He found it in southern Africa's Namib Desert. Word got out that the find was a diamond. People flocked to the region. Kolmanskop grew not long after the find. Say the name KOHL-mun-skop. Germans built Kolmanskop after the diamond discovery. Diamond finds later grew scarce in that spot. Several hours south, people found a richer supply. People moved away. Today, tourists walk through the buildings. Sand inside reaches their knees. Wildlife now lives at Grytviken on South Georgia. Penguins live there. Seals live there. A research station is the only settlement on South Georgia. Say Grytviken as GRIT-vee-kuhn. This old settlement lies at the world's far southern edge. It is a ghost town with a haunted feel. Whaling once made Grytviken a busy port.

4South Georgia is a remote island. The island is in the South Atlantic Ocean. For close to sixty years, companies hunted whales there. The hunting began in 1904. Dozens of companies took part. They wanted whale meat. They wanted blubber. The kill total there passed 175,000 whales. Over time, fewer whales were around the island. By 1965, the whaling firms had left. Everyone left. Buildings remain in Grytviken. Boats remain. Equipment remains. Events in nature can also turn settlements into ghost towns. Eruptions continue on the island of Montserrat. Say the name mon-sur-RAT. Ash now covers areas that once thrived. More than ten thousand people lived on the island before 1995. Montserrat is in the Caribbean. A long quiet had held the island volcano. The quiet had lasted more than three hundred years. The eruption started in July 1995. It has been erupting ever since. Lava spills out of the volcano.

5Gases spill out. Hot ash leaves the volcano in dangerous clouds. People have left the southern half of Montserrat. The capital was Plymouth. An eruption destroyed Plymouth. A permanent move took more than half the island's people. Craco, in Italy, is known for unusual beauty. The empty village has been shown in several movies. Natural events also emptied Craco. A steep cliff holds the village. The cliff is more than 1,300 feet above the land around it. That height is 400 m. Trouble came in many forms across the years. They faced illness. They faced crime. They faced poor farming conditions. They dealt with earthquakes. They dealt with landslides. Craco once held more than two thousand residents. Over one thousand people departed from 1892 to 1922. An easier life was what they went to find. Landslides almost totally destroyed Craco between 1959 and 1972.

6The remaining residents were moved. They went to a valley area. The valley was about 5 miles away. That distance is 8 km. Centralia, Pennsylvania, still had residents in 1983. Before a terrible accident, the main street had looked much the same. Deadly land can make a place nearly impossible for life. Coal mines and mountains define Centralia. About two thousand people lived there in the middle of the 1900s. May 27, 1962, changed Centralia for all the years after. The town trash dump was set alight by firefighters. They wanted to clean it up for a holiday. An old mine pit lay under the dump. The pit was on the outskirts of town. The flames were not fully stopped. Abandoned mines near the pit took in the flames. No one could stop the fire. After the fire burned for years, it moved under the town. Carbon monoxide leaked into people's homes.

7Carbon monoxide is a deadly gas. The ground split into open holes. Many people moved away. Houses were torn down. A few residents refused to leave. A court fight was their way to claim a right to remain. In 2013, eight residents were still in Centralia. Permission let them remain until life ended. Standing buildings are few. Many more years of burning are likely. The main street of 2000 barely matched the old view. Most people had moved away. Crews had pulled down many town buildings. Fire is not the only deadly force. Water can match it. Shoppers cannot enter one Okuma shopping area. The zone came after a nuclear accident. Before March 2011, Okuma was a calm town on Japan's coast. Japan's coast was hit by a powerful earthquake offshore. Huge tsunami waves followed the quake. The waves hit a nuclear power plant. Harmful radiation poured into the air in a huge amount.

8People living near the plant had to move away. Thousands of them were from Okuma. Years afterward, Okuma's radiation was still high. A return date for residents is unknown. They may never be allowed to return. A nuclear accident made an empty town in another country too. The city of Pripyat is in Ukraine. It was home to workers at the Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant. Their families lived there too. A deadly nuclear accident forced people to leave Pripyat. The accident was in 1986. Nature now claims St. Kilda. About 680,000 seabirds live on it. Towns are sometimes left empty because they are too remote. Scotland's St. Kilda is a cluster of small islands. A gap of 110 miles separates them from Scotland's western mainland coast. That distance is 177 km. The islands held fewer than two hundred residents.

9Their stay lasted more than two thousand years. Resources on the islands ran too low as years passed. Growing enough food was beyond what residents could manage. They lacked the means to heat their homes. Many young people moved away. Others were lost to illness. Remote life grew too hard to continue. Larger islands received the final St. Kildans in 1930. There were thirty-six of them. In Ordos, China, homes stand empty. Office towers stand empty. Other buildings stand empty too. Construction of new buildings continues. No one is moving there. Most ghost towns were lived in at one time. The world's largest ghost town is really a ghost city. It has always been empty. A huge coalfield came to light early in the 2000s. The coalfield is in Ordos, in northern China. Kangbashi New Area was built for a very large population. Hundreds of thousands of residents were the planned population.

10The city was poorly planned. Kangbashi is too large. Kangbashi is too expensive. Almost nobody lives in the brand-new city. Villa Epecuen is a ghost town in Argentina. Say the name VEE-yuh e-pe-KWEN. It is located on a salty lake. A bad storm struck the area. A nearby dam failed because of the storm. The town was under water for many years. Some empty towns are old. Others are new. The reasons for them are not all the same. One shared trait links them. They are lonely places. They have secrets to share. The stories put life back into the quiet streets. Shadows and memories may be all that is left. Think about cities you have lived in. Think about towns you have visited. A few of those places could stand empty hundreds of years later. Something could cause the people to move away.

29. Europe and the Black Death

1A map follows the Black Plague from year to year. It marks Lubeck, Amsterdam, London, and Danzig. It marks the Atlantic Ocean. It marks Paris, Kiev, Vienna, and Nuremberg. It marks Venice, Bordeaux, Marseille, and Genoa. It marks the Black Sea. It marks Ragusa, Barcelona, Valencia, and Naples. It marks Rome and Constantinople. It marks the Mediterranean Sea. Colors show the years 1347, 1348, 1349, 1350, and 1351 of the Common Era. Some places on the map stayed mostly untouched. The sight at the docks must have been terrible. The smell must have been terrible too. Messina's port took in a dozen ships of trade during October 1347. Messina is in Sicily. Sicily is part of Italy today. The ships had crossed the Black Sea on a long voyage. Their arrival likely stirred excitement on the docks. Ships of this kind often brought news from distant lands. They often brought goods as well. The people who met the ships received the shock of their lives. They may also have met what would kill them. Most of the crew were already dead. Sailors still hauling ropes were dying. Sailors still handling sails were dying. Open sores oozed on their skin. People in Europe had already heard talk of a terrible sickness in Asia. These ships and these sailors carried that sickness into Europe.

2The sickness was the plague. The name Black Death came into use in later years. It was the worst burst of disease Europe has ever lived through. In the 1300s, nobody had yet tied germs to getting sick. People did not know that bacteria could cause a cold. They did not know that a virus could cause the flu. They did not know a virus could cause other sickness either. Many people then treated disease as something beyond nature. Some blamed the plague on the lining up of the planets. One doctor said a healthy person could catch it by meeting a sick person's eyes. People had also missed the link between the plague and fleas. Fleas were the creatures actually carrying the disease. Those fleas had almost surely hidden on the ships that tied up at Messina. Scientists did not find the right kind of bacteria until 1894. That find came almost 550 years later. One kind of flea carries these bacteria inside its gut. Rats are the animals this flea normally bites for blood. The bacteria killed the rats they had been living with. The fleas then had to search for other animals to bite. People were among the animals they found. A short chain shows the part rats and fleas played. First, a rodent catches the infection.

3Next, a flea bites that rodent. Then the plague bacteria multiply inside the flea's gut. After that, the flea bites a person. Then the person is infected. Filth in people and in cities made the danger worse. In the 1300s, human waste was often thrown into the streets. Garbage was thrown there too. The cities were packed with people. Bugs were regular guests inside houses. A bite from a flea was an ordinary part of life. A bite from some other pest was ordinary too. Some investigators still doubt that fleas alone set the Black Death loose. Other diseases can also set off deadly waves of illness. Even so, the bacterium inside the fleas is still the main suspect. The kinds of sickness people described support that view. One thing seems clear. People started passing the deadly illness from person to person. Pus from a sick person could infect someone else. Blood could infect someone else. Spit could infect someone else. The next victim could catch the illness from an ordinary cough. From that moment, almost nothing could stop the plague. Europe was being crossed by the Black Death as of 1348. Travelers carried it into new places. Traders carried it too. Often, weeks passed before a sick area understood how bad things were. By then, nothing could be done.

4No treatment existed then for a disease this serious. When the illness showed itself, a victim ran a fever. The victim vomited. Swelling appeared. Strong pain came with it. Death might come quickly. A victim might last only a few days. Only one person in five lived, historians believe, once the signs had shown. The word bubonic comes from the word buboes. A bubo is a lymph node swollen until it hurts. They show up in the neck. They show up in the armpits. They show up in the groin. In a person with plague, a bubo can grow as large as a chicken egg. Skin can split when a bubo grows too far. Other forms of the plague can strike the lungs. That form brings a cough with blood. Another form strikes the blood. Patches on the face can turn black. Patches on the hands can turn black. Patches on the feet can turn black. General signs include fever. They also include a lack of energy. The head may hurt. Lymph nodes may swell into buboes. They may leak pus. They may bleed. The stomach may feel sick. The person may throw up. Joints may ache. Bubonic plague bacteria begin their attack in the lymph nodes. Those nodes sit in the neck, in the groin, and under the arms.

5Lymph nodes are important parts of the immune system. Fighting disease is the help these organs give the body. Under plague, the nodes swell up. They turn black with poisoned blood and with pus. They become boils. Those boils are the buboes. The plague can also get into the lungs. The lungs then bleed. The sickness soon arrived in France. It arrived in England. It moved outward in every direction. By 1351, almost no place in Europe had been missed. Once people understood that plague had reached their town, many fell into panic. Scared families left sick relatives behind. Doctors ran from the places they served. Priests ran from the people they served. People from the cities headed for the countryside. They thought the country would be safer. Their flight often carried the disease farther. Few people had a useful plan for stopping it. Desperate people tried cures that did no good. Many burned incense. Many wore flowers, hoping to clean the air. At the least, smoke and flowers may have hidden the terrible smells around them. Many people turned to faith and hoped to be saved. Groups of Christians walked from one town to the next. They struck their own backs with whips that had spikes. They prayed to be forgiven for their sins. They prayed to be spared.

6In some places, Christian groups put the blame on Jews. That blame was wrong. Many Jewish communities were attacked. People from those communities were driven away. Some were killed. A few cities tried ordinary good sense instead. Port cities soon saw that arriving ships were one way the disease came in. They started turning away ships from areas already sick with plague. A wait of forty days was used as a test. The wait was meant to show that the crew did not have the plague. Only after that wait could the crew step onto land. Officials in some areas learned another lesson. If people stopped moving around, the disease spread more slowly. Families who were sick could not leave their houses. Officials limited travel into their cities. They limited travel out of their cities. They limited trade as well. Work crews buried the dead as fast as they could. They feared a body could pass the illness to others. In some areas, public order had fallen apart. Without order, it was hard to make people follow the rules. Picture half the people in your city gone. The shops hold no food. They hold no other goods either. Daily life looked like that for many survivors of the worst period. Historians can only estimate how many died. About eighty million Europeans were alive before the Black Death hit.

7Experts think 40 to 60 percent of them died. The share of deaths was very different from place to place. Cities usually suffered the most. Crowded, dirty neighborhoods let the disease move easily. Nearly 60 percent of the people in London died. Half the people in Paris died. After 1351, the plague slowed down. Researchers still do not fully know why. People who lived through it may have gained resistance. The bacteria may also have shifted into a form that killed fewer people. Five hundred years more brought outbreak after outbreak. None of them matched how deadly the Black Death had been. When it was over, Europe was a very different place. Workers were badly scarce. Too few skilled makers of goods caused serious trouble. Too few laborers caused serious trouble too. Workers could demand higher pay. They could demand better treatment. A new machine, the heavy plow, helped end the lack of food after the plague years. Farmers could turn the soil much faster. Horses pulled the plow. Oxen were no longer the animals doing that pull. With too few peasants to work them, fields were left without seed. Some fields were left without a harvest. Farm animals died because nobody looked after them. Because of that loss, people in Europe did not have enough food.

8Starvation followed. Survivors also changed how they saw society. They changed how they saw the meaning of life. Many started to doubt their trust in the church. Some stood up to their rulers. The power of those leaders had failed a basic test. It had not kept safe the people under their control. Progress showed up in many ways afterward. Farmers tested new methods. They wanted more food from fewer workers. Public health changed for the better. Medicine changed for the better too. More towns grasped why cleanliness matters. They grasped why sanitation matters. They also built better plans for the next wave of disease. Modern medicine has made another Black Death almost impossible. Today's medicines can cure most cases of plague quickly. Cleaner public sanitation helps hold back most kinds of disease. Health programs help too. Alexander Fleming belongs to that later chapter. Antibiotics are a late arrival in human history. Before the middle of the 1800s, some healers noticed a pattern. Certain molds from plants attacked bacteria. Other fungi attacked bacteria too. Study in the 1800s showed much more clearly how microbes and disease are tied. Then, in the 1920s, a Scottish biologist named Alexander Fleming found something by accident. A mold from a plant destroyed a dangerous kind of bacteria. His work produced penicillin.

9Penicillin is a wonder drug. It can kill many kinds of infection caused by bacteria. In 1945, Fleming received the Nobel Prize in Medicine for that breakthrough. In the middle of the 1300s, the Black Death filled Europe with fear. Its mystery was part of the fear. Its horror was part of the fear. Many who survived believed the world was ending. Measured by how many died, it is still the most destructive event in Europe's history. Some historians also connect later useful changes to this disaster. Large changes swept Europe between the late 1300s and the 1600s. That span of years is called the Renaissance. Medicine improved in a major way. Science improved. Art improved. The word Renaissance means rebirth in French. What Europe needed once the Black Death had passed was a rebirth. England felt an economic shock once the Black Plague had passed. Farmers were wanted in great numbers. Laborers were wanted in great numbers too. Pay for them rose by as much as 40 percent. The climb in pay ran through the 1340s and on to the 1380s. That shift in society alarmed the upper classes. It alarmed the nobility. Royal officials passed a law that tried to lock peasant wages in place. Wages were frozen at the level used before the plague. They passed a second law to keep peasants in their home villages.

10They did not want peasants leaving to hunt for better work. Those harsh rules led to a major rising of the people. The rising is called the Peasants' Revolt. It happened in 1381. In that year, King Richard II faces the army of peasants. Some words from this account have set meanings. Bacteria are tiny living things made of one cell. They sometimes cause infection and disease. Boils are red, sore spots on skin that have become infected. Antibodies travel in the immune system so the body can fight infection. Incense is a material that gives off a pleasant smell when it burns. Infected means made sick by an organism or a substance that causes disease. An outbreak is a sudden spread of a disease, or of fighting, or of another unwelcome thing. Pus is a thick liquid, usually yellow-white, made in tissue that is infected. Sanitation covers the work and the tools that keep a place clean or carry waste off. Starvation is extreme hunger, or dying because there is no food. Supernatural describes what science or nature's laws cannot explain. Symptoms are the particular signs of an illness or an injury. Viruses are tiny organisms that infect the body. The word also names the diseases a virus causes.

30. Famous Players of Jazz

1This book starts with the roots of jazz. Then it meets Louis Armstrong, Ella Fitzgerald, and Duke Ellington. Later pages meet more jazz greats and look at jazz today. Early jazz was dance music with a bright feel. Can you name a music style America invented? That music makes people want to move. One kind races in a wild way. Another kind stays slow and keeps a clear tune. Rock and roll came after this music. Think of a trumpet blending with a saxophone. A clarinet can join them. Drums and other instruments can join too. A beat may set your foot tapping. The same beat may set your hands clapping. Was jazz the style you named? That answer is the right one. The United States is where jazz began. The early 1900s were the time of that beginning. Roots of this music reach the years before the Civil War. Jazz stands on a long history of African American music. Part of it grew from songs made by enslaved people. The southern United States held the quarters where those songs were made. Few paths let them share thoughts, and song was one path. It was also a way to share ideas. It carried their wish for freedom. Blues grew from this same tradition. Jazz and blues often blend. The two styles also overlap. Some of the music held sadness. Some of it held joy. A voice could stand in for an instrument. Sometimes they built instruments themselves. An early form of the banjo was one of those instruments. Some songs were learned by memory.

2Singers carried them from plantation to plantation. Improvisation shaped a large share of the music. Players invented it while they went along. Jazz grew out of that early African American music. It also led to rock, to blues, and to hip-hop. Jazz players often improvise side by side. The playing can feel like a joyful game. At the center of jazz, improvisation remains. Musicians get together and jam. Jamming means inventing the music during the playing. They might start from a song they all know. Each player adds a little in one spot. Each player adds a little in another spot. Soon the song is unlike any other. Many great jazz names today belong to African Americans. A few famous players come next. Many people rank Louis Armstrong as the top musician of the 1900s. Some give him the name Father of Jazz. Jazz was already born before Louis. His way of playing made people love the music. His songs made listeners happy. No trumpet before his had sounded like that. He began in a fast style called Dixieland. Later he tried many other styles. His family had little money. New Orleans, Louisiana, was his hometown. As a child he stood outside dance clubs. Lively music played inside while he listened. At thirteen he bought a cornet that was not new. A cornet is a kind of horn. During his twenties he changed over to trumpet. His trumpet was almost always with him. Louis was first to put scat on a recording. Scat turns the voice into an instrument.

3The singer uses syllables in place of whole words. The sounds can seem like nonsense. A strong singer makes scat seem wonderful. Louis told how scat singing got its start. During a recording he dropped the sheet music. The words would not come back to him. He knew the take had to keep going. Syllables came out of his mouth instead. Listeners loved that scat. Other jazz players soon copied the style. A singer who scats becomes one of the band. Audiences of every race enjoyed his performances. Audiences of every nationality enjoyed them too. Louis once said music was his language. His trumpet went with him all over the world. Other languages were not ones he spoke. People still understood him when the horn sounded. Louis Armstrong was born in 1901. The place was New Orleans, Louisiana. He played trumpet. He also sang. His first recording was made at age 22. His first band was called Louis Armstrong and His Hot Five. Hello, Dolly is one popular song. What a Wonderful World is another. Mack the Knife is a third. He died on July 6, 1971. Louis may have been the inventor of scat. Ella Fitzgerald carried that style much further. Her voice did astonishing things. That voice was her instrument. Ella Fitzgerald was born in 1917. Newport News, Virginia, was her birthplace. Stompin' at the Savoy is one song linked to her. A-Tisket, A-Tasket is another. She earned thirteen Grammy Awards. In 1987 she received the National Medal of Arts. The medal honored a lifetime of achievement.

4June 15, 1996, was the day she died. Harlem, in New York, is where Ella began club singing. She was seventeen then. Tap dance had been her goal. A talent contest at the Apollo Theater was her entry. She doubted that dancing would woo the crowd. Singing took the place of the dance. She had never sung for the public before that night. The contest prize was hers. Even now, many African American performers get a start at the Apollo Theater. Arnett Cobb has performed on that stage. Walter Buchanan has performed there too. Ella's voice was warm. It was also versatile. That voice won many hearts. Popular clubs across the country later hired her. Clubs around the world hired her too. The Savoy became a regular stop for her. The Savoy ranked among New York City's hottest spots for jazz. She took up singing with bebop bands. Bebop first grew popular in the 1940s. Its beat makes a listener want to move. Her voice moved along with that beat. She improvised through her bebop singing. She improvised through her scat too. On stage she jammed with some of the world's best jazz players. Some listeners call her the Queen of Scat. In 1979 people named her among the most talented American performers ever. The National Medal of Arts came to her in 1987. Put on A-Tisket, A-Tasket. That song shows why the Queen of Scat title fits. Edward Ellington was his real name. From his teen years, people called him Duke.

5Style showed in the way he dressed. He always looked elegant. His jazz carried that same style. Sophistication was part of the music too. A childhood piano teacher quit on him. Duke was only seven. He then taught the piano to himself. Reading music was another skill he taught himself later. Jazz clubs heard him play when he was eighteen. At twenty-four he moved to New York. The Cotton Club in Harlem became his stage. Duke wrote music, so he was a composer. His tunes were more melodic than most jazz of the 1930s. The same was true in the 1940s. He was an exceptional pianist. He was a bandleader as well. Listeners knew him for a swing style. Take the A Train ranks with his biggest hits. The song is about a subway ride in New York City. The ride went to his home in Harlem. It Don't Mean a Thing is another famous song. Its words say the music needs swing or it has little meaning. His life's work includes over one thousand songs. Some were complex jazz symphonies. Full orchestras were the players he wrote them for. Black, Brown, and Beige is a symphony about African American history. It is also about African American music. In jazz history, few bands are better known than Duke Ellington and His Orchestra. The band traveled through many countries. Countless bands around the world have played music he wrote. The year 1899 was his birth year. Washington, D.C., was his birthplace. His work was as a composer. He was a bandleader and a pianist too.

6The band name was Duke Ellington and His Orchestra. Take the A Train is a well-known song of his. NARAS gave him a Lifetime Achievement Award. NARAS stands for the National Academy of Recording Arts and Sciences. In 1969 he received the Presidential Medal of Freedom. May 24, 1974, was the day he died. Three of the biggest jazz names are now familiar. Louis Armstrong is one. Duke Ellington is another. Ella Fitzgerald is the third. Many more jazz artists belong to the past. A few of them are worth meeting here. Billie Holiday was a blues singer. Her voice could do astonishing things. At times she sang in a slow way. Sadness could be heard in that slow voice. At other times her songs were bouncy. Those songs were full of energy. She made a recording of Strange Fruit. Strange Fruit is a song about racism. The song is heartbreaking. In the late 1930s she toured with a popular band. Artie Shaw was the leader. Every player in that band was white. Hotels often refused her a room. The rest of the band could stay. Many restaurants refused to serve her with the band. In the United States, her fame as a singer was huge. Even so, racism met her in many cities on the tour. Miles Davis played the trumpet. He wrote songs too. The Juilliard School of Music is where his parents sent him. That school is in New York. They wanted classical music to be his study. Jazz was the music Miles loved.

7New York City offered some of the best jazz anywhere. He soon jammed with great jazz bands. Those bands were playing in the 1940s. In 1945 he helped make an early bebop record. Later he created a style of his own. He named it cool jazz. During the 1950s that style was popular. People also called the style cool. He kept testing different kinds of music.
