Rock later had an influence on him. A photo can show Miles Davis jamming beside Kenny Garrett. The shape of Dizzy Gillespie's trumpet helped him hear his own sound better. His nickname came from the way he bounced. He joked on stage. He danced on stage too. The nickname could fit the music as well. Speed was one mark of that music. Energy was another mark. Spontaneity was a third mark. In the 1930s and 1940s he worked with Thelonius Monk. He worked with Ella Fitzgerald in those years. He worked with Duke Ellington then too. He also worked with saxophonist Charlie Parker. Innovators filled that time. In 1953 a person fell on Dizzy's trumpet by accident. The bell bent backward. The bent horn's tone pleased Dizzy. From then on, builders made his trumpets in that bent shape. The bent horn became a trademark. His balloonlike cheeks became a trademark too. By the late twentieth century he was widely known. He was widely respected too. The White House even invited him to play. John Coltrane played tenor saxophone in the 1930s. He was still playing it in the 1940s.

8Swing was one style he used. Bebop was another. Drug troubles touched him, as they did many players then. Alcohol troubles touched him too. The 1950s were when he was able to overcome those troubles. He gave credit to his religion. Faith in God, he believed, helped him through the addictions. After those problems were behind him, his music turned to spirituality. Faith was part of that music too. The music is complex. It is smooth. It is moving. A Love Supreme is his most famous recording. The piece feels gentle. It feels prayerful. Sophisticated rhythms still push it along. Later in life he tried improvisation with a freer form. Traditional rhythms were left behind. Traditional melodies were left behind too. Plenty of his fans disliked that music. Even so, the experiments opened the door to modern free-form jazz. Dave Brubeck stands among the best-known pianists ever. He is an accomplished composer as well. California was his birthplace. The year was 1920. Cattle ranching was his father's work. His mother played piano. She also taught music. Teen years found him playing in local dance bands. College began with a plan to be a veterinarian. Jazz jobs were only a way to pay for school. Love of jazz later forced a new choice. Music would be his life. He started studies in music. He started studies in composition too. The year 1958 was when he formed the Dave Brubeck Quartet. Take Five became their hit song. No earlier jazz tune had sold a million copies. His music still matters now.

9Huge crowds heard him play into his 90s. He kept writing new music in those years too. A live show is the finest way to hear jazz. Who plays jazz now? Lots of people do. Jazz has shaped thousands of musicians. Some of those musicians do not play jazz themselves. Classical music borrows jazz rhythms. Rock and roll borrows them too. Hip-hop borrows jazz rhythms, jazz melodies, and jazz improvisation. Other music styles also flow into jazz. Latin rhythms are one mix players use today. Latin instruments are part of that mix. African drumming is another mix. African singing joins it. Middle Eastern melodies enter the blend. Styles from many lands enter too. Wynton Marsalis plays trumpet. His brother Jason Marsalis plays drums beside him. Wynton performs jazz. He also performs classical music. Many Grammy awards have gone to him. Sold-out concerts have heard him play. Talent for music runs through the whole Marsalis family. Ellis, their father, is a dedicated pianist. He is a music teacher too. He taught his sons to love jazz. He taught them to play it. The sons are Wynton, Branford, Delfeayo, and Jason. Wynton works so young people will love jazz. Jazz for Young People is a concert series where he plays. Lincoln Center in New York is the place. School jazz bands lead many students to love the music. Jazz band draws many students in high school today. New York City hosts a yearly contest for thousands of high school bands. The festival name is Essentially Ellington. Duke Ellington is the person the name honors.

10Other jazz band festivals welcome young players too. Young people all around the country are learning the wonder of jazz. Listening is the best path into jazz. A library's records and CDs are a good place to start. A librarian can help with that search. Librarians may already enjoy jazz. Teachers, parents, or friends may enjoy it too. Any of them can help choose albums. Some radio stations play jazz all day. Others play it for part of the day. Turn the radio dial until a jazz station appears. The announcer's words can teach the players' names. Some names from this book may come through the speaker. Names worth a search include jazz and blues. Ella Fitzgerald and Wynton Marsalis are worth a search too. So are Dizzy Gillespie, Dave Brubeck, and the Apollo Theater. Bebop is a jazz style built on strong rhythms and beats. Fast melodies are part of it. Improvisation is part of it too. Improvised music is made up on the spot. Innovators are people who help something new come into being. They may also help it grow or help invent it. Melodic music puts the tune ahead of the rhythm. It also puts the tune ahead of the harmony. A plantation is a big farm where many people work. Quarters are homes for workers. Enslaved people lived in quarters too. Scat swaps whole words for sounds and syllables. Spontaneity means following a feeling in the moment. A plan is not made ahead. Woo means to win someone over.

31. Gandhi's Road to a Free India

1A map shows lands touched by Gandhi's life and work. India is marked in Asia. Pakistan is marked in Asia as well. South Africa is marked in Africa. The Indian Ocean lies between these places. The book opens with the name Father of India. It then tells about his early life. It then tells about South Africa. It then tells how he led people in India. It then tells about the Salt March. It then tells how change began. It then tells about his death and the legacy he left. A glossary ends the book. Mohandas Gandhi (moh-huhn-DAHS GAHN-dee) was a lawyer. He was a civil rights leader. He helped India gain independence from British colonization. He was a quiet man. His will was powerful. He held a strong belief in fairness. He held a strong belief in freedom. He held a strong belief in peace. Those beliefs changed nations. Many years after his death, people still call him the Father of India. He continues to inspire people around the world. A picture shows Gandhi on the right, next to his older brother. Gandhi was born on October 2, 1869. He was born in India.

2He was born into a large family. The family was Hindu. The family believed in nonviolence. The family believed in caring for other people. His mother prayed daily. She took care of people who were sick. She took care of people who were poor. She was a great example. His father worked as a local government leader. The British ruled India. His father often settled disagreements between the local people and the British. Respect for other people was a lesson Gandhi took from his father. He also learned to respect their beliefs. At eighteen, he sailed to England so he could study law. He attended school in London. There he began to study different religions. He studied their texts. After a few years, he became a lawyer. He then returned to India. He was still very shy. He struggled to find work. In 1888, he agreed to take a job in South Africa. A picture shows him in London. That picture is from 1908. His parents arranged his marriage when he was very young. An arranged marriage was a common custom in India. He married Kasturba when he was thirteen.

3Kasturba was the same age. A picture from 1913 shows Gandhi and Kasturba together. The British ruled South Africa as well. Many laws there discriminated against Black people. The laws also discriminated against coloured people. Coloured is the South African term for mixed-race people. The laws also discriminated against Asian peoples. Gandhi thought this was unfair. He felt compelled to do something. He stayed in South Africa. He worked hard to help the Indians in that country. His work brought attention to him. It also brought attention to the struggles of Indians in South Africa. Leadership among the Indian people came to him. Equality for every group was not his goal at that time. In his belief, Native African peoples ranked below Indians. He argued that the British should treat Indians as equals. He gave a reason for that argument. He said Indians were of a similar heritage. British discrimination against Indian people still continued. A side note is called Changing Views. The idea of racial superiority was common when Gandhi was young. Anti-Black views influenced his early work. His views changed as he got older. For most of his life, he worked to end discrimination of every kind.

4In this period, every Indian in South Africa had to register with the British government. The register was used to limit rights to trade. It was also used to limit rights to land. Gandhi held a protest. During the protest, he explained satyagraha (suh-TYAH-gruh-huh). Holding firmly to the truth is the meaning of that word. He used the word for his own form of protest. His protest was nonviolent. He called on Indians to disobey the law. He called on them to accept the punishments. British police beat many Indians. They jailed many Indians. Gandhi was among those jailed. News of the protests reached around the world. News of the punishments reached around the world too. People were shocked. They spoke out against the government. An agreement with the protesters was forced on the government. Twenty years passed while Gandhi stayed in South Africa. During those years, he stopped wearing Western clothes. He wore a kurta shirt. He wore a sarong. Later, people knew him for a simple dhoti (DOH-tee). A dhoti is a loincloth. A picture shows a police officer confronting Gandhi. Gandhi is leading a protest.

5The place is South Africa. The year is 1913. Gandhi returned to India in 1915. He began working to free India from British rule. A picture shows him on that return in 1915. In 1919, the government passed acts that let authorities arrest people. The people were those who caused political trouble. Gandhi called for people to stay home from work. He also called for them to stay home from school. The stay-home call was a protest. Violence then broke out against the British. Gandhi canceled the protest. A few days later, thousands of people gathered in the city of Amritsar. Their protest was peaceful. It was also illegal. British troops surrounded the protest. Suddenly those troops started shooting. Between 400 and 1,500 Indians died. Many other people were hurt. Until Amritsar, Gandhi had been trying to work with the British government. Everything shifted after what happened in Amritsar. Soon after, he started another protest. That protest was nonviolent. He called for Indians to stop buying British goods. He called for them to stop working for the British in any way. His efforts were somewhat successful. Violent clashes still took place.

6The clashes were between Indian protesters and the British military. People saw Gandhi as the leader of the protest. He was arrested because of the violence. He was sent to prison. He was released after two years. He immediately continued his work. A picture shows Kasturba with her sons. Gandhi and Kasturba supported the same ideas. Kasturba worked with Gandhi. While he was in prison, she took his place. Four sons were born to Kasturba and Gandhi. The marriage continued until her death in 1944. A picture shows Gandhi and some followers marching toward the shore. One of his most successful protests took place in 1930. Salt production in India was allowed only for British companies. Only those companies were allowed to sell it. Any salt that was sold also carried a large government tax. Paying the British for that salt seemed unfair to Gandhi. The salt came from Indian lands. A note about salt explains the coast. Large salt flats lie along India's coast. Seawater evaporates on those flats. Salt is left behind on the ground. Indians could once gather this salt. They could use it for free.

7Then the British government made that act illegal. A map shows India and the salt march route. The starting point is marked. Dandi is marked too. A picture shows Gandhi picking up salt at the end of his long march. On March 12, Gandhi and some followers began to walk. The walk lasted 21 days. It covered 240 miles (386 km). The walk crossed India and went to the sea. Along the way, Gandhi spoke to people. He made speeches to large crowds. By the time he got to the coast, the followers numbered in the tens of thousands. The walk down to the shore came early on the morning of April 6. British soldiers surrounded him. He picked up a grain of salt from the ground. His followers did the same. Gandhi was arrested. More than sixty thousand other people were arrested with him. A picture shows Gandhi with Mohammad Ali Jinnah. Jinnah was an important Muslim leader. The arrests of Indians caused anger throughout the world. Voices against the British grew as more people spoke up. In 1942, Gandhi demanded that the British leave India. He was arrested. India saw violent protests from place to place.

8Talks about a British exit from India opened in 1945. Conflict between Hindus and Muslims shows up often in India's history. Gandhi believed these two groups could live in peace in India. Mohammad Ali Jinnah (muh-HAH-muhd ah-LEE JI-nah) was a Muslim leader. He did not believe Muslim rights would be protected after the British left. He wanted to divide the country. One part would be a mostly Muslim state. The other part would be a mostly Hindu state. A picture shows Muslims climbing onto a train. The train was headed for Pakistan. The agreement that ended British rule was signed in 1947. A split called partition divided the country. Pakistan was the new country formed by the Muslim people. The number of people who moved between the two countries was more than ten million. The move happened with too much speed. There was violence. Hundreds of thousands of people died. A number of Gandhi's peace agreements were only temporary. A note explains fasting. Fasting means going without food. It is a regular practice in many religions. Hinduism is one of those religions. Religious practice often included a fast for Gandhi.

9Fasting was also one of his ways to protest. In 1947, he fasted to protest the conflict in Calcutta. That conflict ended. He fasted again in 1948. He convinced the people of Delhi to find peace. Gandhi's memorial was built in 1950. On January 30, 1948, Gandhi was leading a prayer meeting. A young Hindu man stood in the crowd. He did not approve of Gandhi's work to make peace with Muslims. He shot Gandhi three times. People all over the world felt sadness at Gandhi's death. His funeral drew a crowd of around 150,000 people. Indians called him Mahatma. Mahatma means Great Soul. After his death, he continued to inspire important leaders. Martin Luther King Jr was one of those leaders. Nelson Mandela was another. A glossary gives meanings for words in this book. Conflict is a noun. It means a disagreement of ideas, principles, or opinions. The disagreement is between people or groups. A protest is a noun. It is an action used to express strong disagreement. It can also express disapproval. Fairness is a noun. It means justice and equality. Respect is a verb. It means you understand that someone should be treated with honor.

10It can also mean treating something in a careful manner. Hindu is an adjective. It relates to the major religion in India. That religion includes many gods. It includes a belief in reincarnation. Struggled is a verb. It means someone had a difficult time achieving a goal. It can also mean someone used force to try to stop something. It can also mean someone used force to get free of something. Inspire is a verb. It means to encourage a person to act. Muslims is a noun. Muslims are people who follow the religion of Islam. A tax is a noun. It is a fee collected by a government. The fee pays for services, functions, and operations. Nonviolence is a noun. It is the refusal to use physical force as a response. It is also the refusal to use violence as a response. Will is a noun. It is a strong determination to accomplish something. It can also be called resolve. Political is an adjective. It means having to do with government. It also means having to do with politics or politicians.

32. George Washington Carver's Free Ideas

1The year 1940 shows George Washington Carver at work in his lab. States linked to his life and his work are marked on a map. Iowa, Ohio, and Nebraska are among them. Indiana, Illinois, and Kansas are among them. Kentucky, Missouri, and Tennessee are among them. Arkansas, Oklahoma, and Mississippi are among them. Georgia and Alabama are among them. Texas, Louisiana, and Florida are among them. Missouri is where he grew up. Kansas and Iowa are where he studied. Alabama is where he worked. Carver's birth came in 1864. The place was Missouri. The Civil War was going on. The years 1861 to 1865 are the span of that war. The North and the South, two parts of the United States, were the sides in the fight. Fifteen southern states, called slave states, still allowed slavery when the war began.

2Seventeen northern states, called free states, did not allow it. Ending slavery in every state was the wish of many people in the North. Keeping slavery legal was the wish of many people in the South. A break from the North, and a country of their own, was the plan they tried. A united United States was why the North went to war. Victory went to the North. Freedom came, in time, for enslaved people in the United States. Enslaved is how George was born. Freedom came for him when the Civil War ended. Freedom came for his older brother, Jim, too. Death had already taken both parents. Very young is what the brothers still were. Six was Jim's age.

3One was George's age. They kept living with Moses and Susan Carver. That couple had held them in slavery. Field work with Moses was Jim's job. Sickness often kept George at home, where he helped Susan. Reading was something she taught him. Writing was something she taught him. Sewing was something she taught him. The garden was what he liked best. Exploring nature was something he liked too. The 1870s are the date of a photo of George with Jim, his older brother. Diamond, Missouri, is where their home stood. Plants were a subject Carver taught himself. Animals were a subject he taught himself. More learning is why he wanted school. Segregated is what many schools were then. The same school was closed to a mix of white students and Black students.

4Black students were not allowed at the school nearby. At age twelve, George left home with the Carvers so he could attend a different school. The Watkins family is who he lived with. Meals and a room were what he worked for. Thirteen is how old he was when Kansas became his home. A segregated school in Kentucky is shown in a photo from 1916. College in Kansas was closed to Carver because of his skin color. Highland University in Kansas offered a scholarship when he finished high school. Being Black was why the school turned him away when he arrived. Unhappy is how he felt. Learning was not something he gave up. Farming was one path he took. Painting was a skill he taught himself.

5Another college was what friends told him to try. Art was his subject at Simpson College in Iowa. This time he was let in. Simpson College was a place he liked. Helping other people even more was his wish. Agriculture, the science of farming, was what he chose to study at Iowa State Agricultural College. The first Black graduate of that school is what he became. The first Black professor there is what he later became. Alabama in 1896 is where he moved, and Tuskegee University is where he began to teach. Forty-seven years is how long that teaching lasted. A diploma from Iowa State Agricultural College is what a picture shows him receiving. A large problem for Alabama farmers was something Professor Carver wanted to help with.

6Smaller crops came each year. Harder struggle came each year. Worn-out soil from years of cotton was what he soon saw. Food was about all the money the farmers could buy. They could not pay for fertilizer. That fertilizer would have helped the plants. Long days of picking cotton on plantations were the work of children in the late 1800s. Carver held soil in his hand. It came from a field that was worn out. Southern crops were put to work in his laboratory. Dead leaves, added to the soil, could serve as free fertilizer. Swamp muck could serve that way too. Soil was also helped by planting sweet potatoes after the cotton was picked. Peas helped the soil. Peanuts helped the soil. Nutrients that cotton plants had used up were put back by those crops.

7How to grow these crops was information Carver sent out. How to cook them was information he sent too. Farmers who ate these crops spent less money on food from stores. Selling those new crops was another need. An inventor is what Professor Carver became. Sweet potatoes gained more than a hundred uses from his inventions. Peanuts gained three hundred uses. Work is what a picture shows him doing. Pavement was one peanut idea. Grease was one. Medicines were among them. Peanut coffee was one. Peanut mayonnaise was one. Peanut flour was one. Peanut milk was one. Shoe polish was one. Bleach was one. Sandpaper was one. Still more ideas followed. Peanut butter is something many people think he created.

8He did not create it. A paste made from peanuts was eaten by the Aztecs of central Mexico hundreds of years ago. Even before the Aztecs, the Incas of Peru ate a peanut paste too. Creamy is not a word for that older paste, unlike the peanut food people eat today. Carver's ideas had listeners by 1921. He was invited to speak to Congress. Those people make the country's laws. Laughter from some members was what he met. Fun made of him was what some offered. Ten minutes is the time he had been given. Those minutes were used well. Things that could be made from the tiny peanut were his whole talk. Silence fell on the men. More time was given when the ten minutes ended.

9A law that helped peanut farmers in the United States was passed in the end. The law raised the cost for other countries. They paid more to sell goods inside the United States. Peanuts were one of those goods. Fame like that of many celebrities today came to Carver over the years. A talk from him drew crowds. Jobs were turned down by him. Money was turned down by him. Helping people was what he thought mattered more. Free is what he believed ideas were. Given freely is how he believed they should be shared. President Franklin Delano Roosevelt was greeted by him around 1936. 33,000 dollars is what Carver gave the Tuskegee Institute not long before he died. Farm work he had started was what the gift was meant to carry on.

10About 450,000 dollars is what that sum would be today. The year 1943 is when George Washington Carver died. Seventy-eight was his age. Consideration is how he believed people should treat one another. A whole nation gave him respect. In that era, respect for Black people was rare. Long after he died, his legacy remained. Farming and raising livestock, as a science, are what agriculture means. Well known is what famous means. A natural or chemical substance that helps plants grow is fertilizer. An inventor makes something new. It can be a thing no one had built yet. A teacher at a college or a university is a professor. Kept apart because of group differences, such as race, is what segregated means.

33. George Washington Leads a New Nation

1An American flag is under Betsy Ross's hands in this picture. George Washington sits at the left. George Washington started an oath on a bright day during 1789. The oath would make him president. The road to that moment had been long. The road had also been rewarding. He had already served the country as a military leader. The top political job in the country would now be his. When the oath of office ended, a large crowd cheered loudly. The United States of America had its first president, and that man was George Washington. He took that oath in New York. The date was April 30, 1789. February 22, 1732, was George Washington's birth date. A Virginia plantation was where his parents brought him up. Enslaved people worked in the fields there. George's father died when George was eleven. After that, a half brother helped raise him. As a youth, George had little formal education. He learned more by directing the work at Mount Vernon. Mount Vernon was the family home.

2It was the place George loved best. He also loved the military. A chance to serve would come soon. The family moved when Washington was three. The new home was Mount Vernon, Virginia. The French and Indian War brought him fame for bravery. People knew him as a brave leader. No country called the United States existed yet in that period. Britain held each state as a colony. Another country is in charge of a colony's land. No national army existed then. Armies belonged to separate colonies, not to one nation. Washington began service in Virginia's army in 1752. By 1754, Great Britain and France were at war. Each side wanted land in North America. The French gained many tribes as partners in the fight. People called that war the French and Indian War. Washington led the Virginia army for the British. He helped win a major battle. People began to see him as a strong military commander. He still dreamed of going back to Mount Vernon. Martha Custis became his wife there in 1759.

3Many enslaved people came with Martha to Mount Vernon at the marriage. More than three hundred people were enslaved by the Washingtons as a couple. George's feelings about slavery changed during his life. He ordered freedom for the people his own family had enslaved. That freedom would begin when Martha died. Enslaved people connected to Martha's family were not covered by that order. Martha died in 1802. Those people were then divided among her grandchildren. A portrait of Martha Washington was made in 1876. The painting came long after her death. Thomas Jefferson and others presented the Declaration of Independence. They presented it to the Second Continental Congress. The place was Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. By the 1770s, people in the colonies were unhappy. Great Britain was taxing products in the colonies. Tea was one taxed product. The meeting of colonial leaders lasted close to two months. The meeting was the First Continental Congress. It took place in September 1774. Standing against the British was the plan they formed.

4They wanted to protect the colonists' basic rights. A full break from Great Britain was already acceptable to some leaders. Washington came to the meeting in full uniform. He was ready to fight if Britain rejected the plan. Britain did not agree. The Second Continental Congress met in the spring during 1775. A new government was formed during that meeting. Leadership of a new Continental Army was the job they offered George Washington. The army would fight the British. Independence was the cause the colonists were prepared to fight for. March 1776 brought Washington his first victory. Boston was cleared of the British army by his push. The army moved on to New York City. An easy win over the British was unlikely. They had a powerful army. General William Howe led it. In the summer of 1776, leaders finished the plans for independence. In that same season, Howe's forces attacked Washington's troops. Howe defeated the Continental Army on Long Island, in New York. Three hundred men died there at British hands.

5More than a thousand other men became their captives. Washington had to retreat. Battles from 1776 to 1777, in New Jersey and close by, appear on a map. It labels New York, Connecticut, and New Jersey. It labels Pennsylvania, Maryland, and Delaware. It labels New York City, Morristown, Valley Forge, and Philadelphia. It labels the Atlantic Ocean. It marks a distance of 50 miles. The battles listed are Long Island on August 27, 1776. White Plains comes on October 28, 1776. A move from Halifax is dated June 29, 1776. Trenton comes on December 26, 1776. Princeton comes on January 3, 1777. Brandywine comes on September 11, 1777. Germantown comes on October 4, 1777. Monmouth comes on June 28, 1778. The map marks colonial troops. It marks British troops. It marks a move from New York City on August 25, 1777. Ice floes filled the Delaware River. The temperature was frigid. Washington and his men still crossed. British soldiers taken prisoner numbered more than nine hundred. The Pennsylvania bank of that river was his camp.

6He had an idea. New Jersey was where the enemy had halted. The river's far bank was New Jersey. His men followed him across on Christmas night in 1776. The night was cold. The crossing became a surprise attack. The attack brought an important victory. The war went on for another five years. France became a partner of the Continental Army in 1778. Yorktown, Virginia, was the place Washington marched to in 1781. The British were encircled by his men and by the French forces. The British surrendered. The war was over. The colonists had won. A convention of leaders took place in 1787. The new government still needed a plan. That plan was the Constitution. George Washington led the meeting. His leadership impressed everyone. The request that followed was for him to serve as the United States' first president. The request was no surprise. He did not want the job. Saying yes felt like a duty to him. Mount Vernon was behind him when he departed in the spring of 1789.

7He went to New York City. He became president on April 30. America's first president had many important tasks. Setting up the government was his most important task. The Constitution described that government. In a picture of the 1787 meeting, Washington stands at the right. He was president of the Constitutional Convention. His way of leading fit the task. He led the nation firmly. He led with great dignity. The Continental Army had known that same style of leadership from him. Voters elected him president again in 1792. His second term brought another war. Britain and France were fighting again. France had been an ally in the American Revolution. His declaration kept the United States out of that fight. A treaty was signed. A treaty is an agreement. The treaty stayed unpopular. Many people thought it favored the British too much. They blamed President Washington. The American artist Gilbert Stuart painted the president many times. He made nearly one hundred portraits. Years of service left Washington tired.

8A third term was something he decided against. A Farewell Address was his writing just before he left office. The address offered advice. Partnerships with other nations were something he warned people to avoid. He said that choice could lead to more war. He also warned against an army that grew too big. In 1797, he began his retirement. He spent time with Martha. He oversaw his beloved Mount Vernon. Two years later, Virginia had a cold, snowy winter. Washington fell ill. December 14, 1799, was the date of his death. Family time at Mount Vernon filled his last years. The dollar bill first carried his face in 1869. That was after the Civil War. The design used now first appeared in 1963. George Washington's place in history is firm. Many Americans still admire his courage. They still admire his character. His military leadership is a strong model. His leadership as president is a strong model too. Modern military officers can follow that model. Presidents can follow it too. A congressman of his time praised him in three ways.

9He was first in war. He was first in peace. His countrymen held him first in their hearts. Those words earned him a title. The title is Father of His Country. People often shrink a figure like Washington into a simple nickname. In real life, such figures are as complex as people today. They are as imperfect as people today too. A timeline lists the life of George Washington. In 1732, he was born in Westmoreland County, Virginia. Mount Vernon, Virginia, became the family home in 1735. His 1754 combat belonged to the war called by this name, the French and Indian War. In 1775 he received the top command over the Continental Army. The appointment came during the American Revolution. In 1781, the British surrendered to Washington. In 1787, he presided over the Constitutional Convention. Election as the United States' first president came in 1789. In 1797, he began retirement at Mount Vernon. In 1799, he died on December 14. Character can mean a person's moral strength.

10It can also mean what a person or a place is like as a whole. A colony can be a settled area ruled by another country. It can belong to that country. It can also be a group settled in such a place. Written rules for governing the United States are called the Constitution. A convention is a political meeting. Delegates make plans there. They set goals. They choose people to run for office. Joined forces means worked together toward one goal. An oath is a formal promise. A retreat means moving away from an enemy during a battle. It can mean leaving an uncomfortable situation. It can mean turning back because danger lies ahead. It can also mean turning back because a hard problem lies ahead. An official job held for a limited time is called a term. The period is limited. Peace or friendship can be set down in a formal agreement called a treaty. It is made between two nations or two groups.

34. Gold Fever in California

1A scene from around 1852 shows prospectors mining in California. A prospector searches for gold because getting rich is a possibility. James Marshall went out to look at a ditch. The date was the morning of January 24, 1848. Water moved through that ditch. The ditch served a new sawmill his crew was building. The place was California. Something shiny sat under the water at the ditch's end. Gold was what he saw. Four days after that, he showed the gold to the mill's owner. The owner was John Sutter. Land had been taken by Sutter from the Nisenan people. A deal had been made with the Mexican government. That government controlled the area then. Sutter had kidnapped many Native people. He had enslaved them so they would work the land. He tried to keep the gold find a secret. Sutter's Mill ran on water power. It was a sawmill. Logs there were cut into lumber. Lumber is wood prepared for sale. A map shows land lost to the United States in the Mexican-American War. Today's California is part of that land. Nevada is part of it.

2Utah is part of it. Most of Arizona is part of it. Half of New Mexico is part of it. Parts of Colorado and Wyoming are part of it. Many Native peoples had homes on these lands. Mexico had claimed them. After the war, Mexico turned them over to the United States. Sutter's Mill is marked near San Francisco. The Pacific Ocean is beside them. The Rio Grande is a river on that map. Six days after the gold find, Mexico signed a treaty. The Mexican-American War ended with that treaty. California passed to the United States. A store near Sutter's Mill was run by Sam Brannan. He soon shared the secret. Gold dust filled a bottle he carried. He ran through the streets and shouted about the find. Only 850 people lived in the city. The city nearly emptied once he sold them tools. Picks were among the tools. Axes were among them. Pans were among them. Shovels were among them. The prices he charged were very high. California's gold was not sealed deep underground. Erosion had carried the metal from the mountains into rivers long before.

3Erosion is rock or soil taken away by water, wind, or ice. A method called placer mining was what miners used. Gold could be sifted from the riverbed with a shallow flat pan. Prospectors stood in the rivers. Gravel went into the pan by the shovelful. Gravel is a loose mix of broken rock. Large rocks were taken out. The pan was then swirled with care. Dirt and gravel washed over the rim with the water. Gold flakes, shiny ones, stayed in the bottom. Most days, the gold found was worth only between $10 and $15 in money of today. Stories of gold out west soon arrived in the eastern states. Those rumors were tales passed along that had not been proved. A report went from a California official to Washington, DC. A sample of the gold went with it. Congress heard about the discovery from the president. That news came in December 1848. The report was true. Gold fever grabbed many people. Greed and excitement over a gold find are what that fever means. Greed is a selfish, fierce want for more than someone needs.

4Pay for many workers then was less than a dollar a day. The chance looked like one that comes once in a life. About ninety thousand people joined the rush in 1849. Forty-niners became their name. Some came from across the United States. Some came from Mexico. Some came from Central America. Some came from South America. Some came from Asia. Some came from Australia. Some came from New Zealand. Women made up about 10 percent of them. Black people were part of the rush as well. Some of them were enslaved. Enslavers forced those people to go to California. Free Black people traveled west to seek gold too. A picture from 1852 shows a gold miner lifting gravel. In 1852 the Black population of California was about 2,000. They were about 1 percent of the population. Months could pass on the trip to the mines. The trip cost a great deal. Travel had to be paid for. Supplies had to be paid for. Jobs were left behind by many forty-niners. Families were left behind. A quick fortune and a return home was the plan for many.

5Adventure was the draw for others. People already in California and Oregon showed up first. They reached rivers near Sutter's Mill. Only a short trip with pack animals was needed for locals. Boat trips from Asia, Australia, and South America took longer for immigrants. Crossing the United States took longer too. A map shows the routes. An overland route needed five to eight months. A Panama route needed one month if a Pacific ship could be caught. A Cape Horn route needed six to eight months. Saint Louis, Missouri, is marked. New York City is marked. Sacramento is marked. San Francisco is marked. Panama is marked. Ship travel was the choice of about half the forty-niners. They hoped not to cross lands held by Native peoples. Five to eight months was the sail from the East Coast. The ships went around South America's tip and north again to California. The lowest ticket price was $75. In today's money that is about $2,300. Shipwrecks hit many passengers. Seasickness hit them. Disease hit them. Crowding hit them. Boredom hit them. A shortcut through Panama was the time-saver some chose.

6A canoe and a hike could cover 60 miles of jungle. That stretch is 97 kilometers. Another ship had to be caught after that. Many dangers waited. Bad weather was one. Wild animals were another. Illness and death took many. A ship on the far side might not come for weeks. It might not come for months. Posters told of ships leaving New York City for San Francisco. A drawing made around 1900 shows travelers in a wagon group. That group was called a train. Wagon travel took about as long as the sea trip around the tip. Native peoples still held much of the land between the East Coast and California. Hunting there without permission was common among settlers. A settler is a person who makes a lasting new home on a frontier. Stealing from Native peoples was common too. Attacks on them were common too. Most departures were from Saint Louis, Missouri. It was the farthest-west city of the United States then. The California Trail carried wagons for 2,100 miles. That length is 3,380 kilometers of flat prairie. About 15 miles was a day's wagon travel.

7That is 24 kilometers. The day worked only if the wagon held together, rain stayed light, and the oxen stayed well. Rivers had to be crossed with the wagons. Mountains had to be climbed. Cliffs had to be descended. Deserts had to be crossed. Rain beat the travelers. Wind beat them. Snow beat them. Disease brought still worse danger. Starvation brought danger too. Starvation means extreme hunger, or death from having no food. Accidents brought danger too. Arrival in California did not make life easier. Tents were the prospectors' homes. Twelve hours of work filled a day. Illness took many. They kept working. A fortune might sit in the gravel of the very next pan. A camp picture from around 1850 shows gold miners at El Dorado, California. The camp's name recalls a mythical place of enormous wealth. White miners started treating other gold hunters badly as the hills filled up. Miners from other countries were sometimes forced out by white prospectors. A tax fell on miners from other countries, including China. A tax is a fee collected for government services.

8Many Native groups lived in California. About eighty languages were spoken among them. Attacks pushed Native peoples off their land as forty-niners poured in. Outright killing took thousands. Disease took thousands more. A picture from around 1850 shows Chinese miners looking for gold. California became a state in 1850. Native land was then taken by the government. Fear and hatred soon led to the enslavement of thousands of Native peoples. New laws let settlers arrest Native people for minor wrongs. Fines then had to be paid by work on ranches. The government forced that work. Before the rush, the Native population of California was about three hundred thousand. Twenty years later, more than one hundred thousand of them were dead. A gold find did not bring an easy life. Finders often lost what they found. Robbers were a common sight. Gambling was common among prospectors. Goods cost far more than people were used to. A few early prospectors did succeed. Large companies took most of the riches. Deeper gold was something those companies could reach. Several men linked to the first find ended with nothing to show.

9John Sutter lost his property. His land grant from Mexico was not recognized by the United States. Fortunes slipped away from Sutter. They slipped away from Sam Brannan. They slipped away from James Marshall. Prices in San Francisco in 1849 look wild beside 2021 dollars. A can of peas was $6 then. The 2021 match is about $206. An egg was $1 then. The 2021 match is about $34. A breakfast was $43 then. The 2021 match is about $1,479. A pound of coffee was $3 then. A pound is about .45 kilograms. The 2021 match is about $103. A mule was $360 then. The 2021 match is about $12,384. A pair of boots was $96 then. The 2021 match is about $3,302. Selling goods to miners made some people rich. Sam Brannan became a millionaire. He ranks among the earliest millionaires of the United States. Levi Strauss grew rich by selling tough blue jeans. Cooking businesses were started by women. Boarding houses were run by women. Clothes were washed by women for pay. Mary Ellen Pleasant, a Black American, built her life in business.

10A millionaire's wealth came to her during the Gold Rush. Much of her work stayed secret. Some say her jobs were cook and housekeeper. They say she listened while wealthy people talked. Wealthy means holding a lot of money or possessions. Buying and selling buildings brought her a great deal of money. Buying and selling businesses did too. San Francisco appears in a view from 1851. A later view shows the city again. After the Gold Rush, the winners were white settlers. The United States government was among the winners. Wealthy people were among the winners. Native peoples lived through something very different. About three hundred thousand settlers arrived with the Gold Rush. Most of those settlers were white. They came onto lands that had been Native lands. No state today has more people than California. Its economy is huge. An economy is the way money circulates through work, buying, and finance in a place. That economy is bigger than the economy of most countries. Nearly two centuries have passed since the Gold Rush. California still matches its nickname. The nickname is the Golden State.

35. Great Places Nature Built

1North America holds the Grand Canyon. Mount Everest rises where Asia and Europe meet. Africa holds Victoria Falls. South America is home to Paricutín. Australia holds the Great Barrier Reef. Arizona's Barringer Meteorite Crater shows a mark left by space rocks. Rocks from space can strike Earth and shape amazing landforms. What turns a place into a natural wonder? A wonder is a natural structure that is amazing and beautiful. It makes you wonder how it formed. Nature, not people, builds these landforms. Our planet holds many of them. This book looks at five. One is a canyon of layers cut by a river. One is a young volcano that covered a village. One is a waterfall that takes your breath away. One is a huge coral reef. One is a mountain that towers overhead. The Colorado River began winding across a large plateau some five to six million years in the past. A plateau is a wide area of raised ground. Small bits of the plateau were lifted by the river. The river carried those bits toward the sea. People call this wearing-away by water erosion. Rock type changes how water erosion works. Soft rock breaks with ease. Hard rock fights against breaking. Downstream, the Colorado River hauled away bits of the softer rock. Harder rock stayed in place. Across millions of years, deep canyons were cut into the rock. The Grand Canyon is in Arizona, in the United States. Slow work by the Colorado River shaped the rock into the wonder visitors see now. Rock layers form the canyon walls.

2The layers record what happened there across two billion years. Those same rocks are the wall layers. The top layer is the youngest. Most visitors get their first look from that top. The bottom layer sits near the river. That bottom layer is the oldest. Loose rocks at the canyon bottom go back as far as two billion years. The canyon is a strong place to study rocks. It is a strong place to study Earth's history. The layers are easy to see. They are easy to reach. Geologists come often. Among the earliest geologists on the site was John Newberry. His visit was in 1858. He was the first scientist to write down the layers. Other people had seen the layers before he did. The Grand Canyon is home to over forty-seven reptile kinds. Most of them will not harm a person. Visitors should still watch for snakes. Rattlesnakes seldom bite people. Their bite is venomous. In winter they hibernate inside dens. One den may hold hundreds of rattlesnakes. Rattlers share a home at almost no other time. Spring warmth brings the snakes out of the dens. Each snake then goes its own way. Females bear live young in late summer. About seven to ten days pass before the babies leave the birth spot. Then the first baby skin is shed. The first rattle is added. The young snakes leave to look for food. Each year, a growing rattlesnake loses its skin one time or more. Every shed adds a new rattle on the tail. A full-grown diamondback may reach 2 meters in length.

3That length is about 6.5 feet. Weight can reach 6.8 kilograms. That weight is 15 pounds. A rattler may live 25 years. Native Americans made homes in and near the Grand Canyon. They were there over four thousand years before Europe's explorers arrived. By 1150, most of the early tribes had left. A single group stayed. Some of that group still live in the canyon. Some still work there. Each year, millions of people come to Grand Canyon National Park. They hike. They raft. They look at this grand work of nature. A waterfall in the west end shows water still cutting the canyon. Plenty of nature's works are millions of years old. Their forming began before any humans lived on Earth. Other works have formed in our own time. Paricutín, a volcano in Mexico, is one of those younger works. It began to form in 1943 in a farm field. A farmer found a fissure in his cornfield. A fissure is a crack that opens in the ground. The crack was about 1.8 meters long. That is 6 feet. More fissures opened soon after. Black smoke rose from the holes. Ash piled on the ground and built a cone. About twelve hours later the cone stood 10 meters high. That is 33 feet. After one day it stood 50 meters high. That is 164 feet. Growth continued. In only a week the cone reached 100 meters. That is 328 feet. The eruption of Paricutín came in 1943. Village people hoped their homes would stay safe. A few months later the cone was huge.

4Lava began to reach the town. The villagers had to leave. Homes were left behind. Farms were left behind. Across nine years, lava and ash buried the whole village of Paricutín. San Juan, the nearby town, was mostly buried as well. At the end, only the church steeple of Paricutín still showed. It rose from the black rock. It reminded people of nature's power. Mexico holds Paricutín, with the United States to the north. The Pacific Ocean and the Gulf of Mexico lie on either side of the country. Paricutín is a kind of volcano called a cinder cone. Cinder cones are the simplest volcanoes. Inside Earth, lava is under pressure. A cinder cone starts when lava shoots from a central vent. The lava bursts into the air and cools. It breaks into small pieces. The pieces harden. They fall as rocks and ash, which are cinders. The cinders stack around the vent and build a cone. Most cinder cones have a crater at the top. The crater is shaped like a bowl. Scientists have watched Paricutín grow since the day it appeared, because it is young. Today it stands about 424 meters tall. That is 1,391 feet. It covers 26 square kilometers. That is 10 square miles. Water shaped Victoria Falls, just as water shaped the Grand Canyon. The falls lie by Zambia and Zimbabwe in Africa, near the Indian Ocean. No other falling water on the planet has a greater mass. The drop is 108 meters. That is 354 feet. The water lands in a deep pool.

5Mosi-oa-Tunya is the name people in Zambia use for the falls. The name means the smoke that thunders. The name fits well. Thunder is the boom of water hitting the pool below. Smoke is the water vapor, a mist, rising from the base. The falls stand on the border of Zambia and Zimbabwe. Forming began millions of years ago. A shallow crack opened in the rock across the Zambezi River. Parts of the earth shifted and made that crack. The shift was about 150 million years ago. Year after year the river ran over the rock. In time the water wore the soft rock down. A cliff was left. The river now drops over that cliff. Hard rock stays beside the soft rock. The pool under the drop is a plunge pool. David Livingstone, a Scottish explorer, came to the falls in 1855. Livingstone was likely the first person who was not from Africa to view the falls. He gave the falls the name of Queen Victoria of England. Many people feel that Mosi-oa-Tunya is the truer name. The falls now belong to two national parks. One park is in Zambia. One park is in Zimbabwe. The falls are really six waterfalls side by side. Their names are Devil's Cataract, Armchair Falls, and Horseshoe Falls. Main Falls is one of the last three. Rainbow Falls is another. The Eastern Cataract is the last. A bridge there is a favorite place for bungee jumping. A full moon keeps the park open into the night. Moonlight through the vapor makes a rainbow people come to see.

6Below the falls, people raft and kayak. Some even surf the rapids. Mount Everest stands near the meeting of China and Nepal. The Pacific Ocean lies far to the east. No mountain on Earth is taller. It is one of the most astonishing sights anywhere. Nepali people call it Forehead in the Sky. It belongs to the Himalaya range. The height is 8,850 meters. That is 29,035 feet. Snow on the Himalayas can make them look old. For mountains, they are actually quite young. They rose millions of years ago. Two pieces of Earth's crust collided. The rubbing of those pieces pressed the ground. The ground crumbled and lifted above the sea. Young peaks on Mount Everest are still sharp. They are still steep. Wind, ice, and snow have not had endless years to smooth them yet. The mountain is beautiful to see. Standing there is bitterly cold. About -36 degrees C is the usual temperature. That is -33 degrees F. It can fall to -60 degrees C. That is -76 degrees F. July is the warmest month. Even then the temperature only reaches -19 degrees C. That is -2 degrees F. The air never rises above freezing. Ice and snow at the summit therefore never melt. Every year, storms drop still more snow on the mountain. Fierce cold makes life hard for plants on Mount Everest. It makes life hard for animals too. Few animals besides the wild yak can live on that mountain. Its weight can pass 454 kilograms. That is 1,000 pounds.

7It can stand up to 1.8 meters tall. That is 6 feet. Part of its survival comes from its blood. Compared with other animals, a yak's blood absorbs extra oxygen. On Mount Everest that adaptation saves lives. The air holds little oxygen. Wild yaks graze on the high ground. The first people to settle in the Himalayas believed gods lived in the peaks. That belief held until explorers of the early 1900s began to attempt climbs. Those first climbers learned quickly how harsh the mountain is. Temperatures freeze. Winds are powerful. Weather changes. The terrain is rough. The yeti, also called the Abominable Snowman, is a legend of this mountain and of the Himalayas. Sherpas tell of large footprints seen in the heights. They tell of yak bones that were chewed. A scalp and some skins, said to be from a yeti, were taken by Hillary's team to Europe. They carried them to the United States as well. Scientists there decided the scalp came from a goat. They decided the skins came from a bear. So far, no one has proved the yeti is real. Mountaineers still kept coming, even with every obstacle. Each hoped to be first at the top. Success did not come until 1953. That year Sir Edmund Hillary and Tenzing Norgay reached the top. They were the first men to do it. Hillary came from New Zealand. Norgay was a Sherpa from Nepal. The two mountaineers later accepted awards for the climb. Since that year, climbers from many countries have risked their lives for the summit. A diver can move among coral shapes on the Great Barrier Reef.

8The reef is really many reefs gathered as one. Its length is more than 2,300 kilometers. That is 1,429 miles. Australia's northeast coast is where it lies offshore. Amazing sea animals fill it. Sea slugs live there. Urchins live there. Dolphins, whales, and sharks live there too. Picture a reef as rock in a chain or a range. It sits at the water's surface or just under it. This reef is made of coral. Coral reefs grow in every tropical ocean. No other coral reef is this large. Most coral reefs that are well established are five thousand to ten thousand years old. At a glance the Great Barrier Reef can look like stone. It is not stone. Millions of animals called polyps built it from their skeletons. When polyps die, new polyps grow on the old skeletons. Layer on layer, the skeletons become the limestone base. Algae help build the reef. Sponges help. Shellfish help. The tiny polyp still does most of the building. Coral reefs such as this one grow from the life and the death of polyps. Animals make the reef. The reef then gives food to other animals. It gives them shelter too. Thousands of species live there. The fish alone number 1,625 kinds. Marine mammals number more than 30 kinds. Seaweed numbers 500 kinds. Marine turtles number 6 kinds. The reef is also a key place for breeding. Birds breed there. Sea turtles breed there. The endangered humpback whale breeds there. Few places on Earth are more interesting.

9Few are more beautiful. Scientists like to visit. Scuba divers like to visit. Tourists like to visit. Environmentalists now work with those groups. The goal is to guard the reef. Future generations can then learn from it. They can enjoy its beauty. The reef is alive. Delicate creatures fill it. Snorkelers and scuba divers should follow careful rules. Float above the reef while you snorkel. Do not stand on it. Take photographs. Leave the coral where it is. Fill a saltwater aquarium with fish raised in captivity. Do not fill it with fish taken off a coral reef. Carry your plastics back out. Carry the rest of your trash out too. Learn about coral reefs. Learn how you can help keep them safe. From Arizona to Australia's coast, natural wonders appear in many places. Some were shaped millions of years ago. Others took shape inside one lifetime. Every one of them is special. These landforms are among the biggest on Earth. They are among the highest. They are among the most breathtaking. Travelers will keep coming for generations. Scientists will keep coming. People who seek adventure will keep coming. This book has covered only five: the Grand Canyon, Paricutín, Victoria Falls, Mount Everest, and the Great Barrier Reef. Many other wonders surround us. The Northern Lights are one well-known wonder. They are a show of light in the skies of the Northern Hemisphere. The Barringer Meteorite Crater is another. It is a huge hole in the Arizona desert. The hole is almost a mile across. It is 174 meters deep.

10That is 570 feet. Ayers Rock is a single solid rock, and no larger solid rock is known. It rises 862.5 meters above sea level in Australia. That is 2,830 feet. You might live near a wonder of your own. It could be a towering mountain. It could be a grand river. It could be a cave full of mystery. Places like these can be visited, studied, and enjoyed each day. Breeding has to do with producing offspring. Cinder cones are hills shaped like cones. Airborne lava and ash settle around a volcanic vent and build them. Downstream means going the same way a river flows. Erosion is nature's removal of rock or soil. Water can do it. Wind can do it. Ice can do it. A fissure is a long, narrow crack. Geologists study how Earth began. They study its physical nature, its structure, and its history. Landforms are natural shapes on Earth's surface. Valleys, plateaus, mountains, plains, and hills are landforms. Mountaineers are people who climb mountains. A plateau is a large, high area of flat land. A plunge pool is a deep pool under a waterfall. Polyps are small sea animals without backbones, and coral is one kind. A polyp has a body like a tube. Its mouth has tentacles. A Sherpa belongs to a cultural group in the Himalayas. Sherpas often guide people who climb mountains. Living things that look alike and can have young together form a species. The ground's natural features are its terrain. Water vapor is water in the form of a gas.

36. Hidden Worlds Below the Ground

1A map of this region shows China. It shows Vietnam. It shows Thailand. It shows the South China Sea. It shows Cambodia. Deep in a Vietnamese cave, an explorer stands beside a huge flowstone column. In 2009, a British team of cave explorers set out for Vietnam. The shape of Vietnam is long and narrow. Part of its border meets the South China Sea. Beautiful bays line the country. Large rock formations rise there. Forests cover much of the land. Rivers run through it. These explorers are called spelunkers (spee-LUNK-ers). They had already seen several large caves nearby. This time, the cave was new to science. Ho Khanh, a farmer from that area, was the only person who knew the spot. He led the team to an opening under jungle cover. They went in. They walked for miles. Total darkness was all that waited ahead. In Vietnam's Hang Son Doong, a narrow beam of sun lets an explorer see. Light appeared all at once. A beam of sun cut through the dark in front of them. They moved closer. Long ago, part of the roof had fallen in. The hole left behind let that patch of sun through. The light showed how huge the room was. They stood in the largest cave room known. One part of the room is over 90 meters wide. That width is 300 feet. The same part rises over 180 meters. That height is 600 feet. It stretches over 4 kilometers. That length is 2.5 miles. A whole city block of buildings 40 stories tall could fit inside. Dense jungle hides the entrance of this vast cave. The cave's name is Hang Son Doong. That name means Mountain River Cave.

2The cave is enormous. Sun and rain enter where the roof fell. Dense jungles grow in those hidden spots. A cliff there rises 60 meters. That height is 200 feet. The cavers named the cliff the Great Wall of Vietnam. At first, the cliff stopped them. They came back with gear for climbing it. Then they went on into the inner cave. This is the biggest cave found so far. A bigger one may still exist somewhere. Each year, bold explorers locate caves no one has mapped. This book tells how caves form. It tells about strange shapes inside them. It tells about the living things there. Other caves just as full of wonder as Hang Son Doong appear in these pages too. Any hollow that nature makes in the earth counts as a cave. People usually mean a space a person can enter. They also mean a hollow deep enough that part of it stays dark. Caves can form in rock. They can form in ice. They can form underwater. Caves exist all over the world. Explorers want to see what waits inside. Some people picture every cave as dark and empty. Caves can be beautiful instead. Amazing shapes fill many of them. Unusual animals live in them too. The thrill of a place no one has seen pulls adventurous people in. The challenge of getting there pulls them in as well. People were making homes in caves 30,000 years in the past. Lewis and Clark Caverns in Montana hold some of the largest limestone shapes in the United States. Caves can form in more than one way. Rock, water, and heat are the simple tools nature uses. Those tools carve chambers in the earth.

3Rock under our feet is not all one kind. Limestone is one rock that water is able to dissolve. Surface water seeps down through soil. It also trickles through cracks. If that water meets limestone underground, the rock slowly dissolves. A hollow opens up. For millions of years, that water keeps taking the rock away. The result is a karst cave. Melted lava leaves a lava tube and pours into the ocean at Hawaii's Kilauea. Karst caves start when seeping water dissolves rock that is already there. A hole appears only after a long time. A primary cave is different. The rock held a hollow from the start. The rock formed with a hole already big enough to be a cave. Another rock cave begins with liquid magma or lava in a volcano. Cooling changes that liquid into solid rock. In some volcanoes, liquid lava runs over the ground like a stream. The top of that stream cools fast and hardens. Under the hard top, the lava stays hot and liquid. It keeps flowing. The moving lava leaves long tunnels in the solid rock. People give these long rock tunnels the name lava tubes. Sailors move through a huge cave of the sea on Corfu, an island of Greece. Grains of sand ride in rivers and also in waves from the sea. Very slowly, those hard grains grind rock away. That grinding is called erosion. Caves made this way are erosional caves. They differ from karst caves. In an erosional cave, water grinds the rock by physical force. In a karst cave, water dissolves the rock by chemical action. A sea cave is a common erosional cave. Ocean waves crash against cliffs again and again.

4In time, the waves dig out a hollow. A massive ice cave lies under the Mendenhall Glacier in Alaska. Caves do not form only in rock. A glacier is a huge moving mass of solid ice. At a glacier's top, the Sun can melt some ice. Melted ice turns into warm water that runs in a stream. That water flows down through cracks. It heads toward the soil under the glacier. Ice beside the flow melts because that water is warmer. A glacier cave is the hollow that forms. Caves also exist underwater. The process that carved land caves also carved most caves under water. The rock simply lay under a lake or an ocean. Some underwater caves were dry at first. They flooded when the sea rose in that area. Hikers still have daylight at the mouth of Milodon Cave. That cave is near Puerto Natales in Chile. A cave has three main areas. One is the entrance. One is the twilight zone. One is the dark zone. An entrance can be big or tiny. It may open like a door into a mountain. Sometimes the way in is a hole that falls straight down. It may be only a crack in a boulder. Any section that still receives a little sun belongs to the twilight zone. Seeing clearly with no flashlight means the twilight zone is where you stand. The twilight zone is often cool. It is often damp. Animals live there. Some plants live there too. The dark zone has no light at all. No plants grow there. Some fungi have adapted to the full dark. Some molds have adapted to it.

5Some animals have adapted to it too. A closer look fits karst caves, because this kind appears more often than the others. Water and dissolved limestone build fantastic shapes in them. Many people know stalactites and stalagmites. Drips that fall from above down to the floor are what build them. Dissolved limestone rides inside each drop. Drying leaves small bits of limestone in place. The same thing happens when the water evaporates. Bit by bit, the limestone piles up. Stalactites grow on the ceiling. Stalagmites grow on the floor. These shapes grow only about 2 millimeters a year. That is about 0.08 inches a year. They can still reach over 10 meters. That length is 33 feet. A meeting can occur between one stalactite and one stalagmite. The joined pair is a column. A hint can keep the two names straight. The word stalactite has the letter c. Let that c stand for ceiling. Down from a cave's top is where stalactites hang. The word stalagmite has the letter g. Let the letter g remind you of the ground. The cave floor is where stalagmites aim upward. Drops that run down the sides leave trails of limestone. Those trails can build into a drapery. A drapery is a curtain of rock. Length for a drapery can pass 3 meters. That length is 10 feet. Some are so thin that you can see through them. A soda straw is thin and hollow. It looks like a straw for a fizzy drink. A cave pearl is another rare shape. A pearl may begin as a grain of sand. Water with dissolved limestone wets the grain. When the water evaporates, limestone stays behind. Over time, thin layers build up, just as they do in a pearl.

6Each formation takes a very long time. Drop by drop, the work can last many thousands of years. Creatures small and large make homes in caves. Many scientists sort cave animals into three groups. One group spends its whole life in caves. One group spends only part of its life in caves and can also live outside. One group only visits. Some animals live their whole lives in caves and nowhere else. This group includes certain millipedes. It includes certain centipedes. It includes certain spiders. It includes certain insects. It includes certain crayfish. It includes certain salamanders. It includes certain fish. Life with no light at all is what these animals have adjusted to. Sight is gone in many of them. Eyes are missing completely on some of these animals. A second set of animals uses caves and also the world outdoors. Bats are a clear example. A bat may pass every night in a cave. It must leave to find food. Some beetles live this double life. Some millipedes do too. Some centipedes do too. Some salamanders do too. At Lascaux in France, people long ago passed through a doorway into the Great Hall of Bulls. The outdoors is where a great many of these animals pass most hours. They visit caves for shelter. They visit to rest. They visit to hunt food. This visiting group includes bears. It includes foxes. It includes pack rats. It includes snakes. It includes raccoons. It includes moths. It includes groundhogs. It includes vultures. It includes crickets. It includes humans. The closed space of a cave has seemed like a good home to many people. Hundreds of residents make their homes in caves in Spain's Andalucia.

7Millions of people in the north of China dwell inside caves. People also live in caves in Turkey. They live in caves in North Africa. They live in caves in France. Art on cave walls shows long use. Remains of campfires show it too. People have sheltered in caves across a span of tens of thousands of years. Prehistoric artists who lived in caves painted beautiful horses. They also painted bulls. Walls inside France's famous Lascaux Cave carry these paintings. In 1940, four boys found that art cave by accident. They squeezed through a small hole. They entered a large cavern. Deer and bulls appeared in beautiful paint across those walls. The boys were not hurt. Even so, going into a cave without an experienced explorer is dangerous. Going in without a guide is dangerous too. In time, the main person guiding visitors there was one of those boys. Caves are very fragile habitats. The world outside almost never touches many of these caves. Something new that enters a cave can harm the animals living there. Bats hibernate in caves. A bat that is hibernating has a body that works more slowly. That slowdown lets the bat go months without eating. Frightening numbers of bats in hibernation have fallen ill in recent years. Many of them have died. Caves are too deep for the seasons to change them. Cave temperatures tend to stay between 24 degrees C and 1 degree C. Those readings are 75 degrees F and 30 degrees F. Life in a cave means adjusting to a setting that barely changes. Through a normal winter, bats in hibernation press close in a group.

8Bats with a white fuzz were the first sign of trouble. Cave visitors spotted the problem in 2006. The place was outside Albany, New York. A strange white fuzz ringed the bats' mouths and noses. The bats woke instead of staying in hibernation. Flight carried them outside. Waking in winter is dangerous. With no food, the bats starve. They may freeze instead. Scientists call this White Nose Syndrome. By the years after 2006, the illness had crossed the entire eastern United States. One winter can take more than 90 percent of the bats hibernating in a single cave. Scientists have found a fungus that causes it. They do not yet know a cure. Even bats seen often today could disappear, and some scientists fear that end. Their plea is that explorers avoid every cave where bats are living. Stopping that fungus from traveling farther is the aim. A rock scientist examines crystals larger than any others yet known. Famous caves around the world are known for outstanding features. Mexico's Cave of Crystals holds rooms of giant sparkling crystals. No larger crystals have ever been found. Some are 10 meters long. That length is 33 feet. Mammoth Cave in Kentucky has more than 480 kilometers of passageways. That distance is 300 miles. No cave system yet known on Earth is larger than this one. Krubera Cave reaches farther down than any other cave yet found. It lies in Georgia, a nation in Eastern Europe. The depth there passes 2,000 meters. That depth is 7,000 feet. Spelunkers are not finished exploring it. Chauvet Cave in France is known around the world. The name is said shaw-VAY. It was discovered in 1994. More than 300 paintings from among the oldest on Earth are kept there.

9The paintings date to about 30,000 years ago. Horses appear in them. Lions appear in them. Bears appear in them. Lights, safety gear, and proper tools for climbing belong on every cave trip. Why do spelunkers enter caves? Some want the thrill of testing their bodies. They climb down ropes. They crawl through tiny tunnels. They scuba dive in underground caverns. Learning more about Earth is why scientists go in. Experienced explorers carry helmets. They carry flashlights. They wear warm clothing. They bring rope. They bring kneepads. Farther inside, a tiny tunnel may force a person to wriggle. Pools under the ground may have to be crossed in the water. They may wade through rivers. Inside, water goes drip-drip-dripping. No other sound joins it. Flashlight glow is the sole light around them. Lacey sprays of gypsum crystals grow in Lechuguilla Cave. That cave is in Carlsbad Caverns Park in New Mexico. Excitement comes with time inside a cave. It can also be truly dangerous. Twisting tunnels in pitch black make it easy to get lost. A narrow space can trap a person. A floor may slope sharply and be covered in rock. Floors can be slick with mud. Tough skill at climbing rock is needed in a great many caves. Great fragility is common among the shapes inside a cave. Thoughtless visitors have snapped off tips of stalactites to keep as souvenirs. A second of damage can destroy a shape that took 40,000 years to form. Choose a tour with a guide before you go into a cave. The many wonders can be explained by that guide. A guide can keep you safe.

10A guide can keep the formations safe too. An explorer looks at flowstone shapes inside the lower cave at Carlsbad Caverns Park in New Mexico. Caves open a world that holds deep interest. Where else can you find land no one has explored? Where else can you find formations that seem from another world? Paintings made thousands of years ago are rare in any other setting. If we treat caves with respect, the wonder can go on without end. Some words help tell this story. A chamber is a room or other closed space. It may be natural or made by people. A lava tube is a cave left by a lava flow. The melted core keeps flowing off while the outer lava cools into a hard shell. Collapsed means fallen apart or fallen down. A passageway is a narrow way from one place to another. It may be a tunnel, a hall, a path, an alley, or a route. On a building, the word column can name a pillar. A cave column appears once a stalagmite and a stalactite grow until they touch. A primary cave forms when new rock is made with a hollow already inside. A drapery is a curtain. In a cave, it is a thin sheet hanging from a wall or from the ceiling. Spelunkers are people who explore caves. A stalactite is a pointy rock shape hanging from a cave ceiling. Erosion names a slow loss of rock, or a slow loss of soil. Water, wind, or ice can do that wearing. Up from a cave floor, a stalagmite rises as a pointed shape of rock. A formation is something shaped, formed, or created.

37. Home of Japan's Emperor

1Japan's imperial family lives at the Imperial Palace. Imperial has to do with royalty or with the ruler of an empire. Palace grounds feel calm. Tokyo's modern skyscrapers feel nothing like that calm. Tokyo itself is a huge city packed with those tall buildings. The palace still sits in the heart of the bustle. Wood is the material of buildings on the grounds. Stone is the material of the walls. Gardens there are lush. Lush means full of plants and pleasing to the senses. Older Japan shows in those buildings, walls, and gardens. Land under the palace ranks among the most expensive on Earth. Japan's emperor and empress belong to a royal line. That line is the oldest in the world. Their home is this palace. Japan's main island is where Tokyo stands. The city is an important port. Tokyo Bay and the Pacific coast are its setting. A central place in the city holds the Imperial Palace. Cultural sites and government buildings stand nearby.

2Cultural relates to a people's beliefs, customs, language, or arts. One nearby place is the Japanese National Theatre. Another is Tokyo Disneyland. Emperors did not always live on this site. Military generals ruled Japan for hundreds of years. Shogun was the name for these generals. They were military rulers of old Japan. Power was something they fought each other to hold. Very little power sat with the emperor himself then. Tokugawa Ieyasu was a shogun in the early 1600s. His name sounds like ee-e-YAH-soo. Improvements to Edo Castle were his work. Today's palace stands on that ground. No castle in Japan was larger than Edo Castle. Fire destroyed the main tower in 1657. Only the stone foundation is left. A foundation is a strong base, often stone or concrete, made to carry a building's weight. One palace garden still shows that foundation. His rule opened the Edo period. More than 260 years was the length of that period.

3A revolution in 1868 closed it. A revolution is the forced removal of a government and its replacement by another. Power went back to the emperor. Shogun rule ended. Emperor Meiji decided the imperial capital should shift. The city he chose was Tokyo. His name sounds like MAY-jee. A new palace was ordered on the old castle site. The build met many troubles. Fire was one. Earthquakes were another. Money problems were another. The finished palace came in 1888. Twenty years had passed. Today's palace uses the ground of the original Edo Castle. Japan's imperial family lives there. Outside, the new palace followed a traditional Japanese look. Traditional means linked to a custom kept for a long time. Inside, the rooms did not match that outside look. Japan had once stayed apart from other countries. European cultures interested Emperor Meiji. He left custom behind and picked European decoration. Low tables filled older Japanese dining rooms. Kneeling on the ground was one way people ate.

4Special chairs with no legs were another way. Rice-paper screens covered windows. Woven straw mats covered floors. European tables were requested for the new dining room. European chairs were requested too. Cloth curtains were ordered for the windows. Carpets were ordered for wood floors. Traditional straw mats stayed as well. Original stone walls still ring the palace. Those walls once guarded the site. Edo-period workers built the walls in an unusual way. Earth was shaped first into a narrow form like stairs. A layer of small rocks went down next. Rainwater can drain through that rock layer. Stones were then stacked into walls. Mortar was left out. Mortar is a wet mix that hardens and binds stones. Empty cracks between stones also let rain drain. Heavy rain across hundreds of years has not toppled these walls. This drainage is a reason they still stand. Walls of stone with no mortar also stand up better to Japan's many earthquakes.

5Wood was the first material of buildings on the grounds, palace included. Builders in old Japan liked wood more than stone. Wood buildings came through earthquakes in better shape. Concrete replaced wood when the palace was built again after World War II. An older Japanese look remains anyway. A tea house on the grounds shows the older wooden way. Emperors lived in the palace Meiji built until World War II. Tokyo was bombed in that war by the United States military. Fire spread in from nearby city areas. The Imperial Palace was damaged. Rebuilding came after the war from the Japanese government. 1968 was the year the work finished. Modern methods were used. The look from before the war was kept. Parts of the palace have been rebuilt several times in its history. Some buildings are therefore only about fifty years old. Other parts are older. Some stone-wall sections around the grounds come from the Edo period. A building for the Imperial Household Agency rose on the grounds in 1935.

6The royal family lived in it for a while after World War II. They waited there while the new palace was built. A grounds map names several places. It names the Tokyo Imperial Palace Science Museum.
