It names the Tokyo National Museum of Modern Art. It names Kitanomaru Park. It names the Museum of Imperial Collections. It names the East Gardens. It names the Imperial Palace Outer Garden. It names the palace and Nijubashi Bridge. About 115 hectares is the area of the grounds. That measure is 284 acres. Moats go around the grounds. Defensive stone walls stand there, some from the Edo period. Several museums have a home on the grounds. Four sections make up the palace grounds. One is the Imperial Palace Outer Garden. One is the inner grounds. One is Kitanomaru Park. One is the East Gardens. The Outer Garden is a park open to the public.

7Nijubashi Bridge is famous. It joins the Outer Garden to the inner grounds. Public entry on the bridge happens only twice each year. January 2 is the first time. The emperor trades New Year greetings with visitors that day. The emperor's birthday is the second time. The inner grounds usually stay closed to the public. State duties of the emperor happen in this section. Space inside the palace measures 24,175 square meters. That is about 260,000 square feet. Two stories rise above ground. A basement level sits below. Government ceremonies use the palace. Meetings with important people from other countries use it too. Dinners are hosted there. A home for the emperor and empress stands near the palace. A private Shinto shrine stands near it as well. Shinto is an old religion of Japan. People in it honor various gods of nature. A building for silkworms is also on the grounds. Empresses have raised silkworms there since 1871. A silkworm is the caterpillar of the silkworm moth.

8It makes silk fiber when it builds a cocoon. Ancient kimonos are mended with silk that the empress's silkworms make. A kimono is a Japanese robe, often worn for formal times. Kitanomaru Park became a public park in 1969. The north side of the grounds is where this park sits. Scenery there is lovely. Cherry trees in bloom make spring especially fine. The Science Museum stands in the park. The National Museum of Modern Art stands there too. Edo-period pieces and modern museum exhibits share the park. The mix shows how those pieces can blend. The East Gardens form another beautiful public park. Calm ponds are there. Grassy lawns are there. Trees and shrubs of many kinds color every season. The East Gardens hold the final garden Japan still has from the Edo period. Guard houses from that period can be seen. Walls from that period can be seen. Gates from that period can be seen.

9This area includes the Museum of Imperial Collections. Art owned by the imperial family is shown there. Cherry trees bloom in spring beside the Chidori-ga-fuchi moat. People come to enjoy those blossoms. Modern Japan still needs this palace. Many state duties of the emperor happen here. Tourists come. Local people come. Spring is the busiest draw. March and April bring over one million people to cherry trees along one moat. Care of the palace is serious because its role is serious. About a thousand employees look after the grounds. They also plan when the imperial family will meet and what events will happen. Billions of yen are spent each year to keep the palace going. The yen is Japan's unit of money. Those sums equal millions of United States dollars. In 2016, palace expenses were about 5.5 billion yen. The agency that runs the site spent that amount. The United States match was $48 million. Emperor Hirohito loved nature.

10His reign ran from 1926 to 1989. Killing weeds was not something he wanted from his gardeners. Killing bugs was not something he wanted either. Because of that choice, over 3,600 species of animals and insects now live on the grounds. Over 1,300 plant species live there too. These grounds hold 20 percent of Tokyo's trees. Four views of the gardens show how varied the plants are. Nijubashi Bridge is famous. The palace stands behind that bridge. A quiet place waits in the middle of a busy modern city. Rich forests grow here. Fine gardens grow here. Peaceful ponds wait for visitors. Historic Japanese culture is kept on the site. Traditional building design is one way it is kept. Landscaping is another way. Stone walls that are centuries old are another way. The emperor lives here. The empress lives here. That home is another reason the site matters. For all of these reasons, people in Japan love this landmark.

38. Homes Cut into Desert Cliffs

1The year 1906 created Mesa Verde National Park. No earlier park in the United States was created to honor human work. The park stands where four states come together. Utah is one of those states. Colorado is another. Arizona is another. New Mexico is the last. The park holds many cliff dwellings. They are among the most striking early buildings in North America. The builders used great skill. They had no modern tools. They had no machines. Who lived in these homes? How did they live? Why did they build in the cliffs? Why did they leave? Where did they go after that? The park is in the Four Corners area. Nowhere else in the United States do four state corners touch. A marker shows that point. The marker is a tourist attraction. The early people of the Four Corners left no written records. They are called prehistoric for that reason. We know them from remains of their culture. Those remains were buried in the ground. Mesa Verde is a high, flat desert. Mountains stand around it. The land is arid, which means dry. Dry conditions help preserve what people left behind. Skeletons have lasted. Mummies have lasted. Dwellings have lasted. Tools have lasted. Trash piles have lasted. These remains help archaeologists study the early people. Other remains are pictographs. A pictograph is a painting on stone. Other remains are petroglyphs. A petroglyph is a picture carved in stone.

2Say the park name as MAY-suh VAIR-day. Mesa is a Spanish word for table. Mesa also means raised land with a flat top. Verde is Spanish for green. To understand the cliff dwellers, start far in the past. Scientists know that people were here at least 14,000 years ago. That time was the Ice Age. A land bridge carried ancient people across. They left northern Russia. They entered Alaska, in North America. Hundreds of years later, some descendants traveled far south. They reached the Four Corners. The first people there are called Paleo-Americans. They survived by hunting. They also survived by gathering. They used large spears. They hunted huge herd animals. Those animals included mammoths, which were like elephants. They included mastodons. They included giant bison. People gathered nuts. They gathered berries. They gathered other wild plants. Paleo-Americans did not keep permanent homes. They moved as they followed the animals. They camped in caves. They also built simple shelters. About 10,000 years ago, the Ice Age came to an end. Temperatures became warmer. Many large animals died out. Large grasslands turned into deserts. Change was required of the Four Corners people. They had to find new ways to survive. The name Archaic people comes from archaeologists. Like their Paleo-American ancestors, they hunted. They also gathered. Smaller animals were the ones they hunted. Those animals gave less meat. Plants became a bigger part of Archaic meals.

3They made tools that differed from earlier tools. They made smaller spear points. The points were for deer. The points were also for bighorn sheep. They made nets for rabbits. They made traps for rabbits. They used nets and traps for birds too. They used special tools to chop roots. They chopped seeds, nuts, and wild plants. They used tools to grind those foods. The first grinding stones were made in Archaic times. The first bowls were made then too. People still use tools much like those. Corn was an important food for the Ancestral Puebloans. Important changes had reached Four Corners life by about 2,000 years ago. Growing food was a skill people had gained. They did not have to rely only on wild animals and plants. Farming reshaped daily life. The name for them was Ancestral Puebloans. Hunting and gathering still fed most of the earliest group. They grew corn on open, flat land. They grew squash there too. Grown food could feed a larger group. It also gave them food they could depend on. Extra crops created a storage problem for the Ancestral Puebloans. They made baskets from plant fibers. They also used tree bark. Some baskets held food. A very tight weave let some baskets hold water. A basket could not be set over a fire. Cooks put hot rocks into baskets of water.

4That is how they cooked. They began to make sandals from plant fibers. Yucca was one of those plants. Pictures show the yucca plant. Pictures also show sandals made of yucca fibers. More crops, tools, and baskets meant less travel. People still hunted. They hunted with spears. They hunted with nets. Caves were their homes in this period. The caves were along cliffs. The caves were along canyon walls. A pit house had a smoke hole. It had an entrance. It had a fire pit. The width of a pit house ran from 9 feet to 25 feet. That width is 2.74 to 7.62 m. Walls were made of branches. Brush or grass was used too. Clay or mud covered the plants. Over time, farming fed more of a growing population. Beans joined the crops by about 1,500 years ago. They still grew corn. They still grew squash. A reliable farm supply let them stay in one place. They built temporary houses near their fields. These houses are called pit houses. Each house sat on a shallow pit dug in the ground. Sides and ceilings used tree branches. They used bark. They used soil. Archaeologists think these people were peaceful. No arms made for battle have been found. Their houses stood in the open. Defense is difficult when a house stands in the open. Open house sites suggest enemies did not seem a threat.

5Another important change was the making of pottery. People still made baskets. Clay pots let them cook over an open fire. Clay pots stored food better. The first pottery changed their way of life. In this same time, they started using bows and arrows. A bow was easier than hunting with a spear. A bow was also more successful. These changes let the population keep growing. Food had become more plentiful. People twisted animal hair with cotton string. They made warm socks. They made coverings for the legs. Sometimes they wove feathers with string. The string came from yucca plants. That weave became clothing. One boot was made of turkey feather yarn. Yucca fibers and feathers formed one cord. Chaco Canyon is in northwestern New Mexico. It was an important cultural center. Twelve large pueblos made up the center. The largest was Pueblo Bonito. About 1,000 people lived there. A trade center is what archaeologists see in Chaco Canyon. Larger groups of Ancestral Puebloans were living together around 1,300 years ago. They built rectangular houses. The houses stood completely above the ground. A frame used tree limbs. The frame used bark. Adobe covered the wood. Adobe is soil that contains clay. Over time, they built with sandstone blocks. The blocks were stacked. Adobe held the blocks together. Houses stood side by side. They formed large buildings called pueblos. Pueblo is a Spanish word. It means town.

6It also means village. The people built kivas too. A kiva is circular. A kiva is underground. Kivas were used for holding ceremonies. Several hundred years of success followed for this culture. New farming methods helped the crops. They built dams. They built terraces. Dams and terraces managed water for the fields. Successful crops fed the growing population. The kinds of pottery changed. Everyday pottery was used for cooking. It was used for storing food. Everyday pottery was gray. The outer surface was rough. The rough surface helped heat the food inside. Other pottery was rubbed. It was polished to a smooth surface. The smooth pots were for decoration. They were also for trade. Makers decorated them with complex designs. The designs were colorful. The paint was made from wild plants. Around 900 years ago, the people moved to ledges. The ledges were on cliffs and canyon walls. Cliff dwellings at Mesa Verde are typical of this time. Other dwellings in the Four Corners are typical of it too. Five stories was the greatest height of these houses. They could house hundreds of people. The cliff dwellers used ladders to reach home. They used ropes too. They cut finger holds into the rocks. They cut toe holds into the rocks. About 600 cliff dwellings are in Mesa Verde National Park. Cliff Palace is the largest. It had 217 rooms. It had 23 kivas.

7Storage may have been the job of the tiniest rooms. Some archaeologists think other nations were attacking. The people would need homes they could defend with ease. Many cliff dwellings could be reached only with ladders. People could pull the ladders inside. Enemies then had no way to enter. This idea makes sense. Proof for that reason is scarce. Montezuma Castle is one of the cliff dwellings. Archaeologists were puzzled by the move away from open areas. They think the growing population needed more farmland. Moving to the cliffs freed flat land for crops. Old fields may have been overused. Food no longer grew well in those tired fields. The people may have needed new fields. Warmth in winter is another possible reason. The weather was getting colder. Most cliff dwellings face south. Others face southwest. Rock walls could take warmth from sunlight. Building the dwellings was a huge challenge. The builders did not have machines. They used only stone tools. They were skilled at designing for the space they had. Builders chipped large stones into the right shape for walls. Adobe held the stones. Builders covered the walls with adobe. Sometimes they painted designs on the inside walls. Upper floors began with large wooden poles. The poles lay across the tops of walls. Smaller poles were laid across those beams. Sticks or bark formed the next layer. Then a thick layer of adobe held the floor together.

8Some dwellings had two floors. Most hours were likely spent outdoors. They worked together in groups. Some people cooked. Some ground corn. Some carried water. Some made pottery. Some made baskets. Others wove cloth. Others farmed crops. Others hunted. Others made tools from stone. They also used wood and plant fibers. Children worked beside their parents. They learned by watching. They learned by practice. They took care of turkeys. They played too. Indoor jobs became part of winter. Archaeologists think rooms were used all year for sleeping. Life included important trade with other nations. A map shows some traded items. It also shows where items came from, along possible routes. The items include furs, turquoise, pottery, and shells. They include corn, salt, beads, and cotton. Mesa Verde is marked on the map. The Pacific Ocean is marked too. Descendants still gather in kivas today. That use tells us the old kivas were for religious ceremonies. Family meetings were another likely use. They may have been guest rooms. They may have been workspaces in winter. People entered a kiva with a ladder. A fire pit sat at the middle of every kiva. A shaft brought in fresh air. A small floor hole appeared in half of Mesa Verde's kivas. The hole is called a sipapu. The hole stood for a doorway. One side was the spirit world. The other side was the physical world.

9Archaeologists think these people believed something about every living thing. Each living thing has a spirit self. Each living thing also has a physical self. They believed in life after death. The cliff homes were emptied between 1280 and 1295. New Mexico, to the south, was one destination. They also moved to Arizona. The exact reason for leaving is unknown. Archaeologists think the changing climate played an important role. It got colder. Crops had a shorter growing season. There were long times with little rain. Some times had no rain. Watering crops became harder. Overused soil may have made a full harvest impossible. By the time they left Mesa Verde, most trees may have been cut. Trees supplied firewood. Trees also supplied wood for pueblos. Too much hunting may have wiped out the meat supply. Some people think enemy nations drove the cliff dwellers out. That idea is unlikely. After they left, Mesa Verde shows no remains from other cultures. Deeper ground holds the older remains. Newer remains lie closer to the surface. If enemies had driven the people out, enemy remains would lie on top. When the cliff dwellers left, some moved south along the Rio Grande. The Rio Grande is in New Mexico. Modern Pueblo nations descend from these families. Others moved to Arizona. In time they formed the Zuni nation. They formed the Hopi nation. A photo shows Estella Loretto.

10She is cleaning a bronze statue. Today, members of these nations keep many old traditions. Farming continues for many Pueblo people. It continues for many Zuni people. It continues for many Hopi people. They still perform ceremonies. Their bond with the land is still honored. They still honor the spirit world. They create fine pottery. They create jewelry. They create baskets. They create carvings. They create weavings. Pottery made Ray Tafoya well known. Simple rules help visitors protect ruins and artifacts. Look, but do not take. The law forbids keeping an artifact. Do not touch walls. Do not touch petroglyphs. Do not touch pictographs. Do not draw on them. Do not climb on walls. Many walls are easily damaged. A photo shows a damaged wall. Crowds came to the cliff homes late in the 1800s. Some photographed the sites. Some drew them. Some described them. Some visitors carried artifacts away and sold them. Over time, people grew concerned. They wanted to protect the dwellings from damage. They wanted to protect them from theft. Worry about the sites produced a park in 1906. The park is Mesa Verde National Park. Protection of the cliff homes is a job of the National Park Service. It also lets visitors enjoy them. Exhibits teach visitors how the Ancestral Puebloans lived. Some exhibits are at Mesa Verde. Other exhibits are at sites in the Southwest.

39. Homes in the Dry Lands

1In Morocco, Bedouin friends pause so a photo can be taken in the Sahara. Deserts that ask a lot of people fill this book. First come the Tohono O'odham of the Sonoran Desert. Next come the Bedouin of the Sahara. After them come Mongolian nomads of the Gobi. The close looks at desert life as a way of living. Outside her Sahara home, a Tuareg woman prepares a meal. A dictionary pictures a desert as dry land. Little life can grow on land like that. On some of it, no life can grow. One desert scene may look nothing like the next. Trees may rise in a desert. Cacti may rise there too. Rocky mountains may rise there as well. A usual desert is not dry on every day. Streams cross some deserts. Rivers cross some deserts. Those streams may stay dry for a long stretch. Those rivers may stay dry for a long stretch. Animals of many kinds also make a desert their home.

2One trait is shared by every desert. Beside the rest of the world, these places are arid. Rain or snow in a year is usually under 25.4 centimeters. That measure equals 10 inches. Dry soil is common. Plant life is scarce there. A desert can turn very cold. Sunny weather is typical. Heat is typical too. Near an oasis in the Egyptian Sahara, palm trees grow. In Australian sand dunes, flowers bloom. Running water shows up in the Sonoran Desert. Bolivia holds Earth's largest salt flat. Children run side by side in the Thar Desert, which is in India. Hot deserts sit on five continents out of seven. A desert can be small. A desert can be huge. We can jet from one desert to another. Some of the world's deserts will be our lesson. We will also meet people whose home has been a desert for thousands of years. Staying alive there means adapting to the land around you.

3Food must be found. Water must be found. Shelter must be found. People find these in every other place on Earth too. Getting by is not the whole story for desert people. A desert is much more than that. The desert is home. The desert is a way of life too. Next comes the Sonoran Desert, home of the Tohono O'odham. 310,800 square kilometers is the size of the Sonoran Desert. That equals 120,000 square miles. The southwestern United States is part of its reach. Northern Mexico is part of its reach too. Rain can fail to fall for months. A summer day can climb to 49 degrees Celsius. That reading is 120 degrees Fahrenheit. A winter night can fall below freezing. No other desert on Earth gets more rain than this one. Indigenous people called the Tohono O'odham live here. Their name means desert people. Say it toe-HOE-noh OH-eh-dom. California appears on a map of this region. New Mexico appears on that map. Arizona appears on it. The Pacific Ocean appears on it.

4Mexico appears on it. North America appears on it. Native plants in the Sonoran Desert number more than 2,500. No other desert grows a higher number. Across several centuries, land used by the O'odham was taken. Europeans were among the takers. People from the United States took it too. Thousands of years is how long the O'odham had lived there. Today many of them live on land shared out among four groups. The Tohono O'odham Nation is one group. Another group lives in the Gila River Indian Community. The people there are O'odham. The Ak-Chin Indian Community is another. The Salt River Indian Community is another. Pima Maricopa is another name for that last community. The O'odham language is spoken by all four groups. Tribe to tribe, the speech still differs. Towns are home to some O'odham people. Cities are home to some. Reservations are home to some. Baskets made long ago by her family are what Francine Jose shows.

5More modern ways of living began for the O'odham as more land was taken. Their traditions were still something they fought to keep. Daily life for most O'odham still holds pieces of those traditions. One piece is farming. Another piece is cooking traditional recipes. Another piece is basketmaking. O'odham culture gives an important place to the saguaro cactus. Say the name suh-WAR-oh. Beautiful flowers open on it each spring. Mid-June is when its large red fruit grows ripe. Pickers take that fruit from high on the giant cacti. Upwards of 22 meters is a height a saguaro can reach. That equals 75 feet. The picking is done with special tools. Wine is made from the fruit. Early July is when that wine is used, at the New Year ceremony. About 1907, a picture shows an O'odham woman gathering saguaro fruit. Traditional tools are still used by O'odham harvesters today. Camel races are what young Bedouin riders line up for. Across northern Africa, the Sahara covers 8.6 million square kilometers.

6That equals 3.3 million square miles. Heat in the Sahara is extreme. Dryness there is extreme too. The Sahara is where Bedouin people live, and they live past its edges too. Say the name BED-oo-in. Rain is so scarce that Bedouin life has long been nomadic. Morocco is on a map of this region. Tunisia is on it. Western Sahara is on it. Algeria is on it. Libya is on it. Egypt is on it. Mauritania is on it. Mali is on it. Chad is on it. Sudan is on it. Niger is on it. Europe is on it. The Atlantic Ocean is on it. Africa is on it. Less than 25 millimeters of rain can fall in a year in some Sahara places. That amount is 1 inch. Dried plants form the walls of homes in one Bedouin village in Egypt's Sahara. Color fills the rooms inside a Bedouin home in Oman. Animal herding is the Bedouin way.

7The rainy winter is when Bedouin herders roam the desert. In the past, they moved from oasis to oasis. Wells were dug so they could reach water. Farmland is where they brought the herds back in the dry summer. A Bedouin village in Jordan is called Umm Sayhoun. Low black tents that people could carry were the traditional homes. Those tents are called bayts. Goat hair was used for some. Sheep's wool was used for others. A bayt protects people from the sun. A bayt protects people from the wind. A bayt protects people from blowing sand. Land where Bedouin families lived has been taken over by countries during the past hundred years. Thousands of years is how long those families had been there. Nomadic life has ended for many Bedouin people. Instead, they live in houses and apartments in villages and cities. In their home in Oman, a Bedouin mother and her son share a smile.

8Asia is where a map places the Gobi Desert. The Indian Ocean is on that map. Mongolia is on it. China is on it. A rain shadow desert is the name people give the Gobi. Rainy weather is blocked by mountains. Plants could have grown if that rain had arrived. 1.3 million square kilometers is the size of the Gobi. That equals 500,000 square miles. Southern Mongolia is where it starts, and northern China is where it reaches. The Gobi is where Mongolian nomads live. Cold, windy weather was the setting when they roamed its sand dunes in the older way. Its rocky mountains were part of that roaming too. Horses were in the herds. Sheep were in the herds. Goats were in the herds. Cattle were in the herds. Camels were in the herds. Her animals are moved through the Gobi by a Mongolian woman. Outside his family's ger, a Mongolian boy runs. A round tent covered in felt was home in the older way.

9Yurt is another name for that kind of tent. Ger is what people called this home. Cold nights were kept outside. Other weather was kept outside too. Mongolian nomads are fewer today. Herds belonging to thousands of families were killed by harsh winters. Herding life was made difficult by changes in government. Almost half a million nomads left during the past thirty years. Mongolia's capital is where they went. Work is what they looked for. Food is what they looked for. Their gers came with many of them. Different challenges come with each desert. Their own solutions have always been found by the Indigenous peoples who live there. Only what they could find nearby was used by desert people in the past. A great deal is different today. Modern lives are led by many people in the desert. The old way of their ancestors is still followed by some. Traditions of their desert communities are still celebrated by many.

10Desert life asks a lot. It gives a lot back too. Deep respect for the land is held by people who make the desert home. What the desert offers is used by them. Its power is honored. Its beauty is honored. In the United Arab Emirates, a busy street is where a man leads a camel. Very dry is what arid means. The earliest peoples of a land are who Indigenous describes. Of the present time is what modern means. People who shift from place to place are who nomadic describes. One fixed home is not theirs. A watered spot where desert plants can grow is an oasis. Reservations are certain lands. The U.S. government once made Native peoples live on them. To move about from place to place is what roam means. A practice kept for a very long time is what traditional describes. People who share one culture form a tribe. Land is shared by them as well.

40. How Advertisements Reach Us

1It is often hard to know who appears in an advertisement. The person might be a real doctor or some other expert. The person might be only an actor. When the television comes on, which commercials appear? A commercial is an ad on television or on radio. On a webpage, which products pop into view? A product is something made, grown, or produced, usually so it can be sold. Before the film at a movie theater, what appears on the huge screen? Ads are all around us. An advertisement is a public message about a product, a service, or a cause. The message tries to draw interest or to help sales. We do not always notice the ads. Advertisers do notice us. Each year, businesses spend billions of dollars to advertise. They advertise clothes. They advertise video games. They advertise food. They advertise other products too. You may not realize it, but children are special targets. Teens are special targets too. What young people want is studied by companies. The goal is a wish, in those young people, for the item being sold. Loyal customers, grown from children, are another goal.

2Loyal means giving strong support, or staying attached. All of us are consumers. A consumer is a person who buys products and uses them. Smart consumers need critical thinking. Critical thinking means you analyze information. You look for its purpose. You judge whether it deserves trust. If we learn how ads try to shape our thinking, our choices improve. We can decide more carefully what to buy. Ads aimed at children often use bright colors. They often show toys. They often show cartoon characters. Many stores still use ads in print. Advertising drew a huge sum from companies around the world in 2016. The sum was more than 493 billion dollars. A company needs customers to buy its products or its services. Otherwise the company cannot stay in business. A company pays for ads for two main reasons. First, customers should know the company exists. They should know its products too. Second, the company wants those customers to buy. Media companies also earn money from advertising. Media means mass communication that brings information to the public. Newspapers are one kind of media company.

3Magazines are another. Billboards belong in this business. Radio belongs in it. Television belongs in it. The Internet belongs in it. A newspaper earns money by selling ad space on its pages. A television network sells time for commercials. A radio network sells time for commercials. A website sells pop-up ads. Wherever people go, an ad may already be waiting. Whatever they look at, an ad may be there. Over many years, advertisers learned one main way to sell. That way is discontent. Discontent means unhappiness, or a feeling that something is not enough. Almost every ad tries to convince the viewer of a lack. It says that what a person already owns is not enough. It says the person should want more. It promises that the right product will make life happier. It promises better health. It promises more success. One ad asks why anyone should settle for hair that looks sad. It offers shiny hair instead. Pictures labeled before and after are common in such ads. Ads often promise results that the product may not deliver.

4Advertisers then use three main appeals. An appeal is an attempt to persuade. It can also be a quality that catches interest. These three kinds of ads often go by Greek names. The names are ethos, logos, and pathos. Ethos rests on credibility. Credibility is how believable the messenger seems. An ethos ad often shows a famous person. A doctor or another expert may appear in it. That person uses the product or recommends it. Viewers trust the person on screen. Because of the trust, they are more ready to trust the product. Logos speaks to logic. Logic is thinking that uses reason. These ads often give facts and details. The facts try to prove a point. A truck ad might list facts about how powerful the truck is. Pathos speaks to feelings. Excitement is one feeling it may use. Fear is another. A chosen feeling is what the ad wants from viewers. Buying is the push behind that feeling. It may push them to take some other action. A commercial might show a family enjoying a restaurant. It wants viewers to believe they could enjoy that place too.

5Is there a commercial you still remember? Can you name one that makes you think? Can you name one that makes you laugh? Can you name one that makes you feel good? Commercials like those use appeals such as ethos, logos, and pathos. Often one ad mixes the strategies. Buying is the aim of most ads. Some ads have another job. They ask people to change how they act. They ask people to get help. Ads against smoking are one example. Ads against bullying are another. These messages are public service announcements. The short name is PSA. Advertisers place ads where eyes will see them. They place ads where ears will hear them. Ad spending inside the United States during 2015 was divided as follows. Television took 37.7 percent. Digital and online ads took 32.6 percent. Radio took 7.8 percent. Print, including newspapers and magazines, took 15.4 percent. Directories, including phone books, took 2.5 percent. Ads outside the home, including billboards, took 4.0 percent. Watch the commercials in a television show that children like. The products are often from fast-food restaurants.

6They may be cereal. They may be snack foods. They may be video games. They may be toys. They may be popular clothing brands. A brand is a kind of product from a certain company, known by a particular name. The ads may use cartoon characters. They may use music that sticks in the ear. They may show friends who love to have fun. Those friends catch a child's attention and keep it. Most children do not have jobs. Most children do not have much money of their own. Why, then, do companies spend billions on ads for them? One reason involves adults. The companies want children to ask adults to buy. A child may see an ad for a toy. A child may see an ad for a game. A birthday gift request may follow. An ad may make a child want to see a movie. Then the whole family may go. Studies show that young people's opinions carry weight. The weight shows up when families choose clothes. It shows up when they choose snacks. It shows up when they choose restaurants.

7A second reason for these ads is loyalty over time. Research shows a pattern as children grow. A young person may love one restaurant. The favorite may be a snack. The favorite may be a certain shoe. Often that favorite stays in place during the years of growing up. A loyal young buyer can mean sales for a whole lifetime. To shape those choices, advertisers put many ads in front of children. In the United States, television fills a great deal of a child's week. The average is more than twenty-four hours each week. The Statistic Brain Research Institute reported that figure. The same child sees sixteen thousand commercials in a year. That is nearly forty-four commercials each day. Ads also appear on websites children like. They appear in magazines for children. Companies even pay to place products in the background of a story. The products show up in television shows. They show up in music videos. They show up in video games. Barbie appears in one doll ad as a leader of fashion. A hundred years ago, cupboard shelves did not hold boxes of cereal.

8Today, breakfast cereal sales pass 10 billion dollars every year. Marketing made that change. Marketing is the work companies do so a product will stand out. Marketers want a brand that people recognize in every place they go. Cereal ads often use cartoon characters to draw children in. The characters appear in television commercials. They appear on the boxes. Some characters have movies of their own. Some have television shows of their own. A giant form of a favorite character may even float along a parade route. These efforts are built to make children smile. They are built to make children buy more cereal. Eating more cereal is another aim of those efforts. A cartoon mascot named Tony the Tiger stands for one cereal. The cereal is Kellogg's Frosted Flakes. Sellers can find children easy to reach, in some ways. Most adults have experience that helps them read an ad. They have knowledge too. A trick inside an ad is often visible to them. They often notice when an ad is steering their feelings. These skills of critical thinking grow stronger with age.

9The skills are the power to study information and judge it. Many young people do not have the skills yet. Most children younger than about four or five cannot see a difference. A show and a commercial look the same to them. Under age eight, children may notice that the two differ. Most of them still miss the purpose. They do not see that an ad is trying to sell something. Between the ages of seven and eleven, children learn that an ad is a sales talk. This age group is only beginning to see the methods. A method is a planned way of doing something. The methods are how advertisers try to shape a child's thinking. Fast-food restaurants often borrow characters from movies. They borrow characters from video games. The characters are there to appeal to children. Some countries treat ads aimed at children as harmful. Sweden has banned ads that target children under twelve. Norway has banned those ads too. Quebec, a province of Canada, has banned them too. To ban something is to forbid it by official rule.

10In the United States, advertisers may choose their own approach. They decide how they will reach children. Very few laws hold them back. Without those laws, young buyers need strong critical thinking. They need it to notice the push to want what is for sale. Learning to question ads can be interesting. It can even be fun. You and a friend can keep a record of the ads you notice. You can keep a record of the commercials too. Then you can study the methods in them. Five questions work for any kind of ad. Who made the ad? What is the ad trying to sell? Who is supposed to buy it? How does the ad want us to feel? What does the ad want us to do? Which questions should we ask before we buy? Smart consumers can test whether the advertising is true. Critical thinking is the tool for that test. After the test, we can decide for ourselves. One ad for juice places a real apple beside the juice box. A careful viewer can ask why the apple is there.

41. How Animals Send Messages

1Topics in this book come in a set order. Many ways of sending messages come first. Sound comes next. Sight comes after that. Touch comes next. Chemicals come after touch. Messages between different species come next. New discoveries follow. Meanings of key words come last. A common saying claims that cats and dogs fight. These animals can still be good friends. Human speech is what many people picture when they hear the word communication. Smiles are another human message. Hugs are another. Speech remains one of the most important ways. Speech needs skills that other animals lack. It often needs body parts they lack too. Those animals still communicate. There is no doubt of that. Animals other than humans send messages in many ways. Food is a usual reason. Staying safe is a usual reason. Finding a mate is a usual reason. Letting others know they are present is a usual reason. The methods cover an amazing range. More than one method is normal for most species. An elephant may trumpet from excitement. Anger can bring a trumpet too. Alarm can bring one. Surprise can bring one. Play can bring one. For vertebrates, sound may matter more than any other message. Other forms of communication do not travel as far as sound. The one who receives it need not look at the one who sends it.

2Human speech uses the tongue. It uses the vocal cords. It uses other related body parts. Breath is part of speech too. Chimpanzees rank among the closest relatives of humans. Their vocal structures are similar. Chimps cannot work those structures with human skill or human speed. They cannot use them for complex ideas. They still send messages with them. A playing chimp may laugh. An upset chimp may scream. A loud waaa comes when a chimp meets something dangerous. The same call can mark something unusual. Barks carry news. Grunts carry news. Other sounds do too. The news can tell where food is. It can cross a long distance. A pant-hoot carries a message far away. It opens as a low call full of breath. It rises into a loud scream. The pant-hoot of every chimp is unique. Vervet monkeys have three kinds of alarm calls. A call warns the group that danger is near. The call also names the kind of animal. Each call brings a different response. A snake call makes them stand and search the ground. A leopard call makes them race up trees. An eagle call makes them hide in bushes. Elephants produce many sounds for the members of a group.

3Forcing air through the trunk makes a trumpet. Squeals use the vocal cords. Groans use them too. Infrasonic sounds use them as well. People cannot hear those sounds, because the pitch is too low. The deep calls travel for miles. Elephants can send news across long distances that way. Songbirds also use sound. Their melodies are famous. A song may attract a mate. A song may guard an area. A song may check in with others. A song may ask for help. A song may point to a predator. A song may point to food. A vocal organ named the syrinx produces those fancy songs. Air moving across it lets each side vibrate separately. Two different sounds can come out at the same time. Lined up together, those sounds become complex songs. Whale messages are a popular subject of study. Scientists split whales into two groups by how they catch food. One group has teeth. The other has baleen. The two groups also use sound in different ways. Echolocation is the method of most toothed whales, dolphins among them. They send clicks that bounce off objects. Echoes coming back from fish, and from other animals, show where food is. Sounds bouncing off the seafloor help them navigate. Sounds from other objects help too.

4Toothed whales also make calls, or whistles, for other whales of their own species. Both kinds of sound come from structures called phonic lips. Those lips sit just under the blowhole. Air from the lungs is forced through the phonic lips. Closing the lips makes fatty areas nearby vibrate. The vibration makes sound. The sound moves forward as a beam. Fatty tissue in the forehead aims that beam. The beam goes forward toward an object or toward another whale. Baleen whales do not have teeth. Baleen lines the inside of the upper jaw. It is like stiff bristles. Tiny animals come in with ocean water, and baleen traps them. Low sounds from these whales include grunts and moans. Higher sounds include cries, chirps, and whistles. Male humpback whales make sounds. Females make sounds too. Only males produce the long repeating sounds called singing. Nobody knows why the songs exist. Nobody knows how the songs are made. A humpback song can cross 161 kilometers in the ocean, which is 100 miles, or even more. A single song may run for half an hour. Breath is not the only way animals make sound. A kangaroo warns others of danger by thumping its hind feet. A male cricket chirps by rubbing parts of its wings together.

5The chirp draws a mate. It also keeps other males away. A male pigeon claps its wings over its body. The clap tells a female that he is interested. Sight carries many kinds of messages in the animal world. A tail is especially useful. A male peacock spreads fancy feathers in his tail. Females are drawn by that show. Other males are told that he claims dominance. Joy is what a wagging tail often means for a dog. For a cat, a wagging tail usually means the opposite. A male rabbit shows the white fur beneath his tail. He wants a female to notice him. A mother rabbit shows that same fur to her young. The signal tells them to follow her. Clear visual messages also come from other movements. A bee does a waggle dance after finding nectar. Other bees learn the news from the dance. Flat ears on a horse mean anger. A horse may paw the ground from boredom. Worry can cause the same pawing. Pain can cause it too. Faces show mood for many animals. Other primates, humans among them, use many facial expressions. Emotions travel through those faces. Fireflies are experts at another visual message, bioluminescence. A chemical reaction inside the light organ makes them flash.

6The flash may speak to their own species. It may speak to a different species. It may speak to both. Young fireflies flash to warn predators. The warning says they taste bad. Some adult fireflies flash so they can recognize each other. They also use the flash to choose a mate. Many ocean animals use bioluminescence to send messages. Some jellyfish do. Some octopuses do. Some sharks do. Quick shifts of color are another message. A male panther chameleon changes color all at once to draw a female. He also does it to drive other males off. Mood decides the color of a panther chameleon. Tiny crystals inside skin cells help control that color. In Sri Lanka, one toque macaque (TOHK muh-KAK) grooms another. Touch lets many animals stay close. People show care by holding hands. They may walk with arms linked. An elephant may touch another elephant with its trunk. The touch shows care or comfort. Lions lick one another. They rub against one another. Many kinds of animals tend one another's bodies. The process is called social grooming. Bees clear dust, pollen, and parasites from one another. They reach spots that are hard to clean alone. Primates pick parasites off one another. They also clear dead skin and dirt.

7The work is more than cleaning. It strengthens social bonds. Touch can show dominance instead of care. Male Komodo dragons fight one another over food. They also fight over mates. Huge antlers of bull moose lock together during a fight. The fights are over females. Pheromones are chemicals that carry news to members of one species. Special glands on different body parts make the chemicals. Smell usually detects them. Taste can detect them too. The news they carry can take different forms. Ants are well known for pheromones. One job is a scent trail that other ants follow toward food. Another job is to identify themselves to the rest of the colony. Walking into the colony of another ant species would bring an attack. Pheromones help many animals attract a mate. Among alligators, males make the pheromones. Among moths, females give off the pheromones. Antennae on some males are sensitive enough to catch a female scent from miles off. The antennae of a male luna moth are extremely furry. Those antennae help them locate females quickly. Speed matters. Adult moths live for only one week. Pheromones are also used to mark a territory. A territory is ground an animal defends against others of its own species. A scent mark tells others that the animal claims the area.

8Keeping the others out is one goal. Competition for food can drop. Competition for mates can drop. Competition for nest sites can drop. A rabbit, for instance, leaves a clear liquid on the ground. Glands on the chin supply that liquid. An antelope puts scent from facial glands onto leaves. Some animals mix pheromones with urine, which is pee. Some mix them with dung, which is poop. Hippos use both. A pronghorn antelope uses scent to mark territory. The same scent can warn other pronghorns of danger. Some fish in fresh water make a weak electrical signal. They use it to communicate. They also use it to navigate. An electric organ, usually at the base of the tail, creates the signals. The signals may carry news about mates. They may carry news about territories. They may carry news about other topics. Special cells in the skin sense signals that arrive. Households that keep both cats and dogs see that the two can communicate. Strong bonds sometimes form between them. Nobody knows whether wild species send messages to each other on purpose. Some animals do pay attention to the messages of other species. Scientists call that eavesdropping. One lemur species stays safer from hawks by using warning calls. The calls come from another kind of lemur.

9They also come from two kinds of birds. Any of those calls makes this lemur scan the sky for danger. Birds of some kinds also listen in on warning calls from other species. Some monkeys do too. Other animals do too. More examples of eavesdropping will likely turn up as scientists keep studying wild animals. Listening in helps this lemur stay alive. Eavesdropping is not only about sound. One species of stingless bee picks up the pheromone trails of another species. It then avoids those trails. The trails likely lead where the food is already gone. House mice make sounds that show several emotions. Fear is one. Distress is one. Comfort is one. Study of animal messages is popular. Discoveries keep coming. One recent finding is a surprise. Male mice sing in order to attract a mate. The song is a complex string of sounds. Humans cannot hear them without special equipment, because the pitch is too high. Scientists have set these patterns beside birdsong. Other recent research traced a strange quack heard in the Southern Ocean. Since the 1960s, scientists had been hearing sounds called the bio-duck. People traveling at sea had heard them too. A research team solved that puzzle in 2014. It came from minke whales.

10How these whales quack is still unknown. Why they quack is still unknown. Further research will most likely answer both questions. Each discovery opens new questions. It also deepens understanding of the animal kingdom. It deepens appreciation for that kingdom. The kingdom stays endlessly fascinating. Bioluminescence, a noun, is light made by a living chemical process inside a creature. Complex, an adjective, means built of many parts, or hard to reach or grasp. Dominance, a noun, means having control, power, or a higher standing over others. Glands, a noun, are organs that produce substances a body releases for its processes. Infrasonic, an adjective, describes sound waves whose frequency is too low for human ears. Navigate, a verb, means to find a way across a long distance, or to steer toward a goal. An organ, a noun, is a body part with one set job, such as a kidney or a lung. Phonic lips, a noun, are the structures toothed whales, including dolphins and porpoises, use to make clicks and tonal calls. Structures, a noun, are arrangements of body parts that share one purpose. Vertebrates, a noun, are animals with backbones. Vibrates, a verb, means shifts back and forth very fast. Vocal, an adjective, has to do with voice or with speech.

42. How Blood Moves Through You

1Picture a giant post office if you can. At one end, trucks full of mail come in. They collect new mail that still must be delivered. Then they continue the trip. Each truck follows a route chosen for it. Mail is dropped off along that route. Mail is picked up along that route too. The trucks come back to the post office. The whole job then starts again. Your circulatory system is like that picture. The scale is much smaller. The way it works is also very different. Think of the heart as the chief station that does the pumping. You can picture it as the main post office too. Roads in the picture match your blood vessels. The mail trucks match your red blood cells. Blood moves away from the heart. The mail picture still helps. People say the mail goes out in rain or in shine. Your circulatory system never takes a rest either. The heart keeps pumping. It pumps when you feel too tired to move. Blood keeps rushing through the vessels. It circles through the body. Circulation means movement in a circle. It can also mean movement in a circuit. That is basically how this body system works. Blood travels a circuit around the body. The heart is where the trip begins. The heart is where the trip ends. Blood that has left the heart comes back again.

2Place a hand over the heart. What can you feel? You can feel the heart at work. For the circulatory system, the heart is like command central. It is the main pumping station. The heart is a muscle. Its size is about the same as your fist. No other muscle in the body is quite like it. Arm muscles move when you send a message to the brain. Leg muscles work that way too. The brain sends a message back. That message turns the muscles on. The heart muscle does not wait for you to give an order. It pumps by itself all the time. You do not have to think about the pumping. About 100,000 contractions happen every 24 hours. A week brings 700,000 contractions. A year brings 36.4 million contractions. Imagine a job that runs all day and all night. There is no break. The heart's main job is to pump blood through the body. The pumping keeps blood moving all the time. Follow the path inside the heart. From the body, low-oxygen blood arrives at the heart. A vein called the vena cava is the entrance. The vena cava delivers that blood to an upper chamber. The chamber is the right atrium. A valve lets the blood leave the right atrium. A lower chamber receives it. That chamber is the right ventricle.

3The right ventricle pumps the oxygen-poor blood out of the heart. The blood enters the pulmonary artery. That oxygen-poor blood travels onward to the lungs inside the pulmonary artery. Fresh oxygen is collected while the blood is in the lungs. Pulmonary veins leave the lungs with the blood. They carry it to the left atrium. Another valve opens the path out of the left atrium. The left ventricle receives it. Blood then moves from the left ventricle into the aorta. The aorta is the largest artery. Other arteries branch off from the aorta. Every part of the body receives this oxygen-rich blood through those arteries. Blood leaving the heart holds oxygen gained in the lungs. That blood travels to the far ends of the body. The body receives the oxygen. After that, the blood returns to the heart. The oxygen supply has been used up by then. The heart sends that low-oxygen blood onward toward the lungs. Fresh oxygen fills the blood there again. The journey through the body starts once more. Blood cannot flow just anywhere it wants. It does not float in a random pattern. Tubes called blood vessels hold it. The tubes act as roads. Blood cells use those roads to reach every body part. The largest blood vessel is the aorta. It is the largest artery. Vena cava is the biggest of the veins.

4Several kinds of blood vessels run through the body. An artery is one kind. Blood leaving the heart travels inside an artery. Artery walls are thick. They are strong enough for the pressure of each pump. Blood in the arteries is loaded with oxygen. Nutrients ride in that blood to other body parts. A vein is a second kind. A vein carries blood back toward the heart. Vein walls are thinner than artery walls. Veins are less likely to burst. Valves sit inside veins. The valves stop blood from flowing backward. Vein blood does not hold much oxygen. It is on the way back to the heart. It is on the way back to the lungs too. There it will receive oxygen again. Three layers of tissue build both veins and arteries. Epithelial tissue is one layer. You can say that word ep-ih-theel-ee-uhl. Smooth muscle tissue is the next layer. Connective tissue is the third layer. Join every artery, vein, and capillary end to end. The line would cross the United States 20 times. The length is 60,000 miles. That is about 96,560 km. A capillary is a third kind of vessel. Capillaries are very small. Their walls are very thin. Capillaries can be seen in the eye. They pass oxygen from artery blood to each living cell. They pass nutrients that way too.

5At the same time they hand nutrient-poor blood back to the veins. A pulse is blood vessels moving under the skin. With every beat, a big rush of blood moves along the arteries. Artery walls expand to make room for the surge. That expanding wall is what you feel as a pulse. At rest, count about 60 to 80 beats in one minute. A doctor may listen for a girl's pulse. Count the beats during 10 seconds. Multiply the count by 6. The answer is called your heart rate. First, hop for a minute on a single foot. Then count the beats again. The heart rate will have changed. Exercise gives the heart a workout. Other body muscles get a workout too. A raised pulse helps keep the heart healthy. What is blood? A scraped knee can show the red stuff. It oozes from below the skin. A girl may study the scrape on her knee. To the naked eye, blood may seem only a thick red liquid. A closer look shows a complicated substance. The cells in it are tiny. A microscope is needed before those cells can be seen. Blood cells come in two main kinds. One kind is red. The other kind is white. Certain bones hold a soft tissue called marrow. New red blood cells are made by marrow.

6The spleen is a small organ near the stomach. Old blood cells are destroyed by the spleen. New white blood cells are created by the spleen. Red blood cells are the most common blood cells. They give blood its red color. Red blood cells in one body number over 25 trillion. Each one lives about four months. White blood cells are in the blood too. A yellow liquid carries both red cells and white cells. The liquid is called plasma. A red blood cell is shaped a bit like a donut. The holes are not punched all the way through. Oxygen travels through the body inside red blood cells. Nutrients from food travel in them too. Red cells pass along the lining of the intestines. There they absorb nutrients from food that has been digested. They also remove wastes from cells all over the body. A neutrophil can clean damaged tissue near an injury. It can clean up bacteria there too. White blood cells have a major job. They fight off diseases. That fight helps the body stay healthy. They gobble harmful germs. Bacteria are one kind of germ they eat. Viruses are another kind. The most common white cell is the neutrophil. Say it NEW-tro-fill. Neutrophils are the body's lookouts. They are its guardians. They search all the time for germs that would cause sickness.

7When they find those germs, they wrap around them. Then they eat the germs. Another white blood cell is the lymphocyte. Say it LIM-fo-site. Lymphocytes produce antibodies. Antibodies help the body combat viruses. They help combat bacteria too. An antibody is a chemical. The chemical helps destroy harmful bacteria. It helps destroy viruses too. A lymphocyte does not eat the germs. It tries to wipe them out by chemical warfare. A third white cell is the monocyte. Say it MON-o-site. A monocyte works in the same way as a neutrophil. It eats unknown particles in the blood and kills them. A lymphocyte can sit among red blood cells. Platelets help form scabs. The scab lets a cut heal. Blood has another crucial part besides red cells, white cells, and plasma. That part is the platelet. Platelets are smaller than red blood cells. They are smaller than white blood cells too. Their job matters deeply to the circulatory system. Platelets help the blood clot. After a cut, the bleeding can stop. The platelets make blood cells clump with the plasma. The clump blocks the blood from flowing. Fibers form over the skin as well. Those fibers block the flow even more. In time the fibers become a scab over the cut. Skin under the scab is then able to heal. Blood flow touches many body parts. Intestines supply the blood with nutrients from food.

8Kidneys remove waste products from the blood. Sugar levels in the blood are controlled by the liver. Cleaning the blood is another liver job. It removes poisons. It filters drugs too. One drop of blood can hold 5,000,000 red blood cells. White blood cells in that drop may number from 5,000 to 10,000. It can hold 200,000 to 300,000 platelets. Blood vessels show on the underside of the wrist. They lie just under the skin. A close look at the hand shows vessels beneath the skin. Those vessels do not look red. Red is the color of blood itself. The vessels look dark and purplish. Oxygen is the reason for the two colors. Hemoglobin is a protein inside red blood cells. Say it HE-mo-glow-bin. Iron is part of hemoglobin. Iron lets the blood absorb oxygen more easily. Iron mixed with oxygen turns the hemoglobin red. The red color of blood comes from this mix. The red shows clearly when blood leaves a cut. It also shows when blood leaves a puncture. Most of the oxygen has already been given out by the time blood heads back. Little oxygen remains. The color grows darker. Veins look darker for that reason. They can look purplish. Skin changes the color you see as well. A foot falls asleep when blood flow is cut off.

9Sitting on a foot or an ankle often causes that stop. Tingling starts when blood flows again. Pins and needles may be felt after the foot falls asleep. The feeling lasts until blood flow is normal again. A boxer may have a bruise around an eye. A broken vessel may show in his nose. Bruises look purple. A bruise means a vessel broke under the skin. The skin itself did not break. The spilled blood gathers beneath skin that has not broken. The skin takes on a purplish hue. White blood cells swarm in. They eat the pooled blood. Over time the blood breaks into smaller pieces. The pieces get smaller still. In the end, nothing of that trapped blood remains. The bruise has disappeared. People also call it a black-and-blue mark. Blood is not all the same. It can be sorted into four types. An antigen is the substance that sets the type. Antigens make the blood produce chemicals that fight infection. Two kinds of antigens exist. One kind is A. The other kind is B. Blood with the A antigen is Type A. Blood with the B antigen is Type B. Blood with both antigens is Type AB. Blood with neither antigen is Type O. Knowing your type matters. A bad accident can cause a great loss of blood. A transfusion is then needed to replace it.

10The new blood must be your type. Type O blood is also allowed. People with Type O are called universal donors. Anyone can accept their blood. Donated blood is stored in blood banks. The banks serve people who need transfusions. Children who exercise help their hearts stay fit. Some people say the body is like a machine that stays oiled. In that picture, blood is the oil. The oil lets the body run. The heart is the engine. The engine pumps the oil through the body. Blood vessels are the tubes. Blood flows inside those tubes. Heart, vessels, and blood together make the circulatory system. An analogy compares two things that people do not usually see as alike. An antigen helps the body fight infection. Bacteria are one-celled living things. They can cause disease. Circulation means something moving around a loop inside the body. To clot is to thicken a liquid until the flow stops. Depleted means emptied. Oxygenated means mixed with oxygen. Plasma is the liquid that carries the blood cells. A protein is a needed piece of every cell in the body. A pulse is the heartbeat felt in the wall of a blood vessel. A transfusion moves blood into another person's circulatory system. Viruses are tiny living things. They infect cells and make copies of themselves inside those cells.

43. Hunting the World's Giant Insects

1Everywhere you look, insects are there. Hundreds of them are what you will likely walk past if you step outside. Thousands are possible too. About ten quintillion insects are alive on Earth at any moment, scientists estimate. The figure is 10,000,000,000,000,000,000. Over 900,000 different insect species have been named so far. About 80 percent of all animal species in the world is that share. Two million to thirty million more species may still be unknown. Small size is why many people are glad about most insects. The past was different. Buzzing insects were already here hundreds of millions of years ago. Dinosaurs had not yet walked the Earth. Similarity to insects now is what fossils show. Three body sections belonged to each. Six legs belonged to each. Two sets of wings often belonged to each. Other differences were there in smaller ways. Titans is the right word for these critters. A Meganeura, as it might have looked, appears in a drawing. A dragonfly is what this prehistoric insect resembled. Well over 30 inches, or 72.2 centimeters, was the wingspan. Extra oxygen in the air then is part of why scientists think insects grew so huge. About 35 percent of the air was oxygen long ago. About 21 percent is the share today. The drop took hundreds of millions of years. Adaptation happened. Evolution happened. Smaller size was the result. Giants of their world is what a few insects stayed. Curiosity about these beasts is common. Your curiosity may be sparked too. A bug hunt around the world is about to start.

2A safari in search of giant insects is one way to think of the trip. Billions of smaller insects will be passed while you look for the huge ones. Your own eyes will see them. Fascinating details will be learned. A packed bag and a start are what you need. Surprises should be expected. A slow stream in the southwestern United States is the first stop. Buzzing from a giant darner dragonfly is what you hear. The wingspan is 5 inches, or 12.7 centimeters. Warm air is crossed at over 35 miles per hour, or 56.3 kilometers per hour. A full change of direction in a split second is a turn these insects can make. A glimpse of one comes at that moment. Mosquitoes and other small insects are hunted with that speed and those quick moves. Your arm is where it lands. Fear is not needed. Harmless is the right word for the giant darner. Shimmering wings are what you see in that short look. A black, blue, and green body is what you see. The body is 5 inches long, or 12.7 centimeters. Takeoff comes right after. Tall grass is the path toward the next stop. Other dragonflies flash past. Giant darners are among them. Smaller cousins are among them. Good hunting is your wish for them. Giant darners live near streams. This kind of stream is in the southwestern United States. Five inches, or 12.7 centimeters, is the size shown for a female giant darner. A male is shown too, with a note of where they live.

3The steamy Amazon rainforest is where you have arrived. Dark is the setting. Leaves overhead are tapped by rain. Your flashlight beam is crossed by something in flight. A bird is your first thought. A titan beetle is what a closer look shows, at 7 inches, or 17.8 centimeters. No beetle on Earth is larger. Night is when this creature is active, and your light drew it in. Dead leaves and dirt are what it crawls through. Thick mandibles sit on its head. A pencil could be snapped by them. A stick test is tempting. A hiss comes when you get near, and you back away. A few weeks is all the adult has, as with many beetles. You are not its concern. A mate is what it needs. Flight is possible, but the ground is too low a start because the beetle is so heavy. A tree is climbed with powerful, spiny legs. A branch is the launch point. Your face is the path it takes, and you duck. Laughter follows, and the journey goes on. Seven inches, or 17.8 centimeters, is the size in a picture of the titan beetle, along with where it lives. Takeoff from a branch is shown in another picture. Hot and humid is the afternoon in Thailand. A clear freshwater pond that looks cool is your stop. Aching feet dangled in the water sound tempting. A giant water bug is what you see next. Fresh water all around the world is where this insect lives, which is how you know it.

4About 5 inches, or 12.7 centimeters, is the length. An underwater plant is what it clings to. A minnow is held in its strong front legs. Digestive juices are pushed into the struggling fish by its jaws. What remains is then sucked out. More giant water bugs are nearby. Round eggs stuck on the back mark one as a male. Safety until hatching is why the female glues the eggs there after mating. A missed tadpole is the result of another bug's try. Almost anything they can overpower is food. Frogs are included. Snakes are included. Baby turtles are included. People are not in danger from the bite. Pain from that bite is still among the worst of any insect, a local person said. A big crunchy bite of a grilled giant water bug came right after her words. Perfect is how the water looks. Walking on is the choice you make. Eggs on the back of a male giant water bug appear in a picture. About 5 inches, or 12.7 centimeters, is the length, and the picture notes where these bugs live. Prehistoric times are how long people have eaten insects. Many parts of the world eat over 1,000 types. Water bugs are on the list. Cockroaches are on it. Ants are on it. Dragonflies are on it. Grasshoppers are on it. A rainforest is your path again. Rainforests hold most of the insects on Earth, so the setting is no surprise. Central Africa is the place this time.

5Heavy, thick air is what the rainy season makes. A shuffling sound makes you look around. A female Goliath beetle digs in the soil. She uses her head to dig. A safe place for her eggs is the goal. Patterns on her shell are what you study. A tree is where another beetle lands after buzzing past. A male Goliath beetle is what it is. Horns in a Y shape sit on the front of his head, and that is how you know. Almost 5 inches, or 12.7 centimeters, is his length. Smaller is the female. Length is not why these beetles got their name. The heaviest insects in the world is the reason. Over 3.5 ounces, or 100 grams, looks like this male's weight, near the top of the range. Harmless is the truth, even though the Goliath beetle is big. Waste on the forest floor is its food. An arm climb by the male is a thought you have. Another male's arrival changes your mind. A fight with strength and horns may come if you wait. A quick flight away is what the smaller male chooses. Moving on is your cue too. A female Goliath beetle and a male appear in pictures, each near 5 inches, or 12.7 centimeters, with where they live. The African rainforest is nearly behind you. A dark stream seems to run across the ground. Nearer is where you step, while still keeping a gap. The ground is crossed by African driver ants in the millions. Small worker ants fill the center.

6Ants you see at home are about their size. A wall is formed along the edges by soldier ants. Almost 1 inch, or 2.5 centimeters, is each soldier's length. Large mandibles point outward to guard the ants inside. A hunt is underway, so the danger is high. Blind is what they are. Touch still guides them easily. Smell guides them. Chemical signals guide them. Over twenty-two million ants can fill one colony. No insect on Earth has a larger colony. Hunger is shared by all of them. Food is the aim of millions upon millions as they swarm. Almost any animal they can overpower is eaten, and people are included. A column of African driver ants is what a picture shows. The nest is what you hope to find by following the column. A large rock has a dark wall of movement against it. Living ants are the material of a temporary nest. A breakup of that nest begins as you watch. Food in the area must have been used up. Food is carried off by the column. Millions of eggs are carried. The queen is carried. Over 2 inches, or 5.1 centimeters, is her length. No ant on Earth is larger. As many as two million eggs can be laid by her each month. Amazing is the sight. Leaving is still the best choice when a few ants crawl your way. Tropical Madagascar offers a lovely day. Forest is the path you walk. You sit on a log. A stream moves slowly beside it. Movement in the log comes with a hiss.

7A colony of Madagascar hissing cockroaches is what you have upset. About 2 to 3 inches, or 5.1 to 7.6 centimeters, is each one's length. Black and dark orange is the shell. Shade under the log is where they crawl back. Coming out in the daytime is too soon for them. Fallen fruit and dead plants would be the night search on the forest floor. Flight is impossible for this cockroach, unlike most kinds. Live young, not eggs, are what the females give birth to. A touch is what you offer one of them. A male is what the long horns on its head show. Small holes along its body let out air, and that is the hiss. A spit follows. Gentle and harmless is what these insects are, so you laugh. Pets of this kind are kept by a friend of yours. Staying to watch would be nice. Moving on is the plan. Your friend's cockroaches can be watched later. A pet Madagascar hissing cockroach is what a picture shows. A colony of Madagascar hissing cockroaches appears in a picture, with where they live. The length is 2 to 3 inches, or 5.1 to 7.6 centimeters. Trees on Little Barrier Island, off New Zealand's coast, are your search. The endangered wetapunga, or giant weta, is the goal. The god of ugly things is what the name means in the Maori language. Millions of years on these islands is the history of these strange cricket-like insects. Near disappearance came over the last few hundred years.

8Rats and mice, brought to the islands, eat the slow wetapunga. Leaves of a tree hide one as the sun sets. A cricket mixed with a grasshopper, only much bigger, is the look. About 4 inches, or 10.5 centimeters, was the largest one ever found. The weight was 2.5 ounces, or 70.9 grams. Almost that big is how this one looks. Leaves are nibbled as it crawls slowly along the branch. Trees are where almost its whole life will be spent. Catching it would be possible if you wanted. Flight is impossible. Jumping is impossible. Kicking and biting are not things they do. Leaving it alone is your choice, because town is a run you must make. A release of a number of wetapungas into the wild is planned by a group trying to bring them back. Being there is your wish. A giant weta is what a picture shows. About 4 inches, or 10.5 centimeters, is the size of a female giant wetapunga in a picture, with where it lives. High above the ground, you sit in a rope chair. Papua New Guinea's forest canopy is the view. Miles of lush green surround you. A rising flash of blue comes next. A male Queen Alexandra's birdwing butterfly is what it is. Blue-green and brown wings shimmer as it flaps. Almost 8 inches, or 20.3 centimeters, is the span. A female is looked for and not found. Larger is what a female would be. A lighter brown and a 12-inch wingspan, or 30.5 centimeters, would be hers.

9Papua New Guinea is the only wild home of this rare species, outside of zoos. The forest canopy is the only place there. You feel awe. The male is feeding at a flower. A flutter away comes next. Something else catches your eye on the way down. An atlas moth on a branch is what you pull closer to see. Eleven inches, or 27.9 centimeters, is the wingspan. The snake head moth is the other name those wing-end hooks bring to mind. Three months or more is a possible life for a Queen Alexandra's butterfly. Regular feeding is its habit. A few weeks is the whole life of an atlas moth. Feeding never happens. Southeast Asia is where you have seen this moth before, because it is fairly common. Impressive is still the word for the sight. Almost 8 inches, or 20.3 centimeters, is the male Queen Alexandra's birdwing in pictures, with where it lives. Eleven inches, or 27.9 centimeters, is the atlas moth, with where it lives. The London Natural History Museum is the last stop. Chan's megastick, the world's longest insect, is what you came to see. Six is all that have been found in the world so far. Borneo is where every one of them was found. Longest of them is this one. With legs stretched out, 22 inches, or 55.9 centimeters, is the reach under the glass. Fourteen inches, or 35.6 centimeters, is the body alone. A plain old stick is exactly the look. The year 2008 is when scientists finally discovered it, and the stick look may be why.

10Leaves are its food while it hides in the forest canopy, as people believe other stick insects do. Chan's megastick and where it lives appear in a picture. A danger greater than a driver-ant colony is possible. Learning is the only way to know. Exploring is part of that way. Open eyes are part of it. Changed to fit a new situation or environment is what adapted means. The thick cover of leaves made by treetops is the canopy. Groups of animals that live together are colonies. Changed or developed over time is what evolved means. Remains of a plant or an animal that became stone over time are a fossil. Mouth parts that grab, cut, or crush food or enemies are mandibles. Active at night, not in the day, is what nocturnal means. A wild journey made to search for animals is a safari. Living things that look alike and can reproduce are a species. Moving in great numbers is what swarm means. Giants, beings that are very large or powerful, are titans. Tip to tip on a pair of wings is the wingspan.

44. Ibn Battuta's Years on the Road

1A map of these journeys shows Europe. It shows Asia. It shows China. It shows India. It shows Africa. It shows the Indian Ocean. The map also marks the years of travel. Today, people have many ways to explore other countries. They have many ways to explore other cultures. Trains carry people around the world. Airplanes carry people around the world. Books let people connect without a trip. The Internet lets people learn from one another without a trip. None of this was possible in the Middle Ages. The Middle Ages ran from 500 CE to 1500 CE. Most people could not travel far. One man still traveled 75,000 miles in that time. That distance is 120,701 kilometers. Nearly every country inside the Islamic empire was on his route. He traveled beyond that empire too. The man was Ibn Battuta. Ibn Battuta began his travels soon after the Islamic Golden Age.

2That age ran from 750 CE to 1258 CE. Scientific ideas grew hugely across the Islamic empire then. Cultural ideas grew hugely too. Sea routes began to make long trips possible. Caravans across the land made long trips possible too. More people began to travel. They traded ideas across the empire. They traded goods. They traded services. Windmills were built between 500 and 900 CE. Those windmills still work today. Abu Abdullah Muhammad Ibn Battuta was born in 1304. He was born in Morocco. Say his name as AH-boo, then ab-DUHL-lah, then moo-HAH-muhd, then I-buhn, then bah-TOO-tah. Law was his subject while he was young. At age twenty-one, he chose to go to Mecca. Mecca is in Saudi Arabia. Islam's founder was born in Mecca. The trip to Mecca lasted sixteen months. He stopped in many countries on the way. Those lands were the Dar al-Islam.

3The name means world of Islam. He reached Mecca in 1326. For three weeks, he prayed. He visited sites that matter in Islam. After that, he chose not to return home. Exploring the Islamic empire became his new plan. He wanted to learn about other cultures. He wanted to learn about other people. He wanted to learn about other places. Islam has five Pillars. Each Pillar is a duty. Every Muslim must carry out these duties. One duty is the hajj, a pilgrimage to Mecca. Hajj is said HAJ. Each year, Muslims travel to Mecca from all over the world. Prayer fills five or six days of their stay. They also visit different holy sites. About two million Muslims make this pilgrimage each year. In 1326, Ibn Battuta visited the land now called Iraq. He also visited the land now called Iran. The Mongols had just attacked both areas.

4Eastern Asia was the home of the Mongol warriors. They had traveled west. Many cities inside the Islamic empire were destroyed by them. Ibn Battuta still visited many beautiful cities. Those cities had not been harmed. From 1327 to 1330, he traveled through Yemen. Then he sailed to towns on the coast of East Africa. In those towns, many local people were not Muslim. They did not speak Arabic. Local languages reached his ears there for the first time. People welcomed him everywhere he went. A stay in their homes was offered by sultans. They gave him gifts. They held parties. He heard news of the sultan of India. That sultan was giving Muslim scholars jobs with high pay. India was his next goal, so he could meet that famous sultan. On the way, he visited the land now called Turkey. People of different religions lived there together.

5They worked together in peace. After they conquered Muslim lands, many Mongols converted to Islam. Mongol art had a strong influence in Persia. Persia was a region that roughly matches modern Iran.
