Parts of Mongol art traditions show in Persian fabrics. They show in calligraphy. They show in paintings. They show in pottery. Scientific study was another task Mongols and Persians shared. Fine detail is common on Persian pottery. Ibn Battuta reached India in 1334. A house was one offer from the sultan. The sultan offered him money. The sultan offered him gifts. The sultan also gave him a job. His work in India lasted several years. Most of India was under Muslim rule. Even so, the sultan began to think he was losing power. He thought every person around him was a traitor. Ibn Battuta was soon added to the people he called traitors.

6No proof showed that Ibn Battuta had betrayed him. The sultan still decided to send him away. Five months later, Ibn Battuta was called back. In 1342, the sultan made him an ambassador to China. Ibn Battuta was to go to China. He was to offer gifts to the emperor. The trip toward China was hard. An attack separated Ibn Battuta from the other travelers. Death came close to him on several occasions. At last he reached a port city. Three ships there were ready to carry him and others to China. A storm hit before he could leave. Two ships were wrecked by the storm. The third ship sailed away. It left Ibn Battuta behind. He chose to go on to China by himself. The Maldives were a stop during that journey. Maldives is said MAWL-deevs. He stayed and worked there for two years.

7Next he traveled to Sri Lanka. Sri Lanka is said SHREE LAHNG-kuh. A new ship came as an offer from a sultan of that place. The ship would let him continue the trip. In 1346, he finally arrived in China. The Mongols ruled China then. He was amazed by the people he saw. People of many religions held important jobs. People of many cultures held important jobs too. He thought China was a safe country. He also thought it was a welcoming country. Ibn Battuta stayed in China for less than a year. Then he decided to go back to Morocco. He arrived in 1349. News waiting for him was that both parents were dead. After a few days, he set out again. His first stop was Spain. There he met a young writer. Later, that writer helped him tell the stories of his travels. He next chose to go to Mali.

8Mali is in West Africa. He stayed in Mali for eight months. In 1354, he made his last journey. He was going home again. This time he went home for good. In 1353, he began a book about his travels. The friend from Spain helped him write it. Life in the Middle Ages appears in the book with a wide view. No one had shared a view like that before. Morocco was the place of his death, around 1368 or 1369. Little is known about his life after the travels ended. The book was called the Rihla. Rihla is said RIH-hlah. In Arabic, the word means journey. In the 19th century, historians saw the book as important. It was an important travel book. It gave detailed information about the Islamic empire. It also covered nearby lands in the Middle Ages. The details include political life.

9They include social life. They include cultural life. The book describes that time with accurate details. Other works from the same time seldom include details like these. People often call Ibn Battuta a famous Muslim traveler. He liked learning about cultures unlike his own. He liked learning about religions unlike his own. He liked learning about ideas unlike his own. That interest made him a world traveler. Few people in his time could say the same. His travels opened a window on the Middle Ages. His writing opened that window too. Readers met the people of that age. Readers met the cultures of that age. His writings name Tangier in Morocco. They name Istanbul in Turkey. They name Tehran in Iran. They name Granada in Spain. The writings introduced other people to incredible places. They also introduced the cultures of the Islamic empire. An ambassador is an official sent to a new place.

10The official may also live there. The job is to represent a country or a population. A site is land that holds something in a set place. A sultan is a ruler, often in a Muslim country. A sultan is similar to a king. A sultan is similar to an emperor. A culture is a society with its own ideas. A culture also has its own customs. Sweeping means far-reaching. An empire is a group of nations or people. One person rules them, or one government does. A traitor betrays their own country. The betrayal supports an enemy. A founder is a person who starts something. The start may be a system. It may be an organization. It may be a group. A scholar is a person with a lot of knowledge. The knowledge is usually about one subject.

45. Inside the Country of Brazil

1At Carnaval, parades fill with dancers in colorful costumes. Carnaval is a holiday in Brazil. The parts of this book are place, people, land, history, celebrations, animals, a closing, and a word list. A map shows Brazil in South America. Neighbors and nearby lands on the map include Guyana, Venezuela, and Suriname. French Guiana, Colombia, and Peru are shown. Bolivia, Paraguay, Argentina, and Uruguay are shown too. The Atlantic Ocean appears on the east. The Pacific Ocean is marked to the west. Cities include São Luis, Brasília, Rio de Janeiro, and São Paulo. North America is labeled on the wider map. No country in South America is larger than Brazil. It covers nearly half of the whole continent. The whole United States is only a little bigger. Many smaller countries touch Brazil along its western half. Those countries are Venezuela, Colombia, and Peru. They also include Bolivia, Paraguay, Argentina, and Uruguay. The Atlantic Ocean borders Brazil on the east.

2That coast runs past 7,400 kilometers, or 4,600 mi. The capital is Brasília (bruh-ZIL-yuh). The country's middle is where it stands. Brasília was not always the capital. Rio de Janeiro (REE-oh DAY zhuh-NAIR-oh) is a larger city on the east coast. It was the capital until 1960. The government chose to build a new capital farther inland. Brasília is still new, yet it is growing quickly. The people who live and work there number more than two million. Many of them work for the government. The city is Brazil's political center. Brasília is full of modern buildings. Brazil's people number more than two hundred million. Eight out of every ten live in cities. Several cities here are large. No city in South America is bigger than São Paulo (SOW POW-low). Service jobs employ many people here. Those jobs include construction, banking, education, and healthcare. Many beaches are edged by large hotels and by restaurants.

3They give work to people who live along the coast. Other Brazilians work in factories. They make cars, airplanes, and steel. Many of those factories are around São Paulo. Mining and farming are the countryside jobs. Few countries produce more minerals than Brazil. Iron ore is one mineral, and so are tin and gold. Farmers grow much of the world's coffee on huge plantations. Oranges grow on the farms, along with cassava and soybeans. Hogs and chickens are raised by some of the farmers. Ranchers raise cattle. Brazil grows the most popular coffee type, called Arabica. Many Brazilians like to watch soccer and to play it. They call the game futebol (FOOT-bohl). World Cup titles belong to Brazil more often than to any other country. The World Cup is the world's largest soccer tournament. Pelé is the country's most famous soccer player. People know him around the world.

4Pelé led his team to World Cup victory in 1958. He did it again in 1962. He did it once more in 1970. Brazilians come from many backgrounds. Some descend from the Native peoples who lived there first. Other families trace back to Angola, to the Congo, and to more lands in Africa. Slavery carried those people to Brazil. Plantations used their labor, and mines did too. Many others descend from people who came from far away. Those lands include Portugal, Spain, Germany, and other parts of Europe. Portugal held Brazil as a colony across three hundred years. The main language is Portuguese (POR-chuh-geez). Many rivers flow through Brazil. Crops grow well because the rivers leave the soil rich. One river stands out clearly. Few rivers on Earth are wider than the Amazon River. It is also one of the longest. Its path through northern Brazil covers at least 6,400 kilometers, or 4,000 mi.

5The vast Amazon basin draws its water from this river. That basin covers nearly half of Brazil. It covers about 7 million square kilometers, or 3 million sq. mi. The Amazon rainforest is here too. No tropical rainforest on Earth is larger. No river on Earth moves as much water as the Amazon River. Size gives Brazil five main regions. Landforms in each region differ so much that the regions seem like separate countries. The north and the southeast include highlands. Steep mountains and deep valleys form the Guiana highlands in the north. Hills plus low mountain plateaus form the Brazilian highlands in the southeast. A land map shows the Guiana Highlands and the Atlantic Ocean. It shows the Amazon River and the Amazon Basin. The Brazilian Highlands and Brasília are marked. The Pantanal wetlands, the Pacific Ocean, and the coastal lowlands are marked too. Brazil's long east coast is another region.

6It includes beautiful sandy beaches. Those beaches are a main draw for tourists. The Pantanal lies in central Brazil. It is one of the world's largest freshwater wetlands. It stretches for thousands of miles around tributaries of several rivers. The first known people in Brazil were Native peoples. They include the Guarani and the Yanomami. Different Native groups still live in the rainforests. The Amazon River's banks are another place they live. Portugal took control of Brazil in the early sixteenth century. Many Native people were forced into slavery by the Portuguese. Huge sugarcane plantations were where that forced labor happened. Africa was the later source of people they enslaved. They brought those people to work on coffee plantations. They also put them to work in diamond mines and gold mines. Independence came to Brazil in 1822. An emperor ruled it. Slavery ended in 1888. In 1889, the military forced the emperor to step down.

7Brazil became a republic. Leaders in a republic are usually picked by the people. That did not always happen in Brazil. Dictators often led the people instead. Democracy arrived in 1985, once the turmoil had passed. No Carnaval in Brazil is larger than the one in Rio de Janeiro. The Sambadrome is the special building that hosts it. Religion shapes many of the holidays. The Catholic faith is the one most Brazilians follow. The Portuguese brought that religion to Brazil. Brazilians come from many backgrounds. So customs of other groups are followed too. Those groups include Native peoples and African peoples. No holiday here is larger or more loved than Carnaval. Four days of celebration come just before Lent. Lent is the forty-day time when Catholics prepare for Easter. During Carnaval, people crowd into the streets. They come for parades and dancing. Masks and headdresses finish the elaborate costumes many people wear.

8Life on Earth is most varied here. The Amazon rainforest gives a home to more animals than any other environment. It holds more plants than any other environment too. The toucan (TOO-kan) is one of the colorful birds living there. Jaguars live there. Ocelots (OS-uh-lotz) live there too. Slow-moving sloths hang from trees. The forest floor is the path of giant anacondas. Trees are highways for many kinds of monkeys. Piranhas (pi-RAH-nuhz) are meat-eating fish that hunt as a group in the Amazon River. A group may number one hundred or more. The water carries large manatees as they swim. Other parts of Brazil hold unique animals too. The Pantanal wetlands are one such place. Nests of storks and herons sit in trees of the Pantanal. Colorful parrots fly overhead. One is the blue and yellow macaw. Deer roam the land. Jaguars roam there too. The Pantanal is also home to giant anteaters.

9The golden lion tamarin is another animal of these lands. It is a type of monkey. Sadly, some animals of the Amazon rainforest could vanish from Earth forever. Some animals of the Pantanal could vanish too. Every year, trees are cut for timber. Trees are also cleared to open land for farming. Because of that, animals such as the jaguar are losing their habitat. The golden lion tamarin is losing its habitat too. Many organizations are working with the Brazilian government. They want to keep this loss from happening. Beaches of great beauty are the fame of Rio de Janeiro. Life fills this country. No place on Earth holds more living things than the Amazon rainforest. People in the cities have lively celebrations unlike any other. People in the countryside do too. Land, animals, and people give this huge country plenty left to explore. The population is 211,049,527. The land covers 8,458,417 sq km, or 3,265,813 sq.

10mi. The capital city is Brasília. The primary language is Portuguese. The primary religion is Catholic. The currency is the real (ray-AHL). These facts come from the Central Intelligence Agency in 2019 and from the World Bank. Cassava is a plant with thick roots. Cooks use this plant, which grows in the tropics. Catholic describes the Roman Catholic Church, one branch within Christianity. A dictator holds total power and usually keeps it by force. Highlands are areas with many mountains. The land there sits high above sea level. The Pantanal is a wetland region in south central Brazil. It reaches into Paraguay and Bolivia. Plantations are large farms with many workers. A plateau is flat ground set higher than nearby land. Sugar is taken from sugarcane, a grass of the tropics. A tributary is a river or a stream that joins a bigger river.

46. John Muir's Care for Wild Land

1Long ago, settlers began colonizing the land now called the western United States. Colonizing means starting a place that another country rules or owns. Many settlers talked about conquering nature. For thousands of years, Indigenous peoples there had cared for the land. They had protected it too. Clearing the land was what the settlers wanted. They wanted natural resources for houses. They wanted natural resources for food. They wanted natural resources for clothing. John Muir explored nature with a strong belief. He thought of nature as the heart of the world. Few settlers shared his wish to preserve nature. To preserve means to care for something and save it for later. The natural world stirred a strong passion in him. Passion means deep enthusiasm or strong feeling. Years of living in remote cabins followed from that passion. Remote means far away or cut off. The same passion led him to walk across large parts of California. It took him to Alaska. His travels also reached many regions beyond those places. Untouched wilderness, he believed, was ideal. Ideal here means perfect. Because of that belief, he wanted national parks created. Muir was an eloquent writer. He was an eloquent speaker. Eloquent words are clear, powerful, and elegant. He used those skills to inspire other people.

2He wanted them to join conservation work. Conservation means protecting wild lands and the living things in them. April 21, 1838, is John Muir's birth date. Dunbar, Scotland, is his birthplace. Daniel, his father, worked as a businessman. Daniel's earlier work was as a soldier. Anne, his mother, kept the home. She was a painter. She was a poet. She loved nature. John was the oldest son. Eight children were in the family. Wisconsin, in the United States, became their home in 1849. Daniel wanted to start a farm there. Earning more money was another goal. Strict discipline was Daniel's way. A disciplinarian uses firm training and punishment to keep order. He made the children work long hours on the farm. One small meal a day was all he allowed them. Their health suffered because of that hard life. Today that Scottish birthplace is a museum. The museum tells about his life and his work. During those farm years, John had no formal schooling. Even so, he was hungry to learn. Each morning he got up at 1:00. He was glad to gain five extra hours. He used the hours for his own interests. He taught himself literature. He taught himself math. He taught himself other subjects.

3Free hours in daylight were for exploring. He explored the woods around the family farm. He explored the fields there too. As a teenager he became an inventor. He used part of those early mornings for inventions. The inventions included tools for weather. A later invention was an unusual alarm clock. At a chosen time, the device pushed him from the bed. The invention joined a desk with a clock. It was fixed to a bed that could collapse. At the chosen time, it lit a lamp. Then John slid gently down to the floor. As a young adult, Muir felt restless. He wanted to follow his passion. At age twenty-two, a neighbor made a suggestion. Muir should show his inventions at a fair. That fair, in 1860, was Wisconsin's State Agriculture Fair. It was held in Madison. The inventions were a huge success. The alarm clock drew the biggest praise. One judge was Jeanne Carr. She later became Muir's mentor. A mentor is a skilled teacher and adviser. A cash prize came from Carr. Independence, for the first time, was his. He did not return home. He chose to stay in Madison and attend college. Jeanne Carr was a writer. She was also an amateur botanist.

4Ezra Carr was a professor of geology. Both of them became Muir's friends. Both of them became his teachers. Math and science were the main studies for young Muir at college. The science included botany. Plants are the subject of botany. He left in 1863 without finishing a degree. He called his next school the University of the Wilderness. He walked through parts of Canada. He walked through the Midwest. He watched nature closely. He took odd jobs to pay for the trip. Indiana was where he worked as a mechanic in 1866. An accident at work hurt his eyes the following year. For a short time the injury blinded him. He feared the blindness would last. His sight came back a month later. He suddenly understood something. He was not spending his life on the work he loved most. He quit the job. Nature was where he planned to spend every year that remained. Muir set out on September 1, 1867. He walked about 1,000 miles to reach Florida. That distance is 1,609 kilometers. Later he wrote about the path he picked. He picked the wildest way. He picked the way with the most leaves. He picked the way where the fewest people had walked. A little more than seven weeks passed before the Gulf Coast lay ahead of him.

5A map of this 1867 trip shows how he traveled. Part of the travel was by train. Part was on foot. Part was by boat. The map shows Indianapolis. It shows Ohio. It shows West Virginia. It shows Indiana. It shows Kentucky. It shows Virginia. It shows North Carolina. It shows Tennessee. It shows South Carolina. It shows Alabama. It shows Georgia. It shows Florida and Cedar Keys. It also shows the Atlantic Ocean. Days after he reached the Gulf Coast, he became very sick. The sickness was malaria. Malaria is a dangerous disease of warm regions. Mosquitoes spread it. The next January, he boarded a ship bound for Cuba. He hoped to go on to the Amazon. He wanted to study the plants that grow there naturally. Instead, his travels ended in a different city. San Francisco was the city he entered on March 28, 1868. A question followed about which road left town the soonest. The man asked where Muir hoped to go. Muir said he wanted any place that was wild. From there he walked toward the Sierra Nevada in California. A map marks that mountain region. The beauty of the High Sierra filled him with wonder.

6He felt lonely without other people. Even so, the woods thrilled him. The fields thrilled him too. When he had to choose, he usually chose nature over people. That first stay in the High Sierra did not last long. He came back the next year. For a short time he worked as a shepherd. Later he ran a sawmill near Yosemite Falls. Yosemite is said yoh-SEH-muh-tee. Free time went to exploring the land. Free time also went to celebrating nature. Ralph Waldo Emerson's writings gave him inspiration. Emerson often wrote about why people should connect with nature. In the early 1870s, Muir offered a controversial idea. Controversial means likely to cause strong disagreement. He said moving glaciers had carved Yosemite Valley. A glacier is a large body of ice and packed snow. It stays all year. It creeps slowly downhill. Interest in glaciers took him deep into the mountains. He searched there for evidence. An article about glaciers was his writing project in October 1871. The New York Tribune printed the article. Later, his idea was shown to be right. People were starting to notice Muir. They noticed what he observed in nature. Other naturalists came to his cabin to meet him. A naturalist studies nature and its history.

7Plants and animals are a big part of that study. Emerson was one visitor. Settlers used the name Yosemite for a place that already had a name. The older name is Ahwahnee Valley. Ahwahnee is said ah-WAH-nee. The name means a place like a gaping mouth. The valley is home to several Indigenous tribes. The Ahwahneechee are one of those tribes. The Miwok are another. The Paiute are another. Settlers forced many Indigenous people off the land. The removal was violent. Those settlers are the ones who named the area Yosemite. El Capitan is one famous feature of the valley. Yosemite Falls is another. Oakland, California, became his home in 1873. He planned to write magazine articles there. The articles shared what he had seen in nature. He wanted readers to care about natural places. In April 1880 he married Louisa Strentzel. People called her Louie. He moved to the fruit ranch owned by her family. He was a devoted husband. He was unhappy with the work the ranch required. He still longed for remote places. Louie supported him, so he began traveling again. Louie lived from 1847 to 1905. Her parents were immigrants from Poland. Every subject she studied brought her a perfect score.

8She had a gift for music. She could have played piano in concerts. She chose the family farm, where daily life stayed quiet. She stood by her husband's love of far-off nature. She did not feel that same love herself. The couple had two daughters. One daughter was Wanda. The other daughter was Helen. One photograph shows Muir with his wife and the two girls. Two articles followed his 1889 trip to Yosemite. The articles told about the beauty of the area. They told why protecting it mattered. Other people helped him. They asked Congress to preserve Yosemite. The next year, Yosemite became a national park. It was the third national park in the United States. Muir and many others saw a further need. A group was needed to protect Yosemite. Other sections of the Sierra Nevada needed that group as well. In 1892 he helped start the Sierra Club. He became the club's first president. Since then, the Sierra Club has become a leading group for conservation. President Theodore Roosevelt requested a meeting with Muir in 1903. Roosevelt wanted to understand remote wild places in the West. Muir felt honored by the request. He took the president camping in Yosemite. On the trip, Muir urged him to set land aside as parks.

9Because of that, more of Yosemite joined the national park. Roosevelt also protected many other natural places. A photograph shows the two men standing on Glacier Point. Writing about his travels took more of his later years. Four universities gave him honorary degrees. He also led a fight that lasted twelve years. The fight was about Hetch Hetchy Valley in Yosemite. A planned dam would flood the valley. He worked to stop that flooding. Others worked to stop the dam too. Muir even wrote a letter to Congress. The project was still approved in 1913. The fight did raise awareness about saving natural land. It also helped bring about the National Park Service in 1916. The short name for that service is NPS. The NPS created stronger management for every national park in the country. December 24, 1914, was the day John Muir died. The start of the National Park Service came after his death. His work still helped make that beginning real. The word wilderness can suggest land that nobody cares for. It can suggest land where nobody lives. Many places Muir called wilderness were not empty. Indigenous peoples had lived in them before written history. They had managed them too.

10Muir did not always understand their ways of caring for the land. He did not always agree with those ways. For thousands of years, Indigenous peoples used controlled burns. A controlled burn is a fire that people plan. The fire helps keep a forest healthy. Muir believed something different. He thought the only protection was to prevent every fire. Evidence now supports controlled burns. The burns are effective. They are important. Many nature places carry John Muir's name. Other sites carry his name too. Muir Woods National Monument is one example. It stands north of San Francisco. In the United States, his contributions to conservation were important. National parks grew from his devotion to wild places. Those parks still protect beautiful land for people not yet born. Today the Sierra Club works on many environmental problems. The problems are across the United States. The club also works on climate problems around the world. It works with Indigenous communities. Together they protect land. Together they protect animals. The aim is to keep beloved places safe. The aim is to keep them healthy for many years. Muir's life offers a clear lesson from the facts of his work. He asked people to protect wild places, not only to use them.

47. Katherine Johnson and the Road to Space

1In 2016 astronaut Leland Melvin gave Katherine Johnson an award. The award was the Silver Snoopy Award from NASA. It honored great achievements in flight safety. It also honored success on a mission. People all over the world looked upward in early October 1957. They hoped for a glimpse of something small. They searched for a pinpoint of light. The light moved quickly across a sky full of stars. Sputnik was the name of the object. Humans had never before placed a satellite in space. A satellite is an object that orbits Earth or another body in space. The object may be natural, or people may have made it. In France, observers hunted the 1957 sky for Sputnik. Its weight, as a Russian satellite, was 184 pounds. That weight is 83.5 kilograms. It went around Earth every ninety minutes. Few Americans felt glad about this success. The Soviet Union had sent Sputnik into orbit. To launch something is to send it into space. The United States had a great rival then, the Soviet Union. The contest was political. The contest was military too. Many people feared a change in rocket technology. They feared the Soviets had moved into the lead. On those nights, young Katherine Johnson was among the watchers. At the time, her name was Katherine Goble. She worked as a research mathematician at NACA. A mathematician studies numbers, shapes, and amounts.

2The letters NACA stood for National Advisory Committee of Aeronautics. Aeronautics is the science and the practice of flight. In 1958 the agency received a new name. The new name was National Aeronautics and Space Administration. People call it NASA. Her job was to analyze the numbers that mattered most. To analyze is to look at something with great care. Those numbers guided ideas for aircraft designs. They guided the plans. They guided the tests. The work she loved had required her to get past many challenges. One fact about her was that she was a woman. Another fact was that she was African American. In those years, few people expected a woman to have a career. A career in science was even less expected. A career in mathematics was less expected too. Black women met still harder barriers because of discrimination. Men often ignored them. Men often treated them with scorn. Nearly all of those men were white. They held most of the power in engineering. They held most of the power in the sciences. Her work at NACA began in 1953. Guidance and Navigation was the department she joined. Space was a race, and the United States was behind. The country needed its best minds. It needed machines and methods nobody had imagined before. She had one of the strongest mathematical minds of her time.

3Katherine Coleman was the name given to her at birth in 1918. She spent much of her youth in a small town. The town was White Sulphur Springs, in West Virginia. Her mother taught school. Her father farmed. The parents often told the children two linked ideas. The children were no better than anybody else. Nobody else was better than the children. From early childhood she loved learning. Numbers were her favorite part. She counted everything around her. She counted the steps she walked. She counted the plates she washed. Books she finished were counted too. Second grade was her class by the time she was six. High school was within reach by age ten. The town did not give black students and white students the same schooling. Separate schools were required for the two groups. Eighth grade was the last grade at the school black students could attend. A rented house in Charleston, West Virginia, became the Coleman family's base. They wanted the children to keep going to school. She finished high school at age fourteen. West Virginia State College was her next school. The college served African American students. Her math skill was so strong that a professor built a class for her alone. The class was an advanced one. She finished college at age eighteen. She finished with the highest honors. African American college graduates had few careers open to them.

4For black women, teaching school was one of the rare choices. That is the work Katherine Coleman chose. She did not stay in teaching for long. She fell in love and left the classroom. In 1939 she married James Goble. The next year, a university contacted her. The school was West Virginia University. The president himself invited her to enroll. Only three African American students would be in that graduate group, and she was one. Later, the mathematics program was left behind. She left because she was pregnant. She and James would have three daughters. In time she went back to teaching. In 1865 the United States government ended slavery. This followed the Civil War. Jim Crow laws were then passed by Virginia and by other states. The laws forced white people and black people to stay apart. They could not go to the same schools. They could not eat in the same restaurants. They could not use the same bathrooms. The same jobs were closed to them. Voting was blocked for black people as well. Only in the 1950s and the 1960s did the country begin to undo these unfair rules. At one time, separate water fountains were a common sight. In 1962 she worked at Langley Research Center. A globe sat with her as she worked. People also called the globe a Celestial Training Device.

5A wedding took the Goble family to Virginia in 1952. A government job there was described to her by her brother-in-law. The workplace needed mathematicians. It was hiring black women. In 1953 Katherine Goble started work at Langley. The center belonged to NACA. She joined a pool of workers called computers. A computer today is not what a computer was then. The name computers was used for workers like her, since computing was their task. They used a pencil. They used paper. Simple calculators were among their tools. West Area Computing at Langley was a real group of people. The group was made up of black women who were experts in math. They produced the calculations that many NACA engineers needed. A calculation is math used to work something out. The word can also mean the answer that math produces. Soon she was placed on a research team. The team studied how aircraft were designed and how they flew. She was the only woman on that team. She was the only African American on it. Her special skill was analytical geometry. This kind of geometry describes shapes and places with math formulas. In engineering, designers can use it to solve design problems. In flight work, they can do the same. She earned the respect of the other people on the team. She insisted on a seat in the most important meetings.

6Her manner was gentle, and it was also firm. Colleagues turned to her more and more often. They came when the most important calculations had to be right. She also refused the segregation rules at Langley. Segregation is a rule that keeps groups of people apart. Race is often the reason for the split. She demanded to be treated with dignity. Dignity is a sense of worth and of respect. She used the same bathroom that white women used. She would not eat in the cafeteria area marked for black people. In 1956 her husband, James Goble, died. A brain cancer took his life. She then raised their three daughters alone. In 1959 she married James A. Johnson. A year after the death of James Goble, the Soviets placed Sputnik in orbit. That flight started the space race in earnest. The space race was a contest over exploring space. It was between the United States and the Soviet Union. It began in the 1950s. Her career turned in a new direction. The new direction was space. Earth was orbited in 1961 by the Soviet cosmonaut Yuri Gagarin. In that same year, NASA sent Alan Shepard Jr. into space. The flight belonged to Project Mercury. The project had one large goal. The goal was an American astronaut traveling in orbit. NASA had not done that yet.

7The United States was still behind the Soviet Union. Her greatest strength was finding flight paths. A flight path of a space vehicle is a trajectory. A trajectory is the path an object takes through air or space after launch. She studied where a rocket would leave the ground. She studied where the capsule should splash down. Then she wrote the equations that could make the mission succeed. A mission is a flight with a special job to finish. She had to include the pull of gravity. She had to include weight. She had to include distance. She had to include many other factors. Alan Shepard's flight rested on work of this kind. The duty was huge. When an astronaut rode a rocket toward space, that life depended on the experts. She and her colleagues checked the calculations together. They tested them. They confirmed them. In 1962 NASA was finally ready to place a person in orbit. John Glenn was picked for that critical mission. He was among the most able astronauts in Project Mercury. He was among the most hardworking. A new electronic computer, very powerful, had planned the flight. The machine was also set to control his path around Earth. Glenn did not feel easy about trusting a machine. A human expert was who he wanted for the math check. He asked for her by name. She worked through the numbers again.

8She checked every calculation she had made for the flight. She told the NASA team that the math was sound. The launch could go ahead. A 1970 photo shows her standing with NASA colleagues. John Glenn's departure from Earth came on February 20, 1962. His craft shot out of the atmosphere. He traveled around the planet three times. The Atlantic Ocean was where he splashed down. At last the space program of the United States seemed level with the Soviets. Soon, NASA's skill at flying people in space would move ahead of theirs. To surpass is to go past someone, or to become stronger. Her research remained vital to later missions. Her math helped send astronauts to the Moon. That work came in the late 1960s. The Apollo Program included that work. Trajectories for the trip were part of what she figured out. She also worked out a meeting above the Moon. The lunar lander had to meet the capsule. The capsule would bring the astronauts home. An American flag was given to her as an honor. The crew had carried that flag to the Moon. Later she supported the space shuttle program. Shuttle paths were among her later calculations. NASA retirement came for her in 1986. Asked about the job, she answered in a simple way. She said she loved the work. A math note printed with the story uses this flight.

9The note dates it February 2, 1962. It says he circled Earth three times. He covered about 81,000 miles in all. That distance is 130,357 kilometers. Launch to splashdown took 4.93 hours. For the whole trip, speed averaged about 16,430 miles in each hour. That speed is about 26,442 kilometers per hour. She took part in many major moments in NASA history. She belonged to a team of mathematicians. Successful missions depended on their calculations. A new government agency, NASA, began on October 1, 1958. One purpose was to move aircraft technology forward. Another purpose was to move space exploration forward. The first satellite from the United States went up on December 18, 1958. The satellite was for communications. It went into orbit. Space flight for Alan B. Shepard Jr. came on May 5, 1961. He was the first American to make that flight. Friendship 7 carried John Glenn on February 20, 1962. He was the first American to circle Earth. The first time people walked on the Moon was July 20, 1969. The walk was part of Apollo 11. On April 17, 1970, damage to a craft nearly ended in disaster. The mission was Apollo 13. Remarkable teamwork at NASA saved the crew. Three astronauts returned safely to Earth. On April 12, 1981, the shuttle Columbia blasted into space. It was the first shuttle to make that trip.

10A Saturn 5 rocket carried the Apollo 13 crew upward. The launch left Cape Kennedy, in Florida, in 1970. Retirement did not end her work. She took on a different kind of mission. She visited schools. She told young people about her career. She wanted to share her love of science. She wanted to share her love of math. In 2015 the White House invited her. She was ninety-seven years old. President Barack Obama gave her the Presidential Medal of Freedom there. That medal is the highest honor the country gives a civilian. He called her a pioneer. He said she had broken barriers of race. He said she had broken barriers of gender. He honored her for a further lesson. She showed that everyone can excel at math. She showed that everyone can excel at science. She showed that everyone can reach for the stars. A photograph places Katherine G. Johnson before a NASA research building. The building carries her name. In 2017 NASA honored her once more. The agency opened a new center at Langley Research Center. The center is the Katherine G. Johnson Computational Research Facility. Doubt once surrounded her abilities. Being a black woman was the reason for that doubt. Celebration of her contributions came later. Her work supported some of the greatest scientific achievements of the country.

48. Laura Ingalls Wilder: Stories from the Prairie

1A map of the United States marks the places in Laura's life. Pepin, Wisconsin, was home from 1867 to 1869. It was home again from 1871 to 1874. Madison is Wisconsin's capital. Independence, Kansas, was home from 1869 to 1870. Topeka is Kansas's capital. Walnut Grove, Minnesota, was home from 1874 to 1876. It was home again from 1878 to 1879. St. Paul is Minnesota's capital. De Smet, South Dakota, was home from 1879 to 1894. Pierre is South Dakota's capital. Mansfield, Missouri, was home from 1894 to 1957. Jefferson City is Missouri's capital. Iowa is on the map, and Des Moines is its capital. Nebraska is on the map, and Lincoln is its capital. Illinois is on the map, and Springfield is its capital. Oklahoma is on the map too. A key explains the marks for state capitals. It also explains the marks for Laura's homes. The opening pages stay with the prairie and with the big woods. Hard times follow. Dakota Territory is the next home. Laura then becomes a young lady. Rocky Ridge brings happy years. The Little House books finish the tale. Three was Laura Ingalls's age. Mary, the older sister, walked beside her. Their father led them over a vast prairie. The land rolled under their feet. Warm wind moved through Laura's hair.

2Song came from insects in the rippling grass. Song came from birds too. Homeward, the sky took on pink from the sunset. Gold joined that pink. The little log cabin was the next step, and Laura stopped short. Bed was where Ma lay. A doctor stood there. Tired pride was in Ma's voice. She called the girls to meet a new baby sister. Laura Ingalls Wilder kept this memory from very early years. Power stayed in it. Two forces in that day later shaped her famous books. Family closeness was one force. Love of wild, open spaces was the other. February 7, 1867, was the day Laura Ingalls was born. The woods of Pepin, Wisconsin, were the place. Two was her age when Ma and Pa Ingalls packed every belonging. A covered wagon took the load. Independence, Kansas, was the direction they chose. Childhood after that was full of moves. This trip was only the first. Settler-colonists is the name for the family. White settlers were still new in the American West, and the Ingalls were among the early ones. Vivid memories formed in Laura at the log cabin. The Kansas prairie was the setting. About a year was all the time they lived there. Osage land was where they had settled, and the choice was a mistake.

3Leaving was required. Wisconsin was the return. A picture shows Ma and Pa Ingalls. Caroline was Ma's name. Charles was Pa's name. The 1800s sent settler-colonists west in a stream. Forests came down under their axes. Tough sod, the mat of grass roots on the prairie, was plowed up. Crops were the reason for that work. Survival meant making almost everything. Building was part of it. Growing, knitting, sewing, and cooking were part of it too. Sadness filled four-year-old Laura as she watched. The Kansas prairie slipped away behind the wagon. Baby days in Wisconsin were too early for her to remember. The return opened a wonderful new world. Darkness filled the big woods. Wildness filled them. Mystery filled them. Outdoors was where Laura loved to be. Long winter evenings brought Pa's fiddle. The family listened. That music belonged to the happiest days of her childhood. Laura later put that leaving into words of her own. The wagon held nearly everything from the little house. Beds were left. Tables were left. Chairs were left. New beds, tables, and chairs were things Pa could build when the family needed them. Open prairie was what Pa Ingalls wanted again. Minnesota was the place in his plan for the young family. A bad fever took seven-year-old Laura before the departure.

4Every blanket the family owned went around her. The back of the covered wagon was where they nestled her. One more home fell away behind her eyes. Wide prairie in Minnesota made Laura glad. Pa was glad too. Prairie sod was the stuff of their first house there. Dense grass growing all around them was what sod meant. From a distance the house barely showed. It looked like a gentle hill. A creek bank seemed to lift that hill. A wood house came later, built by Pa. Wheat went into the ground near Walnut Grove. School was where Laura and Mary went. Reading pleased Laura. Writing pleased her. Reciting pleased her. Another baby was on the way for Ma. Happiness lasted a few months. Hardship arrived next. Grasshoppers came in swarms. The swarms were as big as thunderclouds. The family's wheat was all eaten. The crop was gone. Walnut Grove became the place they moved into. Odd jobs kept them going. A hotel had work even for little Laura. She cleaned. She waited tables. A fever then took the new baby brother. His name was Freddie. Another baby lifted their spirits a little. The year 1877 brought Grace. Tough times did not end. Illness came to Mary. Nerves in her eyes may have been damaged by that illness.

5A stroke may have been the cause instead. Certainty about the cause was missing. Blindness would last for all the rest of Mary's life. A new start, Pa decided, lay to the west. Dakota Territory was where he went first. Railroad work was his job there. A train in Walnut Grove carried the family. West was the direction, toward Pa. A picture from about 1880 puts Laura on the right. Carrie is on the left. Mary stands with her sisters. Helena, Montana, appears in a picture from 1869. The railroad had arrived. A town could seem to rise in a single night. Settlers rushed in. Houses went up quickly. Stores went up. Hotels and saloons went up. Dakota Territory had a railroad stop and little more. A town was still in the future. De Smet, South Dakota, would grow from that stop. The Ingalls family were among its first citizens. A few other white families shared that start. A homestead came to the Ingalls family. It lay a few miles out from town. A cabin was Pa's work. A stable was his work. A garden was his work. Winter moved the family above their store. Town life put the girls nearer school. The late 1800s brought violent force from the U.S. government. Native peoples were pushed off their lands.

6White settler-colonists received those lands. Settlers were urged to go west. Homesteads were offered, and the living space was nearly free. The land had been taken from Native people. Racist views of Native people were common among white settlers. Laura held such views. The land she lived on was theirs. Her books often paint Native peoples in a negative light. White settler-colonists appear in a positive light. The choice to stay in town was lucky. Blizzard followed blizzard. The new town took a pounding. Snow blinded the girls. The way to school was lost. Food could not arrive by train. Starvation came close for some families. Hours of work fell to Laura and her sisters. Raw wheat was ground by hand until it became flour. The long winter finally broke. Bloom covered the Dakota prairie when the family went back to the homestead. The farm began to thrive. The new town began to thrive. Laura once set down a belief in a single line. Suffering comes to an end, she held, while love does not. A college for blind students stood in Iowa, and Mary had heard of it. Nothing mattered more to Mary than going there. Schoolteaching was now within Laura's age. Income from that work could help pay for Mary's college.

7The job she took was teaching. The settlement sat twelve miles from De Smet. Sixteen was Laura's age. Goodbye to the family was brave. A full semester away from home was what she dreaded. Surprise waited at the end of the first school week. Almanzo Wilder drove up to the schoolhouse. He was a young homesteader. De Smet was where she already knew him. A beautiful sleigh, pulled by a horse, carried him. Weekends, he said, could include a ride that took Laura home. Long drives over the prairie changed friendship. Love grew from it. A marriage proposal came from Almanzo on one of those nights. Yes was Laura's answer. His homestead outside De Smet became their home. Manly was the nickname she used for him. A daughter arrived soon. Rose was the baby's name. Bad luck then came in a string. Hail destroyed the wheat crop. Illness took Laura. Illness took Manly. Full recovery came for Laura. A limp would stay with Manly for every year that remained. A baby boy was born to Laura. Two weeks was all the life he had. Fire then took the house to the ground. A settler's search began again. Laura wanted a new place and a fresh start. The home she wanted did not take long to find.

8Laura and Almanzo Wilder appear in an 1885 picture. The time was soon after the wedding. Vaccines were few before the twentieth century. Medicines that truly worked were very few. Disease was common. Disease was serious. Many people in Laura's family felt illness. Those years made that pattern usual. Success at Rocky Ridge Farm brought a request from a local newspaper. Laura was asked to write a column. Farm advice was the topic. Pay for writing came to her for the first time in that job. She wrote that the sweet, simple parts of life are the parts that are real. Twenty-seven was her age when the covered wagon rolled once more. A small farm near Mansfield, Missouri, was what she and Manly bought. Foothills of the Ozark Mountains held the land. Rough ground was everywhere. Woods were everywhere. Rocks were everywhere. Home was what Laura knew it to be, and she knew it at once. Hard work turned Rocky Ridge beautiful. Success followed. Contentment came after all the wandering. Rose, the daughter, later left for San Francisco. Journalism made Rose well known there. Writing was how Rose earned her living. Laura had always loved that same work. Comfort had come to life on Rocky Ridge Farm. Laura could ask whether writing might be her path too.

9Memories began to pour onto paper. Some nights the writing lasted until morning. A little desk, built by Manly, was where she sat. A whole book grew from the early years. Towering trees in Wisconsin were the setting. The title she chose was Little House in the Big Woods. A publisher received the book from Rose. Sixty-four was Laura's age. She expected little to come of it. The 1930s were the time. Cars were common. Electricity was common. Radio was common. Hard chores in a dark forest seemed like a story few would want. The book found love from readers at once. Howling winter wind became real on the page. A crackling fire became real. The joyful skip of Pa's fiddle became real. Meeting animals in wild country felt thrilling in her words. Joy from a simple rag doll was part of the story. The girl who held the doll lived as a settler in a rough log cabin. Letters overflowed the mailbox at Rocky Ridge. More books were what young readers begged to have. The Kansas prairie years became Little House on the Prairie. The Walnut Grove years became On the Banks of Plum Creek. A frightful winter at De Smet became The Long Winter. Recovery in that town became Little Town on the Prairie.

10Laura Ingalls Wilder signs her books in a picture from about 1940. Letters from fans were a joy to answer. Travel to readings was a joy. Book signings were a joy. Home with Manly was where she was always glad to go. Rocky Ridge Farm was that home. Both lived past ninety. The year 1957 was the year of Laura's death. The books did not stop. A TV series in 1974 carried them further. Young readers today still find delight in the settler tales. American history now holds her life. Some words in the story have set meanings. Hardship means pain or loss, or whatever brings that pain or loss. In the 1800s, a homestead was a farm plot. Settlers received it from the U.S. government. Income means money that comes in from work or from some other source. A prairie is a broad flat plain dressed in grasses. A publisher is a person or a company that puts writing where the public can get it, on paper or on the Internet. Reciting means speaking aloud the words you remember. Settler-colonists leave one place, stay in another, and claim that land for themselves. A stroke is a fast failure of the brain when blood stops flowing to it. Vivid means bright in a strong way.

49. Life Far Under the Sea

1A deep-ocean anglerfish carries a spine. The end of that spine is bright. The bright end works like a fishing pole. It draws prey close to the fish. A small sea pig lives in the deep ocean too. It moves along the ocean floor. Tiny sea animals in the mud are its food. Microbes in that mud are food as well. A trip to the ocean's lowest place is worth picturing. Which kinds of things might you expect to find? Might the water be dark? Might the water be light? Might you see clearly? Might the water look cloudy? Might the water feel warm? Might the water feel cold? Might the animals look familiar? Might the plants look familiar? Might you find animals that seem strange and new? Might you find plants that seem strange and new? Warmth from the sun reaches the ocean where the water is shallow. The warmth reaches 200 meters below the surface. That depth is 656 feet. Plants that need sunlight live in this warm water. Animals that need warm water live there too. Tiger sharks live in this shallow zone.

2Many species of common dolphins live there too. Other animals live deep in the ocean. The water there is cold. The water there is dark. Vampire squid live in that cold, dark water. Fangtooth fish live there too. Viperfish live there as well. Tide pools hold warm, shallow water. Species of starfish live in those pools. Anemones live in them too. Mussels live in them too. Tiny crabs live in them too. The ocean can be divided into zones from the surface downward. The deep ocean lies below 1,000 meters. That depth is 3,281 feet. The water there is freezing cold. A person can be hurt by water pressure once the depth passes 39.6 meters. That depth is 130 feet. The right protective gear keeps a diver safe. Sport divers wear air tanks. Each tank holds a mixture of gases. The mixture is for safety. Special computers made for diving are part of their gear too. A dive computer shows how far down a diver is. It also shows how much air is left. A hard suit is the protection a diver uses on a deep trip.

3The suits protect them from water pressure. On the deepest trips, a small submarine carries the scientists. Two or three scientists ride together. The craft is called a submersible. Some submersibles have robot arms. An arm can collect a water sample. An arm can collect a soil sample. An arm can collect a small animal. A depth chart marks the ocean in feet from 0 to 22,000. The marks step up by 2,000 feet. The chart shows a sport diver. It shows a diving suit. It shows a submersible craft. At 39 meters, the water is 7 to 13 degrees Celsius. That depth is 128 feet. The same water is 45 to 55 degrees Fahrenheit. At 609 meters, the water is 4.5 to 7 degrees Celsius. That depth is 2,000 feet. The same water is 40 to 45 degrees Fahrenheit. At 6,096 meters, the water is 0 to 2 degrees Celsius. That depth is 20,000 feet. The same water is 32 to 35 degrees Fahrenheit. Oceanographers is the name for people who study the ocean. The word is said oh-shuh-NOG-ruh-furs.

4They often live on a ship for months. They work seven days a week. They also teach at colleges. They teach at universities too. Some oceanographers study what sea animals eat. Those scientists also study where the animals live. Other oceanographers study the ocean water itself. Every oceanographer needs to understand biology. Every oceanographer needs to understand chemistry. Every oceanographer needs to understand geology. Every oceanographer needs to understand physics. Living things are the subject of biology. Chemistry studies what things are made of. It also studies how things react when they touch other things. It studies how things change in that contact. Rocks are one subject inside geology. It is also the study of soil. It includes the processes that shape the Earth. It includes the processes that change the Earth. Physics is the study of natural laws. Energy is one of those laws. Force is one of those laws. Motion is one of those laws. The laws govern how things work. Satellites high in space can track sea animals. The animals travel through Earth's oceans. Scientists attach electronic sensors to some animals.

5Each sensor is a tag. A satellite can follow the signal from the tag. The tag may be fixed to a fin. It may be fixed to the animal's back. The signal shows when the animal travels. It shows where the animal travels. It shows how the animal travels. These tags have followed some fish over huge distances. The farthest of those fish trips reached 75,000 kilometers. That distance is 46,602 miles. Scientists can use the information to predict later movement. They try to tell where an animal will go next. Which kinds of animals make the ocean their home? Where do those animals live? How many animals are there? Oceanographers studied animals for ten years. They counted the animals too. They wanted answers to those questions. They looked at every ocean in the world. One end of the work was the North Pole. The other end was the South Pole. Every living thing they could locate went into the count. The count included the largest whales. It included the smallest organisms. Census of Marine Life is the name of that surprising report.

6The report was released in 2010. More than 540 separate expeditions supplied its data. More than eighty countries were the starting places of those expeditions. Coral sea fans are a place where lionfish swim. A sting can come from their spines. Those spines are long. They look feathery. A predator that comes too close can be stung. Lionfish are native to the South Pacific. People first spotted them in waters of the United States in 2000. Nearby plants hide the leafy sea dragon, since its body matches them. Its two eyes move independently. One eye can look in one direction. The other eye can look in another direction at the same time. Eggs are carried by the male sea dragon. He carries them for the female. The male is the one that gives birth. The Census of Marine Life revealed new information. It also revealed several surprises about extreme habitats. One surprise was about where living things were found. They were found everywhere. They lived at every water level. They lived in every temperature. The hottest places still held marine species. Marine species lived in the coldest places too.

7Deep places held more than seventeen thousand species. Those places had no light. Some of those deep places also had no oxygen. Another surprise was the number of new species. Described new marine species now total more than twelve hundred. More than five thousand species still wait to be described. One shrimp species from the Jurassic showed up in the finds. People had thought that shrimp was extinct. People had placed that extinction more than fifty million years earlier. Giant tube worms live more than 2 kilometers down. That depth is 1.3 miles. They live in hot water. A lava pillar is the source of that bubbling hot water. The Deep Ocean Zone is very cold. Hot volcanoes there number more than one million. Water at a volcano there can reach 400 degrees Celsius. That temperature is 752 degrees Fahrenheit. Heat like that can melt lead. This very hot water is home to eyeless shrimp. White crabs live in it too. Giant tube worms live in it too. Many bacteria live in it too. These animals use sulfur in order to live.

8They do not use sunlight. Huge groups of Arctic animals live in icy water. Huge groups of Antarctic animals live in it too. The water is close to freezing. Some of that water is below freezing. Giant squid live under solid ice. Antarctic cod live under that ice too. The lion's mane jellyfish lives under that ice too. The ice is 700 meters thick. That thickness is 2,296 feet. Many of these species slow their movements. Slower movement saves energy. It also helps them keep from freezing. Red blood cells are missing in the Antarctic ice fish. Its blood is thinner than the blood of most fish. That thinner blood holds a type of antifreeze. This special blood helps the ice fish survive. The fish lives in frigid water under the ice. In cold like that, the blood of most fish would freeze. The census taught scientists something important. Life can adapt further than the scientists' first idea allowed. Animals of the deep ocean have ways to survive. They live in harsh environments. Most other species could not live in those places.

9Species listed by the marine census total more than 230,000. More than 30 million records hold those counts. Species nobody had seen before numbered more than 6,000. Some of the animals were see-through. See-through animals are also called translucent. Some animals had warning lights. The lights turn on. The lights also turn off. The lights help them see in the dark. Feelers took the place of eyes on some other animals. Those feelers were long. The feelers draw prey near. The feelers also help the animals learn what is around them. Some animals had teeth long enough to overlap their jaws. Other animals made their own antifreeze. That antifreeze helps them stay warm. A big-eyed squid lives in cold, deep water. Its home is the northern Atlantic Ocean. Which other living things share the deep ocean? Scientists keep finding new species. They keep finding new facts. Each new look brings more finds. In their view, the counted portion of marine life is only a quarter. Some animals already found have not been documented. Some have not been described. More surprises are still ahead.

10A living thing is adaptable when it can change to fit a new situation. The same word fits a change for one specific environment. Data are facts and information. A measurement is one kind of data. A statistic is another kind. People use data when they analyze. People use data when they plan. An expedition can be a journey. It can be a voyage. It can be the group of people who go. Jurassic names a period in the Mesozoic era. Many dinosaurs lived during that period. Birds began to evolve then. Mammals began to evolve then. Marine means related to the sea. To predict is to say what will happen later. People often predict after they watch carefully. They also use what they already know. A process is a series of actions. Some processes happen in nature. Other processes are steps made toward a goal. A species is a group of living things. The members are physically similar. They are able to reproduce. A submersible is a small vessel. It can operate under water. It is made for deep levels especially.

50. Looking Things Up Before the Internet

1Topics in this book come in a set order. Information and the Internet come first. A visit to the library comes next. News comes after that. A face-to-face meeting follows. Finishing the project comes next. A world of technology follows. Meanings of key words come last. Picture a festival your city holds every year. City history is what the festival celebrates. Your teacher asks the whole class for a report on that festival. The report must include research on the city. It must include the story of the festival. This year's festival events must be part of the report. Several different sources are required. An interview with someone who lives in the city is required too. You can probably already name your first step. A website for the city is likely. Pages on social media are likely too.

2History and current events can be learned there in a short time. The people who help plan the festival can be found there as well. Now shift the scene to a time with no Internet. Where will the project facts come from? Tools and devices for finding facts, people, and places are common now. Reaching those facts was not always easy. A librarian does more than line books up on shelves. Finding information is also an expert skill for that person. Helping others find it is part of the job. You choose the local public library as your start. Books are there. Articles are there. News stories are there. Facts about local events and local people are there. The librarian can point you toward the resources that help this project most. Encyclopedias, the librarian says, are a strong place to begin any project.

3They are books packed with facts on many topics. A set of them is lined up in alphabetical order. One volume usually holds the topics that start with a single letter. An article on your city might be inside. Someone important from the city's past might have an article too. For a long stretch of years, a set sat in most schools and most libraries. Many homes owned a set as well. Sellers walked from house to house with the books. Paying for a full set could take more than one thousand dollars. Printed volumes are no longer the product of most encyclopedia companies. The same works can be read online. After the encyclopedia, you hunt for more books. The card catalog is the tool. It is the system that tracks every book the library owns.

4Drawers of paper cards are what the librarian shows you. Every book has a card. Title, author, and subject are listed on it. You search the cards until one book looks useful. You copy the details from that card. Those details tell you which shelf holds the book. Card catalogs are gone from most libraries now. Computer programs have taken their place. The programs help people locate library information and keep track of it. Old collections stay in many libraries. Newspapers are in them. Letters are in them. Photographs are in them. Books are in them. Some items are historical. Some are simply out of date. Next you are shown the newspapers and magazines the library keeps. Old papers are where you look for stories of festivals from earlier years.

5Recordings of local television news are in the library too. Recordings of local radio news are there. Old photographs of festival events turn up. Video of those events turns up too. News can also tell you about the festival planned for this year. An article might run in the city paper. A story might run on the local news station. The newest papers are at the library. A paper also arrives at your home each day, because your parents get delivery. Seeing that story means watching news in the evening. A printed guide tells you when local news is on. That guide sits inside the local paper. Every channel you can get in your area is listed. Every show on those channels is listed, hour by hour. The guide also describes the week's programs.

6You find the channels your home receives. Then you find the hour when the news airs. Books, articles, and news reports have given you all they can. Planning the interview is the next job. Your reading has suggested people to ask. The paper named the person in charge of this year's festival plans. Local news spoke with a man who has attended fifty festivals. You want to interview him as well. A talk with the city mayor also sounds interesting. A picture shows two students interviewing a man. They use a video camera and a microphone. You will try to reach all three people. Another big book comes out, the phone book. Businesses are listed. Services are listed. Households in your area are listed. A name search can yield a home telephone number. A search for a business or a city office can yield a work number.

7That is how you reach the people who work there. A physical address for each home and each business is listed too. If the person agrees, you still must pick a time and a place. The library is one possible meeting spot. City hall is another. The person's office is another. You write the addresses down. A city map comes next. Every street in town is on it. Important landmarks are on it, and city hall is one of them. The map lets you choose the best route from your house to the meeting. Highways, rivers, and cities appear on many maps. A map cannot show every place. A small town may be missing. A small road may be missing too. New Haven, Connecticut, produced the earliest phone book, in 1878.

8The book was one sheet of cardboard. Fifty names were on it, people and businesses together. Those were all the telephone owners in New Haven at the time. Growth was fast. Hundreds of pages followed. Millions of numbers were listed. Printing still happens. Delivery to homes and to businesses still happens each year. With the facts gathered, you start the report. Neat writing matters, so the teacher can read it. A special folder is where you place the pages. Dirt and wrinkles are what you want to avoid before you hand the work in. Free time comes at last. The television guide might lead you to a show you watch for fun. The phone book might help you call a friend. A nearby video store might be where you rent a movie.

9Renting a movie at a shop, or buying one there, used to draw crowds. Closure came later for a large number of those shops. Getting a movie grew easier through online services. Finding facts is quicker now, and easier, because of modern technology. Those facts once lived only in books or in other printed resources. The 1990s brought wider Internet use, and research for school changed a great deal. Other parts of life changed too. Getting in touch with people changed. Entertainment changed. Which tools do you use for schoolwork? Which tools do you use to reach people? Which tools do you use when you plan free time? The world has been changed by technology in many ways. Something new arrives every day. Change does not stop. A few years from now, today's tools may seem slow.

10They may seem behind the times. How would you describe the world at that point? Contacting, a verb, means getting in touch with someone, usually to give news or to receive it. A festival, a noun, is a celebration, or a planned series of cultural events. Information, a noun, means facts about a person or a thing. Interview, a verb, means asking questions to learn what someone knows or believes. Research, a verb, means studying a subject, especially to uncover new facts. Resources, a noun, are supplies of things that are useful or valuable. Sources, a noun, are people, books, documents, or other items that can support a claim. Technology, a noun, means tools and machines built with scientific knowledge. Topics, a noun, are the subjects people speak or write about.

51. Maria Tallchief, American Ballet Star

1The National Medal of Arts put Maria Tallchief in a wide group of artists. Music was one of their fields. Visual art was one. Singing was one. Writing was one. Filmmaking was one. September 29, 1999, was the date. Washington, DC, was the place. An award ceremony was the event. The award was the National Medal of Arts. No artist honor from a United States president ranks higher. She was one person honored that year. She stood tall and elegant as a dancer. Fifty years earlier, she had swept through the world of ballet. Ballet is dance performed to music, using exact formal steps. No prima ballerina in American ballet had come before her. The path to that title starts on a reservation in Oklahoma. Native peoples were made to live on such land by the United States government. Quiet and shy describes Betty Marie as a child. Her full name was Elizabeth Marie Tall Chief. Betty Marie was the nickname. She was Osage, a name that sounds like OH-sayj.

2Birth came on January 24, 1925. The place was the Osage Reservation in Fairfax, Oklahoma. Ki He Kah Stah Tsa was the family name. Alexander, her father, was fully Osage. Oil on Osage land, found while he was young, made many tribal members rich. Grown up, he owned a large share of property in Fairfax. Ruth was Betty Marie's mother. Scotland and Ireland were lands of Ruth's family. Ruth saw a musical gift in Betty Marie. She saw the same gift in the younger sister, Marjorie. Piano lessons started early for both girls. Dance lessons started early too. The year 1933 found Betty Marie at age eight. A move to Los Angeles was the parents' decision. Fairfax, they believed, could not school the girls well enough. A dance school was found even before a house was bought. Ballet was one dance they studied. Tap was another. Acrobatics was another. Spanish dancing was another. A 1930 picture shows both girls with their mother.

3Rodeos and county fairs soon had them dancing. A fake Native dance was sometimes part of the show. Neither girl liked it. It showed stereotypes people held about Native peoples. A stereotype is an overly simple idea about a group. Such ideas spread widely and are often unfair or untrue. In ceremonial Osage dances, men are the active dancers. Women make a circle around the men. Small sidesteps, back and forth, follow the drum. While the girls grew up, ceremonial Native dances were against United States law. Grandma Tall Chief still took them to Osage ceremonies. Those dances went on in secret. The yearly Osage In-Lon-Schka dance still happens. Secrecy is gone. Photos are still not allowed. Madame Bronislava Nijinska taught ballet. Her brother was Vaslav Nijinsky. He stands among the greatest male dancers of the twentieth century. At twelve, Betty Marie dropped piano. Ballet alone would be her study. Her real life as a dancer opened then. She soon studied with Madame Nijinska.

4The name sounds like nuh-JIN-skuh. This was her first important ballet teacher. Study with her ran from 1937 to 1942. Power entered her dancing in those years. Grace entered it too. Grace is ease and elegance in how someone moves or acts. Feeling entered the dancing too. High school ended in 1942. By then she knew ballet was her path. She later explained the choice. Music she loved grew inside her in a way her hands on an instrument could not show. That music ran through her body. As a teenager she saw that famous ballet dancers were Russian. One company was the Ballet Russe de Monte Carlo. The name sounds like ROOSS DU MOHN-tee KAR-loh. A company is a group of artists who work and perform as a team. This Russian company often danced in the United States. New York was its base. Los Angeles saw the company in 1938. She went to every performance. Great ballet dancers were new to her eyes.

5Amazement was what she felt. A 1944 photo catches her ready to dance in The Song of Norway. New York came after graduation. Several companies refused her. This Russian company hired her as a dancer almost at once. She then took the name Maria Tallchief. George Balanchine joined that company two years after she did. Few people who arrange dance steps in the twentieth century rank as high as he does. A choreographer picks the style and the order of steps in a dance. He started from classical ballet, a style that was graceful and flowing. A new American ballet style was his creation. Strength was required. Speed was required. An athletic body was required. His steps came alive through Maria Tallchief. She became his muse, his deepest source of artistic inspiration. Inspiration is a good influence that leads to a creative idea or act. Ballet filled their life together. Marriage came in 1946. Only five years of marriage followed.

6Work together lasted many more years. Practice could run as long as fourteen hours a day. She wrote later about dancing Firebird. The hall, she said, sounded like a stadium after a score. Balanchine helped found the New York City Ballet in 1948. She joined. A strong following was missing at the start. Firebird in 1949 changed everything. Fame came from that role. She became the company's first prima ballerina. Thirteen years was how long she held the title. The end year was 1965. She once said a wish to move and dance is natural for American Indian people. Happiness, she said, is shown in dance. Sorrow is shown in dance too. She said this is simply how her people are. The words came in 2006. The event was a tribute at the Producers Guild of America's Celebration of Diversity awards. Some of Balanchine's most beautiful roles were made for her. Swan Lake held some of those roles. Another role was the Sugar Plum Fairy.

7That part is in The Nutcracker. Nineteen countries saw her dance with the New York City Ballet. Television saw her dance. The musical film Million Dollar Mermaid saw her dance in 1952. The American Ballet Theatre used her as a guest. A photo shows her with a partner in Balanchine's Swan Lake. Praise followed her wherever she danced. Audiences saw strength. They saw grace. They wanted more performances. Lightness marked her feet. Exactness marked her movements. Her dancing felt grand and also free. That mix set her apart from other ballerinas. Intelligence earned respect. Honesty earned respect. She argued against stereotypes of Native people. She backed a group called America for Indian Opportunity. The Swan Princess in Swan Lake was another role she danced. A 1965 talk with the Des Moines Register covered how she saw steps. A ballerina, she said, receives steps and then makes them her own. The same role gets something different from each dancer. Great individualism was her American belief.

8That belief matched her upbringing. Hard work had made her tired by 1965. She was forty-one. Marriage to Henry Paschen had come nine years earlier. A daughter had been born to them. Dancing stopped. Chicago, Illinois, became her home so she could be with them. In 1975 she became creative director. Her company then was the Chicago Lyric Opera Ballet. She founded the Chicago City Ballet in 1980. Chicago, she felt, needed a leading ballet company. Creative director remained her role until 1987. Later success in ballet came to many of her students. A photo shows her dancing in Chicago with her only child, Elise Paschen. Time also went to preserving Balanchine's work. Preserving means caring for something and saving it for later. His death came in 1983. She insisted his ballets be danced in the way he had set them. Parts of those ballets were recorded by her and by other Balanchine ballerinas. Students could keep learning from the recordings. In 1996 she was named to the National Women's Hall of Fame.

9The honor is for women whose artistic gifts improved American society. Death came in Chicago on April 11, 2013. She was eighty-eight. Praise for her life and work came from around the world. A life list marks the years. Birth on January 24 sits in 1925. The Los Angeles move with her family sits in 1933. Lessons with Madame Nijinska begin in 1937. High school ends in 1942. The move to New York City sits in 1942. Joining the Ballet Russe de Monte Carlo sits in 1942. George Balanchine joins that company in 1944. She joins the New York City Ballet in 1948. The list also marks 1948 as the year she became its first prima ballerina. Firebird sits in 1949. Marriage to Henry Paschen sits in 1956. Elise is born in 1959. Retirement from performing sits in 1965, at age forty-one. The move to Chicago sits in 1965. Founding the Chicago City Ballet sits in 1980.

10Death in April sits in 2013. An American treasure is what Maria Tallchief was. Her home country was the United States. She was that country's first prima ballerina. A new style of ballet took shape with her help. American ballet had not had a Native star before her. People of every background found a wider welcome in that art because of her. Grace moved ballet forward. Talent moved it forward. Achievements moved it forward. An achievement is a success earned by effort. Jacques d'Amboise, another dancer, spoke about her. His name sounds like ZHAHK dahm-BWAHZ. One word, he said, fit her. Grand was the word. He said she was fully that. A 1964 photo shows the two of them dancing at the White House. President Clinton handed her the National Medal of Arts in 1999. A European art, he said, became America's own through her work. He said a Native American woman was a fitting person to do that.

52. Old Forests Full of Wonder

1Different parts of the world hold four old-growth forests. Japan holds Shirakami-Sanchi. The United States holds Great Smoky Mountains National Park. The United States also holds Sequoia and Kings Canyon National Parks. Russia holds the Western Caucasus. What makes a forest old is the question this book asks. The United States is visited. Japan is visited. Russia is visited. A close comes last. Picture a dark, lovely forest. Overhead, trunks wider than cars tower up. Little light is all the smaller plants can take in. The ground is covered by pine needles. Other dead leaves cover it too. Wonder is what an old-growth forest holds, like a scene from a fairy tale. Mystery is what it holds too. That scene is the look of some old-growth forests. A very different look belongs to others.
