2One type of tree is all some hold. Many types are what others hold. The sky is what some trees seem tall enough to reach. A house would stand taller than the trees in other forests. One shared definition of an old-growth forest is something scientists do not have, because of these differences. Shared traits have still been found. At least 150 years is the age many scientists say an old-growth forest must have. Many different ages of trees may stand inside it. Large living trees are included. Large trees that are dead or dying are included. Snags is the name for standing dead trees. Food and shelter for different animals come from fallen trees. Plants receive that help too. Fungi receive it too.

3Dizzy is how a look upward in an old-growth forest can leave you. Levels are how a forest is built. Tall trees such as pines, maples, and oaks fill the canopy. Small trees and bushes fill the understory. Grasses, mosses, ferns, and logs fill the forest floor. More than one layer is what the canopy should have. Holes in the canopy are opened by fallen trees. Sunlight in those holes lets young trees grow. Some of the most beautiful places on Earth are old-growth forests. Some of the most interesting places are too. A lot can be learned by studying them and by taking care of them. California is where Sequoia and Kings Canyon National Parks lie. More than 1,353 square miles is their combined cover.

4That equals 3,504 square kilometers. More than 300 square miles of old-growth forest are held there. That equals 777 square kilometers. Coniferous trees make up most of that forest. California alone is where the giant sequoias among them grow. Among the oldest trees on Earth is where these huge ones stand. More than 3,000 years is a life they can reach. The largest tree in the world is a giant sequoia called the General Sherman Tree. Under the thick canopy of the parks' old-growth forest lives the Pacific fisher. Birds are hunted by it. Small mammals are hunted by it. Porcupine is the meal it likes best. Old-growth forests are the only habitat it has, so some states have begun work to keep it safe.

5California is where both giant redwoods and giant sequoias grow. The same color of bark is what they share. Huge is true of both, yet they differ. No trees grow taller than giant redwoods. Up to 378 feet, or 115 meters, is a height they can reach. Largest, rather than tallest, is the word for giant sequoias. Up to 40 feet across, or 12 meters, is a width a sequoia can reach. Almost twice a redwood's width is that measure. Fog is the best growing weather for redwoods. Dry heat is what sequoias need before their cones open. Sea level is where redwoods grow. A height of 5,000 to 7,000 feet is where sequoias grow. That equals 1,524 to 2,134 meters. Colorful autumn fills Great Smoky Mountains National Park.

6North Carolina and Tennessee, across the country, are where this park takes up more than 800 square miles. That equals 2,072 square kilometers. Forest is almost the whole park. About a quarter of the park is old-growth forest. Deciduous trees make up most of that old-growth forest. Home for the endangered Carolina northern flying squirrel is here. Real flight is not what these small mammals do. Skin between the front paws and the back paws is what they glide on. Fungi are most of what they eat. Fruits and nuts may be eaten too. These animals would be gone if the old-growth forest were gone. Towering trees are not in every old-growth forest. Wild, icy winds blow in some harsh places. Pines grow short and twisted there.

7Spruces do too. Other coniferous trees do too. Krummholz is the name for these trees. Crooked wood is what that German word means. About 6 feet, or 1.8 meters, is all the height they reach. Size should not fool you. One thousand years is the age of some of them. Toward the sun, a Japanese beech tree stretches in Shirakami-Sanchi, Japan. Northern Japan is where Shirakami-Sanchi lies. East Asia has few old-growth forests left, and this is one. Beech trees make up most of it. Just 65 square miles of the mountains is the cover. That equals 170 square kilometers. Most of northern Japan was once covered by forests like this. Cutting by people has removed many of them. Trails are absent from Shirakami-Sanchi. Buildings are absent.

8Protection covers the animals in the area. This forest is where the Japanese serow lives. It is an animal like a goat. Japan has only two other small places where it lives, besides this forest. Insect types in the forest number more than 2,100. Bird types number 87. The black woodpecker is one of those birds. Endangered in Japan is its status. Other plants can be nursed along by a fallen fir in this old-growth forest in Russia's Caucasus. About 1,062 square miles is the stretch of Russia's Western Caucasus. That equals 2,750 square kilometers. The Black Sea is one end, and the Caucasus Mountains are the other. Many different plants live in the area. Many different animals live there. Most of the forests are coniferous trees.

9Home for different kinds of endangered animals is in these forests. A few endangered snow leopards may live here, some scientists think. Signs of the large cats are sometimes found. Paw prints are found. Droppings are found. People have also returned the endangered wisent to this forest. A European bison is what a wisent is. Very complex ecosystems are common in old-growth forests. Hundreds of years of life may have been supported by them. Thousands of years may have been supported too. Many of these places are protected by governments. Many are protected by national parks. Other groups protect them too. Other old-growth forests face danger. Some of those forests are small. Some are large. Delicate environments can be damaged by human actions. They can be destroyed by those actions.

10Forever is how long the beauty may be lost once the forests are gone. Scientific information they hold may be lost forever too. Logging is blocked by laws in some old-growth forests. It is not blocked in others. How much protection is enough is a debate people still hold. Treetops forming a dense layer of leaves are the canopy. Coniferous describes trees and shrubs that stay green and grow cones. Needles or scales are the leaf shapes. Plants whose leaves drop in the fall and grow again in the spring are what deciduous describes. Living things in a community, together with their habitat, are ecosystems. In danger of dying out completely is what endangered means. Very old forests are what old-growth describes.

53. Open Lands Where Grasses Grow

1Topics in this book come in a set order. Grass with no end in sight is the first topic. The biome called grassland is the second. Hot grasslands are the third. Cooler grasslands are the fourth. People who use grasslands are the fifth. A simple grass plant is the sixth. Meanings of key words are the last. Standing in a grassland can make a person feel small. That open land can also leave a person feeling unsafe. Wind there can seem ready to pick someone up. Miles might go by as the wind carried that person. Shock was the reaction of Meriwether Lewis and William Clark when they reached the West. Virginia had been home to both men. Forest scenes were what they expected outdoors. The Great Plains did not match those forest scenes. Grass kept running in all directions. Roughly a third of the United States sits inside this plains region. The region also stretches far into Canada. Few grasslands on Earth are larger. More grasslands are found in other places. Flat land is the usual shape. Grass is what grows. Few trees stand there. Its own character belongs to each land of this kind. Many kinds of environments blanket the surface of Earth. A biome is a region plus the plants and animals living in it. A desert is a biome.

2A rainforest is a biome. An ocean is a biome. Tundra is a biome. A grassland is a biome too. Several factors help form the biomes of Earth. Latitude is one factor. It is the distance from the equator. Elevation is another factor. It is the height over sea level. Both factors help set whether a place stays warm or cool. Climate is a building block of a biome. Land is a building block. Water is a building block. Plants and animals are building blocks as well. A grassland is a huge plain that stays open. Grass is the main vegetation. Trees stand scattered on some of these plains. Other plains have no trees at all. Forest fails to take hold for two main causes. Fire is one cause. Rain is the other, and it is often low. That rain is also hard to forecast. Lightning is a frequent cause of fire on these lands. Some plants here need fire. Their seeds will not sprout without it. Plants that burn help the soil become healthy. Grass can last through a fire. Fresh growth comes from the roots. Those roots stay undamaged. Some trees last through fire as well. Some shrubs do too. Their bark can resist the flames. Grassland covers a great share of the land on Earth. Many separate grasslands exist.

3Two main kinds stand out, tropical and temperate. About one fourth of the land surface of Earth is grassland. Close to half of all land without ice is grassland too. Antarctica is the only continent that has no grassland. Most grasslands lie in the zone between desert and forest. Temperature is not equal in every grassland. Soil is not equal. Rainfall is not equal. Those gaps create several varieties. Two main kinds remain. Hot tropical climates hold tropical grasslands. Their belt runs from the Tropic of Cancer to the Tropic of Capricorn. Grass is the chief plant. Other short plants that do not make wood grow there too. Shrubs appear in places. Trees appear in places too. Cool temperate climates hold temperate grasslands. They lie beyond the Tropic of Cancer. They also lie beyond the Tropic of Capricorn. Grass and plants much like grass are by far the usual cover. A tree or a shrub usually grows only beside a river. Other water that stays all year can support them too. Rainfall sets how tall the grass grows. Drier land grows shorter grass. Wetter land grows taller grass. In some spots that grass reaches 4 meters, which is 13 feet. Huge herds of animals that graze live on some grasslands. A water hole is a dangerous gathering place. Predators can reach the herds more easily there.

4Tropical grasslands fill large parts of Africa, of India, of Australia, and of South America. Africa holds by far the biggest share. Almost half of Africa is grassland. Savanna is the name for tropical grassland in Africa. Some other areas use that name too. The climate stays warm or hot through the whole year. A year of rain averages 51 to 127 centimeters, which is 20 to 50 inches. Thunderstorms are frequent in summer. Plants use the water and grow quickly. The land turns green. Rivers fill up. Water holes fill up too. Animals breed in the summer season. Food is plentiful then. Water is plentiful then. Winter is the usual dry season. Rivers often dry out. Water holes often dry out too. The vegetation turns brown. Fires break out in large numbers. Trees of tropical grasslands have important adaptations for a long dry season. Some keep a store of water inside a thick trunk. Others grow a long taproot. That root reaches down to water that is always present. Thick bark lets some trees survive a fire. Rainfall totals differ widely among tropical grasslands. Those totals control how many trees can grow. Nearly 40 percent of a wet grassland may be trees. A dry grassland may have none. Soil quality also shapes the vegetation. Heavy rain tends to wash nutrients out.

5Only a thin humus layer is left. Humus is the top soil layer. It is made of organic material that is decomposing. Most of the nutrients come from that layer. Little humus leaves the soil generally poor. A baobab keeps water in a thick trunk. Kans grass can grow as tall as 3 meters, which is 10 feet. Tropical grasslands shelter many kinds of animals. Each region has a mix unlike the others. Bats are common in Australia. Rodents are common there. Kangaroos are common there. Big cats abound in Africa. Hoofed mammals abound there too. The capybara lives on grasslands in South America. It is the largest rodent on Earth. Monkeys live there. Marsh deer live there. Giant anacondas live there. Leopards live on grasslands in India. Rhinos live there. Crocodiles live there. Birds live on all of these grasslands. Insects number in the billions. Many animals migrate in the dry season. They travel in search of a meal. Across the savanna in Africa, each hoofed species is adapted to a slightly different meal. One may eat a different kind of plant. Another may eat a different part of a plant. They can share the land. They do not need to compete for one food. The Serengeti Plain in Tanzania may be the tropical grassland people know best.

6Elephants live there. Giraffes live there. Lions live there. Cheetahs live there. Hyenas live there. Ostriches live there. Many other animals live there too. Poaching means hunting that breaks the law. Serengeti wildlife faces this hunting as a major threat. A national park covers a large part of the area. The black rhinoceros is endangered. The African wild dog is endangered. Elephants are in serious trouble. Cheetahs are in serious trouble too. Wildflowers can burst across a temperate grassland. One such grassland is in Turkey. Trees are few on temperate grasslands. Names for these cooler lands change from region to region. In North America the names are prairies. Plains is another name there. In Europe the name is steppes. In Asia the name is steppes too. In Southern Africa the name is veldts. In South America the name is pampas. Climate shifts with location. Closer to the tropics, summer is hot and winter is mild. Farther north, or farther south, summer is warm. Winter is cold and full of wind. Snow is common. Ice is common. Rainfall shifts as well. Close to a desert, a year may bring only about 25 centimeters of rain, which is 10 inches. Close to a forest, a year may bring 89 centimeters, which is 35 inches. Most rain falls in spring. Early summer is rainy too.

7Late summer dries the soil. The grasses turn yellow. Roots on these grasses are long. The roots are also dense. They hold water. They help keep soil from washing away. The gap between summer and winter is often much wider than in a tropical grassland. Summer heat can climb above 38 degrees C, which is 100 degrees F. Winter cold can fall to minus 40 degrees C, which is minus 40 degrees F. Rainfall is smaller than in tropical grasslands. Lighter rain washes away fewer nutrients from the soil. The nutrients build up. Humus forms a thick layer. Richness is the usual state of this soil. Plants here must cope with less rain than plants in tropical grasslands. Animals must cope with colder winters too. Many plants grow only during warmer months. Winter offers less food. Animals are less diverse for that reason. A further cause of lower diversity is that little temperate grassland remains wild. Farms have taken most of them. Ranches have taken most of them too. Smaller animals have survived in greater numbers than larger ones. The larger animals have also been hit hard by hunting. Rabbits are common in North America. Foxes are common. Coyotes are common. Snakes are common. Small rodents are common. Owls live on these grasslands. Hawks live there. Birds that nest on the ground live there.

8Rodents live on the steppes of Europe and Asia. Hares live there. Wolves live there. Lynxes live there. Foxes live there too. Foxes live on the pampas of South America. Skunks live there. Bush dogs live there. Many bird species live there. Some larger animals of these regions live inside protected areas. Wild leopards remain on parts of the veldt in South Africa. Wild zebras remain on that veldt too. Other veldt animals include hyenas and badgers. Weasels live there. Crocodiles live there. Pythons live there. Most tallgrass prairie in North America has been changed into farmland. Ranchland covers much of it too. Today, most tallgrass prairie stands in eastern Kansas. The United States protects a small share of that Kansas region as a national preserve. For thousands of years, balance held on the tallgrass prairie. Rainfall, fire, and grazing made that balance. Bison are the largest grazing animals. Hunters in the 1800s almost drove them to extinction. Bison were brought back onto the preserve in 2009. Coyotes live there. Badgers live there. Lizards live there. Gopher snakes live there. Greater prairie chickens live there. Monarch butterflies live there. Native peoples thrived on grasslands for thousands of years. Plains Native Americans were among them. Australian Aboriginals were among them. The Serengeti region is home to the Hadza people, who were among them.

9European settlers arrived later, and the change to those old ways of living was significant. Farming now takes a large share of the land. Ranching uses much of it too. Grasslands suit the growing of crops. They also suit the grazing of livestock. Temperate grasslands are especially good for this work because the soils are rich. Wheat and other cereal crops cover an estimated 20 percent of grasslands worldwide. Over 40 percent are used to graze livestock. Human life depends on grasslands. The amount of food from them is too large. Large numbers of residents earn their livelihoods from this work. On some of these lands, native people keep a way of life close to the old one. A traditional horseman in Mongolia watches over his horses. A wide sky is part of the beauty of a grassland. At a first look, a grassland may not seem very interesting. Set beside some other biomes, flat land covered in grass shows little variety. Grasslands are special for other reasons. They are fascinating. Consider the grasses. They are humble plants. Few people would call them special. Vast stretches of ground wear this plant. Wind makes them move in a gentle way. They look like a sea that is alive. They survive fire. They survive drought. They survive intense heat in summer. They survive cold below freezing.

10An amazing range of animals feeds on grasses. The animals range from insects to bison. Grasses also put nutrients into the soil. This biome has become the main source of grain for the world. The wonder is simple. Plants this humble matter deeply to the balance of life on Earth. Adaptations, a noun, means a change in a living thing, or in a species, that helps survival in its home. Cope, a verb, means to handle a difficulty and get past it. Decomposing, an adjective, means breaking into small pieces, or rotting. Diverse, an adjective, means unlike one another, or made up of unlike parts. Environments, a noun, means the conditions around living things in a given place, including weather, air, water, soil, plants, animals, and the shape of the land. Grasslands, a noun, means open country where grass is the chief plant cover. Humble, an adjective, means plain and modest. Livelihoods, a noun, means the work people do in order to meet their needs. Nutrients, a noun, means materials a living thing must have so it can stay alive, stay healthy, and grow. Temperate, an adjective, describes a climate or a region whose temperatures stay mild. Tropical, an adjective, describes the hot, humid belt around the equator. Vegetation, a noun, means the plants found in one area.

54. Owls That Hunt Without a Sound

1A picture shows boreal owlets. The parts of this book follow in order. First comes flying in the moonlight. Then come eyes made for the night. Then come ears that work even better. Then come silent hunters. Then comes the question of where owls live. Then comes the idea that owls are out there. A glossary comes last. A picture shows a tawny owl. It is a quiet, moonlit night. You walk along a dirt path. The path is on the edge of town. Suddenly, a shadow crosses your path. You hear no sound. You look up. A dark shape glides over you. It disappears into the nearby trees. The shape is an owl. Owl species around the world number more than two hundred. They come in many sizes. They live on every continent except Antarctica. Many adaptations help owls survive. Those same adaptations make owls seem mysterious. Night is when most owls go hunting. Because of that, they do not have to compete with daytime hunters.

2Eagles are daytime hunters. Hawks are daytime hunters. Falcons are daytime hunters. Owl eyes, like the eyes of most birds, stay fixed in their sockets. A moving object can be tracked only if the owl turns its head. Owls can rotate their heads almost all the way around. The turn is 280 degrees. An upside-down turn of the head is also possible. A picture shows a northern hawk owl. Another picture shows a burrowing owl. A picture compares an owl's pupils. The pupils are small in bright light. They are large in low light. Big eyes are another adaptation an owl has. Total darkness is still too dark for these birds to see. The pupil is the black circle in the center of the eye. That pupil can grow large. A large pupil lets in more light. In dim light, those wide pupils give owls a sharper view than most animals have.

3When light is low, tawny owls see far better than humans. They see as much as one hundred times better. Hairlike feathers grow on owls' faces. Those feathers help owls react to things they touch. On some owls, these feathers grow on the feet as well. Night vision is good, but it has a limit. Owls cannot see things close to their faces. Hairlike feathers work like feelers. They help owls react to a touch. They work sort of like a cat's whiskers. A note explains pellets. Owls cannot chew their food. They cannot grind it either. Small prey is often swallowed whole. The unwanted parts of those animals cannot be digested. Teeth, claws, and small bones are left over. Bits of fur or feathers stick those parts together. The mass ends up in the gizzard. The gizzard is a part of the stomach. Strong muscles there roll the parts into small balls.

4The balls are called pellets. A few hours after an owl eats, it coughs up a pellet. Scientists look through the pellets. They learn what owls eat. Pictures show an owl pellet and an owl skull. Owls have very large ears. Feathers hide the ears. Thin skin covers the ear openings on some owls. A diagram marks a lower ear and an upper ear. To hunt, most owls rely on their hearing. Most owls have huge ears. One ear sits higher than the other. The higher ear hears sounds from above. The lower ear hears sounds from below. Sounds are funneled toward the ears by the flat, round face. Owls can move the feathers around their faces. That movement can send more sound to one ear. It can send more sound to the other ear instead. Prey is easier to find when they do this. A picture shows a barn owl.

5Owls often crush their prey with their strong feet. Sharp talons help them do it. Some owls grip with ten times a human's strength. Owl hearing is very good. An owl can hear bugs in grass. An owl can hear mice in snow. No other group of birds hears better than owls do. A great gray owl can hear a mouse hidden under snow. The snow can be 18 inches (46 cm) deep. Once the owl has located the prey, it can dive under the snow. The dive is how it grabs a meal. A barn owl can hear a mouse moving 90 feet (27 m) away. It can catch a mouse in complete darkness. No tested animal hears better than a barn owl. Excellent hearing also belongs to many of the animals owls hunt. Surprise is required if the owl is going to get a meal. Soft feathers cover an owl's body. Those feathers allow the owl to fly silently.

6A great horned owl will sit high on a tree branch. It watches until the time is right to attack. It is a skillful hunter. It feeds on many kinds of animals. It even feeds on skunks. It even feeds on snakes. In Florida, one great horned owl tackled an indigo snake. The snake was 6 feet (2 m) long. The owl won. The great horned owl also eats birds. Those birds include eight other owl species. Small owls become food for many large owls. Hunting is something great horned owls do extremely well. Another bird can be caught by them while it is flying. A picture shows an elf owl. Another picture shows a snowy owl. Most owls hunt only at night. Snowy owls are different. They hunt mostly during the day. Summer nights in the Arctic are very short. That short night is the reason. The tiny elf owl hunts during the day as well.

7It feeds mostly on insects. The main diet for most owls is small mammals. An owl may eat lots of them. Voles eaten in one year by a hungry great gray owl may number 1,400. Mice eaten by one barn owl over a lifetime may number 11,000. A barn owl lives up to its name. It raises its young in a barn. Some owls can live in many different habitats. The great horned owl is one of them. It lives in swamps. It lives in forests. It lives on prairies. It lives in mountains. It lives in deserts. Barn owls live under bridges. They live in caves. They live in empty houses. They live in church towers. They live in barns, of course. A note calls one snake an owl's housekeeper. Screech owls in Texas hunt snakes. They bring one kind of snake back to the nest alive.

8That snake is the blindsnake. They let it go inside the nest. Maggots feed on the dead animals stored there. The blindsnake eats those maggots. The snake becomes a housekeeper. Burrowing owls can scare away other animals. They do it by making a sound like a rattlesnake. A special habitat is required by some owls. Burrowing owls live mostly on the prairie. They nest underground. They use old burrows. Mammals dug those burrows. When open spaces are hard to find, these owls move. They move to cemeteries. They move to golf courses. They even move near airport runways. Winter does not send spotted owls flying south. Old-growth forests are the only places where spotted owls can survive. By 1994, logging had destroyed most of their former habitat. The share destroyed was 80 percent. To protect the spotted owl, loggers cut down fewer trees. Barred owls have since taken over spotted owl habitat. Today, barred owls outnumber spotted owls.

9That is true in the northwestern United States. A note explains owl sounds. Owls are not always silent. They do not all hoot. They use different sounds to call a mate. They use sounds to warn of danger. They use sounds to claim an area. Each species has its own calls. Those calls set it apart from other owls. A great horned owl can bark like a dog. It can meow like a cat. Barn owls do not hoot. They scream. Their cry is strange. Their appearance is ghostly. Some people once thought of barn owls as bad luck. A picture shows photographers taking pictures of a great gray owl. The great gray owl is the largest species of owl in the world. It is rare to see an owl. Owls are still out there. Dawn in the country is a good time to try to spot one. You can also try dusk.

10Listen for their calls. Stay late enough and someone may call you an owl as well. They would mean a night owl. A glossary gives word meanings. Adaptations is a noun. Adaptations are changes that allow an animal to survive better. The survival is in its environment. Attack is a verb. It means to act harmfully toward something. Habitats is a noun. A habitat is the natural environment of a plant or animal. Mysterious is an adjective. It means odd. It means not easy to understand. Old-growth is an adjective. It relates to very old forests. Prey is a noun. Prey is an animal that is hunted and eaten by another animal. Rotate is a verb. It means to turn on a center. It means to revolve. Species is a noun. A species is a group of living things that are similar. They can produce young. Survive is a verb. It means to stay alive.

55. Pharaoh Ramses II of Egypt

1Artwork shows Ramses II hunting from his chariot. Egypt was the birthplace of Ramses II, thousands of years ago. The year was 1303 BC. His father was Seti I. His mother was Mut-Tuy. Her name is said MOOT TOO-ee. They probably did not expect this son to become king. They were not even part of the king's family. Fame still placed Ramses among the best-known leaders of that age. That age in Egypt is called the New Kingdom. Ancient Egypt's most important pharaohs include him. The weight of a huge Ramses II statue is 6.58 mt. That weight is 7.25 t. The statue is now in the British Museum. Several years before Ramses, the king was Horemheb. Horemheb liked Ramses's grandfather. He made that grandfather next in line for the throne. When Horemheb died, the grandfather became king. The grandfather died a few years later. Then Ramses's father was king.

2Ramses stood next in line. A map shows Egypt while Ramses II ruled, from 1279 to 1213 BC. It includes the Orontes River. It includes Kadesh. It includes the Mediterranean Sea. It includes the Nile River Delta. It includes Pi-Ramesse. It includes the Nile River. It includes Abydos. It includes Thebes. It includes the Ramesseum. It includes Karnak. It includes the Red Sea. It includes the Egyptian Empire. It includes Abu Simbel. It includes the Hittite Empire. It includes Nubia. Carvings show crown prince Ramses beside his father. One carving shows them roping a bull. A gift for the god Amun appears in another carving. Ramses II learned how to rule by working with his father. His father was the king. One surviving artwork shows Ramses helping with a religious duty. Another shows father and son roping a bull. Ramses needed strength to lead an army into battle.

3Roping bulls helped him get ready for a king's duties. Riding wild horses helped too. He became an army captain while he was very young. Seti I gave him titles. Seti I also gave him tasks as a leader. Those tasks prepared him to rule. By his teenage years, people treated Ramses as an adult. Experience was the reason he traveled beside his father. He had his own home. The first marriage happened around his fifteenth year. Rulers of that time often had more than one wife. Ramses followed that custom. Many historians think he married eight times or more. Nefertari was his first wife. She stayed his favorite. Nefertari received an elaborate building as his dedication. Huge statues decorate it. The statues show Nefertari. They show Ramses. They show their children. Workers cut the building from solid sandstone. An old wall scene shows Ramses in battle with his two sons.

4During the rule of Seti I, Ramses led the army. People came to know him as a brave soldier. His two oldest sons appear with him in stone carvings of a battle. Historians think that battle was in Nubia. At that time, Nubia was a country south of Egypt. Nubia had plenty of gold. It had other riches too. Kings of Egypt often fought the kings of Nubia. They fought over who would control Nubia. Seti I died when Ramses was about twenty-five. Ramses then became king. Luxor, Egypt, is the site of a giant Ramses II statue. Before Seti I died, he had started a huge hall. The hall was at the temple at Karnak. It held 134 tall columns. Artists decorated the walls in a special way. They cut away the backgrounds. They left raised pictures behind. Ramses soon told workers to carve in a different way.

5They cut the images straight into the stone. The new way made carving easier. It also made carving faster. He may have wanted the change so the work would speed up. He doubled the speed of building. No other king built as many temples as he did. The Great Hypostyle Hall has columns that make visitors look tiny. That hall is at Karnak. It covers 5,000 square meters. That area is 50,000 square feet. Ramses seemed eager to hurry his father's building program. Abu Simbel received two temples from him. One temple was for himself. Nefertari's temple was the second one, and it was smaller. The Ramesseum is a temple he built in Thebes. A new city for the capital was another project of his. The city put him closer to his long-time enemies. Those enemies were the Hittites. They lived in the land now called Syria.

6The Ramesseum was made to honor Ramses II. It was also made so people would remember him forever. His chariot is the setting in this artwork. About 2,500 chariots belonged to the Hittites by themselves in that battle. A win over the Hittites came not long after Ramses II took the throne. He took back lands they had seized. Later, the Hittites captured Kadesh. Syria's trade depended on Kadesh, an important city. About twenty thousand soldiers followed Ramses to that place. He also led two thousand chariots. Few events of his rule are as famous as that fight. It still holds the record as the world's largest chariot battle. Ramses said he faced the enemy alone. Reports called the battle a clear Egyptian victory. Scholars have read the Hittite records. A draw is how they read the result of the battle. Many carvings show the battle of Kadesh.

7Other works show it too. The details are not the same in the Egyptian account. They are not the same in the Hittite account. Many miles still seemed to separate him from the Hittite army, which he placed to the north. He split his forces so he could take Kadesh. One group went ahead as scouts. A full day separated that second group from the men ahead. A third group circled wide. That group would attack from the far side. Then Ramses learned the Hittites were much closer. He sent for help. The Hittite army attacked his men. His own account says the enemy was held back by him alone. He said the rest of his army ran. Other records tell a different story. They say part of his army arrived just in time. That arrival saved the king. Both sides lost many people. The two kings stopped the fight.

8Ramses took his army home. Wall carvings of the battle were ordered by him through the following year. Every carving showed him as a hero. War nearly returned between the two nations after fifteen years. This time Ramses chose not to fight. A new war would have been far to the north. Peace was the agreement he reached with the Hittite king. Two copies of the peace treaty still exist. One copy is in Egyptian hieroglyphs. The other copy is in the Hittite language. Giant sculptures of Ramses II stand at Abu Simbel. One stands on each side of the temple's main entrance. Natural causes were the reason for the death of Ramses II. He was ninety-one. His thirteenth son became king. Even so, the dynasty was nearly finished. One hundred fifty years after his death, the New Kingdom ended. Today people call him Ramses the Great.

9His art helps us know the people of his time. His buildings help us know them. His records help us know them. A timeline of Ramses II runs from 1310 BC to 1210 BC. Ramses II's birth year was 1303 BC. In 1298 BC, Ramses I became pharaoh. In 1291 BC, Seti I became pharaoh. In 1289 BC, Ramses II married his first two wives. In 1281 BC, he put down a revolt in Nubia. He took his two oldest sons into that battle. In 1279 BC, Ramses II became pharaoh. Work on the Ramesseum started around then. In 1274 BC, the battle at Kadesh took place. In 1264 BC, work on Abu Simbel began around then. In 1259 BC, the Ramesseum was completed. In 1258 BC, he made a peace treaty with the Hittites. In 1252 BC, Nefertari died. In 1244 BC, Isetnofret died. She was his second wife.

10Around then he married his first Hittite princess. Work at Abu Simbel was completed around then too. In 1231 BC, he married a second Hittite princess. In 1213 BC, Ramses II died at age ninety-one. Something ancient comes out of the distant past. A carving is an object of wood, stone, or clay. Figures or designs are cut into it. Control can mean power over a situation. It can mean power to limit what someone does. It can mean power to manage an object. Inside a culture, a custom is the old, usual way to do something. A duty is a job, a responsibility, or an obligation. A dynasty is a line of rulers from one family in a country. A historian is an expert on history. A pharaoh was a ruler in ancient Egypt. A temple is a building used as a place of worship.

56. Rome's Great Commander

1Ancient Rome holds a high place among world civilizations. Languages in Europe still show a link to that city. Cultures in Europe show a link to it too. So do many governments there. Julius Caesar was among its most famous leaders. Skill as a war commander made him famous. He was a very clever politician. Success followed him in politics. Speaking in public was one of his strengths. The people of Rome loved him. His soldiers loved him as well. War brought out a tough side of him. Politics brought out that same tough side. Fear of Caesar spread among his enemies. They believed a king's title was what he wanted. The years of his birth were years of great trouble in Rome. Help for citizens with little power was one of his aims. Help for citizens with little wealth was another aim. A better Rome was the change he wanted. The story of ancient Rome starts in today's Italy. The growing lasted past a full thousand years. Rome became one of the world's mighty empires. Stable years were rare in that long stretch. Peaceful years were rare too.

2Neighbors often met Rome in war. People who lived far away met Rome in war too. At times, Romans even fought other Romans. Early Rome was a city-state under kings. One large city sat at the center of a city-state. Countryside around that city belonged to it as well. Old stories tell of six kings who ruled well, one after another. A seventh king ruled with cruelty. In 509 bce the people rose up. They forced the king out of power. A map of those years is labeled Roman Republic, 200 to 100 bce. The Atlantic Ocean appears on that map. Gaul appears there too, in the land now called France. Macedonia appears on the map. The Black Sea appears on it. The city of Rome is marked. Spain is marked. Asia is marked, in the land now called Turkey. Greece is marked. The Mediterranean Sea is marked. Africa is marked. One area shows the Republic in 200 bce. A wider area shows the Republic in 100 bce. In the years before Caesar's birth, the Republic was still growing. Romans chose to live without kings.

3That choice started the Roman Republic. Every year, voters picked two men called consuls. Running the government was the consuls' job. Rich men in a group called the Senate gave the consuls help. Great political power sat with those men. The name for them was senators. Growth of the Republic came quickly. Lands across the Mediterranean Sea fell under Roman control. North Africa was one place those lands lay. Each conquered region was made into a province. Rome's plan was to govern the new lands. An army was set up to keep control. Wealthy citizens were picked to rule each province. Food from the provinces was sold. Enslaved people from the provinces were sold too. Those sales added to the rulers' wealth. Common citizens back in the city had a hard life. Money was scarce for many of them. The new provinces brought them no benefit. Dishonesty had spread through the Senate. The distance between rich and poor grew too wide. Civil war then broke out. In a civil war, two groups inside one country fight. Control of that country is what they fight for.

4July of 100 bce was the month of Julius Caesar's birth. That birth sits more than two thousand years in the past. His family was old. Wealth was not something the family had much of. Importance in public life was limited for them too. A strong education came from his parents. His father died when Caesar was sixteen. At that age, Caesar became the head of the family. The same year, he married Cornelia. Her family backed the common citizens of Rome. Control of the city sat with a rich man called Sulla. Sulla ordered Caesar to leave Cornelia. Caesar refused. The refusal put him in real danger. It also showed he would not obey Sulla. Joining the army was his way to get clear of Sulla. Service took him to provinces far from the city. He rose to the rank of officer. Fair treatment won the admiration of his soldiers. Sulla's death came in 78 bce. Caesar then felt safe enough to come back. Politics in the city drew him in again. He worked beside a powerful man named Pompey.

5Cornelia later died. Caesar's next wife was Pompeia, Pompey's daughter. Once settled, he believed he saw a way to repair the troubled government. He promised land to the people. He promised help in finding jobs. Love from the people followed those promises. A picture from this part of his life shows him leading troops. The troops are crossing a river. The river crossing leads into Italy. Voters made Caesar consul when he was forty. The consul post lasted one year. After that year, he became governor of Gaul. Rome counted Gaul among its major provinces. Its land is today's northern Italy and today's France. Powerful Roman legions stood under his command. A legion was an army. Loyalty grew because he was smart. Loyalty grew because he was brave. He insisted on fighting next to his own men. Many tribes lived in Gaul then. A tribe was a group of people. Those tribes were brave soldiers. They were skilled soldiers too. Earlier Roman attempts to beat the tribes had failed. Caesar's armies were sometimes smaller than the tribes they faced. The tribes fought fiercely to keep their land.

6Laurel leaves formed a crown on his head in his later years. One tool of war was the battering ram. Soldiers rolled it up to a wall or a building. A shed gave those soldiers cover. They swung the ram on chains. Strong gates broke under the blows. Skill showed in Caesar and in the legions he led. They were also violent. Years of war put every tribe of Gaul under Roman control. Daily life for those people changed for good. Europe's later growth felt the effect. Language in Europe felt it. Government felt it. Education felt it. Ten years was the time Caesar spent in Gaul. Fame at home grew from that success in war. While he was away, political friends worked for him in the city. His tie to Pompey stayed in place. Pompey married Julia, Caesar's daughter. A third man joined them. That man was the wealthy politician Crassus. Together, the three formed a very powerful group. A painting by Lionel Royer shows a northern leader after a battle. That leader is giving up to Caesar. As time passed, Pompey wanted the fame Caesar had.

7Crassus died. Julia died too. After those deaths, Pompey and Caesar turned into bitter enemies. Common Romans stayed on Caesar's side. Rich senators backed Pompey. Some legions in Italy backed Pompey as well. Caesar wanted to return to the city. He was told by the Senate to lay down command of the army that stayed loyal to him. Jean-Léon Gérôme painted him in the role of a commander at war. Fear of murder kept Caesar from coming back alone. He brought his loyal army with him. That army was mighty. He knew the return broke Roman law. He still believed he had to stand against Pompey. A civil war of deadly force then broke out between the two armies. Caesar drove Pompey's troops out of Italy. Defeat of those troops came quickly. Pompey himself was killed. One image shows Pompey. Another painting, called Caesar's Murder, was made by Karl Theodor von Piloty. The Senate welcomed Caesar when he led his army back. Important work filled his time in the city. New laws were passed to help common citizens. Those laws also aimed to give people jobs.

8Great buildings rose under his rule. He even changed the calendar. Love for him spread among the people. That love was absent among many wealthy senators. They feared he would hand too much power to common people. They feared he would hand too much wealth to common people. They also feared a king's crown was his goal. Two years after the return, a group of senators made a plan against him. Those senators murdered him. The calendar change is worth a closer look. Romans had kept time by the Moon's place in the sky. Ten days were missing from that old calendar. Caesar worked with astronomers on a new one. The new calendar followed Earth's path around the Sun. Accuracy improved a great deal. The month of July carries Caesar's name. A coin from 44 bce shows his face. The other side of the coin shows Venus. His family said they came from the goddess of love. His murder set off a civil war even worse than the one before. When that war ended, the Roman Empire began.

9Conquered peoples across the empire took up Roman ways. Language in Europe felt the empire's mark. Culture in Europe felt it too. The world we live in still carries that shape. A map labeled Roman Republic, 45 bce, shows the lands at his death. The Atlantic Ocean is on that map. Gaul, now France, is on it. Macedonia is on it. The Black Sea is on it. Rome is on it. Spain is on it. Asia, now Turkey, is on it. Greece is on it. The Mediterranean Sea is on it. Africa is on it. A record of Caesar's life can be set out by year. In 100 bce, Julius Caesar was born. In 84 bce, his father died. In that same year, he married Cornelia. From 83 to 82 bce, Sulla gained control of Rome. In 78 bce, Sulla died. Caesar came back to Rome that year. In 69 bce, Cornelia died. In 67 bce, he married Pompeia. Pompeia was Pompey's daughter. From 60 to 59 bce, he was back in Rome again. Voters elected him consul in that span of years. Crassus, Pompey, and Caesar stood together in a group of great power.

10In 58 bce, he was made governor of Gaul. He defeated the northern tribes. In 54 bce, his daughter Julia died. Julia had been married to Pompey. In 53 bce, Crassus died. In 49 bce, civil war began. In 44 bce, Caesar was murdered. Some words from his story have set meanings. Call something ancient when it belongs to the deep past. A civilization is an organized society. Such a society has a steady food supply. It has a government. It has a written language. It has a religion. To conquer is to take control, usually by force. Corrupt means dishonest in order to gain something. An empire is a collection of nations or peoples. One person or one government rules that collection. Military means having to do with a country's armed forces. A politician works in politics. Often that person holds office. Often that person runs for office. A province is one of the largest areas inside some countries. Stable means balanced and steady. It also means reliable and not likely to change.

57. Sugar in Surprising Foods

1Sugar is a main ingredient in gooey desserts. It is also a main ingredient in sweet treats. You probably already know that. A surprise is waiting, though. Sugar shows up in foods besides desserts. Sweet treats are not the only ones either. Extra sugar is put into foods that may surprise you. Some of those foods may surprise you. Plant foods contain sugar as part of nature. Milk also contains sugar as part of nature. The sugar in milk is called lactose. The sugar in most fruits is called fructose. The sugar in most vegetables is fructose too. Apples are full of natural sugar. Pears are full of natural sugar. Natural sugar fills grapes. Many vegetables contain sugar. Many vegetables also contain starch.

2Starch breaks down into sugar. People sprinkle white crystals on cereal. They sprinkle the same crystals in coffee. Those crystals are table sugar. Table sugar is sucrose. Fruit and vegetables do contain sugar. Table sugar is a different kind from that sugar. Sucrose comes from sugarcane. Sucrose also comes from sugar beets. Sugarcane is a tall grass. Warm regions of the world are where it grows. Farms on Pacific islands grow sugarcane. Farms on Caribbean islands grow it too. Sugar beets are root vegetables. Cooler regions of Europe are where they grow. They grow in cooler parts of North America too. When we eat sugar, the body changes it. The body changes starch the same way.

3Both become a simple sugar called glucose. Glucose enters the bloodstream. The blood carries glucose through the body. Glucose in the blood gives energy to the body's cells. It powers the muscles. It powers the organs. The body needs this glucose in order to stay alive. Look at the names lactose, fructose, sucrose, and glucose. Each name ends the same way. The ending -ose marks a substance as a sugar. Milk sugar is changed into glucose. Plant sugar is changed into glucose too. Glucose enters the bloodstream. The bloodstream carries glucose to every part of the body. Glucose is fuel. The fuel keeps the body working. Energy is the reason our bodies want sugar. Lots of added sugar is still not a healthy choice.

4Food alone can supply every bit of sugar the body needs. Naturally sweet foods can supply that sugar. Starchy foods can supply it too. Modern diets include many foods that hold natural sugar. Many of those foods also hold added sugar. Cakes have added sugar. Cookies have added sugar. Ice cream has added sugar. Candy has added sugar. Foods that seem like an odd place for sweetness may still contain extra sugar. Vitamins are plentiful in a fruit smoothie. It also has some natural sugar. A healthy choice is how it may look. Some smoothies have added sugar too. Sugar in a smoothie can match the sugar in one glass of soda pop.

5Foods with little sweetness can still contain sugar. Prepared tomato products often have added sugar. Pasta sauce is one example. Ketchup is another example. This is also true of smoothies. It is especially true of low-fat prepared foods. In those foods, sugar often takes the place of fat. Sugar can also come from cereal eaten at breakfast. Many cereals include sweet bits. The bits may be candied fruit. The bits may be marshmallow. Even a cereal of plain flakes may get a dusting of sugar. The dusting makes the cereal taste sweeter. Some people eat cereal every morning. They may not notice how much sugar they take in. Many cereals contain added sugar. A chart compares the sugar in one cup of each cereal.

6Unfrosted shredded wheat has 0 grams. Toasted oats have 1 gram. Corn flakes have 2 grams. Crisp rice has 3 grams. Raisin bran has 18 grams. Health problems can follow when sugar intake is too high. If glucose in the blood falls too low, cells lack energy. The body then cannot keep working well. High blood sugar is a surplus of glucose carried in the blood. High blood sugar can damage cells. The heart can be harmed if high blood sugar lasts a long time. It can harm the brain. It can harm the kidneys. It can harm the eyes. It can harm other organs. People with diabetes must be extra careful about sugar.

7Diabetes is said dy-uh-BEE-teez. Their bodies cannot process the sugar level in their blood. Blood sugar can rise too high very easily. It can also fall too low. Blood sugar is something people with diabetes keep track of. A limit on eaten sugar may be necessary for them. Many people with diabetes cannot make enough insulin. Some of them cannot make any insulin. Insulin is a substance that helps the body process blood sugar. Lab-made insulin is what those people must use. Insulin cannot be put into a pill the way many medicines can. A pill of insulin would break down during digestion. People who need insulin can take it in a shot. They can also use a small pump placed inside the body.

8The pump sends insulin through a tube. Users of lab-made insulin often inject it with a device. What can people do to keep added sugar low? Eating more food in its natural form is one way. Another part of that way is to eat less processed food. Machines in factories make processed foods. Sugar is often added there. Food that people cook is often healthier than factory food. Food that people prepare is often healthier too. Home-cooked food can be a healthier pick than food from a factory. Another way is to pick a healthier snack. Fresh fruit is a better choice than a cookie. Nuts are a better choice than a donut.

9Yogurt with no added sugar is a better choice than ice cream. Natural sugars supply plenty of energy. Starches supply plenty of energy too. That energy helps the body keep working properly. Natural foods can help a person stay away from too much added sugar. Sugar makes food taste sweeter. Too much added sugar could harm our health. Our bodies get plenty of fuel from natural sugars. Starches do that too. Fresh fruit tastes good. Fresh vegetables taste good. The body benefits from them too. The bloodstream is the flow of blood through a person. The same word fits the blood flow of another animal. Diabetes is a disease of blood sugar. The body cannot properly control how much sugar is in the blood.

10Fructose is a very sweet sugar. It is found in fruits. To fuel something means to supply power. It can mean supplying energy. It can mean supplying strength. Glucose is a simple sugar. The body gets a major share of its energy from it. Lactose is the sugar found in milk. Prepared can mean made ready for use. It can also mean finished earlier. Processed means treated or changed. The change may improve something. The change may help it last longer. Sucrose is the sugar in sugarcane. It is also the sugar in sugar beets. Cooks and bakers often use it. It is the white sugar put on the table.

58. Terry Fox Runs for a Cure

1Topics in this book come in a set order. A journey starts the story. High school and the years after it come next. Then life crashes. An athlete's life follows. A plan takes shape. A run across Canada comes next. Support grows. Terrible news follows. The race continues. Meanings of key words come last. Reporters hear from Terry at the finish of his run. His parents stand beside him. A big goal drove Terry Fox. Money for cancer research was what he hoped to raise. The method was a run that crossed all of Canada. Distance would pass 8,000 kilometers, which is 5,000 miles. Spring of 1980 was the start of that historic crossing. Day two was already hard. A blizzard was on the route. A prosthetic leg was what he ran on. At age eighteen, surgeons took the right leg off above the knee. Attachment of the prosthetic was to the residual limb.

2The Fox family is shown relaxing together. From the left, beside Terry, are his father Rolly, his brother Darrell, his mother Betty, and his sister Judith. Winnipeg and Vancouver, both large cities, were where he grew up. Determination showed in him from early on. He loved sports and games that were hard and that lasted for days. Finishing a project mattered to him, no matter the time required. Childhood joys included bike rides. Baseball and soccer were favorites too. His little brother Darrell often played with him. His best friend, Doug Alward, often played too. Beating the odds was how his determination showed. Making the basketball team in eighth grade was the prize. At the start he was not a strong player. Height was not on his side either. Basketball was harder because of both facts. Practice, and a lot of it, still got him on the team. Hard training at basketball continued.

3A place on the high school team followed. Senior year brought a shared award, Athlete of the Year, with his buddy Doug. Classwork went well too. Graduation came with As and Bs. College was next. A future job was not yet clear to him. Basketball, he knew, was something he wanted to keep. The year 1976 took him to Simon Fraser University. British Columbia was the province, and this university held its best basketball team. Earning a roster spot became his new focus. He did earn a roster place in college. Life then started to change. November of his first college year brought a car crash. The crash did not hurt him badly. Soreness stayed in his knee. He told no one about the pain. Basketball practice, he thought, was the only cause. Late winter brought a limp. His mother took him to a doctor. The news was not the news they had expected.

4A rare cancer was the source of the leg pain. No one knew the cause. This cancer can spread fast. It can also threaten life. Cancer names a group of diseases. Body cells grow out of control in these diseases. The start can be almost anywhere. Bones can be the start. Blood can be the start. Cancer cells can then move through the body. Lumps called tumors can form. A cancer cell can keep doubling. A healthy cell does not grow in that same way. A tumor can come from that growth. Deaths worldwide have cancer as their second-leading cause. Anger was his first feeling. Fear was with it. Many feelings followed as treatment began. The hospital became his home. Doctors removed the right leg above the knee. Their hope was to stop the cancer from spreading. A promise that it would never return was not possible. After surgery he learned to walk on a prosthetic leg.

5Chemotherapy filled the months after that. His body grew weak. His hair fell out. The treatment did fight the cancer. Other patients were in the hospital too. Their path through the disease looked much like his own. One of them was Greg Scott, age ten. Cancer had taken one of Greg's legs as well. Sports were still open to him, though the forms were somewhat different. Summer of 1977 was when wheelchair basketball began. He trained hard to make his body strong. That habit had been his all his life, for every sport he loved. Running was the next try. A prosthetic leg made the first steps awkward. Mastery came soon. Regular training followed. His first race put him in last place. A strong sense of accomplishment stayed with him anyway. He had finished the event. Like other people who had lived through cancer, he faced a hard chance.

6The disease might come back. A plan grew out of that fear. He would help the search for a cure. Canada would be the route of a run. Cancer research would receive the money. A run this large would be difficult. Attention for a good cause was his hope. Planning was careful. Marathon of Hope was the name he chose. Doug, his friend, would travel with him. Doug would drive a van. Terry's stride was a hop. Springs in the artificial leg could reset after each step because of that hop. Thousands of training kilometers came first. Readiness followed. April 12, 1980, was the opening day of the Marathon of Hope. The place was St. John's, on the east coast, in Newfoundland. Difficulty was there from the first step. Days ran long. Weather stayed freezing. Rest came inside the van, where Terry and Doug huddled and made snacks.

7Narrow country roads were a danger when shared with drivers. His legs ached every day. Sunburn came quickly. Some towns saw him arrive exhausted, with nobody waiting. Other towns lined the streets with whole communities. Cheers greeted him. Money was given for the cause. A break in the van is when he studies a map. Province followed province. Each day covered a marathon's distance. Each week left him exhausted. Spirits rose when his younger brother Darrell joined the trip. Growing crowds lifted him too. Late June brought coverage from dozens of television stations. Newspapers were covering it too. The whole country began to follow. Canada's prime minister followed as well. Toronto brought thousands of people along the streets. Money for cancer research poured in. July 11, 1980, brought a huge welcome at Toronto City Hall. A map traces the Marathon of Hope across North America. Canada is marked. The United States is marked.

8Mexico is marked. St. John's is the start, dated April 12, 1980. Newfoundland appears. Prince Edward Island appears. Nova Scotia appears. Quebec appears. Ontario appears. Charlottetown, Montreal, Ottawa, Toronto, and Thunder Bay are on the map. September 1, 1980, is marked as the finish. Scales show distance in miles and in kilometers. The date was September 1. The distance was 5,300 kilometers, or 3,300 miles. The run then stopped without warning. Sickness had been building. Doctors in Thunder Bay, Ontario, were the reason he paused. The cancer had returned. He wanted to continue. Continuing was impossible. A flight home was required so he could get care. News of his death shocked people across Canada. It shocked people around the world. Sadness spread with that shock. A large crowd came to the funeral in Vancouver. Eight more months of treatment followed. Death came on June 28, 1981. The Marathon of Hope was never finished by him.

9His goals were still met. Millions of people learned that cures are needed for different kinds of cancer. Funding that research stayed his aim even after death. Family members founded the Terry Fox Foundation in 1988. Money is still raised by it. New people still learn the cause from his story. Yearly runs that carry his name draw many people. Each event lasts one day. Cancer research is what the money supports. Canada hosts these runs. Countries around the world host them too. More than 800 million dollars has been raised so far by the Terry Fox Foundation. The funds go to cancer research. No complete cure has been found yet. Progress has still been huge. Survival rates have climbed because of new treatments. Donations from groups like the one in his name have played a large part. His run showed a clear lesson. One person can change something in the world.

10Stanley Park in Vancouver is one place runners gather for a Terry Fox run. They honor his memory. They raise money for cancer research. An athlete, a noun, is a person trained for sports or games that need strength, speed, and skill. Chemotherapy, a noun, is care that uses chemicals, most often to kill cancer cells. Determination, a noun, is the drive to keep working toward a hard goal. A disease, a noun, is an illness, not caused by injury, that changes how a body works. Limping, a verb, means walking unevenly, often because a leg or foot hurts. A marathon, a noun, is a running race of 26 miles, about 42 kilometers. Prosthetic, an adjective, describes an artificial stand-in for a missing body part. Research, a noun, is careful study of a subject. A residual limb, a noun, is the part of a limb left after amputation.

59. The Country Called Mexico

1A map places Mexico in North America. It shows the United States to the north. Belize, Guatemala, Honduras, and El Salvador appear nearby. South America is marked beyond them. The Pacific Ocean lies to the west. The Gulf of Mexico lies to the east. The Gulf of California and the Baja Peninsula are shown. The Yucatán Peninsula is shown too. Mexico City is marked on the map. The parts of this book are place, people, land, history, animals, a closing, and a word list. North America's southern region is where Mexico sits. The United States is on one side, and Belize and Guatemala are on the other. The Pacific Ocean lies to Mexico's west. The Gulf of Mexico is on the eastern coast. The Gulf of California divides Mexico's main land from the Baja (BAH-hah) Peninsula. Mexico also includes some islands. Those islands lie along the east coast and the west coast. Visitors entering Chapultepec Park meet a monument honoring war heroes. The park holds history museums.

2It also holds lakes. Few cities in North America are older than Mexico City. It is the capital of Mexico. The city sits in a basin with mountains all around. More than nineteen million people live there. Mexico City has many beautiful parks. No park in the city is bigger than Chapultepec (chah-pool-te-PEK) Park. Inside it are a zoo, gardens, and fountains. Museums, lakes, and forests are there too. Long before today, by thousands of years, Indigenous peoples occupied this land. They built amazing civilizations. The year 1517 brought Spanish explorers to Mexico. By 1521, the Spanish had made Mexico a colony. About two thirds of people in modern Mexico are mestizos (mes-TEE-zohz). A mestizo has both Indigenous and European ancestors. The other third includes Indigenous peoples. It also includes people descended from the Spanish. Other arrivals to the country are part of that third too. Today Mexico has more than 120 million people. Most Mexicans speak Spanish. No other country has as many Spanish speakers as Mexico.

3Some people speak Indigenous languages. City life is the choice of most of the population. More than three fourths of Mexico's people are city dwellers. Oil and silver are among the natural resources Mexico has in abundance. Coffee, sugar cane, and cotton leave the country by the ton every year. Art, and the people who make it, have brought the country fame. Painting goes far back there, and so do sculpture and dance. Mexico is known for large paintings called murals. Diego Rivera was one of its most famous mural painters. One famous mural shows the Zapotec at work beside the Mixtec. Those two Indigenous groups lived in ancient Mexico. They are making jewelry from gold. Jade and turquoise are among the precious stones in that jewelry. That mural hangs in the National Palace in Mexico City. Many other murals hang there too. A land map names the Sonoran Desert and Copper Canyon. It names the Chihuahuan Desert and the Baja Peninsula.

4It shows the Gulf of California and the Sierra Madre Oriental Mountains. The Mexican Plateau and the Gulf of Mexico are marked. The Yucatán Peninsula and the Pacific Ocean are marked. Mexico City, Pico de Orizaba, and the Sierra Madre del Sur Mountains appear as well. Mexico has many mountain ranges. Some of the mountains are also volcanoes. That top peak is a volcano rising 5,700 meters, or 18,701 ft. Its name is Pico de Orizaba (PEE-koh day OR-ee-SAH-bah). Mountains run along the west, the east, and the south. Mexico also has canyons of amazing depth. People say northern Mexico's Copper Canyon outsizes the Grand Canyon. A plateau fills the middle of the country. That raised, flat land is a good place for roads and towns. This area holds most of the people. Two peninsulas stick out into the ocean. One is the Baja, and the other is the Yucatán. Limestone is the rock of the Yucatán Peninsula.

5Over thousands of years, some of that rock dissolved away. Spectacular caves are the result. Some are underground caves full of water, called cenotes (sih-NOH-teez). Northern Mexico is crossed by the Sonoran Desert and the Chihuahuan Desert. The Sonoran Desert has more kinds of plants than any other desert in North America. Parts of the southeast are covered by rainforests that are warm and wet. Many kinds of plants and animals live in those forests. Lagoons, marshes, and swamps fill the low land along the coast. The Caribbean Sea lies beyond that eastern coast. Lakes are few in Mexico, and rivers are few too. One river is very well known. Mexico stands on one side of this river, and the United States stands on the other. Mexicans call it the Rio Bravo del Norte. The words mean a wild river belonging to the north. Rio Grande is the name used in the United States. Indigenous peoples were the first to live in Mexico.

6One of the best-known groups is the Mayan people. Great cities rose under the Maya, along with temples and pyramids. They did this without the wheel. They also did it without help from animals. They made beautiful works of art. They studied the stars and the planets. They developed a calendar. Visitors can see El Castillo (kahs-TEE-yoh). It is a pyramid the Mayan people built long ago. Central Mexico received the Aztec people in the early 1300s. An island in a lake held the capital they built. Mexica is another name for the Aztec. The name Mexico comes from that name. Their capital was Tenochtitlán (tay-nohch-teet-LAHN). That site would become Mexico City. A huge empire grew under the Aztec. Hernán Cortés, a Spanish explorer, reached the Aztec capital in 1519. Two years were enough for him and his followers to seize the land. They had conquered the Aztec people. Pancho Villa and Emiliano Zapata led the Mexican Revolution. Spain turned Mexico into a colony in 1521.

7It stayed a colony until 1821. That year the people fought a war and won freedom from Spanish rule. In the century after that, many people were poor farmers. They struggled to survive. The share of land in European hands was 95 percent, and those owners were wealthy. Rights for farmers were scarce, and workers had few as well. They had little money. During the Mexican Revolution, from 1910 to 1920, the people rose up. They changed the government. Mexico became a democracy. That change helped daily life get better for people who were poor. Extremes still mark Mexico today. The rich form one main social class, and the poor form the other. The middle class is very small. Daytime is when the jaguar stays active. Even so, it hunts mainly at night. Many kinds of animals live in Mexico. The jaguar has an important place in old Indigenous stories, songs, and prayers. It lives mainly in Mexico's rainforests.

8It is the largest cat species in North America. Length can be about 2 meters, or 6 ft. It has black spots. Its roar sounds like a loud cough. Water appeals to the jaguar, which sets it apart from many other cats. It swims, plays, and hunts there. Its main food is the peccary. The peccary belongs to the pig family, and it is small and wild. Extinction is a real danger for the jaguar. Saving these cats is the goal of many people. Ancient Maya held the quetzal (keht-SAHL) sacred, and so did the Aztec. Mexico's rainforests are where it lives. Bright color marks the male, as it does for many kinds of birds. The female looks plainer. Bright golden green covers the male's head and also his chest. Its back is blue. Its belly is red. Tail feathers may stretch past 1 meter, or 3 ft. Those bright colors help the male attract a mate. The red-knee tarantula lives in the mountains of Mexico.

9Patches at the leg joints give this large, hairy spider its name. The patches are reddish orange. From leg to leg, the span is 15 cm, or 6 in. It grows very slowly. Thirty years is a possible age for the female. The ends of its legs can sense vibrations. They can also sense smells and tastes. Mexico's Copper Canyon is a popular place for visitors. It is hard to reach. A train carries visitors through the area. Diversity is what marks this country. Modern cities stand here, and ancient ruins do too. Deep canyons lie inside high mountains. Deserts on this land are dry, while the rainforests are wet. Its long history fascinates people around the globe. Its natural beauty does too. On a visit, which sight would you choose before the others? The population is 127,575,529. The land covers 1,964,375 sq km, or 758,450 sq. mi. The capital city is Mexico City. The primary language is Spanish.

10The primary religion is Roman Catholic. The currency is the peso (PAY-soh). These facts come from the Central Intelligence Agency in 2019 and from the World Bank. A basin is a sunken spot in Earth's surface. Canyons are deep valleys with steep sides. Rivers usually form them. Cenotes are deep natural wells. They form when surface limestone falls in and shows the groundwater below. A colony is a land or a country ruled by another country, or belonging to it. Lagoons are shallow, calm bodies of water. They can lie between a reef and the shore. The center of an atoll can hold one too. Mestizos are people with Spanish and Indigenous ancestry. A peninsula is a long piece of land with water around almost all of it. A plateau is flat ground lifted above the land around it. Rainforests are thick forests, usually in the tropics. They get a lot of rain. They hold many kinds of animals and plants.

60. The Country That Counts Joy

1What is happiness? Each person may hold a different meaning for that word. In Bhutan, said boo-TAHN, people treat happiness as a feeling of being content. They also think of it as feeling satisfied. The country's leaders share that view. They believe joy matters more than money. That belief is why the government asks citizens about their happiness. The question goes out every few years. Leaders use the answers to make life better in the country. A nickname for Bhutan is the Kingdom of Happiness. Four quick facts add to the picture. Bhutan lies in the Himalaya Mountains. The language of the people is Dzongkha, said ZOHNG-kah. Many people speak English as well. For centuries, bows and arrows defended the borders. Archery holds the place of national sport in the present day. Bhutan has a king. Bhutan also has leaders chosen by vote. A map places China beside Bhutan. It places Nepal, India, and Bangladesh nearby. It marks the capital, Thimphu. It marks the Great Himalaya Range. It marks the Lesser Himalaya Range. Low plains fill the south, while the high Himalaya Mountains fill the north. Every few years, citizens receive a survey about how happy they are.

2The first survey of that kind took place in 2007. The wish to track happiness started long before the survey. In 1972, a new king received his crown. King Wangchuck was only sixteen years old. Love for the nation filled the young king. He wanted the country to grow. He also wanted it to stay true to its values. He knew how other countries measured growth. They used GDP, which means Gross Domestic Product. GDP adds up the value of every good a country produces. It also adds up the value of every service. The Tiger's Nest Monastery holds to the side of a cliff. The cliff stands thousands of feet over the Paro Valley. GDP did not seem like the right measure for Bhutan, in the king's view. Buying and selling were not what pushed daily life forward. Farms and villages are common. Factories are few. Cities are few. Bhutan is mostly a Buddhist nation as well. Buddhists see happiness as an important step toward a good life. For them, joy is not only a matter of money. They believe a person must look hard at many parts of life. The person must also work to improve those parts.

3King Wangchuck wanted the whole country to focus on those parts. Gross National Happiness was the name he gave his plan. People shorten the name to GNH. The plan was a guide for how the country should grow. His full name is King Jigme Singye Wangchuck. GNH rested on four ideas, and people called them pillars. The first pillar looked at how the people were governed. The second looked at how people worked together. Play among people was part of that second pillar too. The third pillar was about keeping Bhutanese culture alive. The last pillar looked at the part people play in the natural world. These four areas touch every life. They touch each person's sense of well-being. Leaders believed the four pillars had to stay in balance. Without that balance, happiness in life would be hard to reach. When a big decision came up, the king weighed all four pillars. His ministers did the same. The measure of GNH is built on values held in Bhutan. One value is governing that is good. One value is careful handling of money. One value is careful growth of society.

4Firm backing for how Bhutanese people live is another value. One value is care for the natural world. At the start, the four pillars were only gentle suggestions. Nobody measured them. Nobody saved data about them. Signs posted around the country asked for help in a cheerful way. The signs asked people to help the country grow in the GNH way. Citizens did their best. People lived simply. For the most part, they seemed content. They wore traditional clothes. Each day they sat quietly for a few moments and calmed their minds. They looked after nature. They took pleasure in nature. Families spent time with one another. Bhutan remained a quiet country. The modern world stayed outside its protection. Then change began. Giant wheels used for prayer appear all over the country. Some stand at bus stops. Some stand at monasteries. Morning time for calming the mind is part of school lessons linked to GNH. Television entered the country legally for the first time during 1999. The Internet was allowed in at the same time. Citizens started learning more about lands beyond the border. Outsiders were drawn by beauty that had not been changed.

5They were drawn by a peaceful way of living. Many of them came to visit. Bhutan then stood with one foot in the past. The other foot stood in the modern world. Children watched television. Children played video games. Citizens started to use cell phones. Leaders feared the old traditions would disappear. They feared the old values would disappear. If that happened, people might also lose the old sense of what made them happy. Bhutan has no stoplights. Thimphu, the capital, once had one of them put in place. Citizens had it taken down. They felt a stoplight was simply too modern. A traffic officer guides cars through the downtown instead. Young monks crowd around a cell phone. The scene is a monastery in Paro. The government decided happiness needed a closer watch. It also needed a way to be improved. Leaders wanted a tool more exact than the four pillars by themselves. A system of measurement would let them follow happiness. It would help them handle change. The Ministry of Planning in Bhutan set to work. First the ministry named nine areas. Those areas are called domains. The domains grew out of the four pillars.

6Each domain was then split into many smaller categories. Dozens of categories were used. Taken together, the domains and the categories form a full picture of happiness. The picture is called the Gross National Happiness Index. The government next wrote questions about the areas in GNH. One question asked how much a person enjoys life. The questions were joined into a survey for the people. Each kind of answer was given a number. Adding the numbers produced a score for that person. Nine domains make up the index. They are well-being and standards of living. They are health and the use of time. They are the environment and the strength of community life. They are education. They are cultural resilience, a word for toughness. They are good governance. The GNH Centre Bhutan is the source of that list. In 2007, the government tried the survey in some places. By 2010, it was ready for every district. Bhutan has twenty districts. Teams carried the survey to villages in the mountains. They carried it to farms in the valleys. They carried it to the cities. Lives were described that year by more than seven thousand citizens.

7The full population was over seven hundred thousand. The answers were studied with the GNH Index. A bigger number meant a higher level of happiness. The survey named the groups who were least happy. Women were in that group. Farmers were in that group. People without schooling were in that group. Older people were in that group. The government started to improve care for health. It started to improve ordinary daily life. A further survey was taken in 2015. Leaders compared the two sets of results. Happiness for the country as a whole rose by 1.8 percent. A very happy rating covered upwards of 43 percent of the population. Flags of prayer are a common sight on the land. They stand for peace. They stand for harmony. One group of people was left out of the survey. They are the Lhotshampa, said loh-TSUHM-puh. They are citizens of Bhutan whose families came from Nepal. They lived in the south of the country. The nineteenth century was when they came out of Nepal. They came to farm rich soil. The Lhotshampa follow Hinduism. Their faith, Hinduism, differs from Buddhism. During the middle of the 1980s, leaders began a campaign.

8The campaign told every citizen to wear Bhutanese clothing. It told them to speak Dzongkha. It told them to practice Buddhism. Tens of thousands of Lhotshampa departed during the 1990s. Some went to Nepal. They settled in camps there. By 2015, nearly 85,000 of them were living in the United States. A return to Bhutan is still the hope of many. A woman of the Lhotshampa holds her child. She does physical work on a project that is building a road. The project is in Thimphu. Some parts of the survey moved in the other direction. People gave less time to volunteer work. Fewer people felt that tradition mattered. The government plans to use the survey to lift happiness. One idea is to hold festivals.
