The festivals would invite communities into old traditions. The aim is a day when 100 percent of the people are deeply happy. Other countries have paid attention to Bhutan's index. Some built their own tools for tracking how happy citizens feel. In 2007, Thailand made a national happiness index much like Bhutan's. South Korea built a scale of its own. Great Britain built one too. In 2009, a large company asked people in the United States about happiness.

9A report from 2016 names the ten happiest countries. Denmark is number one. Switzerland is number two. Iceland is number three. Norway is number four. Finland is number five. Canada is number six. The Netherlands is number seven. New Zealand is number eight. Australia is number nine. Sweden is number ten. The World Happiness Report of 2016 is the source. Its data show the same ten countries on top in every year that was surveyed. The order of those ten can change. The United States stood at 13th. Bhutan stood at 84th. Each rank comes from a country's average score. A scale running from 0 up to 10 was how citizens scored daily life. In this kingdom, earning money sits behind a person's own happiness. In 2011, the United Nations studied happiness across the world. The United Nations is a body made of countries from around the world. The earliest World Happiness Report came off the press in 2012. Countries in that report were ranked with ideas like the ones in Bhutan's index. In that same year, March 20 received an official name. International Day of Happiness was the title given to that date.

10Why that date? On March 20, the Sun lines up with the equator of Earth. Day and night last the same number of hours. The date stands for a belief of the king of Bhutan. He believed balance is the key to a whole nation's happiness. Some words from this account have set meanings. Analyzed means studied with care. Balance is a state where different parts are equal, or where each part has the right share. The word can also mean harmony. Buddhist describes Buddhism. Siddhartha Gautama's teachings in ancient India are the root of that religion. Decline means a steady loss of amount, of quality, of value, or of strength. An index is a number used to measure something. It can also show that the level of something has changed. Meditated means the mind was made calm and focused. The calm may come in silence. It may come through chanting. Asking questions of a group is how a survey gathers information. Tracking means watching how something moves ahead, or following it. Traditions are beliefs or practices handed down from one year to the next.

61. The Curious Life of Albert Einstein

1Contents. Hidden things is marked 4. A gifted, curious boy is marked 5. Work at the patent office is marked 8. The miracle year is marked 10. A star on the rise is marked 12. How fame was used is marked 15. The move to America is marked 17. What Einstein changed is marked 20. A life lived well is marked 22. The word list is marked 23. The word finder is marked 24. Hidden things. Einstein once wrote a line about knowledge. Knowing what is true does not, by itself, show what ought to be done. Albert Einstein was five when his father showed him a compass. The compass was magnetic. Young Einstein felt curious. His father spun the case around and around. The needle stayed still. Einstein was too young then to know why. Years later he wrote down the lesson. A secret seemed to sit behind ordinary things. That curious child grew into a curious scientist. His life was spent trying to name that secret. Because of his work, people think about the universe in a new way. Curiosity about hard problems led Einstein to great discoveries. Einstein played the violin as a child. He still played it as an adult. A gifted, curious boy. He once listed a few things. A table was one. A chair was one. Fruit in a bowl was one. A violin was one. He asked what else a man needs in order to feel happy. March 14, 1879, was the day Albert Einstein was born. The place was Ulm, a small town in Germany. His family shaped his life in a strong way.

2Maja was his younger sister. She was his closest friend in childhood. His mother was named Pauline. When Albert was six, Pauline urged him to learn the violin. For all his remaining years, the violin stayed near him. A fact to know. As a child, Einstein had an odd habit when he spoke. He said a thing out loud. A second time, he said it only to himself, almost too softly to hear. He outgrew the habit. He later became a strong speaker in public. A photo pairs him with Maja, his sister. His father introduced him to math and science. Two uncles did the same. Albert was still young. They turned algebra into a game. Several books arrived by way of a friend of the family. The books were about science and mathematics. Einstein would read for hours at a time. A book about geometry was a favorite. He had a view about teaching. A lesson should feel like a gift that has real worth. It should not feel like a hard duty. In school, his gift for math was clear. His gift for science was clear too. School methods still drew his questions. To young Einstein, teachers mostly made students memorize. He wanted to think in a creative way. Memorizing was not enough for him. At only 17, he was accepted at a university. It was one of the best science universities in Europe. Again, he followed his own questions about how things worked. He also asked why they worked. He often skipped class. The extra time went to his own ideas.

3Professors thought he might have a bright future. They also thought he needed to do as he was told. A United States stamp later honored his creative thinking. He said imagination matters more than stored knowledge. The patent office. After he finished school in 1900, a job was hard to find. The teachers able to help with jobs disliked how independent he was. They did not like his curiosity either. Bern, Switzerland, became his workplace in 1902. The job was in a patent office. He checked devices that people sent in for patents. He had to see if each device worked as the inventor claimed. The job let him stay curious. It let him question ideas that others already accepted. On his first day, the boss gave an order. When you lift an application, treat the inventor's claims as untrue. Einstein worked at that patent office in Bern, Switzerland. A fact to know. Einstein held several patents of his own. One patent was for a compass. Large amounts of iron did not affect it. The compass became standard gear on metal ships. It was used on submarines too. A pump was covered by another patent he held. The pump was for refrigerators. It was quieter and safer. Pay from the job gave him freedom. The work schedule gave him freedom too. He could explore his own ideas. In spare time he met with friends. The friends shared his curiosity about science ideas. They named their group the Olympia Academy. During these years he had steady work. He also had strong friendships.

4Ideas from these years would alter science for good. The miracle year. By 1905, three years had passed at the patent office. In that time he built new scientific theories. He soon felt sure enough to share them. He sent papers to a journal. It was the best German science journal of that time. The journal printed the first paper in March 1905. Four more papers followed soon. Paper number one said what light is. It also explained how light acts. The second paper and the third paper proved something. Molecules and atoms, the tiny parts of matter, were shown to be real. The papers described how those parts moved. The last two papers gave new explanations. They dealt with space, with time, and with objects that move. H H O His papers helped explain molecules such as H2O. Scientists were astounded by his writing. In six months he challenged ideas about the universe. Those ideas had stood for over two hundred years. People were amazed at the source of the ideas. The writer was a patent clerk. He was 26 years old. He was not a physics professor. His science career began to rise. Meetings invited him to explain the new ideas. The 1905 winner of the Nobel Prize in physics sent congratulations. Physics professor Max Planck was already well known. Planck began teaching Einstein's ideas to students. People later called 1905 Einstein's miracle year. Planck taught those theories in his university classes. A star on the rise. Einstein said something surprising about the world. The world can be understood.

5That fact is the hardest thing about it to grasp. In 1909 a university finally offered him a teaching job. He took the job. The following years took his teaching to more than one school. He was looking for the best place for his research. He kept developing the earlier ideas. The work was hard. He often feared the path was a mistake. The ideas were so new that they felt unfamiliar. He often feared he was losing his mind. Then 1915 brought a breakthrough. He found how to join the earlier ideas into one system. The name he chose was the general theory of relativity. His ideas changed modern astronomy in a deep way. Scientists honored him by naming a sky object Einstein's Cross. The diagram is not to scale. Point B is where a star seems to be. Point A is where the star really is. The labels also show the Sun and Earth. Gravity from the Sun bends light that comes from a star. The bend makes the star look as if it sits at B. The star is really at A. Once again, scientists around the world grew excited. In 1919, British scientists tested the theory. They measured where a star appeared during a solar eclipse. If Einstein was right, the star would seem to sit in one place. It would actually sit in another. The star showed up almost exactly where Einstein had said. Headlines in newspapers around the world announced the result. Overnight, Albert Einstein became famous across the world. Time Magazine named him Person of the Century.

6Not everyone was pleased. Many scientists had built careers on ideas he showed were wrong. Other people refused the theories because testing them was difficult. Einstein knew the theories were not perfect. Each new paper still helped more people see why the work mattered. How fame was used. He urged people not to chase success alone. He wanted them to become people of value. As fame grew, so did the demands. People wanted interviews. They wanted photographs. They wanted speeches. They wanted writings. Einstein had mixed feelings about the attention. The demands took time he could have used on other work. He also knew fame could carry his ideas to more people. He used fame to point at issues he cared about. Avoiding war was a belief he held deeply. In 1914 he signed a letter against German entry into World War I. Only three scientists signed that letter. More than 100 scientists signed a letter that supported the war. He said a country cannot stop a war and prepare for one at the same time. A fact to know. During the 1930s, articles for everyday readers started to appear from him. The series title put The World first, then the words as I See It. The pieces covered war. They covered liberty. They covered progress. They covered education. They were not only about science. He also worked with groups in many countries that sought peace. He wrote often about beliefs against war. Magazines in the United States said he helped create the atomic bomb. He pushed back against that claim. Other people used his discoveries to build the bomb.

7He did not take part in the project. A letter from him did go to President Franklin Roosevelt. The letter warned that the bomb could cause terrible destruction. News reporters greeted Albert Einstein and his wife, Elsa. The year of that New York arrival was 1921. The move to America. He named the strongest reason for work at school and in life. The reason is pleasure in the work itself. Pleasure in the result matters too. So does knowing the result has worth for the community. Einstein and others worked for peace. Through the 1930s, Germany drew nearer to war anyway. Einstein left Germany in 1933. The Institute for Advanced Study gave him the new post. The institute is in the United States. One picture shows a meeting at his home in Princeton, New Jersey. The year was 1949. India's prime minister, Jawaharlal Nehru, met him there. Einstein enjoyed sailing. He and his family soon joined the close community of Princeton, New Jersey. Visitors from out of town asked where he lived. Townspeople answered with a question. Are you expected? That question kept strangers from bothering him at home. For the next 22 years he tried to stretch his ideas into new areas. He believed a further theory could join his ideas with the newest theories. He worked as hard as ever. He never again reached the kind of breakthrough from the miracle year. He also never matched the breakthrough of the general theory of relativity. He still gave speeches. He still wrote articles. He still met often with other scientists.

8One picture shows Albert Einstein speaking with secretary Helen Dukas. Her work beside him ran from 1928 to his death in 1955. What Einstein changed. He said few people see with their own eyes. He said few people feel with their own hearts. Curiosity led to many useful products. From his thinking on light, scientists found fresh ways to manage light. Those steps later led to television cameras. They led to remote controls. They led to flat-screen computers. Laser light was first imagined by Einstein. Laser light is used in CDs. It is used in DVDs. It is used in store checkout equipment. His proof that atoms and molecules exist led to new products. It also improved many products. Shaving cream is one. Toothpaste is one. Personal computers are another. Portable phones are another. There are more besides these. His theories give scientists a frame for studying objects in space. The Helix Nebula is one such object. New theories still grow from his scientific ideas. Ideas about how the universe began come from his work. Ideas about the nature of space come from his work. Ideas about strange objects called black holes come from his work. He said the key is to keep asking questions. A life lived well. Einstein once spoke about mystery. He called it the most beautiful experience a person can have. He said it is enough to understand a little of that mystery every day. Work continued up to April 18, 1955, the day he died. He wanted to understand nature's greatest mysteries. He changed how people think about time.

9He changed how they think about space. He changed how they think about energy. He changed how they think about matter. Those four things are the foundations of the universe. Word list. Algebra, a noun on page 6, is a kind of math. Letters and other symbols stand in for numbers. Astounded, a verb on page 11, means greatly amazed or surprised. Atoms, a noun on page 10, are tiny pieces of matter. Compass, a noun on page 4, is a tool for finding direction. Its needle always points north. Geometry, a noun on page 6, is math about lines, angles, surfaces, and shapes. Molecules, a noun on page 10, are a substance cut down to the smallest bits that still count as that substance. Mysterious, an adjective on page 22, means not easy to understand. Patent, a noun on page 8, is a document. It gives an inventor the right to earn money from an invention. Physics, a noun on page 11, is the science that studies matter and energy. Solar eclipse, a noun on page 13, is the stretch of time when the Moon hides the Sun from our view. Theories, a noun on page 10, are possible explanations. Universe, a noun on page 4, means everything that exists out in space. Word finder. Beliefs are noted on pages 15 and 16. Born is noted on page 5. Compass is noted on pages 4 and 9. Curious is noted on pages 4, 5, and 11. Curiosity is noted on pages 7 through 9, and on pages 20 and 22. Fame is noted on pages 15 and 16.

10Family is noted on pages 5, 6, and 18. Father is noted on pages 4 and 6. The general theory of relativity is noted on pages 12 and 19. Inventor is noted on page 8. Job is noted on pages 8, 12, and 17. Laser light is noted on page 20. Magnetic is noted on page 4. The miracle year is noted on pages 10, 11, and 19. Mother is noted on page 5. 62. The Game Played with a Ball and a Goal

1In 2015, a club called South China played a team from the Philippines. South China wore red. The match was in Manila. China won the match 6-1. Games have pleased people around the world across thousands of years. Many of those games have players kick a ball. The ball goes toward another team's goal. Today, soccer stands far ahead as the favorite of these games. People ask why the sport is loved everywhere. People can play soccer outdoors. They can play in all kinds of weather. Learning this sport does not take long. It helps players stay fit. A ball is the one piece of gear a game requires. Best of all, the game is great fun. People played early kinds of soccer in Britain. The games began in the eighth century. The game was called football. It was much rougher then. There were not many rules. Early in the 1900s, a hard schoolyard did not suit such a rough game. New rules were invented.

2Schools made the game a regular activity. The reason was the teamwork players need. England in 1863 is where the modern game started. In that year, two sports became different. One was soccer, also called association football. The other was rugby football. Feet do most of the ball work in soccer. Hands are part of how rugby players move the ball. Late in the 1930s, the modern rule book took shape. The rules continue to change even today. About 65,000 people filled the stands for an 1897 final. The place was Crystal Palace. The match was the English FA Cup Final. Aston Villa played Everton. A map shows what people call the game. Football is the name used in most countries. North America calls it soccer. Several other places use a similar name. Italy calls the sport calcio. South Korea calls it chukku. Almost every country has people who play. The sport is popular with adults. It is popular with children. People of every background love it.

3People of every religion love it. Outdoor play is what most people picture. Indoor play is possible too. Only two Summer Olympic Games have left out men's soccer. The women's event joined the Olympics in 1996. Soccer has been played in the Paralympics since the Paralympics began in 1984. Blind soccer joined the Paralympics in 2004. About 240 million people play soccer. They play in more than 300,000 clubs. Fan counts are estimated up to 3.5 billion. That is half of all the people on Earth. Professional soccer has one group in charge. Its name is Fédération Internationale de Football Association. People call it FIFA. FIFA was founded in 1904. As of early 2015, FIFA had 209 member countries. National teams from those countries try to reach the World Cup. One champion of the world is decided by the World Cup. The World Cup field holds thirty-two teams. The World Cup takes place every four years. A photo shows two players battling for the ball.

4Alistair-Patric Heselton is from Great Britain. Rene Renteria is from the United States. In the United States, one main group leads soccer. It is the United States Soccer Federation. People also call it U.S. Soccer. The letters USSF name it too. There are professional teams. There are men's teams. There are women's teams. There are children's teams. Students of every age enjoy the game. A formal team is not required for fun. Two players are enough for a game. They can play in a park. They can play on a playground. Some youth teams play in other countries. A Colorado team in blue played one such match. The other team was the Yuwa Supergoats, in red. That club is a girls' team from India. They played in Blaine, Minnesota. The date was July 12, 2014. A soccer field is called a pitch. One measure of the pitch is 49 to 100 yards. That measure is 45 to 90 m. Another measure is 100 to 131 yards.

5That measure is 90 to 120 m. The goal line is also called the endline. If the ball crosses that line, a goal kick or a corner kick is given. A goal counts in one case only. The ball must cross between the goal posts and go into the goal. The corner arc is the spot for a corner kick. The goal box is the spot for a goal kick. The sideline is also called the touchline. If the ball goes out there, the match restarts with a throw-in. The halfway line crosses the middle of the pitch. It is also called the midfield line. A kickoff takes place at the center spot. That spot is also called the center mark. During a kickoff, the center circle has a limit. Players from the kicking side are the only ones allowed inside. The penalty arc has a limit too. No other player may stand there during the penalty kick. The penalty area is called the box.

6In that area, a goalkeeper may use the hands. The penalty spot is a circle. It sits 12 yards from the goal line. That distance is 11 m. The ball is placed there for a penalty kick. A net stands at each end of the pitch. A score comes from putting the ball in the opponent's net. Stopping the opponent's score is the other job. Eleven players make a full team. One player is the goalie. Ten players are field players. Three groups make up the field players. Scoring is the main job of forwards. Blocking scores is the main job of defenders. Midfielders switch between these two jobs. A chart uses letters for the jobs. G means goalie. D means defender. M means midfielder. F means forward. Some forwards are called strikers. Field players may touch the ball only in set ways. They may use their feet. They may use their legs. They may use their chest. They may use their head.

7The goalie can use the hands inside the penalty area. Stopping shots is the goalie's chief task. Those balls have gotten past the team's defenders. A kickoff opens the match. Play begins with the ball on the center spot. The opening pass goes from one starter to a teammate. That side tries to carry play toward the far end. The aim is to score a goal. A ball sent out of bounds can switch control to the opponent. That happens when the starting team sends the ball out. If the other team sends the ball out, the starting team keeps control. A player may treat an opponent too roughly. The other team is then given a free penalty kick. Goalies need to be quick. A dive is often the way to reach the ball. The dive is meant to prevent a goal. Many skills are required of field players. The skills include kicking and dribbling. They include passing and trapping. They include heading and shooting.

8Players must also know how to take the ball away. They use different kinds of tackles. The game gives players strong exercise. A match can demand as much as 7 miles of running. That distance is 12 km. A fast start is required. They must be able to stop quickly. They need enough strength to last the whole match. Short foot moves with the ball are called dribbling. Taking the ball from an opponent is called tackling. A header sends the ball with the head. A pass sends the ball to a teammate. A shot is a kick aimed at a score. Many people rank Pelé above every other player the sport has known. Say his name PAY-lay. Brazil's national team used him at center forward. He played from 1956 to 1977. He made 1,281 goals during his career. That number set a record. His retirement came more than thirty-five years ago. His record still stands. Briana Scurry began as a goalkeeper in 1994.

9Her team's full name was United States Women's National Team. People call it the USWNT. Fame followed a save that helped win a 1999 title game. Every trophy in her field came to her during her career. She earned every award in her field too. A 2017 honor put her in a hall of fame. Its name is National Soccer Hall of Fame. Women's soccer got its first major star in Mia Hamm. Two Women's World Cup titles for the United States included her help. One of them was the first Women's World Cup, in 1991. Two gold medals from the Olympics were hers as well. Marta Vieira da Silva plays as a forward from Brazil. FIFA chose her as World Player of the Year on six occasions. The years were 2006 to 2010. She was named again in 2018. Her World Cup total reached seventeen goals in 2019. That goal made her the highest World Cup goal scorer of all time. The mark counts players across genders.

10Pay for women has stayed below men's pay, even with such records. Megan Rapinoe is a member of the USWNT. She protested for equal pay. In 2022, U.S. Soccer agreed to a new plan. The plan pays men and women the same. Youth soccer is very popular. It welcomes many ages. It welcomes many abilities. Some teams mix girls and boys in the same matches. Students who do not play yet can ask a simple question. Does their city or town have a soccer club? Age groups often get their own club teams. Skill building is part of a coach's help. The rules are another lesson from a coach. New friends are easy to find at a club. Players learn about fair play. Players learn about teamwork. Fun is the only goal for many students. A later professional career is the aim for others. Either way, they take part in an exciting sport. People all around the world love it.

63. The Highway Called the Main Street

1The year is 1957. You have just folded down the top of a cherry-red convertible. Rock and roll pours from the speakers. Open road is all that lies ahead. Could the drive get any better? You do not think so. Then you pass a cone of ice cream that stands three stories high. Sprinkles cover the cone. Your mouth starts to water. Should you stop? Next comes a cheeseburger as big as a bus. A quick lunch sounds better than the cone. When night comes, you park beside a hotel room built in the shape of a teepee. The day on the road was spectacular. No one can say what the next day will bring. The trip was not a daydream. You have just traveled the highway Americans have loved most. The highway is Route 66. It carries drivers across the whole American West. A road map of this country is a familiar sight to many drivers. The lines look a lot like a giant spiderweb. Huge highways bend from one coast to the other. They link nearly every city along the way. Travel is simple. A person gets in a car and goes.

2A hundred years ago, a drive across the country was much harder. Most of the so-called roads were only small dirt trails. Cars broke down often. Cars sank into mud. Tires burst on sharp rocks. In the 1920s, the government of the United States built a new set of highways with numbers. The aim was an easier crossing of the country. The first of those highways to be paved end to end ran 2,400 miles. That distance is 3,862 kilometers. It started in Chicago, Illinois. It ended in Los Angeles, California. People named it Route 66. Many spoke of it fondly as the Main Street of America. That road was tested soon after it opened. Hard times arrived in the United States during 1929. Many banks failed. People lost the money they had saved. Jobs disappeared for a great many workers. A large number lost their homes too. The years that followed are called the Great Depression. At the same time, drought after drought ruined many farms. A drought is a long stretch with no rain. New Mexico, Texas, Oklahoma, Colorado, and Kansas suffered the worst of it.

3People call that time the Dust Bowl era. All at once, many families had to leave home. They went searching for a better life somewhere else. A lot of them believed California was a kind of paradise. Thousands packed what they owned. They made the long trip toward the west. Their path was a new road with the name Route 66. The Grapes of Wrath is a classic novel by John Steinbeck. It was published in 1939. The story follows a family leaving Oklahoma during the Dust Bowl years. They take Route 66 in search of work in California. Steinbeck called the highway the mother road. He also called it the road of flight. California received 1.2 million newcomers in the course of the 1930s. Hard years of the Dust Bowl pushed over 500,000 of those people to leave. World War II gained the United States as a fighter on December 8, 1941. Soldiers had to move fast toward the West. Workers had to move too. Supplies had to reach military bases there. Route 66 carried them. Eating places began to appear along the road. Places to buy fuel appeared.

4Places to sleep appeared. They served the people working for the war. The war ended in 1945. People across the country celebrated. Soldiers returned home. They started families. They bought cars. Many wanted to point those new cars west. Sights such as the Grand Canyon were waiting. Route 66 was the way to reach them. The years after the war were strong years for the highway. Shiny new cars rolled up and down it. Yellow Checker cabs rolled along it too. A top hit in 1946 was the song that carried the name Route 66. Its best-known line told listeners to go have fun on that road. The song helped the highway grow famous. In 1960, a new show on television took the same name. It followed two friends. A Corvette carried the two friends across the land. They did not have one fixed destination. They were hunting for adventures along the way. The Nat King Cole Trio sang the first version of the song in 1946. By 1970, a newer highway had taken travelers around most of the old Route 66. Popular culture in America still carried the old road up to the century's close.

5The television show was on the air from 1960 to 1964. The story jumps forward to 2000. His family rode with him on a drive along Route 66. He was the director at Pixar Animation Studios. Playful buildings along the way captured his heart. Bright art along the road captured his heart as well. He chose to turn the drive into a film. The film was a Disney and Pixar movie called Cars. It reached audiences in 2006. Lasseter sent a crew down the highway to study it for the film. Characters in the film grew out of art the crew found beside the road. They also placed real landmarks from the highway into the movie. On screen, Holbrook's Wigwam Motel in Arizona appeared as the Cozy Cone Motel. Cars did so well that many families planned their own trips on the old road. The route became popular once more. A rusty tow truck in Galena, Kansas, gave the idea for a character. That character is called Tow Mater. A map of stops shows Canada and the Pacific Ocean. It shows the United States and California.

6It shows Chicago and Route 66. It shows the Atlantic Ocean and Illinois. It shows Missouri and Los Angeles. It shows Arizona, Oklahoma, New Mexico, and Texas. It shows Mexico. The Gemini Giant stands in Wilmington, Illinois. Paul Bunyon stands in Atlanta, Illinois. The Brooks Catsup Bottle Water Tower stands in Collinsville, Illinois. The Red Rocker stands in Fanning, Missouri. The Blue Whale stands in Catoosa, Oklahoma. Slug Bug Ranch is in Conway, Texas. Cadillac Ranch is in Amarillo, Texas. The Wigwam Motel is in Holbrook, Arizona. Elmer's Bottle Tree Ranch is in Oro Grande, California. The Cabazon Dinosaurs are in Cabazon, California. Chicken Boy is in Los Angeles, California. Museums stand along the road in every state the route crosses. Motels stand there too. Shops stand there too. Travelers also find art that exists nowhere else. They find old stations for fuel. They find theaters. Live shows are part of the trip as well. In New Mexico, a visitor can watch a Native American dance ceremony. A rodeo is one show a visitor can watch in Oklahoma. One promise holds. Boredom is hard to find on Route 66. Cadillac Ranch sits in a field where cows graze.

7In 1974, a group of artists buried ten Cadillacs with their noses in the dirt. The back bumpers point straight up toward the sky. Visitors have coated the cars in spray paint. The cars now show every color of the rainbow. Five Beetles from Volkswagen, bright with color, rise out of the soil nearby. Conway, Texas, is the town that holds the VW Slug Bug Ranch. As a boy, Elmer Long saved bottles. He also saved other things people had thrown out. This folk artist built Bottle Tree Ranch from bottles he reused. He used parts from cars. He used other objects, and buffalo jawbones were among them. Desert wind passing through the open bottles fills the air with music. Which present fits a wife who already owns all she wants? Hugh Davis built his wife a blue whale to mark their anniversary. The whale is 80 feet long. That length is 24.4 meters. For many years, children loved to slide down the whale's body. They loved to dive into the swim spot below it. The Blue Whale in Catoosa, Oklahoma, has a walkway along its back.

8Chicken Boy stands in Los Angeles, California. The Gemini Giant stands in Wilmington, Illinois. Paul Bunyon stands in Atlanta, Illinois. Route 66 really is a land of giants. A factory in California made thousands of huge statues from fiberglass. Businesses along the highway used the statues to catch a driver's eye. The factory added costumes to the figures. Some became cowboys. Some became Native Americans. Some became astronauts. Some even became chickens. Food is the subject of many of the largest statues. Giant oranges wait beside the road. So do giant donuts. So do giant cheeseburgers. So do giant cones of ice cream. The sight can make a rider hungry. Collinsville, Illinois, may show a passing driver the biggest catsup bottle on Earth. It is the Brooks Catsup Bottle Water Tower. Dinny cost 300,000 dollars to build. He was made from materials found along the road. On this highway, creatures from long-ago ages greet visitors in every shape and size. Among concrete dinosaurs in America, Dinny is likely the biggest. He is 150 feet long. That length is 45.7 meters. Cabazon's dinosaur park in California is the place where he stands.

9Claude Bell built the park so people would visit his restaurant nearby. The restaurant was called the Wheel Inn. The park of dinosaurs grew famous. A movie from 1985, Pee-wee's Big Adventure, even showed it. Mr. Rex is a Tyrannosaurus rex as tall as three stories. He is a favorite sight at Cabazon Dinosaurs. Fame brought cars to Route 66. The traffic was heavy. All that weight and use made the highway get worse over time. New interstates went up around the old road between 1956 and the close of the 1980s. An interstate is a fast highway with many lanes. As more drivers chose the interstates, shops on Route 66 had a hard time. Many of them shut. Paint that was peeling became a common sight. Signs that had faded became common. Windows covered with boards became common. Protection for Route 66 became law in 1999 through the Congress of the United States. The government offered money to businesses. It offered money to towns. The money helped pay to keep important landmarks in good shape. Today the old highway is growing popular again. People come back for artwork that makes them smile.

10They come back for a spirit of making new things. The best way to feel that spirit is to drive the road yourself. The drive can give you an American day you will not forget. The Red Rocker stands in Fanning, Missouri. Some words from this account have set meanings. To bypass is to go around something, often so the job is quicker or easier. A convertible is a car whose roof can fold back or come off. To deteriorate means to grow worse as time passes. Fiberglass is a light, strong material made from thin threads of glass. Sometimes those threads are set inside a material like plastic. A highway is a main road linking towns or cities. A landmark may be an important old building or site. It may also be an object on land that marks a place. Preserving means stopping decay, or keeping something as it first was. A road trip is a journey by car or other vehicle to places away from home. To transport is to carry a load from its first place to a second place.

64. The Lives of Snakes

1A black-headed python is one kind of snake. A banded sea krait is a snake of the sea. A horned viper has small horns above its eyes. Thousands of kinds of snakes live in the world. Only a few hundred of those kinds are dangerous. Snakes are unusual animals. Snakes are reptiles. Lizards are reptiles too. Turtles are reptiles too. A snake has a long body. A snake has no arms. A snake has no legs. Snakes are not all the same length. Snakes are not all the same pattern. Snakes are not all the same color. Scientists have identified several thousand snake species so far. A species is a group of living things that are much alike. Living things in one species can reproduce. Some snakes live in the oceans. Some snakes live in the trees. Some snakes live on the land. Snakes have adapted in many unusual ways. Those ways help snakes survive. Antarctica is the one continent where snakes do not live. A few places have no wild snakes at all. Ireland has no wild snakes.

2New Zealand has no wild snakes. Iceland has no wild snakes. Twenty-five years is as long as a wild snake may live. The green anaconda is one of the largest snakes. Nearly 10 meters is the length a grown green anaconda can reach. That length is about 33 feet. The same snake can weigh more than 230 kilograms. That weight is more than 507 pounds. The thread snake is one of the smallest snakes. A thread snake is only about 10 centimeters long. That length is about 4 inches. Like other reptiles, snakes are cold-blooded. Their body temperature changes when the temperature around them changes. A snake basks in the sun when it needs to get warm. A snake rests in the shade when it needs to cool down. Winter makes snakes far less active. Cold winters are survived by some snakes. Those snakes do not hibernate. They go underground to stay warm. They may rest beneath a pile of rocks. They may rest beneath a log. Another way to stay warm is to gather in a group.

3The snakes loop around one another. The group shares body heat. Red-sided garter snakes live in Canada. They hibernate in bunches through the winter. In spring they come out of their dens. They come out so they can mate. The protein in snake scales is the same one found in people's fingernails. A snake skeleton has a long backbone. It also has ribs. It also has a skull. It also has a jawbone. The hard bony skull protects the brain. A snake can have from about 150 vertebrae to more than 400 vertebrae. Vertebrae are the bones of the backbone. A snake can move in many ways because it has so many vertebrae. More vertebrae usually belong to a snake that is very limber. Limber means able to bend and twist with ease. Scales cover a snake's body, as they do on other reptiles. Scales give the body protection. Scales are nearly waterproof. Dryness is easier to keep because of the scales. The snake's skin is where the scales grow.

4Color shows on some of the scales. Camouflage is the job of the scales on other snakes. Camouflage is a color, pattern, or shape that lets an animal blend in. It can also help the animal hide. Walking is impossible for snakes because they lack arms and legs. They use muscles and scales to move. Belly scales grip the surface under the snake. Muscles help the body move from side to side. That side-to-side motion is called a slither. A snake can climb a tree by using muscles together with scales. A bush viper is one of these snakes. For some snakes, shedding lasts as long as fourteen days. The scales can look like one sheet of skin. Snakes do not really shed a whole skin. What comes off is the outer layer of each scale. Most snakes shed up to four times a year. This shedding is called molting. During molting, the snake rubs on a hard object. The object may be a rock. The object may be a log. Rubbing makes the outer layer stretch.

5Then the outer layer splits. Out crawls the snake, and the old layer stays where it was. Snakes shed for more than one reason. Mature snakes shed less often than young ones do. Young snakes are still growing. Growth does not make a snake's skin stretch. Skin on many other animals can stretch during growth. Snakes also shed to get rid of parasites. Parasites are living things that feed on another living thing. Parasites may cling to the old outer layer. Scales protect a snake's body. Scales also protect its eyes. Eyelids are missing on snakes, which sets them apart from many animals. A snake's eyes do not move. A clear scale covers each eye. Drying out is blocked by that clear scale. Blinking is impossible for this reason. Snakes also differ from many animals in how they hear. No ear sits on the outside of the skull. Hearing happens through the jawbone. Inward through that bone travel the vibrations of sound. Vibrations are trembling movements. The vibrations reach the inner ear.

6The inner ear sends a signal to the brain. Snake eyes adapt to light in different ways. The way depends on whether the snake hunts by day or by night. A forked tongue helps a snake find where a smell comes from. The snake uses that tongue to smell. Scent particles stick to the tongue while it is out. The snake pulls the tongue back in. The tongue touches the roof of the mouth. A special organ there identifies the scent particles. Some snakes can sense heat from warm-blooded animals nearby. Pythons can do this. Boas can do this. Pits near the eyes sense even a tiny change in temperature. Pit vipers have two sensitive pits. Those pits send a heat image to the brain. Heat coming off a prey animal lets the snake notice it. Judging distance is easier because of this adaptation. This matters when the snake hunts in the dark. Snakes are carnivores. A carnivore eats meat. Meat from other animals is eaten in more than one way. A bite from some snakes sends venom into the prey.

7Venom is a type of toxin. Other snakes are constrictors. Death comes when a constrictor squeezes its prey. Sneaking up is the method used by some other snakes. They grab it with powerful jaws. Then they swallow it alive. In most snakes, the upper jaw and the lower jaw are loosely connected. The loose connection lets the snake spread its jaws. Large objects can pass into a mouth opened that wide. Snake teeth are mainly made for catching prey. They are also made for holding prey. Fangs curve backward. Regular teeth curve backward too. The backward curve pushes food toward the throat. A snake can push its breathing tube out of its mouth. Then it can breathe while it swallows prey whole. After the prey is swallowed, strong muscles go to work. The muscles move the prey into the stomach. They also move it into the intestines. The stomach walls make strong acids. A liquid-like mass is what those acids make of the prey. That mass is easy to digest.

8The green mamba is one of these hunting snakes. A baby snake may hatch by biting through its shell. Most snakes in cold places mate in late spring. Some of them mate in early summer. Any month of the year can be mating time where the climate is warmer. The male usually goes away once mating is finished. Egg-laying is the path for about two-thirds of all females. They lay the eggs in a safe place. Then those females leave. Snake eggs have soft shells. The shells are leathery. The young grow inside the eggs. Most young grow sharp teeth. They use the teeth to cut the egg and hatch. After hatching, a baby snake must survive on its own. Survival is not easy. Birds eat baby snakes. Some mammals eat baby snakes. Baby snakes are also food for different snakes. Some females keep the eggs inside the body until they hatch. Rattlesnakes do this. Some females give birth to live young. Common garter snakes do this. Some snakes make venom, but not all snakes do.

9The snake injects venom through fangs. The fangs sit on the jaws. Venom is made in sacs behind the eyes. Some fangs are hollow. Venom can flow through a hollow fang from the sac. An eastern diamondback rattlesnake is a venomous snake. Venom flows from the sacs and through the fangs. A dice snake is another kind of snake. Snakes do not chew their prey. They swallow prey whole. Snakes use venom to defend themselves. They also use venom to attack. A defensive bite usually carries more venom. The venom paralyzes the prey. To paralyze means that parts of the body cannot move. Some snakes inject venom and then let the prey go. They track it by smell until it dies. They also track it with heat sensors. Other snakes hold the prey with their teeth until it dies. Some hold the prey with the body. Eating comes after the prey is dead. Among snakes that make venom, not even half can kill a human with it. Poison is one danger, and venom is a different danger.

10A body can take poison in by absorbing it, by eating it, or by breathing it. Venom is injected into a victim. The injection can be a bite. The injection can be a sting. Other animals inject venom by stinging. Scorpions do this. Certain insects do this. An eyelash viper is one kind of snake. A spitting cobra is another kind. A black-collared snake is another kind. A green tree python is another kind. Snakes are among the fiercest predators in the animal kingdom. They have survived in most environments. Specific changes in their senses fit the natural world around them. Some snakes are long. Some snakes are short. Some snakes are large. Some snakes are tiny. Some snakes strike with venom. Some snakes are constrictors. Snakes are fascinating animals. Earth's natural environment needs the part that snakes play. A fang is a long, sharp tooth. Some fangs are hollow. A reptile is a cold-blooded animal with a backbone. Scales or horny plates cover a reptile's body.

65. The Long Story of Wigs

1The parts of this book welcome you, explain what a wig is, and tell what a wig contains. Later parts cover the earliest wigs, baldness in Greece and Rome, French fashion, wigs that grew extreme, and wigs in our own time. A word list closes the book. Human-hair wigs last longer than other kinds. They are also easier to cut and style than wigs made of synthetic fiber. Hair has obsessed people through all of history. From the culture of ancient Egypt to life today, wigs have been part of that fixation. Wearers have chosen wigs for more than one reason. Fashion is one. Rank in society is another. Wig styles include full wigs, toupees, and extensions. A full wig covers the entire top of the head and both sides, down to the neck. A toupee hides only a band of scalp, running forehead to crown and ear to ear.

2At the back, the wearer's own hair still shows. Hair pieces and fiber pieces are what extensions are. Pins, clips, or glue fasten them to a wig or to natural hair. The goal is a thicker look or a longer one. Many materials can form a wig. Wool, animal hair, and straw are three of them. Plastics such as nylon are another. Double-sided tape can hold a toupee against the scalp. Before the 1700s, a wig was often built for a single wearer. The price was so high that most people could not pay it. Makers formed a tight cap on a fabric base sized to that head. The cap received loops of hair, which were then tied. Beeswax, resin, or glue fixed the hair to the base. After that, the hair could be styled to the fashion of the day. In the 1700s, a new kind of wig caught on.

3It could fit more than one person. A ribbon along the cap's edge could be pulled tight so the wig hugged the head. Because many head sizes could wear it, the price dropped. More people could finally afford a wig. Weavers use a tool called a ventilating needle. Holes in the base let the needle pull hair through before the strands are knotted. Egyptian tomb paintings show wigs, and so does Egyptian sculpture. The oldest wigs anyone knows date to 1400 bce in Egypt. Many Egyptians wore them in order to resemble their gods. Wigs also hid bald spots. Thick hair on the head signaled strength and health. People believed the gods had fancy hair that was thick and braided, so wig makers copied that look. In a film about Cleopatra, Elizabeth Taylor wore three different wigs for each hairstyle. Every level of Egyptian society used wigs.

4Human hair, set off with ivory and silver, was the wig worn by the king and his family. Wealthy buyers could pay for wool or for goat hair. The poor could manage only wigs of straw. Daily life in Asian cultures has rarely included wigs. Theater costumes are a different story. For many centuries, wigs have mattered in Japanese theater and in Chinese theater. Styles worn by Greek goddesses, including Aphrodite, may have guided the wigs of Greek women in the same era. In later centuries, rich people also put on false hair. Noblewomen of ancient Greece and of Rome piled wigs on top of their heads. Braids and curls covered those wigs. A higher pile of braids and a more ornate design signaled a higher place in society. Blond hair was the favorite color, and red hair was just as loved. Some Romans kept enslaved people whose hair was blond or red.

5Fresh hair for wigs was then on hand whenever it was needed. Wealthy Romans, both men and women, used wigs to cover bald spots. Certain emperors wore wigs as a disguise. Unrecognized, they could walk outside the palace. They hoped to hear what ordinary people truly thought of them. Julius Caesar ruled Rome from 100 to 44 bce, and his hair was thinning. The loss upset him. His first try was to grow the back long and comb it forward over the scalp. The trick failed. Cleopatra, his girlfriend and the last pharaoh of Egypt, then offered a mixture meant to stop hair loss. Ground-up mice went into it. Horse teeth went into it. Bear grease went into it. The potion failed too. Caesar chose a laurel wreath instead, because it was easier. Battle victory was the meaning of the wreath.

6It also did a fair job of hiding his bald head. After the Roman Empire ended, wigs fell out of favor for several centuries. Early in the seventeenth century they returned. King Louis XIII of France put them back into fashion. He was twenty-three when his hair started to thin. He feared people would respect him less if they saw him going bald. His answer was a wig cut to look like his natural hair. Noblemen wanted to resemble their king, so they adopted the same wig. A portrait shows King Louis XIII. Some illnesses cause hair to fall out. A doctor may advise a wig for a patient in that situation. The wig can lift a sick person's mood. It can also help that person gain more from treatment. Some people now donate long hair so groups can make wigs for children without hair. As court clothing grew more fancy, hairstyles had to grow more fancy as well.

7Demand rose for wigs and for extensions. Women arranged real hair and false hair over a frame, or over a pillow, set on the head. Pig fat set the style, and powder held everything in place. Some of these shapes stood three feet, or 0.91 m, tall. Model ships were built into certain hairstyles. The work was so hard that women left the style in place for months. They neither combed it nor washed it. A cartoon from 1778 shows one example of these wild French fashion wigs. Pig fat in those styles spoiled as days passed. Cockroaches moved in. Mice and even rats built nests in the hair. Women carried a long stick that ended in a claw. The claw let them scratch inside the hair without wrecking the wig. By the eighteenth century, new groups in Western Europe had taken up wigs.

8Doctors wore them, and so did lawyers. Judges wore them, and so did members of the church. Court uniform in the United Kingdom still includes wigs for judges and for lawyers. One picture shows an English court of the eighteenth century. Another shows judges meeting at Westminster Abbey. Toupees became fashionable in that era too. They stayed ornate, and they were still powdered. Their gain was comfort. A toupee was much cooler than a full wig, and much lighter. By the 1850s, many Europeans had given wigs up. Ordinary people had come to see a wig as a luxury that only the rich could buy. The old label bigwig fits Sir Edward Willes, an English judge of eighteenth-century Britain. Bigwig means a person of importance. The word appears in a 1703 English journal named English Spy. Biggest wigs, and the fanciest ones, sat on the richest and strongest people of that age.

9Better technology in the twentieth century let makers spin wigs from nylon threads. Nylon looked like human hair and cost far less. It also came in more colors, so people had more choices of look and of locks. People still put on wigs for many reasons. Some want to seem fashionable and stylish. Some only want a new appearance. A person who is bald, or who is losing hair, may wear a wig in order to feel better. A clown may wear one as part of a costume, and an actor may do the same. Styles keep shifting with the fashion of the moment. Human history still gives wigs an important place, whether long, short, fancy, or plain. Nobody knows what the next great hairstyle will be. More than 200,000 people join a Carnival Parade in clown wigs and costumes.

10An empire is a set of nations or peoples under one ruler or one government. An extension is a length of hair or of fiber. Pins, clips, or glue hold it on a wig or on a person's own hair. Fashion means the popular styles of the moment, or the customs of how people look and act. An obsession is an extreme interest that makes someone think about a person or a thing far more than usual. Ornate means covered with a great amount of decoration. Resin is a sticky, sweet-smelling substance taken from tree sap. A toupee is a false piece of hair that covers part of the head, usually to hide hair that is gone. Wealthy means a person has lots of money or many belongings. A wig is a head covering made from hair or from material that looks like hair.

66. The Olympic Games Through Time

1In 1980, María Colón of Cuba throws in the women's javelin final. The win is hers. A world record at that time came from her throw of 68.4 meters. An Olympic record came from it too. Every two years, top athletes meet from many lands. The Olympic Games are where they test one another. This huge sporting contest draws hundreds of countries. A gold medal goes to first place in each event. A silver medal goes to second place. A bronze medal goes to third place. Where these Games began is a question you may ask. Almost 2,800 years ago is the start. Ancient Greece is the place. A map shows ancient Greece around when those first Games were held. The Black Sea is marked. Mount Olympus is marked. The Aegean Sea is marked. Delphi is marked. Asia Minor is marked. Eretria is marked. Thebes is marked. Athens is marked. Olympia is marked. Corinth is marked. Argos is marked. Sparta is marked. The Ionian Sea is marked. The Mediterranean Sea is marked. Rhodes is marked. Crete is marked. Greek areas are marked. Other lands are marked too. Greek city-states are noted on the map. Regions called city-states were how Greece was split long ago. One main city belonged to each city-state. Wars between these city-states were common.

2The first Olympic Games were created in 776 bce by the people of Olympia. Athletes from every city-state came if they were among the best. Honor for Zeus was the point of the Games. He was king of all the Greek gods. Violence filled the chariot races. Deaths happened in those races. Races were the only events at the start. Fourteen different events came soon after. A stadium held most of the contests. A special racetrack held the chariot races. Danger was part of some events. Hurt was common for the athletes. Death was sometimes the result. The Olympics is the short name many people use. Olympia, the city of the first Games, is where the name comes from. The ancient Olympic grounds appear in an artist's picture. A stadium was there. A horse-racing track was there. A gymnasium was there. A living area was there for athletes. The Temple of Hera was there. A bank was there. A market was there. A swimming pool was there. The Temple of Zeus was there. Areas for boxing were there. Areas for wrestling were there. Only a simple olive branch went to first place. All through Greece, winners were still honored. Money from a hometown often marked a champion's victory. The ancient Greeks were brought together by the Games.

3The hotel in Olympia was used by visitors. The swimming pool was used by them. The sauna was used by them. A market stood on the grounds. A bank stood there too. Business deals were made during the visit. Family reunions were held there too. A religious meaning was part of the Games for some visitors. The huge statue of Zeus in the temple drew many people. A procession, the parade of all the athletes, pleased others. A large feast drew everyone. Peace was the greatest gift of the Olympics for the Greeks. No fighting during the Games was the agreement of the city-states. Travel through Greece felt safe each time the Olympics were held. Sky and thunder belonged to the god Zeus. A lightning bolt is what pictures often put in his hand. Men were the only Olympic athletes at the start. Competing was closed to women. Watching was closed to them too. Watching was allowed for girls. A festival only for female athletes was made by the Greeks in 470 bce. Those Heraean Games are where women athletes compete. Greece was taken over by the Roman Empire in 146 bce. The Games went on anyway. Around 393 ce, Theodosius brought them to an end. Say his name thee-uh-DOH-shee-us. His faith was Christian.

4Every festival that honored the Greek gods was banned by him. Olympia no longer drew people for the Games. Pottery kept the memory. Statues kept the memory. Writings kept the memory. Stories of the Olympics were still heard more than one thousand years later. Olympia was a place many historians hoped to locate. Searches for the grounds began in the 1700s. Most of the old buildings had been located by historians by the 1890s. Olympia mattered to a man from France. Peaceful competition was what he liked about the Games. A new contest for sports across the whole world was his aim. The International Olympic Committee, or IOC, was formed by him in 1894. Around 200 bce, that stadium entrance was built, and Olympia still shows its ruins. Peaceful competition was meant to be the center of the new Games. Love of sports was meant to be the center too. Money was not supposed to be the prize, in the spirit of the old Games. A champion would receive an olive branch along with a medal of silver. Professional athletes, the ones who were paid, would be kept out. Amateur athletes were the first group the Games were for. Rules that blocked paid athletes were eased by the IOC as time passed.

5Before that change, medals were taken from Jim Thorpe. He was an American athlete. He also belonged to the Sac and Fox Nation. Gold medals from 1912 in the pentathlon and the decathlon were stripped away. Fifteen events is the total of those two contests. A short time playing professional baseball was what the IOC learned about. The medals were never returned. Many people still rank him with the greatest athletes ever. The first modern Olympic Games were held by Greece in 1896. A crowd of thousands came to watch. Fourteen countries sent two hundred athletes. Success is what those new Games found. Men run the marathon when the Summer Games of 2008 are held in Beijing, China. Pheidippides is the soldier behind the marathon in those first modern Games. Say his name fy-DIP-ih-dees. He was Greek. The Greeks won a great battle. The place was the fields at Marathon. News of that victory had to be run to Athens. The distance was over 40 kilometers, or 26 miles. The whole way was run by him. The news was shouted by him. Exhaustion then made him fall and die. That same distance is what a modern marathon covers. The two Olympics after that did not go as well. Poor conditions were what the athletes lived in.

6Many events were ruined by bad weather. Those mistakes became lessons for the organizers. Fame came to the new Games. Popularity around the world came too. The 1900 Summer Games in Paris, France, are announced on a poster. An opening parade, an old custom, was taken up in 1906. Country flags were carried as athletes walked into the stadium. A colorful opening ceremony belonged to every Olympics after that. Many ceremonies in 1920 included a new flag for the Olympics. Linked rings appear on the flag. The settled continents, working together in peace, are what the rings stand for. When those 2008 Summer Games end in Beijing, China, the flag is brought down. In 1924, the first Olympic Winter Games were held in France. The athletes who came numbered more than 250. Women and girls made up 11 of them. Norway's Sonja Henie was one skater. Say her name SOWN-yuh HEN-ee. Her age was 12. Last place was where a fall left her. She came back four years later. Gold medals were hers at the Winter Games in 1928, in 1932, and in 1936. Fame followed. Films and large ice shows were where she later starred. Summer Games competition for women began in 1928. Women's events grew longer on the list every year after that.

7The torch relay had its start in 1936. Each Olympics puts it in the opening ceremony. A special guest receives the torch from the last runner. A past Olympic champion is often that guest. The flame that burns during the Games is lit by that guest with the torch. At the opening of the 2000 Olympics, Australian sprinter Cathy Freeman lights the cauldron. An individual gold medal won by an Aboriginal woman came first from her. A symbolic Olympic torch was first lit in 1928. The running of the relay began in 1936. Lighting of the relay torch happens in Olympia. From Olympia, runners carry it to the new host city. Hand to hand, each runner passes the torch onward. Several countries are crossed by the torch. Oceans are crossed before the Games receive it. A new weightlifting world record is set at the London Games of 2012 by North Korea's Un Guk Kim. Risk sometimes reaches the peaceful aim of the Olympics. Three Olympics have been canceled by wars. Troubled times have held other Games. Protest has kept some athletes home. Protest has kept whole teams home. One year was added to the wait for the 2020 Summer Games by a global pandemic. COVID-19 during the Olympics ended the chance to compete for many athletes.

8A hard time was the setting for the 1936 Games in Berlin, Germany. Germany was led by Adolf Hitler. Support for him came from the Nazi Party. A racist group is what the Nazis were. Their actions would cause World War II. That war came a few years later. Racist ideas were spread by them through the 1936 Olympics. Amazing performances came from eighteen Black American Olympians. Those performances embarrassed the Nazis. The runner Jesse Owens was one of them. Several world records were his before the Olympics. A famous college track star is what he already was. Stronger than ever is a fair name for today's Olympic athletes. Better training is one reason. Better equipment is another. In 1924, the men's 50-kilometer cross-country ski race took the winner 3 hours and 45 minutes. The distance is 31 miles. In 2022, that same event was finished by the winner in a time just past 1 hour and 11 minutes. A full-time job is what training is for many Olympic athletes. Youth is often when the training starts. Special centers for Olympic training have been built by many countries. Seven days a week is how Deanna Price trains as she prepares. The photo shows her in a college meet. Chloe Kim won Halfpipe gold at the Winter Olympics in 2018.

9She won Halfpipe gold again in 2022. Olympic officials must watch for cheating. Drugs are used by some athletes so they can grow stronger. Tests for those drugs are given by officials. Medals can be lost if an athlete is caught. A judge may cheat so a home country can win. Wrong points may be given to hurt an athlete or to help one. Points may be traded with judges from other countries. Fair Games for everyone are the IOC's responsibility. A flawless pairs skate came from Canadians Jamie Salé and David Pelletier. Gold was finally theirs after a judging scandal was uncovered. Contests among athletes with different disabilities are what the Paralympic Games show. Italy in 1960 held the first Paralympics. Twenty-three nations sent 400 athletes. More than 4,400 athletes met at the 2021 Paralympics. They came from 162 nations. The pairing with the Olympics began with the Summer Games in 1988 and has continued. Runners who cannot see well are helped by guides in bright yellow vests. The guides keep them in their lanes. A tether, a piece of rope, joins the pair at the wrist. Bigger and more popular is the path the Olympics keep taking. Watching the world's best athletes is a pleasure for many people.

10Amazing surprises often appear. A rich history is the source of the wonder. Peaceful sports between different countries stay exciting after all these centuries. From long ago, or very old, is what ancient means. People trained for sports or games that need strength, speed, and skill are athletes. A formal act or ritual is a ceremony. Lands that each held one major city plus the territory around it were city-states. A contest where people or teams play in order to win is a competition. An orderly formal parade is a procession. Earning pay for an activity, not doing it only for pleasure, is what professional means. A room made for bathing in steam is a sauna. A statue is art you can walk around. It is often formed like a person or like another animal. Ancient Greece and Berlin are topics the back of the book points to. City-states and events are topics too. The International Olympic Committee and Jesse Owens are topics. The marathon and the Nazis are topics. Olympia and the Olympic flag are topics. Ancient Olympic Games and modern ones are topics. The Olympic grounds and the opening ceremony are topics. The procession and Theodosius are topics. The torch, violence, and war are topics. Women and Zeus are topics.

67. The Republic on the Peninsula

1A busy street runs through Seoul. Republic of Korea is the formal name of this country. Iceland is a bit larger than this East Asian country. The southern share of the Korean peninsula is where the country lies. Water borders it on three sides. North Korea is the neighbor that sits nearest. The peninsula's northern share is the home of North Korea. A map of the wider region shows China. It shows Asia. It shows North Korea. It shows Africa. It shows Seoul. It shows Japan. More than nine million people live in Seoul, the capital. Seoul is the largest city in the country. Few cities anywhere on Earth are larger. The city sits on the Han River. The northwest is the part of the country that holds the city. Trade in the modern age is centered in Seoul. It is a modern center of business. It is also an important place in history. The downtown sits inside walls that went up over six hundred years ago.

2Palaces that are centuries old stand inside those walls. Royal gardens stand there too. The city's heart holds one palace from the group known as the Five Grand Palaces. Among many South Koreans, respect for older people matters. Standing up is expected when someone older enters. In a handshake, the younger person waits. The older person is the one who offers a hand. In 2007, a young boy waited to shake the president's hand. The number of people living in South Korea is above forty-nine million. The national language is Korean. Chinese is spoken there as well. Japanese is spoken there as well. English is spoken there as well. People work in many kinds of jobs. Many of those jobs are in manufacturing. Workers make cars to sell in other countries. They make ships. They make technology. Many jobs are in computers and in fields tied to computers. In this society, family carries great weight. Sometimes several generations share a single home.

3Many people keep careful written records of older family members. The records cover those relatives' life stories. Keeping the records is a way to show respect. Holidays bring special food as another sign of respect. Holidays also bring ceremonies. Success, for most Koreans, starts with schooling. Many sources agree on a point about students there. Students in Korea are ranked with the strongest readers in the world. A mother and her son read together in a bookstore. The shop is full of books written in English. Many parents urge their children to learn more than one language. Most of the land is hills or mountains. Farming needs flat ground, and only a small share of the land is flat. Along the coast on the east rise the Taebaek Mountains. The name is said TE-bek. Other ranges of mountains split away from that chain. Those ranges run roughly from east to west. Most peaks are shorter than 1,219 meters. That height is 4,000 feet.

4Mount Halla rises higher than every other peak. Mount Halla is a volcano. It rises 1,950 meters. That height is 6,398 feet. The eastern shore is fairly straight. Rivers and streams break the rest of the shoreline into many pieces. They form more waterways than anyone can count. Small islands along the coast number more than 3,500. A land map marks the Bukhan River. It marks the Taebaek Mountains. It marks the Han River. It marks Seoul. It marks the Korea Strait. It marks Jeju Island. It marks Mount Halla. From the mountains, Seoul in autumn is a beautiful sight. Low ground is traced by streams and by rivers. Tides here rank with the highest found anywhere. Near Seoul, the water level changes by about 9 meters. That change is about 30 feet. The rise is enough to cover a house of three stories. Most people in the countryside live on the coastal plains. Many also live on the inland plains. Those plains provide fertile land for farms.

5Farmers grow rice there. They grow fruits. They grow vegetables. The country has four seasons. Winter temperatures average minus 6 degrees C. That average is 21 degrees F. Summer highs run from 27 to 32 degrees C. Those highs are 80 to 90 degrees F. The season of rain arrives with summer. In that season the country receives more than half of its rain for the year. Dangerous storms sometimes strike during those rains. Heavy flooding is common. Recorded history in the Korean region goes back more than two thousand years. Human life in that region goes back ten thousand years. Distance from other lands left this region on its own. Out of that separation came a people with a shared way of life. They shared one language. They shared one political system. Across the centuries, kingdoms tried to take the land. China once held control of Korea. The Mongolian Empire once held control too. In 1905, Japan took control.

6Korea was turned into a colony of Japan after five more years. Speaking the Korean language was forbidden in those years. Many people protested. They refused to drop their traditions. Control by Japan ended only when Japan yielded as World War II closed. At the war's end, Korea wanted to run its own government. Instead, the land was split into two zones of occupation. Soldiers of the United States held the peninsula's southern half. The northern half was held by a country known as the Soviet Union. Leaders of the nation did not agree on the shape of a new government. Democratic elections were required by the United Nations. South Korea held those elections. North Korea set up another kind of government. A leader was put in place there with no election. In 1948, the United Nations gave official recognition to South Korea's government. The recognition treated that government as the government for both halves. The south was invaded by North Korea after two years.

7The aim was to join the split nation by force. The invasion began the Korean War. The war cost 2.5 million lives. It nearly destroyed both countries. Fighting stopped in 1953 under a cease-fire agreement. Four kilometers of land remained as a strip between the two sides. That width is 2.5 miles. War is still the official state between the two countries. They had agreed to stop the fighting for a time. They never agreed to peace. Soldiers from the south patrol the border. Until the middle of the twentieth century, the south was mainly a land of farmers. Because of that, many big holidays follow the moon calendar. Seollal is the local name for New Year's Day. The name is said SAHL-lahl. Among these festivals, Seollal ranks first. On this day, each person becomes one year older. People mark the day with food. They mark it with gifts. They mark it with games. They mark it with fortune-telling. Chuseok is a festival of the harvest.

8It is another important holiday. Families go to the graves of relatives. They clean those graves. They leave offerings of food to show respect. Parents place objects before a baby when that child turns one. The baby chooses a favorite. A book in the baby's hands means the child will be smart. Money in the baby's hands means the child will be wealthy. A large spread of food at the party stands for wealth for that child. Different kinds of kimchi draw a waiting line of customers in a Seoul market. Food in Korea is full of flavor. Kimchi is a bit like sauerkraut with a spicy taste. Kimchi is served at nearly every meal. Rice is served then too. Fish is an important part of the meals. Seafood is important too. Pounded rice cakes are important as well. Those cakes are called tteok. The name is said DAWK. Some people repeat an old saying about meals. The saying names food as the best medicine.

9They believe health comes from what they eat. They believe sickness comes from what they eat too. Another belief says certain foods can bring a good harvest. Luck is the reason people eat a porridge of rice mixed with red beans. The luck is wished for farmers. Night makes the busy streets of Seoul shine. Across many centuries, the people here grew strong. Hard work was one cause. Respect for their families was another cause. Respect for one another was another cause. That way of living helped them stay strong when times were hard. South Korea is a modern power in the world. The country has a bright future. Its people have a bright future too. A country report from July 2021 lists a set of facts. The population is 51,715,162. The land covers 99,720 square kilometers. That area is 38,502 square miles. The capital city is Seoul. The main language is Korean. The main religions are Christianity and Buddhism.

10The money is called the won. Some words from this account have set meanings. A cease-fire is an agreement to pause the fighting during a war. A colony is a settled area controlled by another nation. Sometimes that control is taken without permission from the people living there. Democratic describes a government run by the people. Free elections, held often, are part of that government. Fertile describes land that produces many plants or crops, or that is able to. A generation is a span of about twenty to thirty years. It is the time between the birth of parents and the birth of their children. Harvest names the season for picking crops and bringing them in. A peninsula is a long piece of land with water around almost all of it. Rural points to life in the countryside, away from city streets. Surrender is the act of giving up. It can mean agreeing to stop a fight, often to save oneself.

68. The Road to a Free Mexico

1Mexico City is one place where people mark Mexican Independence Day. An Aztec calendar is a reminder of Mexico's deep past. Be in Mexico on September 16 and a huge celebration surrounds you. Parades fill the streets. Fireworks light the sky. Food, confetti, and toys are part of the day. Horseback riders take part. Rodeos take part too. Many happy people join in. Mexican Independence Day falls on September 16. Mexicans use the day to honor their country's freedom. Independence means self-rule. After Mexico won it, Mexicans could form their own government. They could control their own economy. They could make their own laws. Mexicans believed the freedom was worth the hard work. They believed it was worth the sacrifice. Mexico lies between the Gulf of Mexico and the Pacific Ocean. Long before Mexico was a country, millions of people lived on the land. These Native peoples had been there since time immemorial. That phrase means a past so old that no one remembers when it began. People from other lands had not arrived yet. Three major civilizations grew on the land. Each one had its own culture. They still shared some ways of living. Hunting animals fed them for a long time. They gathered wild plants for food. Later they began to farm. Maize was their most important crop. Maize is corn. The Olmec were the first great civilization. Home for them was the low coast beside the Gulf of Mexico. The climate let them grow maize.

2They could harvest it twice in one year. Plenty of food helped the civilization grow strong. Around 200 bce, Olmec civilization came to an end. Changes in the environment may have caused the decline. Those changes reduced the water supply. Olmec statues still remain. A tall pyramid among the ruins tells a great deal about Maya life. The Maya were the next major civilization. Their start was around 300 ce. By around 900 ce they were thriving. They excelled in architecture. They excelled in art too. They made astronomy tables. Those tables were quite accurate. They built a number system that included zero. For that time, zero was an advanced idea. The Olmecs did not leave written records of daily life. The Maya did leave written records. Historians know much more about the Maya because of those records. The Maya, like the Olmecs, were an advanced people. Their decline came suddenly. No one knows the exact reasons. Changes in the environment are one possible cause. War is another possible cause. Overpopulation is another. Shifting trade routes are another. A mix of these causes and other factors is possible too. The Aztec were the third great civilization. By 1430 they ruled the land later called the Valley of Mexico. After that they spread across the whole country. They built a grand city named Tenochtitlán. Lake Texcoco held the island where the city stood. Tenochtitlán had 200,000 residents. Another 400,000 people lived nearby. In the early 1500s about 1.2 million people lived in this area.

3Mexico City occupies that ground now. In 1517 a Spanish ship sailed from Spain to Mexico. Francisco Hernández de Córdoba led the ship. White-skinned people were a new sight for Mexico's Native peoples. Sailboats were new to them too. So were cannons and gunpowder. Horses were new to them as well. The Spaniards seemed, to them, to be gods. They thought Quetzalcóatl led those gods. Quetzalcóatl was a god of tribes the Aztecs had conquered. Montezuma was the Aztec emperor. Jewelry was among the gifts Montezuma gave those Spaniards. Precious stones were part of the jewelry. Silver and gold were part of it too. The gifts made the Spaniards want still more riches. On the way home to Spain, Native tribes were attacked. They stole more riches. They kidnapped people to take to Spain. April 1519 brought another group of Spaniards across the sea. Hernán Cortés led this group. Cortés wanted the land to become his own. Native forces outnumbered his group by a great amount. The Aztec Empire then reached places such as Tuxpan and Tenochtitlán. It also included Coatzacoalcos, Huaxyacac, and Tehuantepec. The lands stretched from the Gulf of Mexico toward the Pacific Ocean. Cortés made an alliance with the Tlaxcallan Empire. That group had long been enemies of Montezuma. Cortés marched into Tenochtitlán with his men and his allies. The beauty of the city amazed them. Every building was painted white.

4Montezuma held dinner parties for the Spaniards. He asked them to stay. Cortés answered by imprisoning the Aztec ruler. Indigenous people far outnumbered the Spaniards.
