Cortés needed the Tlaxcallan alliance in order to succeed. Later, Montezuma was killed. No one is sure who killed him. Spaniards may have done it. His own people may have killed him because his actions made them angry. More Spanish fighters arrived soon after. Many Indigenous people died of smallpox, and Aztecs were among them. The Spanish brought that disease to Mexico. When the Spaniards took Tenochtitlán, two-thirds of its people were dead. The dead numbered about 130,000. Ruins of Tenochtitlán still stand. Catholic churches in Mexico remind people of Spanish rule. By 1550 Spain ruled Mexico. Smallpox and other diseases came from Europe. The diseases weakened Indigenous populations. About 20 million Native people lived in Mexico when Spain took the country. One hundred years later, about 1.2 million Native people were left in all of Mexico. Rivalries already existed between Indigenous tribes. Those rivalries helped Spain succeed. The diseases helped too. Spain ruled for three centuries. The rulers called the land New Spain. Wealth was the conquerors' main goal. They stole treasures from Mexico. They set up a system of land holding. Spanish immigrants owned the land. They forced many Native people into slavery. Two classes formed in Mexico. One class was very rich. The other class was very poor. Street protests are allowed for people in Mexico today.

5As years passed, some settlers from Spain had children with Indigenous people. The children carried both Spanish and Indigenous heritage. In time these mixed-heritage people became the majority. They called themselves true Mexicans. They did not call themselves only Spanish. They did not call themselves only Native. By the start of the 1800s, Mexicans longed for their own country. Other countries had fought to win freedom. Those fights gave Mexicans hope. They no longer wanted Spanish rule. They wanted a free land led by Mexicans. A revolution would be needed. A revolution is a fight that removes one government. Another form of government takes its place. Their aim was a government of their own in place of Spain's monarchy. The Creoles were one group in Mexico that thought about revolution. Creoles were born in Mexico. Their parents had come from Spain and settled there. People born in Spain stood above the Creoles in rank. Creoles were treated as second-class citizens. They lacked the privileges of people born in Spain. They were unhappy with how little political power they held. They began planning a revolution. The Spaniards found out about the plans. The Creoles had to begin earlier than they had planned. A priest rang a church bell in the first hours of September 16, 1810. His name was Miguel Hidalgo y Costilla. The town was Dolores. He shouted Viva México. He shouted Viva la independencia. People call this the cry of independence. Hidalgo was urging Mexicans to fight Spanish rule.

6He wanted them to claim Mexico as their own. October closed with 80,000 people in his army. The soldiers conquered two large cities. They conquered a few other places as well. They lost in the biggest city, which was Mexico City. In 1811 the Spanish captured Hidalgo. They killed him. The struggle for independence did not stop. Another priest became the next leader. His name was José María Morelos y Pavón. In 1813 he held a congress. That meeting produced the earliest formal demand that Mexico be independent. A constitution for a republic in Mexico was his writing. In 1815 the Spanish captured Morelos. They killed him. Before the execution he gave a last statement. He said God would know if he had done well. If he had done ill, he asked infinite mercy for his soul. For the next five years, independence had no real leader. Most rebels were dead at the hands of Spanish soldiers by 1816. Spain's king made Mexicans pay taxes. His harsh acts turned many people against Spain. Those people had stayed out of the revolution before. In 1820 a revolution broke out in Spain. Creoles saw a good moment to try again. In 1821 a Spanish colonel was sent to stop a rebellion. His name was Agustín de Iturbide. He brought 2,500 Spanish soldiers. The rebellion was in central Mexico. Spanish troops came into Mexico City. A group of powerful Creoles spoke with Iturbide.

7They persuaded him to turn against his Spanish commander. He agreed to fight on their side. His army would join the Creole fight for independence. In return, Iturbide would set the terms of that independence. In September 1821 he led troops who took Mexico City. The city was the center of Spanish government. He named himself head of Mexico's new independent government. Spanish officials fled. They went back to Spain. Mexico's National Palace stands on the ground where Montezuma's palace stood. Mexico was independent at last. The fight for freedom had lasted 11 years. As many as 600,000 lives may have been lost. The long sacrifice finally brought freedom. The people were free. The Creoles still could not agree on a plan for government. Some wanted a monarchy with a king in charge. Others wanted a republic like the one in the United States. In a republic, the citizens hold the power. In 1822 Iturbide chose the plan himself. He created a monarchy. He named himself Emperor Agustín I. He did not rule well. The people turned against him. By 1823 he was dethroned. Dethroned means he was removed from power. A congress was then set up. A democratic country's constitution came from that congress. The plan was similar to the United States. Big disagreements were still there. A strong government at the center was the wish of one group. That group wanted Roman Catholicism as the national religion. Another group wanted more power for the states.

8That group wanted people free to worship as they chose. In 1824 the groups finally reached a compromise. Each side gave up some demands so an agreement could be made. Mexico became a republic. A national government was formed. A congress with two houses led it. Each state had a governor. Each state had a legislature too. Guadalupe Victoria was the first president. The year 1821 saw the making of Mexico's national flag. Green stands for independence. White stands for religious purity. Red stands for the Spanish people who joined the Mexican fight. Red also stands for the blood of Mexican heroes. Cinco de Mayo is May 5th. It is not Mexican Independence Day. It marks a victory of a small Mexican force over the French army. The battle was the Battle of Puebla. During Independence Day events, a girl in traditional dress may ride a horse. Mexicans will not forget the events that brought independence. Each year the day is a huge fiesta. Fiesta is a word for a celebration. The fiesta happens all through Mexico. Nightfall on September 15 is when the celebration begins. People gather in town centers across Mexico. In Mexico City, about half a million people pack the city square. Everyone watches the clock. The crowd grows quiet when the clock hits 11 pm. The president of Mexico steps from the palace onto the balcony. Red, white, and green are the colors of the sash he wears.

9He carries a large Mexican flag. He rings the same liberty bell Hidalgo rang. Hidalgo had used the bell to call people to fight Spain for freedom. The president shouts Viva México. He shouts Viva la independencia. Hidalgo had used those same shouts years before. Loudspeakers carry the president's voice. Crowds fill city squares for the celebration. Traditional clothing is only one piece of the Independence Day events. The crowd screams Viva México. The crowd screams Viva la independencia. People toss confetti. People blow horns. Everyone sings the national anthem of Mexico. An anthem is a song of pride in a nation. Fireworks in red, white, and green light the sky. Matching ceremonies happen across Mexico at that same hour. The next day is September 16, Independence Day. Parades are part of the celebration. Feasts are part of it. Rodeos are part of it. Horseback riding competitions are part of it. Flowers in red, white, and green decorate statues of Hidalgo. The day is a fine celebration. It honors a fight that was long and hard and, in the end, rewarding. A statue of Hidalgo keeps that memory present. A timeline can show the path to independence. By 15,000 bce, the first known Native inhabitants were living in Mexico. The Olmec civilization prospers in the years 1200 to 200 bce. From 300 to 900, the Maya civilization prospers. From 1300 to 1520, the Aztec civilization prospers. The year 1325 is when Tenochtitlán, the Aztec city, is begun.

10It is begun on Lake Texcoco's island. In 1520 Hernán Cortés takes Tenochtitlán for Spain. Enemies of Montezuma fight on the side of Cortés. From 1520 to 1810, Mexico is a Spanish colony. Father Miguel Hidalgo y Costilla is the leader of the 1810 revolution against Spain. Agustín de Iturbide is the leader, in 1821, of the war that wins independence. Mexico wins independence in that year. In 1822 Iturbide becomes Mexico's first emperor. In 1824 Mexico becomes a republic. Guadalupe Victoria becomes Mexico's first president. Independence means being free of another group's control. It also means being free of that group's influence, support, or help. Civilizations are groups of people who share one way of living. Native describes someone who belongs to a place by being born there. A compromise is a choice in which each arguing side yields something. The yields let both sides agree. A republic is a government whose power sits with the citizens. Creoles were born in Mexico, while their parents were born in Spain. A revolution takes one government out of power. A different government is put in its place. A ruler who is dethroned has been put out of power. Time immemorial is a past too far back for people to know or recall. Fiesta is a Spanish word for a festival. Immigrants are people who leave one country and settle in another. An anthem is a song that carries a nation's pride.

69. The Science Behind Cooking Food

1Topics in this book come in a set order. Cooking and science come first. What food contains comes next. What cooking is comes after that. Sweet changes follow. Protein changes follow those. Fat in the kitchen comes next. Favorite foods close the story. Meanings of key words come last. Picture the meal you like best. If dinner tonight could be anything, what would you pick? A salad is one answer. Most other answers are foods that have been cooked. Cooking adds heat. Flavor changes. Texture changes. Color changes. Boiling in water is one method. Frying in oil is another. Baking in an oven is another. Grilling over flames is another. Many further methods exist. Cooking and eating happen every day. The question is how cooking works. Science is a bigger part of the answer than you might expect. Heat reaches food in different ways, depending on the method. Textures and flavors then take many forms. Food comes in many kinds. Most of what people eat is built from three major nutrients.

2Proteins are one. Carbohydrates are one. Fats are one. The body needs all three. Energy is one reason. Building cells is another. Like everything else, these nutrients are made of tiny particles called atoms. Bonding of atoms produces molecules. Molecules can bond into large groups. They can also bond into chains. Digestion breaks those chemical bonds apart. The body can then absorb nutrients, use them, and store them without waste. A water molecule is written H2O. One atom of oxygen and two atoms of hydrogen make that molecule. Salt holds one sodium atom, written Na, and one chlorine atom, written Cl. Nutrients arrive from many foods. Amounts and types are not the same in every food. Meat, beans, and eggs are among the easiest protein sources. Every body cell uses proteins. Jobs for proteins are many. Hair and fingernails are built with their help. Muscles are formed with their help. Oxygen rides through the blood with their help. Some people call complex carbohydrates good and simple ones bad. A healthy diet can include both.

3Carbohydrates matter a great deal. Fruits hold them. Vegetables hold them. Grains hold them. The body treats them as fuel. That is why athletes often eat a high-carbohydrate meal before a contest. Fiber, starch, and sugar are what carbohydrates are made of. Fiber and starch are the complex kind. Sugar is the simple kind. Simple carbohydrates are digested fast. Energy rises quickly. Fruits contain the simple kind. Dairy contains it too. Complex carbohydrates take longer to digest. They supply more energy. Grains contain the complex kind. Starchy vegetables, potatoes among them, contain it too. The third of the major nutrients is fat. Oils hold them. Butter holds them. Nuts, meat, and dairy hold them too. Stored energy can last a long time because of fat. Other jobs belong to fat as well. Organs get protection. Warmth is provided. Outer layers of cells are built with its help. Getting food ready to eat takes many processes. Vegetables get chopped. Ingredients get mixed. Things get boiled in water. Items get baked in an oven.

4Each process changes the food somehow. Physical changes are one kind. Chemical changes are the other kind. Shape or form is what a physical change alters. The atoms inside the item stay the same. Chopping a carrot changes its shape. Smaller pieces are the result. The pieces are still carrot. A pot of water that receives heat will, in time, begin to boil. Water molecules start moving faster. They also spread apart. Spreading turns liquid water into a gas called water vapor. Bubbles in the boil are that vapor. The form of the water has changed. The molecules are still water. A chemical change is different. Molecules react with one another. Some bonds break. New bonds form. New molecules appear. New substances appear. Turning a chemical change backward is often impossible. Bread shows this, and the ingredient list is short and simple. One item is flour. Yeast is one. Water is one. Mixing makes dough. Baking follows. Every stage splits molecules and lets them join in new ways.

5No path leads back from the loaf to the separate ingredients. The molecules have changed. A new substance has been made. Gluten belongs to this kind of change in bread. Water joins flour, and two proteins from the flour, and the result is gluten. Heat is usually what cooking adds, and the aim is a chemical change. Color can shift. Texture can shift. Flavor can shift. Digestion can get easier too. Raw potatoes hold some complex carbohydrates the body cannot fully digest. Wheat flour holds some of those too. Cooking breaks those complex carbohydrates into smaller molecules. The body can digest the smaller ones. Safety is another result. Harmful bacteria sometimes live on food. Harmful fungi sometimes live on it too. They need the same nutrients people need. Sickness can follow. Very high heat is usually too much for them. Cooking usually kills those bacteria and fungi. The food is then safer to eat. Among cooking reactions, caramelization is one you will meet often. It happens when heat meets carbohydrates.

6Many foods go through it. Caramel candy is made this way. Heated sugar molecules begin to move. The sugar melts. A solid becomes a liquid. More heat makes the molecules collide. They break apart. They join again as new molecules. During this, the sugar turns brown. It also grows sweeter. Caramel is the result. Melting and boiling drive water out of the sugar. That loss lets the molecules react. A caramel molecule may taste sweet. It may taste nutty. It may taste buttery. Some caramel hardens as it cools. Longer heating makes the sugar softer and stickier. Onions break down more the longer they cook. Sweetness rises too. Full caramelization of onions takes about an hour. Sugars in fruits go through the same kind of change. Vegetables and other foods do too. Many recipes open by caramelizing onions. A raw onion is crispy. It is also acidic. Cooking makes it soft and sweet. Heat breaks the carbohydrates in the onion apart. Those carbohydrates are sugar molecules. Smaller molecules are the pieces.

7The smaller pieces taste sweeter. The sugars turn brown. Cooked onions get their brown color from that change. Broken carbohydrates also soften the vegetable's structure. Color, flavor, and texture all shift under caramelization. A similar change hits foods that hold both proteins and sugars. The name is the Maillard reaction. The French chemist Louis-Camille Maillard is the source of the name. He first described the reaction in 1912. Meat is a common place to see it. Raw meat is usually red or pink. It can look slimy. Cooked meat turns brown. The texture grows firmer. Heat on a steak, or on a hamburger, lets proteins meet simple carbohydrates and react. New kinds of molecules appear. The reactions repeat again and again. Many new molecules are added. Flavor comes from them. Cooked meat gets its brown color from those molecules too. That is why people often call this the browning reaction. Full understanding of the Maillard reaction is still out of reach. Proteins come in many forms. Carbohydrates come in many forms.

8Ways for them to interact are many. Distinct flavors and smells in meats follow from that variety. Toasted bread is another example. More time in the toaster lets more molecules react. Color grows darker. Flavor grows stronger. Heat on an egg makes protein molecules unwind. New bonds form among them. Eggs make this protein reaction easy to see. A raw egg looks like a slimy liquid. Cooking makes it firmer. The reaction is called denaturation. Heat makes protein molecules change shape. They bond with nearby proteins. New shapes and new links make the egg firmer. The change cannot be undone. A cooked egg cannot become raw again. Fats help cooking in several ways. Flavor and texture are added. Heat spreads more evenly. Pots and pans are less likely to grab the food. Melting is one main effect of heat on fats. Melting counts as a physical change. Tight packing is how molecules sit in cold butter. That packing keeps the butter solid. Motion never fully stops. Even solid butter has molecules that vibrate.

9Added heat makes them move more. They slide past one another. Solid butter becomes liquid. Take the heat away, and the molecules slow down. The butter turns solid again. Milk is the source of butter, so protein and carbohydrates are in it too. More heat on melted butter brings the Maillard reaction. Too much heat on butter, or on other fats, starts another chemical change. Burning begins. Molecules in burned fat, or in other burned food, break down. New molecules taste burned and smell burned. Food can turn black. The new molecules can leave as smoke in the air. Return to the meal you like best. Which nutrients are in it? Do they pass through a physical change, a chemical change, or both? Candy goes through important changes. A steak dinner does too. So does all the food people buy, eat, and cook each day. Favorite smells come from those changes. Favorite flavors come from them. Stomachs stay full. Bodies stay energized. Knowing the science can guide healthier choices.

10It can also make food more enjoyable. Caramelization, a noun, is the process in which heat splits sugar molecules, so food turns golden brown and tastes sweet. Ingredients, a noun, are the foods a recipe uses. The Maillard reaction, a noun, is the process in which high heat splits sugar and protein molecules, so food browns and the taste grows more complex. Collide, a verb, means to crash into something. Complex, an adjective, means made of many parts. Molecules, a noun, are the smallest pieces of a substance that can exist alone, and each one holds two or more atoms. Digests, a verb, means the body works on food and makes energy from it. Nutrients, a noun, are materials a living thing needs so it can live and grow. Distinct, an adjective, means different enough to notice. Reversed, a verb, means undone, or turned into the opposite. Efficiently, an adverb, means with good use of time or of resources. Texture, a noun, is how something feels when touched.

70. The Scientist Who Questioned the Sky

1Music, a ceiling lamp, and the planet Venus all belong in one scientist's story. Each of those things helped a discovery happen. History ranks him with the greatest scientists. About five hundred years have passed since his life. Galileo Galilei is his name. Italy is the country where he was born. 1564 is the year of that birth. The Italian Renaissance was the age around him. Excitement filled those years. Discovery filled them too. Painters of that age formed new ideas. Writers formed new ideas. Mathematicians formed new ideas. Musicians formed new ideas. Builders formed new ideas. Scientists formed new ideas. Renaissance man is a name that fits Galileo. The name means skill in several subjects at the same time. Many fields gave him ideas to use. Scientific discoveries grew from that mix. Questions were the habit he did best. That habit later brought him real trouble. The same questions changed how the world is understood. His Italy lies along the Mediterranean Sea.

2France is a nearby land. Spain is a nearby land too. Pisa is a city in Italy. Rome is a city in Italy too. Corsica lies in that sea. Sardinia lies in that sea. Sicily lies in that sea. The Atlantic Ocean is the water farther west. England appears farther north on a map of this region. Germany appears there too. Austria appears there too. Switzerland appears there too. Vincenzo Galilei was the father of Galileo. Playing music was Vincenzo's work. Writing music was his work as well. He wanted to understand music's workings. He taught the young boy to stay curious. Curiosity stayed with Galileo for life. He paid close attention to things around him. He kept notes on what he noticed. He then tried to explain the notes. Math drew him in as a young man. Physics drew him in too. Matter is one part of what physics studies. Energy is another part.

3Motion is another part. A major physics find came a year before university. 1582 was the year. Wind blew that day. Galileo entered a church. A large chandelier hung above him. This lamp is a fancy one that hangs from above. Branches that hold lights are common on it. Outdoor wind set the chandelier moving. Some swings stretched only a few inches across. Other swings stretched as far as a few feet. The timing was the strange part. A wide swing lasted as long as a narrow one. The chandelier lesson was about keeping time. A weight moving back and forth can mark equal times. He later drew up the first clock that worked by a swinging weight. Clocks of this kind are still in use. Grandfather clocks use a pendulum for the same job. Math classes were not his start at the University of Pisa. He joined that school in 1583. His father wanted a doctor in the family.

4Medicine never gripped him as math and physics did. The degree was never finished. Lack of money pushed him out in 1585. Tutoring math filled the following four years. University life pulled him back before long. He returned to the University of Pisa in 1589. Teaching mathematics was his job then. Padua soon invited him to teach math. His time in Padua lasted eighteen years. Tests of his ideas went on through the teaching years. Gravity was one interest. This natural force pulls objects toward one another. One pull is toward the center of Earth. Several experiments used falling objects. Those tests found one shared speed for a heavy object and a lighter object. Famous finds were still ahead of him. The sky is where the largest finds began. A famous tale is told about the Leaning Tower of Pisa. The tale says a young professor let two cannonballs drop. One ball weighed much more than the other.

5The tale says both reached the ground together. People today cannot be sure the tower drop really took place. Word of a spyglass from the Netherlands reached him in 1609. Faraway things looked much nearer through that tube. He had never looked through one. He still worked out a build of his own. He improved that first build. Distant objects could appear thirty times bigger. What he had made was a telescope. This tool makes far things seem close. He turned it toward the heavens. That choice shaped the rest of his life. Astronomy changed because of it. This science studies stars. It studies planets. It studies comets. It studies other things found in space. He looked over the solar system and met one surprise after another. A solar system is a family of space objects traveling around a star. No European before him had observed sunspots. Those are dark patches that show on the Sun at times.

6He found four moons, the largest ones Jupiter has. No one before him had seen Venus change shape. The Moon changes shape in a like way. Venus can look full. Venus can look new. Venus can look like a crescent. Each of those looks is a phase. A phase is one of the ways a planet or moon can appear from Earth. Some finds went into a book in 1610. He published them, which means he made the writing public. The Starry Messenger was the book's name. The book brought fame. A price came with the fame. He sketched the Moon in 1610 as the telescope showed its surface. NASA later sent a probe that carried his name. The year of that Moon trip was 1992. The probe scanned the surface in detail. Many of the same features he noticed are clear in that scan. During his lifetime, lots of people set Earth in the middle of our solar system.

7They expected the other planets to travel around Earth. They expected the Sun to travel around Earth too. Their picture of Venus used small circles inside one big circle around Earth. In that picture, a crescent would be the only shape of Venus seen from Earth. His telescope showed Venus with many shapes. He was looking from Earth. One shape was full. One shape was nearly full. One shape was half. One shape was a crescent. One shape was new. Those shifts in shape pointed to another route. The route for Venus seemed to go around the Sun. The route for Earth seemed to go around the Sun too. These solar-system finds put him in deep trouble. His ideas about the planets did not please the Catholic Church. The religious beliefs of that age explain why. For centuries the Church had taught a single sky picture. The Sun, in that teaching, moved around Earth. The Moon moved around Earth.

8The planets moved around Earth. The stars moved around Earth. Venus and its changing shape gave a large clue. The clue said Venus and the other planets moved around the Sun. He was not the first to suggest that idea. His finds were still the best proof yet. The proof said Earth was not the center. He published writings on what he had found. The Church grew angry at those writings. In the Renaissance, the Church held power to punish beliefs it would not allow. He had been bold enough to call the Church wrong. The year 1633 brought a trial. The Church put him there. A trial is a court path that decides guilt or innocence. The finding was guilty. Death was held over him as a threat. He had to call his earlier finds lies. Those finds were accurate, which means correct and exact. Leaving home was not allowed for the rest of his life.

9Publishing was banned for him too. A liked but unproved story says he spoke back to the judges. The legend has him claim, very quietly, that Earth moves anyway. He had just been pushed to deny that Earth travels around the Sun. Records of the trial do not hold this story. The story is probably false. The short claim that Earth moves anyway still grew famous. People repeat it to this day. He went on writing about what he had found. He even got a book out after the Church told him not to publish. Two New Sciences was that book. 1638 was its year. It was his final published work. Blindness had come by that point. His health kept growing worse. Death came in 1642. Natural causes were the reason. People now know his view of Earth's path was right. Earth travels around the Sun. Great discoveries of his age grew from his skill at asking questions that mattered.

10Many of his ideas are still in use by scientists. Math, he said before anyone else, can be used to state how nature's laws work. Those laws are scientific truths that explain how nature works. He was also among the first to demand measurable data in experiments. Measurable means a size or an amount can be fixed in units. Other scientists could test his ideas more easily because of that demand. He set an important example for people in science who came later. Isaac Newton was one who followed. Albert Einstein was another. Father of modern science is what he is called today. In 1757 his book was taken off a list of banned books. The Catholic Church made that change. In 1992 the Church formally said he had done no wrong. The Church of today accepts many scientific facts that have been proved. For centuries it has given support to scientific research.

71. The Silver Prize of Hockey

1In 2012, Jordan Nolan played for the Los Angeles Kings. He carried the Stanley Cup through his hometown. That town is Sault Ste. Marie. Sault Ste. Marie is in Ontario. Ontario is in Canada. A 1996 photo shows a rough moment by a net. Dino Ciccarelli played for the Detroit Red Wings. His sweater showed number 22. A shove sent him into the other goalie. The goalie was John Casey. John Casey played for the St. Louis Blues. The moment came during Game 7. That game was a Western Conference final. Fans call the Stanley Cup hockey's greatest prize. Those same clubs met in the 1996 Game 7. The winner would move on to a larger series. That series is the playoff final of the National Hockey League. People shorten the league name to NHL. The score stayed tied at 0-0. The tie lasted into a second overtime period. Steve Yzerman was the captain of Detroit. Say his last name EYE-zur-mun. He gathered the puck.

2He raced to the blue line. He shot the puck into the net. The building started to shake. Thousands of fans cheered. Many people place that goal among the most exciting in hockey history. Men's hockey clubs share one aim every season. They want to win the Stanley Cup. First the regular season ends. Then the real season starts. Fans feel the biggest thrill during the playoff season. The Cup is named for Lord Stanley of Preston. He was Governor General of Canada. He held that office from 1888 to 1893. In 1889, he watched a hockey game for the first time. He fell in love with the sport. He decided a trophy should be made. The trophy would go to the top team. The Cup was finished in 1892. Canada's top hockey team would receive it. A deal in 1915 let two hockey leagues chase the prize. Later those leagues joined with others. New leagues formed as well. Lord Frederick Arthur Stanley was born in 1841.

3His life ended in 1908. In 1926, only one league still competed for the Cup. That league was the NHL. The prize received a longer name. People called it the Stanley Presentation Cup. In 1947, the name became the Stanley Cup. The NHL then had only six clubs. One club played in Toronto. Another played in Montreal. Another played in Detroit. Another played in Chicago. Another played in New York. Another played in Boston. Those same clubs still play in the NHL. Fans call them the Original Six. The first Cup was silver. It stood 7.2 inches high. That height is 18.5 cm. It was 11.4 inches wide. That width is 29 cm. The year 1963 sent the first Cup to a museum. That museum is the Hockey Hall of Fame. A newer Cup is the one used today. Five bands circle the bottom. With all five bands, the Cup is 35.25 inches tall. That height is 89.54 cm. Among major sports in North America, hockey is alone in one habit.

4Year after year, the sport keeps that one trophy. Winner names are shown on the bands. A full band is moved to that same museum. A new band is then placed on the Cup. A 2019 chart shows the road through the conferences. Teams travel a long road before they can lift this prize. The road is also difficult. Hockey fans would not want that road to be easier. The league sorts thirty teams under two conferences. Metropolitan is one division inside the Eastern Conference. It also has the Atlantic Division. Central is one division inside the Western Conference. It also has the Pacific Division. Only sixteen teams out of thirty reach the playoffs. A division sends its three leading teams onward. Each conference then fills two more places. Those places go to the next two highest teams. Once the playoffs start, four rounds remain. Each round is a best-of-seven series. Teams play conference quarterfinals first. Conference semifinals come next.

5Conference finals come after that. The Stanley Cup finals come last. The Eastern Conference champion meets the Western Conference champion. Many fans call that series the most thrilling playoff in sports. The 2019 chart lists the clubs in the chase. Eastern names include Tampa Bay and Columbus. Boston and Toronto are listed too. Carolina and Washington appear as well. New York is on the chart. The New York Islanders are on the chart. Pittsburgh is shown too. Western names include St. Louis and Nashville. Dallas and Winnipeg are listed too. San Jose and Calgary appear as well. Colorado is on the chart. Vegas is on the chart. The chart gives one series result. St. Louis won 4-3. One photo shows Willie Mitchell. He played for the Los Angeles Kings. He holds the Stanley Cup up high. Mount Benedict is under his feet at the summit. Mount Benedict is in Canada. Few playoffs in sports ask as much as this Cup chase. Winners can act excited once they hold the Cup.

6They can act silly too. Clark Gillies played for the New York Islanders. He let his dog take food from the Cup. André Roy played for Tampa Bay. He had the Cup carry an engagement ring. Three different teams dropped the Cup into a pool. They wanted to test whether it would float. The Cup sinks. In 1928, New York mayor Jimmy Walker handed over the Cup. The champions that year were the New York Rangers. A famous stoppage came in that final. Game 2 matched the Rangers with the Montreal Maroons. The game had to wait. Hurt struck the goalie who played for New York. He had to leave the ice. Clubs in that era kept only one goalie. Coach Lester Patrick asked to use a goalie from Ottawa. That goalie was sitting in the stands. The Maroons coach wanted his club to win. He said no. Patrick's players asked him to go in goal. They promised to play extra hard.

7Lester Patrick wore number 9. He played for nine different teams. His career lasted twenty-four years. The New York Rangers were one of those teams. Minutes later he skated out in full goalie gear. He had been a defense player. He had not played goalie very much. Who won the Stanley Cup could depend on this game. Before he faced a shot, he spoke to his team. He told them not to fail him. He allowed only one goal. The championship later went to New York. Patrick was forty-four years old. He became the oldest player to take part in a Cup final. His record still stands today. That 1942 win over Detroit stopped a long Toronto wait. The Maple Leafs had gone nine years without the Cup. In that final, Toronto trailed in the series. The series score was 3-0. Game 4 brought a risky choice from the Maple Leafs coach. He took out two star players. He put in two newer players.

8He wanted the club to work harder. His plan worked. Toronto's comeback was amazing. They won the next four games. A later note tells of another return. The Boston Bruins beat the St. Louis Blues. Boston had the Cup again after the win. Boston had waited twenty-nine years. In 1970, Game 4 of the final reached overtime. The Boston Bruins were chasing a sweep. They wanted to beat the Blues 4-0. Bobby Orr was the Bruins captain. He stood before the net of St. Louis. A teammate passed him the puck. Orr sent the puck past the goalie. At that moment, another player tripped him. Orr flew through the air. He looked like a superhero. Many people call that picture the most famous photo in hockey. The Detroit Red Wings added a giant octopus in 2008. The octopus was inflatable. It helped start their games. Hockey games have produced many odd events. The octopus story is the strangest. Detroit in 1952 is where the custom started.

9A champion then needed eight wins. The wins came in two best-of-seven series. Two fans from Detroit made a toss during one game. The toss landed on the ice. The thrown object was an octopus. The octopus was dead. Eight legs stood for eight needed wins. Detroit won the Cup that year. People came to see the octopus as good luck. A single game in 1995 brought a bigger toss of animals. The ice was covered with them. The count was thirty-six. Twenty seasons in the NHL filled Wayne Gretzky's career. Say his name GRET-ski. During the 1980s, the Edmonton Oilers looked like a club of stars. Gretzky led them. They won five Stanley Cups. The five wins came within seven years. Terry O'Reilly was a coach in Boston. He talked about Gretzky. He called Gretzky the best. He said a rule should send Gretzky to a different club each year. Gretzky was not the only Oilers star. Six other Oilers from that time entered the Hockey Hall of Fame.

10No club since has been called a dynasty. Hockey gives a second big prize called the Isobel Cup. It is named for a daughter of Lord Stanley. Women's hockey counts her among its first leaders. In 2016, the prize received a clear job. It goes to the top professional team in women's hockey in North America. A photo shows the Boston Pride celebrating. They had won a championship. The prize in the photo is the NWHL Isobel Cup. The date is March 27, 2021. Every year, fans hold one hope. They want a favorite team to win the Stanley Cup. Players have wanted a drink from this prize across more than a century. They skate for that chance. They score for that chance. They make saves for that chance. No person can say what a Cup playoff will bring. Any team might win. The real winners are the fans. Those fans live around the world.

72. The Story of Chocolate

1One shop that sells chocolate stands in Ontario, Canada. The shop gets cocoa beans from the people who grow them. Those growers live in Central America and in South America. Contents. The opening section is marked 4. The section on the source of chocolate is marked 5. The section on getting beans ready is marked 8. The section on turning beans into chocolate is marked 11. The section on chocolate history is marked 15. The section on chocolate now is marked 19. The closing section is marked 22. The word list is marked 24. Opening. Ask someone to name a sweet they like best. Chocolate is the answer you will probably hear. Love for chocolate reaches people in every part of the world. No other sweet is eaten in a greater amount each year. Where the chocolate starts. Learning that chocolate grows on trees may surprise you. A finished bar still cannot be picked from a branch. What grows on the tree does not look like candy. The taste is different too. The smell is different too. The tree has a name, cacao, said kah-KOW. Beans inside big pods are the start of chocolate. The trees need a place near the equator. Heat and wet weather are what they need.

2A map uses shade to show those growing places. The shaded band sits close to the equator. Blossoms do not come out on the branches. They come out on the trunk. Tiny flies carry pollen to the blossoms. Large farms grow most cacao trees today. Many small flowers appear on the trees. Flowers open through the year. Only some of those flowers become pods. Most of them never do. A new pod starts out green. A ripe pod may look golden. A ripe pod may look dark red instead. Beans for chocolate sit inside those pods. Getting the beans ready. Workers pick pods from the cacao trees. That picking begins the path to chocolate. The pod shell is not the prize. Each pod holds from 20 to 50 beans. Those beans are the tree's seeds. A great many beans are needed. A great deal of work is needed too. Gathering the pods comes first. A person then splits each pod. The blade is large and is called a machete, said mah-SHET-ee. Someone scoops the beans from the split pods. Brown is the color you might expect. Cream is the color you see. Soft pulp coats each bean. The beans are nowhere near ready to eat as chocolate.

3Open a pod and the beans show themselves. Piles or boxes hold the beans. A cover goes over them. Air reaches the beans. Cream color shifts toward purple. The inside of each bean changes as well. People call that inner change fermentation. More than seven days may pass during fermentation. Chocolate taste is still missing. Chocolate smell is still missing. One worker lays the fermented beans out in a flat layer. Sun drying may also last more than seven days. Drying follows fermentation. Workers turn the beans during drying. Large cloth bags are filled next. Those bags travel to the people who make chocolate. Beans become chocolate. Dried beans that have fermented do not turn into chocolate quickly. Many steps make the work hard. Cleaning comes before anything else. Leftover pulp has to go. Bits of pod have to go. Makers then mix beans of different kinds. The mix is how they hit the flavor they want. Roasting comes after the beans are clean and blended. Flavor grows stronger in the roast. Only then does a chocolate smell rise from the beans. The thin outer shell grows easy to crack. A machine takes the beans next. Shells crack inside that machine. Fans blow the cracked bits off.

4At a candy factory, giant drums do the roasting. What remains is the meaty center, called the nib. Grinders crush the nibs. Crushing continues until a liquid forms. Molds receive that liquid. Cooling turns it into bars. The bars taste bitter. No sugar has been added yet. Presses squeeze the bars. The goal is to pull out cocoa butter. Cocoa butter is a yellow liquid. It is the key part of chocolate. A screen lets the cocoa butter drain. Workers save it for the chocolate people eat. Two words to compare. Is it cacao or cocoa? Tree products traveled to places where English is spoken. Speakers there changed cacao into cocoa. Writers still choose cacao when they mean the tree. They also choose it for flowers, for pods, and for beans. Cocoa is the usual word for products made from those beans. Pull cocoa butter away and a solid remains. That solid is ground until it is powder. Dairies receive the powder. Bakeries receive it too. Baked goods use it. Dairy foods use it. Conching is the name of the final step. Big rollers inside machines do this work. Sugar is rolled with the other items. Cocoa powder is one of those items. Cocoa butter is another.

5Further ingredients join them. Rolling continues until the mix feels smooth. Molds receive the smooth mix. Cooling makes it solid. Workers lift the chocolate from the molds. Packages wrap it so it can be shipped. Roasting is step one. Shelling is step two. Grinding is step three. Mixing is step four. Conching is step five. Pouring is step six. Cooling is step seven. Wrapping is step eight. How chocolate began. Historians believe chocolate use in Mexico is very old. Central America has that same long history. The start was almost 2,000 years ago. People there drank chocolate. A taste today would not match modern chocolate drinks. Bitterness was the main flavor. Coffee is a fair comparison. A French book from 1685 includes a drawing. The drawing shows an Aztec man holding chocolate. Hernan Cortes was an explorer from Spain. He helped people in Europe notice the worth of cacao beans. Montezuma, the great Aztec ruler, offered Cortes a drink. Cacao beans were the base of that drink. Aztec people said their gods drank it. They also believed the beans added wisdom. They believed the beans added strength. Beans even served as money. Cortes cared more about that money use than about drinking. He planted cacao trees for a supply of bean money.

6Growing the trees was his method for growing money. Montezuma welcomed Cortes as a guest. Later, Cortes and his men seized the Aztec leader. Many Aztec people were killed. Gold was what Cortes wanted. Chocolate was not. Cortes returned with know-how for making the drink. Places outside the Americas still did not like it much. Spanish cooks started stirring sugar into the drink. Popularity followed that change. Through the 1600s, chocolate stayed a special food. The 1700s were much the same. Kings and queens ate it. Strong leaders in the church ate it. In the 1600s, mills began grinding cacao beans in large amounts. Output rose. Price fell. Someone invented milk chocolate with a smooth texture. That new form made people want chocolate even more. A shop for chocolate opened first in 1657. London, England, was the city. During the 1700s, a steam-powered grinder was invented. Factories could then turn out tons of cheaper chocolate in a hurry. Many countries now treat chocolate as a favorite treat. Children eat it. Adults eat it. A timeline of chocolate. Mexico and Central America saw the earliest human use. People there also spent chocolate as money. Hernan Cortes took cacao beans to Spain. He took the drink method with him.

7A man from France opened London's first chocolate shop. Someone invented a cocoa press. Someone sold the earliest solid bar of chocolate. A man from Switzerland made milk chocolate first. He sold it as well. Milton Hershey started the Hershey Chocolate Company. The United States was the place. A later invention filled chocolates with other sweets. Worldwide, people use over four million metric tons of cacao beans. A fact to know. New studies find that eating chocolate can help health. Dark chocolate is the kind linked to those benefits. Milk chocolate is not the kind they point to. Less heart disease is one possible benefit. Lower blood pressure is another. Certain shops sell nothing but chocolate treats. Chocolate now. Factory chocolate mostly becomes candy. Some of it is mixed into ice cream. Some becomes a chocolate drink. Some goes into baked goods. Mexican cooking uses a sauce called mole, said MOLE-ay. Chocolate is part of that sauce. Every year, people consume tons of chocolate. To consume it is to eat it. Switzerland leads the world in chocolate eaten per person. Many other countries in Europe share that love. Here is the yearly amount per person, from highest to lowest. 1. Switzerland: 22.4 lb, or 10.1 kg.

82. Austria: 20.1 lb, or 9.1 kg. 3. Ireland: 19.5 lb, or 8.8 kg. 4. Germany: 18.0 lb, or 8.2 kg. 5. Norway: 17.9 lb, or 8.1 kg. 6. Denmark: 17.7 lb, or 8.0 kg. 7. United Kingdom: 17.5 lb, or 7.9 kg. 8. Belgium: 13.2 lb, or 6.0 kg. 9. Australia: 13.0 lb, or 5.9 kg. 10. Sweden: 12.9 lb, or 5.8 kg. A large share of the world's cocoa comes from West Africa. Children do much of the harvest work there. School is missed because of that work. The hope is to earn money for food. Danger is part of the job. The job is hard. Children work because of poverty. Higher pay from chocolate companies to farmers would help end it. A few small companies already pay more. Extra pay lets a family live. Children can attend school instead of harvesting. One photo shows a young child picking pods. Moahe is nine. He helps with the drying of beans where cocoa is grown. Ivory Coast is where both children work. When you next eat chocolate, remember the work behind it. Closing. Far from the cacao trees, chocolate was slow to catch on. Makers added extra foods over the years. Sugar was added.

9Milk was added. Huge amounts are eaten around the world today. No other sweet is more popular. Try this recipe for yummy chocolate brownies. An adult should help with cutting. An adult should help with hot food. 1. Collect the foods. Butter or margarine: 1 cup. Unsweetened chocolate: 4 squares, and each square is 1 oz. Baker's chocolate is another name for it. Eggs: 4. White sugar: 2 cups. Vanilla extract: 2 teaspoons. All-purpose flour: 1 1/2 cups. Baking powder: 1 teaspoon. Salt: a pinch. 2. Heat the oven until it reaches 350 degrees F. That same heat is 180 degrees C. Grease a pan by rubbing fat on it. The pan measures 9 inches by 13 inches. It holds 15 cups. 3. Put butter in a pan on the stove. Keep the burner at medium. When the butter has melted, move the pan off the burner. Stir chocolate in until it melts and blends. Put in one egg, then stir well. Repeat until all four eggs are in. Sugar goes in next. Vanilla goes in next. Keep stirring until you see a smooth mix. 4. Take another bowl. Stir flour together with baking powder and salt. The wet mix of butter, chocolate, and sugar goes into the flour.

10Stir gently. Stop when no dry flour remains. 5. Empty the bowl into the greased pan. Smooth the top so the layer is even. 6. Leave the pan in the oven for 25 to 30 minutes. The brownies are ready when they start to pull from the pan sides. 7. Wait until the pan is cool. Only then should you cut the brownies. You will get 12 brownies. Word list. Cacao, a noun on page 5, is the tree that supplies chocolate. Conching, a noun on page 13, is the smoothing of a chocolate mix. Consume, a verb on page 20, means eat or buy. Fermentation, a noun on page 9, is a natural change seen in many foods and in plants. Ingredients, a noun on page 13, are the separate foods in a dish or a recipe. Machete, a noun on page 8, is a knife that is long and thick. Mills, a noun on page 17, are machines that grind. Animal power, wind, or steam can run them. Mole, a noun on page 19, is a spicy sauce from Mexico. Chile and strong chocolate are in it. Solidified, a verb on page 13, means a liquid or a gas has become solid.

73. The Wild Tournament of March

1City College of New York took the men's Division I trophy in 1950. The group behind that level of play is the National Collegiate Athletic Association. People often shorten the name to NCAA. North Carolina State entered the 1983 event with little expectation of a win. Even so, the team won in the last seconds of the last game. The name March Madness says what the event feels like. College basketball's big tournament opened during March of 1939. Only eight teams played in that first year. As the years passed, the event grew. Even now, it still rolls across the country each March. The feeling is still wild. Sixty-eight teams play, and one loss ends a team's run. With a setup like that, any result is possible. College sports inside the United States are directed by the NCAA. From 1939 on, some of basketball's greatest talents have passed through this March event. Many athletes who later earn pay for the sport first sharpen their skills in college.

2The road into professional basketball runs straight through this tournament. The National Basketball Association includes many players who came through March Madness. People shorten that league's name to NBA. Shaquille O'Neal first showed what he could do in the men's NCAA event. Magic Johnson did the same. Larry Bird did the same. Stephen Curry did the same. Many other stars did the same. Stephen Curry's college team came before his years in the NBA. One photo shows a guard after a shot in the final second beats his team. The date on that photo is March 20, 2016. A basketball life ends at college for most of the players. For them, the chance at glory will not come again. Athletes in the NCAA are students the whole time. They are not paid to play. Love of the game is why they play. Some players have dropped to the floor when a game ends. Some have burst into tears.

3At times the tears come from joy. At times they come from sorrow. Fans love them either way. The path to the title is long. The path is also hard. As of 2016, teams are sorted by region. They make up 32 conferences. Those conferences are not all the same size. Every team wants a spot in the tournament. Only 68 teams get in each year. The winner of each conference gets in with no extra vote. That accounts for 32 teams. A committee of ten people picks the other 36. The members cast votes on a Sunday. The Sunday falls a few days before play begins. In 2015, the pep band at the University of North Florida celebrated. The mascot celebrated too. Their team had just learned it would play. A 2016 chart of the men's Division I title chase shows the South side. The rounds on it include a first round. They include a second round. They include games inside each region.

4They include national semifinals. They include the Final Four. They include the championship game. Teams named on that chart include Kansas and Oregon. They include Austin Peay and Colorado. They include Connecticut and Maryland. They include Oklahoma and Villanova. They include South Dakota State and California. They include Hawaii and Arizona. They include Wichita State and Miami of Florida. They include Buffalo and Iowa. They include Temple and UNC Asheville. They include Syracuse and North Carolina. The winner of each of the four regions moves on to the Final Four. Those regions are the East, the South, the Midwest, and the West. In 2016, Villanova won the South. Villanova also won the title for the whole nation. This March contest draws more interest than nearly any other sports contest in America. People who do not follow sports still enjoy it. A large part of the fun is filling out a bracket. A bracket is a grid that lists every team in the event.

5People use it to say who will win each game. The winner is the team that moves ahead. The grid also shows the path a team must travel to reach the title game. Each year, a bracket is filled out by millions of people nationwide. Some take the safe pick and choose the teams ranked highest. Others love to cheer for teams that are not expected to win. Almost everyone is trying to outpick somebody else. That somebody may be a relative. It may be a friend. It may be a person from work. It may be a stranger on the internet. In 2011, a high school librarian named Diana Inch wanted in on a pool at her school. Some men there were running their own pool. They would not let her join. In the end, her picks beat every one of theirs. Her picks also beat more than three million entries in an online contest. That contest asked people to name the whole Final Four.

6A past president of the United States, Barack Obama, is one of the best-known fans. A paper copy of the men's bracket was what he pointed to in 2010. The team that won that year visited him at the White House. His own choices did not go very well. In 2015, a girl of eleven wrote him a letter. Her bracket had beaten his. She said he was a fine president. She added that picking winners was not his strongest skill. He wrote back and included his bracket. He said he would ask her first the next time. In 1982, the NCAA began a March tournament for women. The first field had 32 teams. The field has since grown to twice that size, which is 64 teams. No women's team in this event's history has been stronger than the Connecticut Huskies. They have won eleven national titles. The Tennessee Volunteers come next, with eight titles. Tennessee's Lady Volunteers drove at the Rutgers Scarlet Knights in the title game of 2007.

7During the Final Four stage in 2016, Breanna Stewart takes the ball. A claim made by some people puts her above every other player in women's college basketball. She played for the Huskies. Four titles in a row went to her team across 2013, 2014, 2015, and 2016. The honor called Most Outstanding Player was hers in every one of those four seasons. The stories people love most from this event are called Cinderella stories. A Cinderella team is an underdog. Few people give that team good odds. Because one loss ends the run, any team can fight through to a surprise victory. That surprise is called an upset. In the 1985 title game, almost nobody gave Villanova a real chance against Georgetown. Georgetown was the champion from the year before. That same year, Georgetown stood at rank number one. Villanova was ranked eighth. Many fans thought only a miracle could bring Villanova a win.

8No miracle was required. What the team needed was the greatest game of its life. Any basket that is not a free throw counts as a field goal in this sport. The basket is worth two points or three points. Distance from the basket sets the value. Field goal percentage tells how many of those shots succeed. The count is measured against every field goal the team attempts. A higher share is a better result. A player in a Villanova uniform shows joy after the 1985 win over Georgetown. Villanova stunned Georgetown by making more than 78 percent of its field goals. The team hit 22 baskets out of 28 tries. That game gave Villanova its first national title in men's basketball. The final score was 66 to 64. Michigan State's Spartans carried the second rank when the 2016 opening round began. Fifteenth was the rank given to Middle Tennessee's Blue Raiders. The Blue Raiders stunned fans by knocking Michigan State out.

9The score was 90 to 81. The Blue Raiders did not take the whole tournament. They did ruin a great many brackets in that first round. Plenty of people want to mark a perfect bracket. In 2014, a prize of one billion dollars was offered for a perfect set of picks. Nobody claimed it. The miss is easy to understand. The chance of calling every game right sits between two very small odds. One figure is 1 in 5 billion. The other figure is 1 in 135 billion. Nobody, so far as records show, has ever handed in a perfect bracket. Each March, millions of people soak up the excitement. They watch underdog teams on a surprise run. They watch new stars rise. They watch a shot at the buzzer decide a game. A dream may come true. A dream may fade for good. That is March Madness. Good luck filling out a bracket. Players from Duke shout with joy after the men's title game in 2015.

10The women's Final Four of 2014 is the round Maryland reaches. Some words from this account have set meanings. A bracket is a grid that pairs opponents during a tournament. A committee is a group of people who look at a choice or decide what to do. A miracle is an amazing event. It is especially an event that nature or science cannot explain. Predicting means stating a future event, often from notice or from experience. Professional describes earning money for an activity instead of doing it only for pleasure. Qualifying means showing the skill required before entry is allowed. The skill may open a hard contest or a certain job. A single-elimination tournament is a string of games in which one loss stops the loser from going on. Underdogs are the side expected to lose a contest. An upset is a result nobody expected in a game or other contest.

74. The Work a Night of Sleep Does

1Soft sheets and a slow drift into happy sleep are a pleasure many people know. Rest comes from a good night of sleep. Recharge comes from it after a busy day. Proper function depends on sleep for people. It depends on sleep for most other animals too. To function is to work the way one should. Minds stay sharp because of sleep, just as they need food and water. Bodies stay fit and ready because of it. One fact can surprise people. About a third of a life is spent asleep. Twenty-five of those years are sleep if a person reaches seventy-five. Sleep arrives on its own. It arrives on a regular pattern. Waking again is easy. Often a loud noise is enough. The old questions remain. What sleep is, and what brings it on, took centuries of struggle to chase. Aristotle, a thinker of ancient Greece, called sleep a quiet rest caused by eating. The idea is less odd than it first seems. A big meal leaves most people ready for a nap. His belief went further. Warm vapors, he thought, rose to the head while food was digested.

2He thought those vapors cooled and traveled to the heart. Sleepiness, in his view, followed that path. The vapor story is not true. The kind of food still changes how sleepy a person feels. Some foods bring a sleepy feeling. Chocolate is different because it has caffeine. Foods like that can lift you up rather than settle you down. Brain waves can be recorded by a special machine. Those waves are patterns of brain activity measured as electric currents. Eighteenth-century researchers offered another guess. Sleep toxins, they thought, piled up through the day. A shutdown of the brain was the end they expected. Sleep, in their view, let the substances fade. The effects wore off. Early in the twentieth century, a clearer picture appeared. Hans Berger, a German researcher, ran studies that changed the view. Shutdown is not what the brain does. Activity in the brain changes as waking turns to sleep. A brain that stays somewhat active in sleep was a new and important idea. More research followed his discovery.

3The goal was to learn how the brain works while someone sleeps. The cause of those brain-wave changes was the next question. A substance called adenosine builds up and affects the brain. Sleep is triggered by it. The body makes adenosine on its own while energy is spent. More of it reaches the brain as a day goes on. Many waking body processes slow down once it is there. The old toxin idea was not completely wrong. A wave chart can show six conditions. The first is awake. The second is awake with eyes closed. The third is falling asleep. The fourth is asleep. The fifth is deep sleep. The sixth is asleep and dreaming. Recording machines show a surprise about a sleeping brain. Activity can match a waking brain. Dreaming is when this is especially true. More activity shows up as tighter waves. Rest shows up as looser waves. A brain that stays on during sleep still has a job. Learning is part of that job. Memories and information are made solid for long-term use. To solidify is to make something stronger or more definite.

4Much of what we learn first sits in short-term memory. That memory is a good temporary hold for new information. Temporary means it lasts only a limited time. Forever is too long for that hold. It is not large enough. It is not strong enough. Information has to shift into long-term memory. Sleep is when the shift happens. New connections form in the brain. Information and knowledge get reorganized. The result locks learning into long-term memory. Recalling and using the learning gets easier. A sleep study lets scientists look at a man's brain and body. Athletes at a professional level need sleep. Body tissues grow stronger. Energy levels are restored, meaning put back as they were. Brain activity is not the only change when sleep starts. Body processes change too. Awake hours are when cells and molecules get broken down as energy is used. A molecule is a substance's tiniest piece that can stand on its own. One or more atoms make each molecule. The breakdown has a name, catabolic activity. It is a body process that takes cells apart for energy.

5Sleep brings a shift to anabolic activity. That process builds or mends cells. New molecules form. Cells are built up. Tissues are restored. Growth is helped. Athletes cannot do without this. Training keeps breaking down cells and tissue. Enough rest is needed to rebuild those tissues and make them stronger. Babies sleep so much for a related reason. Most infants double birth weight in five months. That growth needs more building up than breaking down. Illness is another reason sleep matters. Wounds heal with its help. The immune system grows stronger. That system is a network of cells and tissues working against infection. Antibodies move through the body on this network. Energy in sleep goes to healing rather than to other jobs. Studies find more white blood cells made during sleep. Those cells fight illness. Bed rest is what doctors suggest for sick patients because of this. White blood cells multiply in sleep. The immune system is strengthened, and sickness is fought. Sleep disorders come in several kinds. Three well-known ones are insomnia, narcolepsy, and sleepwalking.

6Insomnia means a person cannot fall asleep. Reasons differ from person to person. Stress may be a cause. Worry may be a cause. Chronic pain may make sleep impossible. Disease may do that too. Narcolepsy is nearly insomnia's reverse. Sleep can arrive on its own at any moment. Sudden muscle weakness can occur. A person may fall and be unable to move. People watching these attacks can be startled. Sleepwalking is fairly common in the teen years. Adults rarely do it. Eyes stay open as the person moves. Ordinary acts can happen, such as walking, talking, or chores. Less than ten minutes is a usual episode. Waking the person is not dangerous. It is not harmful. Leading the person back to bed is the better choice. Sleep's benefits do not arrive all at one moment. Cycles repeat through the night. Researchers monitor sleepers to see how sleep works. To monitor is to watch progress over time. Brain activity is tracked. Eye movement is tracked. Breathing rate is tracked. Heart rate is tracked. All of those measures go into one graph, a hypnogram.

7Stages of sleep show up as a pattern on that graph. Two types of sleep exist. One is rapid eye movement sleep, called REM. The other is non-rapid eye movement sleep, called NREM. Three stages sit inside NREM. Stage one brings drowsiness and the start of a doze. Twitches can happen. A sudden jerk into waking can happen. A feeling of falling can come with it. Ten minutes is the longest this stage tends to last. A sample hypnogram shows five cycles. Clock times run from 11 pm to 7 am. Waking, REM, and NREM stages 1, 2, and 3 can each appear. A chart estimates a typical adult night. About 5 percent is stage 1 NREM. About 50 to 60 percent is stage 2 NREM. About 20 to 25 percent is REM. About 15 to 20 percent is stage 3 NREM. People differ in how long each stage lasts. Age is one cause of the difference. Stage 2 NREM is when muscle activity falls. The outside world fades for the sleeper. Memories begin moving from the short-term store to the long-term store.

8Half of sleep, or more than half, is spent in this stage. Stage 3 NREM is when dreaming begins. Permanent memories form then. Breathing rate is at its lowest. Heart rate is at its lowest. Blood pressure is at its lowest. Brain temperature is at its lowest. Waking someone from this deep sleep is usually very hard. Nearly a quarter of sleep time belongs to this stage. REM comes after the NREM stages have cycled. It is quite unlike the other stages. Closed eyes dart quickly from side to side, as the name suggests. A fast ball game is a fair picture of that motion. Brain activity sits near waking levels. Breathing sits near waking levels. Heart rate sits near waking levels. The brain races instead of resting. The body, though, is fully still. Muscles are almost completely paralyzed. Wild dreams in this stage may be the reason. Dreams come in REM. They also come in deep sleep. Every night includes dreams. Memory of them is not guaranteed. Two traits are shared by all dreams.

9They unfold like stories. The dreamer is usually a main character. Pleasant is not the mood of every dream. Nightmares are frightening ones. A distressed waking can follow. Young children can suffer extreme nightmares called night terrors. Screaming can happen. Thrashing can happen. Full waking does not. People have asked what dreams mean for centuries. Gods, in ancient cultures, were thought to send them as messages. Some people today treat dreams as imagination's product. Others think a hidden message may sit inside. A tired head on a pillow should set many helpful things in motion. Trouble in the daytime follows when those things fail. Too little sleep can make a person cranky. Sleep-deprived is the word for not having enough sleep. More mistakes show up at work. Creativity drops. Problem solving gets harder. The body struggles along with the brain. A stable body temperature is harder to hold, studies show, when sleep is too short. Healing of wounds takes longer. A nap does not always replace a solid night. Mood can still improve after a quick nap.

10Alertness can improve. Performance can improve. A steady sleep schedule helps a person fall asleep. A schedule is a plan for when things will happen. The same habit helps a person stay asleep through the night. Weekends count too. Good sleep habits need to stay consistent. Bedtime should be the same each day. Waking time should be the same each day. The body then keeps a regular clock. Screen time should be limited. Television right before bed is a poor choice. Computer work then is a poor choice. A tablet then is a poor choice. A smartphone then is a poor choice. Hard exercise just before bed should be avoided. A walk is fine. Anything harder may raise body temperature. Sleep then becomes difficult to start. A warm bath beforehand can help if sleep will not come. A warm shower can help too. Muscles relax. A peaceful sleep is easier to enter. Healthy bodies follow from good sleep habits. Sharp minds follow too. A simple warning is worth keeping. Skip the snooze, and you just might lose.

75. The Work of Wildfire

1Burned ground inside Yellowstone from the 1988 fires appears on a map. Montana, Idaho, and Wyoming meet the park. Lines show the park boundary. Lines show state boundaries. A scale bar reads 50 miles. It also reads 50 kilometers. The United States is labeled. Yellowstone's fire opens this book. Costs of fire come next. Causes of fire follow. People's changing ideas about fire come after that. Living things that are adapted to fire are part of the story. Fuel that piles too high is part of it too. A new way of thinking about fire closes the book. Two world records belong to the Yellowstone fires of 1988. Taxpayers paid 120 million dollars, a cost no earlier fire had matched. One day of those fires burned more acres than any earlier single day. The day's total was 165,000 acres. No national park in America is older than Yellowstone. Love for it runs deep. Summers there are hot and dry. Lodgepole pines stand packed together. Fire in the forest is familiar ground for the park. The record fire before 1988 covered 25,000 acres. More than 2.2 million acres make up Yellowstone. Fires of that earlier size can be absorbed now and then. Summer of 1988 brought a set of fires that were huge. Lightning lit most of them. Careless people lit the rest. Forests beyond the park border started some of the blazes.

2Those blazes then moved inside. Fires caused by people were fought from the first moment. Fires from other causes were left to burn. July 14 changed the wind. Speed rose as high as 80 miles per hour. The same speed is 129 kilometers per hour. Orders told many visitors to get out of the park. Firefighters arrived in their place. The number from across the country was 25,000. A start was all those fires had managed so far. Afternoons brought high wind. Embers rode that wind far up in the sky. New fires began where the embers landed. August 20 brought wind of a fierce kind. Burning that day covered 165,000 acres. Black Saturday became the name of that day. Almost two months passed while firefighters battled the flames. September 11 brought rain. Early snow came with it. Wet weather did what thousands of firefighters could not. The fires were dampened. Burned land by then covered 44.5 percent of the park. Forests around the park burned too. The full area was 1.2 million acres. Delaware is nearly that large. A measure helps make the size clear. 640 acres fit inside one square mile. The 1988 burn of 1.2 million acres equals 1,875 square miles. The same area is 4,856 square kilometers. Devastation met the eyes of many observers. Blame fell on the choice to let natural fires burn.

3Ruined was the word they used for the park. Questions rose with the smoke. Was the ruin of Yellowstone real? A wildfire left to burn can be wise at some times. Is there any purpose in a wildfire besides destruction? Destruction is common in a forest fire. Death is sometimes the result. Severe fire can heat gases to 1,600 degrees Fahrenheit. The same heat is 871 degrees Celsius. Boulders have split in half under blasts of that heat. Chainsaws have melted. Three types describe forest fires. Low travel, sometimes under the soil, marks a ground fire. Ground fuel is what it eats. Flame is rare. A walker could pass over it and never notice. A surface fire stays low as well. Greater heat is its mark. Trees burn. Brush burns. Enough fierceness turns that fire into a crown fire. Size is what sets crown fires apart. They are the big ones. Speed can reach 10 miles in an hour. That rate is 16 kilometers in an hour. Flame height can reach 300 feet. That height is 91 meters. All three types can live inside one forest fire. Fires and burned acres in the United States fill a chart. The span is 1960 through 2010. Points fall on 1960 and 1965. They fall on 1970 and 1975. They fall on 1980 and 1985. They fall on 1990 and 1995.

4They fall on 2000, 2005, and 2010. Fires are counted by the thousand. Acres are counted by the million. Recent years show fewer fires. Those same years show more acres burned. Energy from a fire can equal an atomic bomb. The release can take as few as five minutes. It can take as many as fifteen. A milder fire can still wreck property. It can wreck homes. Human lives can be lost. Other animals can die too. Plants sometimes hold the soil in place until fire takes them. Flooding then finds the land more vulnerable. Landslides find it more vulnerable too. Big fires send smoke that pollutes the air. Breathing grows hard. Brown color can fill a sky hundreds of miles off. Smoke is the smell in that air. Millions of acres burn in wildfires across the nation every year. Fighting them costs the United States more than 1 billion dollars. About 8 million lightning strikes hit the world each day. An estimated 17,400 fires in the United States each year start from lightning. Forest fires are most common in the western part of the country. Forests are plentiful there. Summer heat and dryness are plentiful too. Lightning often comes with that summer. Natural causes of wildfires have a leader, and lightning is that leader. People light many forest fires as well. Purpose is usually absent.

5Accidents explain many of them. A campfire left partly burning can be the start. Some starts are less direct than that. Wheels of a passing train can throw a spark. Dry grass can catch. Nearby woods can catch. Most fires end on their own. A few acres are the usual size. Trees are never touched by some of those fires. Huge growth does happen in a few fires. Early firefighters in South Dakota appear in a picture. Sand is their tool for managing a fire. A fire can be put out by sand. A fire can be slowed by sand. Spreading can be stopped by sand. Newness does not belong to forest fires. What people feel about them has shifted as time passed. Westward Europeans of the 1800s fought every fire they met. Balance between fire and forest had existed before that fight. Wildfires had no one opposing them. Ownership of the forests was missing, and that was part of the reason. The year 1872 made Yellowstone a national park. Long after that year, visitors still read fire as pure destruction. Park leaders read it the same way. Early caretakers of parks and forests saw firefighting as good stewardship. A rule from the U.S. Forest Service arrived in 1935. A fire was to be out by the day after it began. Sense seemed to sit inside that rule.

6Protection of the forest was the hoped-for result of putting fires out. Jeff Henry stands in a 2008 picture. His hands hold a photo from the Yellowstone fire of 1988. New trees stand beside him. The spot matches the older photo. Twenty years lay between the two views. Many forests can live through a fire. Native plants often gain from fire. Native animals often gain from it. Invasive plants meet an enemy in fire. Harmful insects die in it. Nutrients fill the ash of burned plants. Soil takes that ash back in as it dissolves. Wildlife habitat can improve. Dark, crowded tree stands open into sunny vistas. Grass grows there and feeds animals. Thousands of years have given fire a shaping role in certain forests. Lodgepole forest is one kind shaped this way. Other kinds are shaped this way too. Patches of forest burn, place by place. The pattern continues until the whole landscape has been through the cycle. Grassy meadows replace the burned patches. New trees rise in those meadows later. Food for many species comes from the mix of places. Shelter comes from that mix too. Time for a full cycle is not the same in every forest. A thousand years may be what one forest type needs. A hundred years may be enough for another type. Adaptation to fire shows up in many plant species of these forests.

7Yellowstone's burned trees make the pattern plain. Reproduction for some lodgepole pines depends on fire. Pinecones open because of the heat. Seeds leave the cones. Closed cones simply wait if fire never comes. Snow lay over the park within a month after the huge fire of 1988. Spring melt the next year watered living roots under the ground. Seeds under the ground got that water too. Charred earth sent up grasses rich in nutrients. Elk arrived to eat. Deer arrived to eat. Wildflowers opened the next spring. They opened that summer too. Ruin was not the end of the burned park. Undergrowth is cleared by fire. Light then gets to the forest floor. New plants can rise. Living with fire is hard for many people. Danger is something everyone knows in fire. Great damage is something fire can do. A forest does not look the same to every person. Wood is all some people see. Grass for grazing cattle is all some people see. A pretty tent site is all some people see. An ecosystem that sometimes must burn is not what they see. Logic seems to say every fire should be put out, if the forest is only wood, grass, or a camp. Trouble follows when fires are stopped from running their course. Forests can be hurt. People can be hurt. Needles pile up if fire never comes.

8Brush piles up. Logs pile up. Debris on the floor then feeds a harsher fire. Control can slip almost out of reach when fuel is too heavy. The West has seen many severe forest fires in recent years. More than one factor is at work. Climate change is among those factors. Forest experts, in the main, still point to one cause. Fuel on the ground is too thick. So many wildfires were stamped out that the fuel could pile up. Cost is another problem in the fight. Federal, state, and local governments have paid more each year since 1960. Less than 100 million dollars was the old yearly cost. More than 3.3 billion dollars is the cost now. Other programs lose money to this huge bill. Those programs clear trees. They clear brush. High-risk areas are where they work. Prevention of fires is what the clearing can do. A smaller reach and a milder fire are the least it can do. Water is one tool firefighters may spray on a blaze. Controlled burns are another tool. Plants can be cleared so a break opens in the fire's path. Some of these fires could be left alone. Remote country still allows that choice. Remote country is rarer than it once was. People keep arriving. Land beside U.S. wildlands has been built on fast since 1990. Two million acres a year is the rate.

9Three acres a minute is about the same rate. The years 1990 to 2008 saw 10 million new homes. U.S. wildlands near urban areas hold those homes. High wildfire risk faces many of them. Very high risk faces many of them. Homes are burning in greater numbers. Surprise is the wrong word for that result. Each year of the 1960s, wildfires destroyed about 200 homes. An average year in the 2000s saw about 2,700 homes burn. Wildfires were again the cause. California, on its own, loses about 700 homes in a typical year. Wildfires are the cause.
