Spending in that state reaches 165 million dollars a year. The money covers homes lost to wildfire. It covers homes damaged by wildfire. Millions more go to a second effort. The effort tries to stop those homes from burning at all. Risk comes with a fire allowed to burn beside these houses. Risk also comes if every one of these fires is stopped. A 2009 picture shows a house in Santa Barbara, California. Wildfire is moving toward it. A natural cycle is visible to Yellowstone visitors. Ashes from the massive 1988 fire now hold growing trees. A burning wildfire makes a truth hard to hold in mind. Destruction and production can live in the same event. Yellowstone stands as living proof. Dead gray lodgepole pines covered the mountains after that 1988 fire.

10Miles of those stands stretched on. Lush young stands grow there today from the seedlings. Height increases every year. Offspring of those trees will repeat the pattern someday. Fire is the reason that future can happen. Nature has a job for wildfire. That job deserves people's thought. Standing back can be the wiser act in some cases. The fire then finishes its own work. Some words in this book have set meanings. An acre measures land, and it equals 4,840 square yards. The patch is a little smaller than a football field. An ecosystem joins living things with the habitat they share. Embers are wood or coal that still glows from a fire. Adapted means fitted to conditions that are new or special. Invasive describes a newcomer that harms a place and is not native there. Climate change means weather patterns that shift for a long time, on Earth or in one region. Reproduce means to bring forth offspring like the parent life. Cycled means a landscape passed through the same series of events again and again. Debris means leftover scraps after the rest is destroyed or gone. Stewardship means careful, responsible care by someone who looks after a place. Devastation means damage or destruction on a great scale. A wildfire spreads with great speed through forest or other wild ground.

76. The Worth of the Water We Use

1Tadpoles appear in the picture, racing the clock. A puddle in rock is shrinking under them. Water that seems to be everywhere opens this book. The tiny share people can actually use comes next. Pollution and its impact follow. Population and its impact follow that. Climate change and its impact come after. Ways to ease the trouble close the book. Depth and age both set a record at Russia's Lake Baikal. It is the deepest freshwater lake on Earth. It is the oldest too. Water is not on many people's minds very often. It seems to surround them, so the question stays quiet. Faucets release it. Sidewalk puddles hold it after rain. Lakes hold a great store of it. Rivers hold a great store of it. Bottles are one way people buy it. Fountains are one way people sip it. Imagine a glass the size of the whole United States. Every drop of water on Earth would go into that glass. Height would have to reach 145 kilometers before the glass could hold the load. The same height is 90 miles. A supply that large might seem like enough for every drinker.

2The real situation says otherwise. Usable water is no more than a drop beside the whole bucket. Value in water is rising fast, past the value of gold. The value of oil is being passed as well. Usable water on this planet is not sufficient for all people. Salt fills most of the water on Earth. Drinking it is not possible. Watering crops with it is not possible. Fresh water is only about 3 percent of the total. Most of that fresh water is locked away. Polar ice caps store some. Glaciers store some. Rock layers underground store some. A chart sorts the world's water. Salt oceans hold 97 percent. Fresh water holds the other 3 percent. Ice caps and glaciers hold 68.7 percent of the fresh share. Groundwater holds 30.1 percent. Surface water holds 0.3 percent. Lakes hold 87 percent of fresh surface water. Rivers hold 2 percent. Swamps hold 11 percent. Drinking water around the world draws 25 percent to 40 percent from groundwater. Food crops draw even more from it. The groundwater store is getting smaller. A very dry region of the world holds Saudi Arabia.

3Water is still within reach there. High income is what makes that reach possible. Fresh water is richer in some countries than in others. Enough for people's needs is still true in most places. Getting the water to people is not always possible. Hard-to-reach sources can be tapped by high-income countries. Wells are often too costly for people in low-income countries. Dams are often too costly too. Only 1 percent of Earth's fresh water is open to human use today. Three main reasons keep that 1 percent scarce. Pollution is one reason. Population is another. Climate change is the third. Waste of many kinds fills the Amazon River in a picture from Brazil. Many continents face a huge problem in water pollution. Years of dumping have sent harmful chemicals into the Amazon River. South America is the region. Oil drilling has poisoned stretches of that river. Gold mining has poisoned stretches of the river and of the forest beside it. Untreated fresh water is common in other low-income places. Sickness from pollution takes added forms there. Running water is missing. Indoor plumbing is missing. Human waste stays outdoors. Bacteria crowd that waste.

4Rivers and streams then receive the flow. Drinking draws on those polluted waterways. Cooking draws on them. Bathing draws on them. Hospital studies tell a hard fact. Unclean drinking water put half of the world's hospital patients into their beds. A mobile phone is more common worldwide than a toilet. Clean drinking water reaches most people in high-income countries. The United States is one of those countries. Pollution still causes other troubles there. Farming produces the water pollution seen most often. Rain carries farm chemicals into waterways. Rain carries animal waste from farms into waterways. Old sewage treatment puts waters of the United States at risk. Oil puts them at risk. Further kinds of pollution add to the threat. A 2014 picture shows a man who lives in Pennsylvania. A jug of polluted water is in his hands. An official in Washington, D.C., is the person he shows it to. He and his neighbors wanted nearby oil and gas work to end. October 30, 2011, was a birthday in the Philippines. The baby was a girl. Her birth brought the number of people on Earth to 7 billion. Experts look ahead to 2050 and expect 9.7 billion people.

5Food will be a need for each of them. Energy will be a need. Land will be a need. Water will be a need. A larger population leaves less water for each person. Safe drinking water is out of reach today for 1 person in 9. Studies are the source of that finding. Daily life for many of them runs on less than 8 liters. Two gallons is the same amount. The year 2050 will place more than half of all people in lands where water is scarce. Daily use is compared on a chart. One American uses 100 gallons in a day. Liters for that amount number 379. Many people elsewhere use 2 gallons in a day. Liters for that amount number 8. A second chart sets water use next to population. Trillion cubic meters per year is the unit for water. Billions is the unit for people. A cubic meter equals 264 gallons. Shortages can reach even high-income countries. Heavier use by people in those countries is part of the cause. A family of four in the United States uses 1,514 liters on an average day.

6Gallons for that total number 400. Each person in the family uses about 379 liters. Gallons for that share number 100. A few users could keep up that pace without emptying the supply. Limited sources now support ever more people. Los Angeles County is expected to hold thirteen million people by 2050. Less water moves in the rivers those residents use than moved there before. The global supply of water sits under a threat from climate change. Scientists look toward 2050 with a hard forecast. Serious water shortages may face more than five billion people. Climate change would drive that result. Too much carbon dioxide in the air brings climate change. Other greenhouse gases add to it. Fossil fuels, when burned, make a large share of those gases. Heat from the Sun stays close to Earth's surface because the gases hold it. A greenhouse holds heat in a similar way. Temperature on Earth rises overall. Warming changes weather from region to region. Rain can arrive in unusual amounts. Snow can arrive in unusual amounts. Floods can follow. A dry place can turn much drier.

7Lower-than-normal rain or snow, lasting a long time, is a drought. Water runs short as a result. Drought risk is highest in the Mediterranean. The Middle East shares that high risk. Most scientists in the United States link an extreme drought to climate change. The Southwest has suffered it. California has suffered it. Trouble in the Southwest has lasted since 1999. Nevada depends on the Colorado River. Arizona depends on it too. Shrinkage is what the river shows. Fresh water from it serves twenty-five million people. Desert residents will likely cut daily water use if rain does not rise soon. Dependence on the Colorado River reaches nearly forty million people. A trickle is all that remains, and the flow dies before the sea. A boy fishes where rivers run. The Himalaya mountains feed many of those rivers. High glaciers in the Himalaya are melting because of climate change. Fresh water for 1.5 billion people depends on those glaciers. India is home to part of that population. Pakistan is home to another part. Six other countries in Asia hold the rest. Slow melt was the old pattern. Rivers below received water a little at a time.

8Speed is the pattern now. The melt is fast. A shrinking supply is the long-term result. Severe floods along the rivers have come from that same fast melt. Ranching in many countries has less water as the climate shifts. Crop growing has less water. Energy production has less water. Nature pays a price. Wildlife pays a price. Cities fall into arguments. States fall into arguments. Countries fall into arguments. Saving water is something each person can try. Help can come from that effort. Answers must also be sought by whole countries. Salt water is being turned into fresh water by workers in Saudi Arabia. Desalination is the name of the process. Expense is high. Building the plants is beyond the budget of many countries. Small steps remain open to one person. Shut off the tap while you brush your teeth. A rain barrel can store water that plants will use. Leaks should be fixed. Drips should be fixed. Meat can be eaten less often. Beef, on average, needs 125 times the water of an equal amount of potatoes. Showers can be shorter. A bath is the better choice if you want to take your time.

9Used goods can replace new goods when that choice exists. Recycled goods can replace them too. A bike ride helps slow climate change. A walk helps. Public transit helps. Competition for water appears on a chart. Domestic use is one share. Industrial use is another. Agricultural use is the third. The world figure for domestic use is 8 percent. The world figure for industrial use is 22 percent. The world figure for agricultural use is 70 percent. High-income countries use 11 percent at home. They use 59 percent in industry. They use 30 percent in agriculture. Low-income and middle-income countries use 8 percent at home. They use 10 percent in industry. They use 82 percent in agriculture. Singapore is testing whether dirty water can be made clean. Wastewater is the target. Dishwater is one source they hope to reuse. Toilet water is another. Bath water is another. Shower water is another. The same idea is already used in some other countries. One-third of Singapore's water comes from this reuse. Fast growth is the pattern in cities. About 70 percent of the world's fresh water still goes to agriculture.

10A hard choice sits in that fact. Cities might need some of the water that farms now use. The water trouble of this planet is difficult to solve. Conserving the resource is still necessary. Value sits in every drop that lasts. Our own generation needs it. Generations still to come need it too. Some words in this book have set meanings. Climate change means weather patterns that last in a new form, for Earth or for one region. Conserve means to guard a natural place or a resource so the supply lasts longer. Fossil fuels are coal, oil, and natural gas, taken from the ground for energy. Fresh water means water without salt. Glaciers are great bodies of ice and pressed snow. They last through the year and creep downhill. Pollution means harmful substances put into air, water, or soil, and it also means the harm that follows. Population means every member of one species inside a set area. A resource is a store of something with value or with strong usefulness. Scarce means hard to find, because only a small amount is present.

77. The Young King of Macedonia

1A map shows Alexander's empire at its peak in 323 BCE. The map includes Macedonia. It includes the Caspian Sea. It includes the Black Sea. It includes Taxila. It includes Asia Minor. It includes the Hydaspes River. It includes Persia. It includes Babylon. It includes the Mediterranean Sea. It includes India. It includes Alexandria. It includes the Persian Gulf. It includes the Euphrates River. It includes Egypt. It includes the Nile. It includes the Arabian Sea. It includes the Red Sea. One mark shows the empire. Another mark shows an exploration route. Nearly 2,300 years ago, a powerful leader lived. His name was Alexander the Great. He was king of Macedonia. Macedonia lay just north of ancient Greek land. He set out with his armies. He wanted to explore new lands. He wanted to claim new lands. He used violence to build a great empire. He used force as well. 356 BCE was the year Alexander was born. His father was Philip II. Macedonia's king before Alexander was Philip. A powerful army supported Philip.

2Philip kept Macedonia under his control. Control of most nearby Greek city-states stayed in his hands too. Alexander's mother was Olympias. Her firm hope was that her son would take the throne someday. His future was not sure. Olympias was not pure Macedonian. Another wife might give Philip a child. The throne's right could pass to that child. Macedonia's kingship came to Philip II in 359 BCE. Olympias understood her own place at court. If her son became king, she would remain among royalty. Alexander's horse was named Bucephalus. The horse stayed with him as a constant companion. Alexander's strengths showed when he was young. At age twelve, he watched his father refuse a horse. Philip would not buy it because it was too nervous. Alexander saw why the horse was afraid. It feared its own shadow. He turned the horse's head away from the shadow. Then he could calm the horse. He rode the new horse with ease. He named it Bucephalus. Alexander and Bucephalus would stay together for life.

3Philip was often away while Alexander grew up. Special teachers were his choice for getting his son ready to rule. Young men in Greece had to learn hunting. They had to learn horseback riding. They had to learn sword fighting. They had to learn the javelin. They had to learn the bow. Alexander and his friends needed those skills. The skills could keep them alive in battle. Philip asked Aristotle to teach Alexander. Aristotle was a very wise man. He taught science. He taught history. He taught other subjects. People respected Aristotle for his intelligence. He helped Alexander value art. He helped Alexander value culture. A statue of Aristotle stands on display in his birthplace. That place is Stagira, Greece. A mosaic from about 100 BCE may copy an older painting. People think the painting was made around Alexander's lifetime. As Alexander grew older, his responsibilities grew. One duty was leading soldiers in battle. Growing up, he was trained for the throne. A woman born in Macedonia became Philip's wife in 337 BCE.

4Philip's killing came the following year. Alexander's mother then had the baby killed. She also had the baby's mother killed. Soon her wish came true. Her son became king. As soon as Alexander became king, he asked his father's army for support. Persia had long been an enemy of Greece. Greek land in Asia Minor had been seized by Persia. Alexander's goal was rule over Persia. Philip had shared that goal. He wanted lands beyond Persia too. Many Greek city-states supported him. Other states were unsure about the young king. They were unsure about his grand ideas. Those states could block him from ruling Greece. His plans for Asia could be ruined by those states too. He used violence to keep his rule. He used violence to silence people who opposed him. One year after he became king, he left for Asia Minor. A map shows Macedonia and the city-states. It shows the Black Sea. It shows Pella. It shows Asia Minor. It shows the Aegean Sea.

5It shows Delphi. It shows Thebes. It shows Corinth. It shows Athens. It shows the Mediterranean Sea. The map marks some of the strongest capitals of the Greek city-states. Alexander reached Asia Minor in 334 BCE. The ground received his spear when he threw it. With that act, he claimed the land. Soon the Macedonians fought the Persians. The battle was at the Granicus River. Horseback training was strong in both armies. Foot training was strong in both armies too. The Persians fell quickly. Macedonian speed defeated them. Macedonian power defeated them too. The march continued for Alexander and his men. Greek cities beside the Aegean Sea were freed by them next. Darius III, king of Persia, came to fight them. The fight was the Battle of Issus. The Persians lost to the Macedonians again. Darius got away. Alexander knew how to approach a battle. He often used great speed. He often used surprise. Alexander traveled south in order to stop Persian ships. Cities beside the Mediterranean Sea came under his control.

6Some cities fought back. Macedonian strength still dominated. Macedonian skill dominated too. In 332 BCE, he marched south into Egypt. The Persian leader there gave him control. Alexander gave government positions to Egyptians. He allowed their religion. The Egyptians welcomed him. He then chose to go farther into Asia. He went east of Asia Minor. A last battle with the Persians came in the land now called Iraq. The year was 331 BCE. Darius fled the battlefield again after the loss. Alexander now ruled the Persian Empire. He traveled through Persia to find Darius. A local leader killed Darius first. An old mosaic shows Darius facing Alexander. The scene is the Battle of Issus. As Alexander took more land, he began to follow Persian customs. He took control of more people too. He asked his men to follow those customs. He began to demand a bow from the Macedonians. Persians already bowed to him. Many Macedonians disliked the new ways. Alexander took his father's army when he became king.

7Part of its strength came from a tight shape in battle. The shape was a rectangle called a phalanx. Generals who had fought beside Philip helped Alexander. His skill as a battlefield leader was outstanding. He seemed to know what an enemy would do. He knew just when to attack. He knew just where to attack. A Greek soldier carried a short knife called a dagger. He wore a helmet. He carried a shield. Foot soldiers in tight rows carried spears. Some spears were 6 meters long. That length is 20 feet. Europe had never known an empire as large as the one Alexander the Great built. It held land in Greece. It held land in Asia. It held land in Africa. In 326 BCE, the Macedonian army struggled farther east. India was their name for the land they had entered. That land is now Afghanistan and Pakistan. They won a hard battle. Alexander kept going through India. He was badly hurt on the way.

8He was able to recover. He kept urging the men onward. The push put them in great danger. They had been fighting for nearly ten years. Many men were tired. They wanted to go home. At last they convinced him to turn back. They reached Babylon in 323 BCE. A fever took hold of Alexander. In June of that year, he died. He died with his thirty-third birthday still ahead. His empire included parts of Europe. It included parts of Asia. It included parts of Africa. His life was about more than taking land. It was about more than ruling people. His early schooling may explain part of what he did. Traditions in the new empire became part of what he learned. He also forced Greek culture onto new lands. Later rulers took him as a model. Later explorers took him as a model too. They also forced their culture on other people. Those people were the Indigenous peoples they overtook. No battle went against Alexander during fifteen years of conquest.

9His military skill was new to the world. His genius was new to the world too. Was Alexander really that great? Historians think Bucephalus died in 326 BCE. The death came after a battle in India. A timeline of Alexander runs from 360 BCE to 320 BCE. In 356 BCE, he was born in Macedonia. In 344 BCE, Bucephalus became his horse. In 343 BCE, Aristotle became his teacher. In 340 BCE, Alexander stopped a war. The war was on Macedonia's eastern border. In 336 BCE, Philip was killed. Alexander became king of Macedonia. In 334 BCE, Alexander advanced into Persia. He won the first battle. In 333 BCE, he defeated Persians led by Darius. The battle was Issus. In 332 BCE, he invaded Egypt. People there welcomed him. Asia in 331 BCE was where he beat Darius and the Persians. In 327 BCE, he went on conquering central Asia. He pushed into India. In 326 BCE, he won his last battle in India. In 323 BCE, he died in Babylon.

10A city-state is a city or an urban area that acts as its own country. To claim something is to say it is yours by right. It can also mean calling it your property. Control can mean power to shape a situation. It can mean power to limit someone's behavior. It can mean the power to manage an object. Dominated means held continuing power over something. It can also mean a commanding presence. An empire joins many nations, or many peoples, under a single ruler. One government can be that ruler. To explore is to watch and learn about an area. The learning comes by traveling over it or through it. Intelligence is the ability to learn. It is also the ability to understand. A javelin is a long spear. People throw it in sport. People throw it in hunting. People throw it in war. A responsibility is something a person is supposed to take care of. It can also be something others expect that person to handle.

78. Two Voyagers Beyond the Outer Planets

1A picture shows Saturn and one of its moons. Voyager 1 took the photograph on November 3, 1980. Cloud tops on the planet show the shadow of the moon. The parts of this book follow in order. First comes one tough job. Then comes planning. Then comes the main task. Then come Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune. Then comes the Golden Record. Then comes the final task. A conclusion follows. A glossary comes last. A picture shows Voyager 2 leaving the ground. The launch was from Kennedy Space Center in Florida. The year was 1977. The ride into space was on a Titan/Centaur rocket. When the two Voyager spacecraft launched in 1977, a tough job waited. Their mission was to explore Jupiter. Their mission was also to explore Saturn. Those are two giant planets in the outer solar system. Their journey would be long. It would be difficult. If something broke, no one could go fix it. The space probes would have to make do with what they had. The people who built the Voyagers made the craft tough.

2Their computers were set up to deal with problems. The problems were ones that might happen. By today's standards, those computers would seem like toys. A modern cell phone holds thousands of times more power than they do. A working life of only about five years was the scientists' guess. Information from the twin spacecraft is still arriving on Earth. A note asks if we can still hear them. By 2025, the distance to Voyager 1 is about sixteen billion miles. It uses a radio to communicate with Earth. The radio is only as strong as a small lightbulb. About seventeen hours pass before one of its signals arrives. NASA's Deep Space Network catches the faint signal. It uses giant antennas around the world. A picture shows a giant satellite dish in Australia. The dish is part of that network. A diagram explains a gravity assist. A planet's gravity can bend the direction a spacecraft is traveling. It can also change the craft's speed. A spacecraft slows down if it passes just ahead of a planet.

3A pass just behind a planet makes the spacecraft go faster. The diagram shows Jupiter's orbit around the Sun. It shows Jupiter. It shows a Voyager spacecraft. The Voyager launches were carefully timed. In the late 1970s, four outer planets lined up. Their orbits placed them in a nearly straight line. With the planets so close together, one trip could visit several of them. The rockets that launched the Voyagers had a limit. They could get the craft only as far as Jupiter. Scientists solved that problem with Jupiter itself. Jupiter's strong gravity gave each spacecraft a boost as it passed by. A path diagram marks both craft and the Sun. Voyager 2 launched on August 20, 1977. Voyager 1 launched on September 5, 1977. Voyager 1 reached Jupiter on March 5, 1979. Voyager 2 reached Jupiter on July 9, 1979. Voyager 1 reached Saturn on November 12, 1980. Voyager 2 reached Saturn on August 25, 1981. Voyager 2 reached Uranus on January 24, 1986. Voyager 2 reached Neptune on August 25, 1989.

4A note calls the lineup a rare opportunity. The arrangement of planets at launch was a very rare event. A clock can help explain why. Picture a clock with hands for hours, minutes, and seconds. No two hands travel around that clock face at the same speed. A passing hand lines up with the hand it overtakes. All three hands align much less often. Lineups among the planets' orbits are rarer still. The four planets' arrangement during the Voyager missions is rare. It happens only once every 175 years. A list tells what is on board. The craft carry electromagnetic sensors. They carry a plasma sensor. They carry television cameras. They carry a low-energy charged particle sensor. They carry a cosmic ray sensor. They carry a low-gain antenna. They carry a power supply. They carry a high-gain antenna. They carry the Golden Record. They carry a boom for measuring magnetic fields. They carry a radio antenna. At first, the main task was limited. The Voyagers were to study Jupiter and Saturn.

5They were to study their larger moons. They were to study Saturn's rings. The spacecraft carried cameras. They carried scientific instruments. Those instruments let the Voyagers gather information. The solar system made more sense after what they found. Jupiter was the first planet the Voyagers aimed for. No planet in the solar system is larger than Jupiter. Humans had already studied Jupiter through telescopes. The Voyagers sent back new information. They showed the Great Red Spot in a new way. It is a huge storm. The storm spins constantly. A faint ring turned up around Jupiter. They also found ice sheets on one of Jupiter's moons. They found volcanoes on another moon. A note places Jupiter in the solar system. Jupiter is the fifth planet from the Sun. It is the first of the outer planets. The discoveries continued at Saturn. The two craft reached Saturn nine months apart. The years were 1980 and 1981. Close-up pictures showed new rings. Those rings could not be seen from Earth. Saturn is the solar system's second-largest planet.

6Surprises also turned up among Saturn's moons. Several small moons do a special job. They keep the ice and dust around Saturn in separate rings. The Voyagers also discovered something about Titan. Titan is Saturn's largest moon. Titan had an atmosphere. No other planet in our solar system has a larger set of rings than Saturn. Voyager 1 turned toward deep space once the Saturn flyby was finished. Voyager 2 continued toward Uranus. All its instruments were still working. NASA decided to keep going. Uranus offered no clear hints about what would be found. The odd look of this planet comes from a tip onto its side. The pass by that planet came in January 1986. It discovered a magnetic field around Uranus. The field does strange things. The cause is the planet's sideways position. A picture of the rings makes the tilt clear. The rings show how the planet is tipped on its side. Neptune's deep blue color comes from a gas in its atmosphere. The Uranus flyby went well. NASA then sent Voyager 2 onward to Neptune.

7August of 1989 was when the craft arrived at the planet. Five moons that no one had known were found there. Deep space became Voyager 2's next path. Voyager 1 had already started a journey of that kind. The Voyager mission does more than gather information. A message also rides with them beyond the solar system. The information is a message. The message is for non-Earth life forms. Those life forms are called aliens. The message is on a Golden Record. Each spacecraft carries one on its side. The record includes music. It includes art. It includes spoken greetings from around the world. Nature sounds have a place on it too. The outside of the record has a map. The map shows Earth's location in the universe. Aliens could use the map to find Earth. That probably will not happen anytime soon. Voyager 1 will not come close to another star for a very long time. The wait is at least forty thousand years. A picture shows Doctor Carl Sagan.

8This famous American astronomer led a committee. What went onto each Golden Record was picked by that committee. A note tells about the first messages to aliens. The Golden Record is not the first message humans have sent into space. Pioneer 10 and Pioneer 11 are the only other human-made objects on this kind of path. They are currently headed out of the solar system. Each carries a metal plaque. The plaque shows drawings of a man and a woman. It also shows Earth's position in the universe. The planet studies are finished. The Voyagers now have a new job. No earlier probe had gone out to study space past our solar system. A diagram shows interstellar space. It shows a bubble of strong solar wind coming from our sun. It shows our sun. It shows Voyager 1. It shows a larger bubble of weak solar wind. That weak wind trails behind our sun. It shows Voyager 2. It shows the orbit of Neptune. Neptune is the farthest planet from our sun. A time line lists important dates for the Voyager missions.

9In 1977, Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 launched 16 days apart. In 1979, the Jupiter flybys brought discoveries. The discoveries were volcanoes and ice on Jupiter's moons. In 1980, the Saturn flybys brought another discovery. Titan had an atmosphere. Voyager 1 began its trip out of the solar system. In 1986, Voyager 2 reached Uranus. In 1989, Voyager 2 reached Neptune. It began its trip out of the solar system. In 1990, the Voyager Interstellar Mission began. In 1998, Voyager 1 passed Pioneer 10. It became the most distant human-made object in space. In 2012, Voyager 1 entered interstellar space. The conclusion quotes a project scientist. Project scientist Doctor Edward Stone says the mission laid our solar system open to view. The two spacecraft ended up exploring every giant outer planet. They explored forty-eight of their moons. Deep space is coming into view for the first time because of the Voyagers. A glossary gives word meanings. Atmosphere is a noun. It is a layer of gases surrounding a planet, a star, or a moon.

10Explore is a verb. It means to observe and learn about an area. The learning comes by traveling over or through it. Gravity is a noun. It is the natural force that tends to pull objects toward each other. One example is a pull toward the center of Earth. Instruments is a noun. Instruments are tools. They are especially tools used for making or recording measurements. A magnetic field is a noun. It is an area around a magnet or a moving electric charge. A magnetic force is present there. A mission is a noun. It is a flight of a spacecraft or an aircraft. The purpose is to complete a special assignment. Orbits is a noun. Orbits are the paths taken by objects in space. Those objects circle around other larger objects. Probes is a noun. Probes are devices sent to difficult or dangerous places. They send back information. The solar system is a noun. It is a group of objects in space that orbit a star.

79. Water Found Across Earth

1This book tells where water is found on Earth. The opening part names the places that hold water. The next part names the forms water can take. A later part tells how water changes form. Another part tells why water is important. The last part gives meanings for special water words. The oceans are full of water. The lakes are full of water. The ponds are full of water. Rivers hold water that flows. Streams hold water that flows. Water flows under the ground. Some water stays in the air. Frozen water is found on some parts of Earth. Snow is one kind of that frozen water. Ice is one kind of that frozen water. The ground has flowing water under it. The air holds water around Earth. Some parts of Earth show their frozen water as snow. Some parts of Earth show their frozen water as ice. You can find water when you look at an ocean. You can find water when you look at a lake. You can find water when you look at a pond.

2You can find flowing water when you look at a river. You can find flowing water when you look at a stream. You can find flowing water when you look under the ground. You can find water when you think about the air. You can find snow on some parts of Earth. You can find ice on some parts of Earth. All of these places hold water at one time. A picture can show the water on Earth. The water in the picture has various shades of blue. One area of water may have one shade of blue. Another area may have a different shade of blue. Over two-thirds of the surface of Earth lies under water. Most of the water is salt water. That salt water belongs to the oceans. The oceans hold most of the water. There is much less fresh water. Many plants depend on fresh water. Those plants need fresh water for survival. Many animals depend on fresh water. Those animals need fresh water for survival.

3Some fresh water is aboveground. Aboveground fresh water stays on the surface. Other fresh water is groundwater. Groundwater lies underneath the surface of Earth. In what ways do you use Earth's water? The shades of blue mark water in the picture. More than one blue shade can show up there. Water reaches across most of Earth's surface. Salty water from the oceans makes up most of the water. Fresh water is the much smaller part. A plant depends on fresh water in order to survive. An animal depends on fresh water in order to survive. You might find some fresh water aboveground. You might find other fresh water as groundwater. The groundwater is fresh water under the surface. People use Earth's water in many ways. The fresh water aboveground is only part of all fresh water. Groundwater is the fresh part that stays underneath. Liquid is the state of water seen most often on Earth. Pour liquid water in a container. The water then matches the shape of the container. Liquid water keeps moving at all times.

4Sometimes you cannot see that movement. Currents move the water in rivers. Currents move the water in streams. A current is a constant flow of water. A lake often has the name standing body of water. A pond often has that same name. Water in a lake moves very slowly. Water in a pond moves very slowly. The slow movement is why people use the standing name. Currents also move ocean water. Some ocean currents run along the surface. Other ocean currents run underwater. Waves form as the ocean water moves. The waves crash. Winds can change how strong an ocean current is. The Moon can change how strong an ocean current is. The Sun can change how strong an ocean current is. One picture shows several containers of water. Each of the containers has four cups of water in it. Some water is not liquid. Liquid water freezes when it becomes very cold. The freeze creates ice. Ice is the solid form of water. Ice floats on top of liquid water.

5People make many different shapes from ice. Carving is one way to shape the ice. You have probably made a cube of ice. A cube of ice can cool a drink. An artist can carve a sculpture from ice. Sheets of ice lie across the South Pole. A picture can show those ice sheets on the South Pole. Much frozen water gathers at the North Pole. Much frozen water gathers at the South Pole. Both poles keep a large share of the frozen water. The North Pole is one of the coldest areas on Earth. The South Pole is one of the coldest areas on Earth. The poles are the coldest areas on Earth. Glaciers are huge masses of ice. A common home for glaciers is at the poles. Much of the fresh water on Earth is held in glaciers. The fresh water stays stored in the glacier ice. Which states of water show up in the picture? Some of Earth's water is a gas. People call that gas water vapor.

6Water vapor is a state you cannot see. The vapor stays invisible all the time. This vapor is present all around people. A hot day puts more water vapor into the air. A cold day puts less water vapor into the air. Humidity names how much vapor the air contains. The air has a thick feel on a hot humid day. A large amount of vapor is concentrated in that air. Ice sheets let you see frozen water at the South Pole. A glacier lets you see fresh water held as ice. Vapor is the state that stays invisible. The South Pole picture is filled with sheets of ice. Huge glaciers at the poles hold a lot of fresh water. A snowperson stands in the sunlight. The snowperson absorbs the sunlight. The snow heats up. The heated snow melts. Melted snow becomes liquid water. Earth's water is constantly changing. The change takes water from one state to another. Frozen water melts when heat reaches it. Melting produces liquid water.

7Liquid water freezes when it is cooled. Freezing produces ice. Liquid water is able to turn into a gas. Picture a puddle on a hot day. Did you ever see a puddle dry up during that heat? The liquid water leaves the puddle. Energy from the Sun causes the change. The liquid becomes a gas. People name the change evaporation. Evaporation happens on each day. Each day the Sun shines hard on bodies of water. Those bodies of water lose liquid through evaporation. Energy from the Sun makes the water evaporate. Water vapor can cool in the air. Cooling changes the vapor. The gas becomes liquid water. People name the change condensation. A snowperson gives a close view of melting. A hot puddle gives a close view of evaporation. Cooled vapor gives a close view of condensation. Heat is the cause of melting. Cold is the cause of freezing. Energy from the Sun is the cause of evaporation. Cooling is the cause of condensation. The water keeps changing among these states. The absorbed sunlight is what heats the snow.

8Evaporation sends liquid water into a gas. Condensation brings that gas back into a liquid. Clouds can drop rain onto the desert. The rain is water falling from those clouds. A cloud is made from tiny drops of water. Condensation is how the tiny drops form. The tiny drops stick together. The drops become larger. The larger drops are heavier. A drop falls when it grows large enough. The fall can be rain. The fall can be snow. The fall can be another kind of precipitation. Some rain soaks into the ground. Melted snow soaks into the ground. The water that soaks in becomes groundwater. Precipitation fills rivers. Precipitation fills lakes. Precipitation fills streams. Precipitation fills ponds. Groundwater fills rivers as well. Groundwater fills lakes as well. Groundwater fills streams as well. Groundwater fills ponds as well. Bodies of water lose water by evaporation all over the world. Later the water condenses. Other places on Earth get the water after that. Rain keeps plants alive. Rain allows plants to grow.

9People require water in order to survive. Other animals require water in order to survive. Water has other purposes too. One purpose is fighting fires. People can fight a fire by using water. Evaporation takes water out of bodies of water. Condensation changes that vapor next. Precipitation brings the water down after that. Some soaked water is kept as groundwater. Rivers gain water again from this path. Lakes gain water again from this path. Streams gain water again from this path. Ponds gain water again from this path. One side of the picture shows polluted water. The other side shows water that is not polluted. Caring for Earth's water is important. Water pollution has human activities as its main cause. Sometimes water pollution occurs naturally. Littering makes the water dirty. Dumping trash makes the water dirty. The dirty water can become unusable. Polluted water makes people sick. Polluted water makes plants sick. Polluted water makes animals sick. Would you want to drink water that is polluted? Would you want to play in water that is polluted?

10Concentrated means gathered together. Concentrated also means collected together. A gas is matter. A gas can freely change shape. A gas can freely change size. Often a gas cannot be seen. Glaciers hold large amounts of ice. Glaciers hold large amounts of snow. Glaciers are found year-round. A glacier slowly moves downhill. Groundwater is water held underground. Soil can hold groundwater. Rock can hold groundwater. Groundwater often feeds springs. Groundwater often feeds wells. Invisible means unable to be seen. A liquid is able to flow. A liquid can change shape. A liquid keeps its size. Precipitation is water coming down to the ground. Hail is one kind of precipitation. Sleet is one kind of precipitation. Rain is one kind of precipitation. Snow is one kind of precipitation. A solid has a firm form. A solid has a hard shape. Dirty materials in bodies of water count as water pollution. Harmful materials in bodies of water count as water pollution. Caring for the water closes the book.

80. Water Under Our Feet

1Ten days after her well went dry in August 2014, a woman in California received drinking water. The delivery was 300 gallons. That amount is 1,136 liters. Picture a morning when the faucet gives only a gurgle. That is what happened in part of California. Fruits and vegetables made that region well known. Years of drought meant little rain. Huge volumes of groundwater were pumped up so the crops could be watered. About two thousand wells were dry in 2014. California is not the only place facing a water crisis. Groundwater is water held in the ground. Rocks, sand, and soil under the surface hold it in their gaps and cracks. A chart splits all the water on Earth. Saltwater makes up 97 percent. Freshwater makes up 3 percent. Of the fresh share, ground ice and permafrost make up 70 percent. Surface water makes up 0.3 percent. A geology survey of the United States is the source of those shares. A globe can make the planet look full of water for every need. Most of Earth's water sits in the oceans. That ocean water is full of salt. Freshwater is what keeps us alive.

2The fresh share of water on Earth is very small. Most of that small fresh share is frozen. Of the water people can use, scientists place 99 percent below the ground. Aquifers beneath the land hold most of this groundwater. An aquifer is a layer of rock, gravel, and sand below the surface. It holds groundwater the way a huge sponge holds water. Rain flows very slowly into surface soil and rock. Melting snow does the same. That slow flow feeds the aquifers. Thousands of feet is how far down an aquifer may go. It can stretch for thousands of miles. Aquifers feed rivers. They feed lakes. They feed wetlands. Their fresh water supplies the best farming lands in the world. Billions of gallons have been pumped up by people across more than a hundred years. They drink that water. They grow crops with it. They meet other needs with it. Even in a drought, towns above aquifers can draw groundwater. Farms above aquifers can draw it too. It keeps many people from going hungry. It keeps many from going thirsty. The trouble is that levels are falling fast.

3Saving this resource, which gives life, is a task for people everywhere. Farming in many dry places depends on one method of bringing water to the fields. Experts say people pump groundwater faster than nature can put it back. From 1995 onward, nearly two out of three wells in the United States have shown lower water. Wheat fields across America draw on one enormous aquifer. That store of water cannot last forever. About sixty years of change in the water level appear on a map. The years are 1950 to 2010. A geology survey of the United States supplied the figures. In South Dakota, the change was 0 feet, or 0 meters. In Wyoming, the level fell 0.4 feet, or 0.12 meters. In Nebraska, it fell 0.9 feet, or 0.27 meters. In Colorado, it fell 13.2 feet, or 4.02 meters. In Kansas, it fell 22.8 feet, or 6.95 meters. In New Mexico, it fell 15.1 feet, or 4.6 meters. In Oklahoma, it fell 12.3 feet, or 3.75 meters. In Texas, it fell 36.7 feet, or 11.19 meters. In some places, today's level is largely the same as before. In other places, the level has crashed.

4A scale on one drawing runs from 0 to 140 feet under the ground. More than 120 feet is how far the water dropped in one well in Texas. That drop is 36.6 meters. Grape fields made California's Sonoma Valley famous. In some farming areas, people compete for the water below them. In Northern California, winemakers grow grapes. Those grapes need large amounts of water. Deep wells have been drilled by many people who make wine there. A sure supply of the groundwater they require is what they are after. Their businesses might have to close if that water is missing. Many smaller farmers nearby are not happy. Homeowners nearby are not happy either. They blame the winemakers for taking too much of the water everyone shares. In many cases, smaller owners cannot pay to drill a deeper well. Their shallower wells go dry. Picture many straws in one drink. Extra straws are something certain farms and businesses are able to buy. They can pay for longer ones. Big questions follow from this. Who owns the water under the ground? What counts as fair? One cubic kilometer contains 1000000000000 liters.

5That amount is 264,172,052,358 gallons. It would fill about 400,000 swimming pools of Olympic size. A country's population can affect how much water it uses. Even so, the countries here use different amounts for each person. India has 1,225 million people. Its yearly water use totals 251 cubic kilometers. Each person there uses 205,000 liters a year. China has 1,341 million people. Its yearly total is 112 cubic kilometers. Each person there uses 83,500 liters a year. The United States has 310 million people. Its yearly total is 112 cubic kilometers. Each person there uses 361,300 liters a year. Pakistan has 174 million people. Its yearly total is 65 cubic kilometers. Each person there uses 373,600 liters a year. Pakistan has lost almost 75 percent of its water supply over a vast distance from here. The drop covers the last sixty years. Many people in Pakistan blame India. India, they say, sits next to them on the south side. Dams built by India are what they complain about. River water that used to arrive, they say, is now stopped by those dams. Because of that, they say, farmers in Pakistan have had to pump extra groundwater.

6India says the blame sits with Pakistan alone. India says Pakistan has handled its water poorly. Four wars between these neighbors already belong to the past. Today, both face shortages of water. Both face growing competition for freshwater. Worse fights about water are what experts expect ahead. If nothing changes, they estimate a shortfall by the year 2030. The world would then be 40 percent short of the water it needs. Severe lack of water could reach nearly two billion people around the world. Food shortages could follow. Famine could follow. War itself can grow out of shortages of this kind as time passes. Experts give that warning. A shortage of water has already brought that result in certain places. A civil war that began in Syria in 2011 may have been helped along by drought. Many Syrians have died because of that war. In many places, groundwater matters that much. It can mean green farms or dusty fields. It can mean life or death. Two children from Syria stand outside their shelter. Many wildfires have followed times of drought. An old saying fits this problem.

7Staying clear of trouble is easier than escaping trouble later. The same idea is true for water under the ground. Action now is far easier than a rescue after the aquifers have gone dry. Planning ahead is required. Climate change is a rising threat against the water supply. Nearly every scientist who studies Earth now shares one conclusion. The planet is getting hotter. Some areas may also get less rain as the climate shifts. Finding fresh water may grow more difficult. Another huge challenge is the growing number of people on Earth. The planet's population now stands above eight billion. By 2100, the expected total is eleven billion. Every person will need water to drink. Every person will need water to grow food. We must manage groundwater now. We are able to do it. Our best hope is to use less water. Our best hope is also to leave more of it in the ground. Experts estimate a large share goes to crops. About 65 percent of fresh groundwater pumped by Americans goes onto fields. Yet many ways of watering waste a lot. They spray water on the surface.

8They sprinkle it there too. Much of it is lost when it evaporates. More farmers now use new methods so every drop counts. New meters watch how wet the soil is. The meters turn the water on and off. Less water is wasted that way. Many orchards of fruit and nuts now bury the pipes. Less water is lost when the pipes are buried. Blossoms cover the almond trees. Less water is needed to grow wheat than to grow corn. Choosing different crops can also help save groundwater. Certain farms in Kansas, and others in Iowa, are leaving corn behind. A corn crop drinks a great deal of water. Other crops are taking its place. New methods of farming are what farmers require. Empty aquifers will force a change on anyone who will not choose to save water. A record drought forced California to cut water use. Half the former use was all that remained after only six months. Hundreds of gallons each week can be saved by small changes in homes across the United States. That saving can happen in an average American home. A 2016 study measured how homes in the United States use water.

9No other item in a house uses as much water as the toilet does. Daily toilet use was 33.1 gallons per household. That is 125.3 liters. The study also counted the clothes washer. It counted the dishwasher, leaks, and other uses as one group. It counted the faucet. It counted the shower and the bath. The other daily household amounts were 31.7 gallons, or 120 liters. Another amount was 26.3 gallons, or 99.6 liters. Another was 23.9 gallons, or 90.5 liters. Another was 22.7 gallons, or 86 liters. A study from 1999 found higher toilet use than the later study found. Less water reaches the sewer from some toilets of newer design. Some families flush less often. They do not flush after every use. Putting in low-flush toilets lets a home use less groundwater. Taking shorter showers helps too. Running a washing machine only when it is full can save gallons. Running a dishwasher only when it is full can save gallons too. Some people take out thirsty grass. Plants with smaller water needs take the place of that grass. Keeping groundwater safe for later years is hard work.

10Drinking water is a basic human need. Crops must be watered or there will be no food. Even so, we can be smarter about this resource now. We can be smarter far into the future. Dry summers lead some owners to replace grass with plants of another kind. Some words name the ideas in this account. Rock, sand, and other material in a layer under the ground make an aquifer. It can hold water and soak it up. Climate change means lasting shifts in the weather patterns of Earth or of one region. Conserving means guarding a wild place, or a resource, so the supply lasts. A crisis is a dangerous or unstable time that demands attention. Little rain, or no rain, across a long dry spell is a drought. Soil or rock below the surface is where groundwater is stored. It often feeds springs and wells. Irrigation means giving water to land so that crops have a chance to grow. To monitor is to watch how something moves forward. Something valuable, or very useful, in a ready supply is a resource.

81. When a Rattlesnake Strikes

1Contents. The opening is marked 4. Rattlesnake basics are marked 6. Fangs and venom are marked 10. Symptoms and treatment are marked 12. Rattlesnake first aid is marked 16. The closing is marked 17. Safety tips are marked 18. What to do if you see a rattlesnake is marked 19. The word list is marked 20. Opening. The year was 1951. Jennifer Owings was nine. A horse carried her through the hills. Home was a ranch in New Mexico, and the hills were close by. She got off the horse to look for a new path. A sandstone ridge stood above, and the path might lead to its top. She climbed a cliff face. She reached up and grabbed a ledge. A sudden pain screamed along the side of her hand. A rattlesnake had struck her. A photo shows Jennifer at age 9. Home was a twenty-minute ride on horseback. Halfway there, she felt dizzy. She felt sick to her stomach. She grew afraid she might die before she reached the house. She still made it. Jennifer's ride to the nearest emergency room was with the ranch foreman. The medicine for rattlesnake strikes was gone, the doctors said. For most of three days, Jennifer was not conscious. Her symptoms were severe. She survived. Today she hardly has a scar from the strike. Luck was on her side. What follows a strike on a person? What treatments do snakebite victims receive?

2What can you do so you are not struck? Keep those questions in mind as you learn about rattlesnakes. Their strikes can be deadly. A caption says a prairie rattlesnake struck Jennifer. Basics of rattlesnakes. Thirty-one rattlesnake species have been identified by scientists. People often call them rattlers. Most of the United States is part of their range. They live in Mexico. They live in Central America and in South America. Southern Canada is part of the range too. Deserts can hold them. Swamps, forests, prairies, and mountains can hold them too. Dry land in the southwestern United States holds the greatest numbers. Northern Mexico is another common place. A map shows ranges for six common rattlesnakes of the United States. The six are the Eastern diamondback rattlesnake and the Western diamondback rattlesnake. They also include the prairie rattlesnake and the timber rattlesnake. The pigmy rattlesnake and the sidewinder complete the six. A fact to know. Hearing, for these snakes, happens through the jaws. The jaws rest on the ground. Vibrations from nearby animals, including people, travel into those jaws. Harm to people is why their reputation is bad. Set people aside for a moment and look at the snakes themselves. Few hunters in nature work as efficiently as these creatures. They surprise small animals. They also catch prey inside burrows. Total darkness does not stop them from finding prey. Smell is one main sense they use.

3Heat is the other. Special areas on the face can sense heat. A prey animal can sit completely still. Body heat still gives it away. The tongue picks up smells. A special organ in the head receives those smells. They do not seek people out just to cause harm. A meeting with you is something they would rather skip. They do know how to defend themselves. They defend themselves when they think danger is near. The rattle on the tail is a warning. People and other animals are being told to keep back. Ignore that warning and a strike can follow. Most people struck by a rattlesnake do not die. They do suffer terrible pain. Skin grafts are needed by some victims, Jennifer among them. Some need medical care that is even more extreme. Bad scars can remain. Other body damage can be permanent. A fact. A rattle is not a warning you can always expect. A strike can come with no rattle first. Sometimes the rattle comes after the strike. For Jennifer, buzz and strike felt like one moment. Knowing how rattlesnakes behave can protect you. Like all reptiles, rattlesnakes are coldblooded. Outside air sets whether the body runs warm or cool. A sunny spot is the choice when they feel too cold. Too warm, and they move under a rock. They may go to an underground den instead. Shade is the likely resting place on a hot day where rattlers live.

4Rocks, bushes, or cacti may cover them. Imagine a cool morning and a trail that climbs steeply. A rock overhead becomes the handhold you use to climb. If the top of the rock is hidden, a warming rattlesnake may be there. You might startle it. Jennifer learned that lesson the hard way. Fangs and venom. Two long fangs do the work of putting venom into prey. The fangs are hollow. Glands in the head send venom toward the fangs. Muscles squeeze the glands. From there it shoots into the fangs and on into the victim. A closed mouth keeps the fangs folded. Before a strike, the snake extends the fangs and spreads them. The fangs break the prey's skin. They sink into flesh. Venom goes in. The head pulls back quickly. A deeper bite gives venom a greater chance to kill the prey. Labels on a diagram name the parts. They include the fang sheath, the nostril, and the eye. They include the fangs and the upper jaw. They include the venom gland, the breathing tube, and the lower jaw. A word to know. Call rattlesnakes venomous. Do not call them poisonous. Venom is a harmful substance an animal forces into a victim's bloodstream. Poison usually means something harmful when it is swallowed, breathed, or touched. Extra fangs sit tucked inside the upper jaw. A broken fang is replaced when another drops into place.

5Fangs are also shed several times each year. Nature supplies extras because a rattler cannot survive without fangs. Birth is live. Most other snakes hatch from eggs. A rattler does not. No rattle is present yet on a baby, so no warning shake is possible. It can still inject venom. Every strike can be deadly. Age does not remove that danger. Small size does not remove it either. Fangs on a grown Eastern diamondback can reach 27 millimeters. That length is 1 inch. Venom causes three kinds of damage. It moves through the bloodstream. It destroys red blood cells. It keeps blood from clotting. It paralyzes muscles. The heart is one of those muscles. Muscles that control limbs are included. Muscles that control breathing are included. Venom also attacks cells directly. Organs break down. Tissues break down. Look closely at one photo and you can see fang marks on a person's foot. How do you know a rattlesnake struck you? Seeing the snake is one way. Hearing the rattle is another. Some people never see the snake or hear it. Age can leave a victim unable to explain the event. A pair of marks is what you might expect. A rattler can strike after losing one fang. Then only one mark may show. Telling a rattlesnake wound from other wounds is not always easy. Symptoms develop over many hours. Other causes are what doctors try to set aside first.

6Bee stings are one cause they consider. Spider bites are another. Redness is a common symptom. Swelling is common. Severe pain is common. Bruising may spread out from the strike. People may feel weak. They may go into shock. Symptoms differ from one victim to another. Some have blurry vision. Some feel sick to their stomach. Some faint. Up to the late 1990s, antivenin reached only about half of victims. Antivenin is the medicine for a rattlesnake strike. A person's reaction to that medicine could be extreme. The reaction might put a life in danger. Doctors now have a safer kind of antivenin. They can use it more often. Try this math. Antivenin, also called antivenom, costs a great deal. One vial is about $3,000. A common amount is 20 vials, and some patients get 30. Hospital time for one 78-year-old lasted two days. That person received 20 vials. Other hospital costs were added in. The full bill came to $132,000. How much of the bill was the antivenin? How much was the rest of the hospital cost? Jennifer had no antivenin. Most of those three days passed while she was not awake. When she woke, a terrible burning pain would not stop. Bad swelling showed on the side where the fangs hit. It bruised badly. She feared the tight skin would split open. For some people the swelling is worse. Surgery is needed to reduce the pressure.

7Another operation is needed by about one person in four. That surgery cleans out dead tissue destroyed by the venom. A photo shows rattlesnake antivenin. A scar in one photo comes from a skin graft. The graft covered a woman's wound. Once Jennifer could go home, she rested for months. Three weeks after the strike, most symptoms had grown less severe. Her hand was still swollen. Her arm was still swollen. The skin was dark and peeling. Three grafts of skin closed the wound once healing had gone further. Some victims need further surgery. The goal is to get blood flowing again near the strike. Full use of the hurt part never returns for about one person in four. Removal of a body part is sometimes required when tissue damage is severe. Rattlesnake first aid. Do these things. Get away from the rattlesnake. Call 9-1-1. Remove rings. Remove bracelets and boots. Remove anything that swelling could make tight. Keep the victim as calm as you can. Calm keeps the venom from circulating through the rest of the body. Have the victim lie down. The injured part should rest below heart level. Soap and water on the wound are wise if they are available. A victim who must walk for help should sit before starting. The sit should last 20 to 30 minutes. Then move in a calm, slow way. Moving slowly limits how far the venom spreads.

8A hospital should be the goal, reached as quickly as possible. Do not do these things. Do not cut the wound. Do not try to suck the venom out with your mouth. That almost never helps. A mouth sore can let venom into the blood. Do not put ice on the wound. Do not put anything else cold on it. Cold makes the injury worse. Closing. Rattlesnakes belong to the web of life. Many food chains depend on them as a link. Small animals such as mice would become too many if these snakes disappeared. Nature's balance would be upset. A right to live belongs to rattlesnakes, as it does to all living things. They can be frightening. They endanger people who startle them or threaten them. In rattlesnake country, respect the animals that live there. The respect is much like following rules in a friend's home. Keep your eyes open. Think about their habits. You will be safe. Tips for staying safe. To avoid rattlesnakes, try these steps. Watch carefully outdoors in places they inhabit. They blend into the places around them, so take care. Look in every direction. A strike can reach at least half the snake's body length. A rattlesnake can grow to 1.8 meters, or 6 feet. Any hour of day or night can find them active. Stay alert at all times. A dark hiding spot is never a place for your hand.

9Unseen rocks and other unseen surfaces are no place for a hand or a foot. Wear thick shoes in rattlesnake country. High leather boots are better if you have them. Tall grass is better avoided. Do not run ahead of family or friends on a trail. A younger child should stay close to an adult. Avoid ditches beside the road. Rattlesnakes like to hunt there. Never move closer to a snake just to learn whether it is a rattler. If you see a rattlesnake, remember these steps. If one appears suddenly and close, freeze. The next move is a very slow step backward. A sudden move may make the snake think it is in danger. Do not back into a second snake. Do not surprise a rattler. A feeling of being trapped or cornered is dangerous to create. A trapped snake may strike to defend itself. If the snake is trying to get away, let it go. Chasing it for a closer look can excite it. An excited snake may turn on you. Never touch a snake you think is dead. It might only be playing dead. Think about this. Some strikes are accidents. Most people who are struck made poor choices. They thought getting close would be cool. They wanted to see the snake excited and rattling. Some thought picking one up would test their courage. Those choices ended in severe pain. They ended in scars that last.

10They ended in huge hospital bills. Word list. Antivenin, a noun on page 13, is a medicine that stops the damage from a venom. Shock, a noun on page 13, is a medical condition that may follow a severe injury. Bruising, a noun on page 13, means dark blue or black patches on skin. Skin grafts, a noun on page 8, means skin taken from one place on the body and set on another. Clotting, a noun on page 11, means sticking together so bleeding stops. Species, a noun on page 6, are groups of related living things. Traits set them apart from other groups. Breeding can happen between members that share a species. Heeded, a verb on page 8, means paid attention to. Injects, a verb on page 10, means forces something into a body with a needle or with fangs. Symptoms, a noun on page 12, are the particular signs that illness or injury shows. Paralyzes, a verb on page 11, means makes a body part unable to move. Unconscious, an adjective on page 5, means not aware and not mentally awake. Prey, a noun on page 7, means the animals eaten by other animals. Venom, a noun on page 10, is a poison put in by some animals. They use it on prey and for defense. Rule out, a verb on page 12, means to remove something as a possibility.

82. When a Test Causes Fear

1Stage fright is also called performance anxiety. The thrill, for most entertainers, includes the wait before they go on. They may feel excited. They may feel a little nervous. Once the spotlight is on them, the jitters fade. Jitters are feelings of nervousness or unease. Some performers feel much more than a little nerves. They may begin to sweat. They may find it hard to get a full breath. Their hearts race. Fear freezes them. Walking onstage becomes impossible. Stage fright is the name for this terrible anxiety. Anxiety means being worried or nervous. Students can feel something like stage fright when an exam is near. They may be so afraid that they cannot focus. Clear thought may slip away. Sickness is possible too. People call this test anxiety. A person can be stopped cold, much as stage fright can. Students can take steps to face the fear. They can still do well on a test. Helplessness is a common sign of test anxiety. Disappointment is a common sign too. Everybody feels anxious at some time. The feeling is not always harmful.

2Anxiety is uncomfortable. A purpose still sits inside it. Survival is what this natural reaction is built to support. It appears when we feel threatened. It appears when we are under stress. Stress is physical or emotional tension. It can feel like worry. There are two kinds of stress. One kind is good stress. The other kind is bad stress. A challenge that a person can handle is what brings good stress. Think of a student who receives homework. The student has enough time to finish. The student has enough resources to finish. Homework of that kind counts as good stress. A motivator is one role good stress can play. A motivator is a person or a thing that gives you a reason to act, think, or feel. Looking ahead to something unpleasant can create anxiety you do not need. Looking ahead to something scary can do the same. Good stress and bad stress feel different. Fun can belong with good stress. A challenge can belong with good stress. Healthy tension can belong with good stress.

3Positive anticipation can belong with good stress. Fear can belong with bad stress. Panic can belong with bad stress. Exhaustion can belong with bad stress. Depression can belong with bad stress. Both kinds of stress are needed. Together they support a healthy balance of emotions. Bad stress comes when a challenge seems impossible. It also comes when a challenge feels overwhelming. Overwhelming means the feelings or thoughts are too heavy to manage. A challenge may not truly be impossible. Low confidence can turn that challenge into bad stress anyway. The two kinds of stress create different amounts of anxiety. Good stress brings just enough worry to help a person focus. Then the person can do the task well. Bad stress does the opposite. It may cause panic. A person in a panic feels overwhelmed. That person cannot think clearly. That person cannot focus clearly. This anxiety may appear as stage fright for a performer. It may appear as test anxiety for a student. Both kinds of stress usually begin outside the body. The anxiety itself is inside the head.

4It starts in a brain area called the amygdala. Amygdala is said uh-MIG-duh-luh. A diagram of the brain can show a cross section. The diagram marks where the amygdala sits. The brain keeps processing information all the time. That information comes from the five senses. If the information signals a threat, the amygdala goes to work. It sends messages to glands. The glands release chemicals called stress hormones. Hormones are chemicals that change how the body grows and develops. At once, the hormones put the body into a threatened state. Breathing speeds up. The heart speeds up. Blood moves toward the muscles. Blood moves away from the parts of the brain that solve problems. The muscles tighten. They are ready to battle a danger. They are ready to run from it. Fight-or-flight is the name for this response. Early humans survived danger with its help. It was useful when prehistoric people met a physical threat. The threat could cost them their lives. A hungry beast was one such threat. To be endangered is to be put in harm's way.

5Today, most people are not worried about fleeing hungry beasts. We worry instead about school demands. We worry about work demands. Will the grade be a good one? Will the assignment be done on time? Those worries cause stress. They are not events that threaten life. A doctor first described fight-or-flight in the early twentieth century. America was his country. Walter Bradford Cannon was his name. Animal reactions to a threat were what he studied. Freezing, which means staying perfectly still, was the first impulse. A predator might miss an animal that stayed still. Failure to freeze led the animal to get ready to fight. Or it prepared to run. People came to call these reactions freeze, fight, or flight. Cannon then asked a further question. He wondered whether humans reacted in a similar way. They do. Control of the response sits in the oldest and most primitive brain parts. It happens in an instant. It is almost impossible to control. A sudden crash can set off freezing.

6It can set off fighting. It can set off flight. Modern life is ahead of what human biology was built for. The brain reacts in the old way even when the stress will not kill us. The body reacts that way too. It has done so for thousands of years. Fight-or-flight switches on. A performer with stage fright freezes. That performer is ready to flee the spotlight. A student facing a hard test may react as ancestors did. Those ancestors were facing real danger. Blood rushes to the muscles. The heart pounds. Breathing gets faster. For someone with test anxiety, this reaction does more harm than good. A test taker needs clear thinking. A test taker does not need to get ready for a fight. More blood should reach the brain. It should not all go to the muscles. The body does not know that. It is no wonder focus becomes so difficult. Failure is not the certain end of test anxiety. Students can handle the anxiety and still do well. They have more than one way to do it.

7Step one is stopping good stress from becoming bad stress. Preparing the mind is the best method. Preparing the body matters too. Do not treat the test as a challenge that is too big to face. Use it as a reason to learn. Start by being sure you understand the material. Know what the test will cover. Put that material into a study plan. Use the days before the test to follow that plan. Practice quizzes are worth trying. The quizzes let you feel what the real test will be like. Then you have tools for the questions. You have a better chance to do well. Help can come from a partner who studies with you. A partner with a positive attitude can build the attitude you need. That attitude supports success. It helps you stay out of bad stress. The body needs preparation too. Avoid being overly tired on test day. Avoid being hungry. Memory loss can follow when fatigue sets in. Fatigue means weariness of the mind or the body. If memory slips, it is hard to recall what you studied.

8A full night of rest helps. Healthy food helps. Then you can be at your best in body and in mind. Some foods carry nutrients that can calm anxiety in a natural way. Blueberries are one of those foods. Almonds are one. Garlic is one. Fish is one. Oranges are one. Oatmeal is one. Leafy greens are one. Bananas are one. Water is one. Avocados are one. Yogurt is one. The next step is to notice your body. Catch anxiety's signs early, before growth sets in. Metacognition can help you do that. Metacognition means knowing your own thinking. It also means knowing your own learning. You notice what the mind is doing right then. You notice what the body is doing too. Picture yourself on a mental balcony. From up there, you watch yourself. A hard question may make you shift in your seat. Does your mind go blank when you meet a word you do not know? Does your mind wander at that moment? If you recognize your own stress signals, you can plan.

9You can keep the anxiety from growing stronger. When anxiety starts, a test taker can use strategies to push it back. Anxiety comes from the body's natural answer to stress. Lower the stress, and the anxiety drops too. If one question stops you, do not stew over it. Skip that question. Move to one you can answer. Moving from bad stress toward good stress lowers anxiety. It keeps you moving. You can come back to the hard questions later. Do not load yourself with too much pressure. Pressure is a tense, urgent feeling. It can come from having too much to do. It can come from expectations that are very high. A few questions will likely stump you. The aim is to do your best. Perfection is not the aim. A deep breath deserves a quiet moment. Close your eyes and let your body ease. Some students freeze when they try to write. They can jot words on scrap paper. They can doodle on that paper. The marks do not need to be special.

10The point is to take action. Action pushes back against anxiety. Writing anything can break the block. Soon, ideas start to come. Then you can shape your answer. If panic is strong, step back for a moment. Stop and shut your eyes. Breathe in a slow, deep way. Picture a peaceful place. Let the picture calm you down. As you relax, remember that you prepared. Remember that things can still go well. Test anxiety can feel scary. It can feel frustrating. You can still push it back. Remember that everyone feels anxiety sometimes. A test can bring it on. Other stressful moments can bring it on too. You might be taking a test. You might be performing. You might be in a competition. The same calming strategies can help you stay cool. Treat stress as a motivator. Do not let it act only as a block. Handling anxiety well helps you show your best. That is true for the test. That is true for other challenges too. More preparation brings more confidence.

83. When Color Vision Is Different

1A picture shows a box of crayons in two ways. The top view is full-color vision. The bottom view is red-green color blindness. The top view makes a red crayon much easier to choose. Imagine that same box of crayons. To many people with color blindness, the crayons look like shades of gray. They also look like shades of blue. They also look like shades of yellow. Color blindness is also called CVD. CVD means color vision deficiency. Most people with CVD still see some colors. The world simply looks a little different to them than it does to other people. Every color of the rainbow is hidden inside white light. Wavelength is what makes one color unlike another. Objects look different in color for a clear reason. They absorb some wavelengths of light. They reflect other wavelengths.

2Red wavelengths bounce off a strawberry, so the fruit looks red. It absorbs most of the other wavelengths. The cornea gathers light from the outside world. It starts to focus that light. Rods and cones line the wall of the retina. The eye's rear wall is where the retina lies. Those cells register what is being seen. The iris opens the pupil. The iris can also close the pupil. That change lets more light in, or less light. The optic nerve carries messages to the brain. The messages tell what the eye is seeing. At the eye's center, the pupil is an opening. Light passes through that hole. The lens changes shape. The new shape finishes the focusing. People see color because the eyes answer differently to different wavelengths. Light that enters an eye is focused on the retina.

3Tissue in a thin layer makes up the retina. The eyeball's rear is where it lies. The light triggers rod cells. The light also triggers cone cells. Signals from those cells travel to the brain. The brain uses the signals to build images. Inside the retina, rod cells notice the smallest changes. They let you see when light is very low. They cannot tell one color from another. Cone cells handle color vision. Cone cells come in three types. One type absorbs blue light. One type absorbs green light. One type absorbs red light. Together, these three main colors let us see thousands of colors. CVD happens when one or more cone types are not working. Most animals see fewer colors than humans do. Dogs see only shades of blue and yellow.

4Bulls cannot see red. Humans see fewer colors than some animals. Some birds are in that group. Bees are in that group. Butterflies are in that group. They can see wavelengths that are invisible to humans. A picture can set human vision next to honeybee vision. Telling red apart from green is difficult in this red-green form of CVD. This is the most common color-vision problem. Among men, about 1 in 12 is affected. Among women, about 1 in 200 is affected. The green cone cells are usually the problem. Green objects then look reddish. Yellow objects look reddish too. Sometimes the cause is a missing red cone cell. Sometimes that red cone cell is damaged. Then red objects look green or black. Orange objects look green or black. Yellow objects look green or black.

5A picture can show full-color vision beside red-green CVD vision. The colors compared there are yellow, green, and red. Missing blue cone cells can cause blue-yellow CVD. It also happens when those cells do not work properly. This kind of CVD is fairly rare. Worldwide, about 1 person in 10,000 has it. Red-green CVD is more common in men.
