Men and women get blue-yellow CVD at the same rate. A picture shows that complete CVD is very rare. The most serious kind is complete CVD. It happens when two types of cone cells have problems. It can also happen when all three types have problems. Light bothers people who lack several cone types very easily. They may have trouble seeing clearly. Bright light makes that trouble worse. Most of what they see depends on rod cells.

6Some rely on rod cells for all of it. Rod cells work only in low light. Worldwide, about 1 person in 30,000 lacks every cone type. With no working cone cells, a person sees only shades of black. That person also sees shades of white. That person also sees shades of gray. Some traffic lights use shapes along with colors. The shapes give information to drivers who have CVD. Challenges can come up for people who have CVD. Reading a map may be hard. Children with CVD may struggle with some classwork. CVD can also create safety problems. Fire hydrants are often colored red. They are often colored yellow too. Emergency equipment often uses those same colors. The colors are meant to make the objects easier to see. A person with CVD may miss bright colors.

7Those colors stand out for people with full-color vision. CVD may affect the job a person chooses. Some jobs require full-color vision. Airline pilot is one of those jobs. Other jobs are harder for people with CVD. Photographer is one example. Almost any job is still possible for most people with CVD. Doctors use several tests to learn whether a person has CVD. One test uses pictures made of dots. The dots differ in size. The dots differ in color. Hidden numbers in the pictures show up for a person with full-color vision. That person can also see hidden shapes. A person with red-green CVD may miss those hidden symbols. A test picture asks if you can find hidden numbers in the circles. It also asks if you can find hidden shapes.

8So far, doctors cannot cure CVD. New tools now help people manage this condition. Smartphone apps can name a color. They can choose colors that go well together. The apps can help a person pick matching clothes. They can help a person tell which fruit is ripe. A grocery store is one place to use them. Researchers have made high-tech sunglasses too. Certain wavelengths of light are blocked by the sunglasses. With them, a person with red-green CVD can tell those two colors apart. Special glasses can open a much more colorful world for that person. Squirrel monkeys were the animals in a recent red-green CVD study. In an experiment, a squirrel monkey may take a color-vision test. Those monkeys had no red cone cells. They could not tell red from green. The missing gene was placed into eye cells of the monkeys.

9About twenty weeks later, the monkeys could tell red dots from green dots. A wish for full-color vision may come true before long. CVD is fairly common. It is usually not a serious problem. Finding CVD early helps a person understand what they see. It also helps the person adjust. Daily life goes well for most people with CVD. The world may look slightly different to them. That difference does not stop a rich life. It does not stop a fulfilling life. An app is a computer program made for a mobile device. A cell phone is one such device. Color blindness means a person cannot tell two or more colors apart. Doctors call this color vision deficiency, or CVD. Cone cells sit in the retina.

10They sense color. They work best in bright light. A gene is a basic unit of heredity. A trait moves from parents to children through a gene. Rear eye tissue is another name for the retina. It receives images. It sends those images to the brain. Rod cells also sit in the retina. They sense light. They work best in dim light. Sensitive can mean able to notice a small change. It can also mean easily irritated. It can also mean easily damaged. Tissue is material made of a group of cells. Those cells are similar in form. They carry out one job. A wavelength is the distance from one high point of a wave to the next high point. It can also be the distance from one low point to the next low point.

84. When Earth Shakes, Erupts, and Floods

1Contents. The opening is marked 4. Deep under the planet is marked 5. Earthquakes, the terrible trembling, is marked 7. The Richter scale is marked 10. Volcanoes, the enormous explosions, is marked 11. Tsunamis, the wild waves, is marked 16. The closing is marked 21. The word list is marked 22. Smoke climbs from a volcano that looks calm. Opening. People usually picture the ground as steady. They picture the ocean as peaceful. The ground rests quietly under our feet. Waves touch the shore in a gentle way. Forces far below can end that calm in a moment. Those forces make violent shaking. The shaking is called an earthquake. They also blast out ash, gases, and rock that is hot. Those explosions are called volcanoes. They make huge waves called tsunamis, said tsoo-NOM-ees. This book explains these amazing events. It also explains the forces that set them off. Far below the surface. Under the soil, rock, and water, our planet keeps changing. Earth's top layer is built from giant pieces of rock. The pieces fit like a puzzle. The pieces are called plates. Plates form the continents. Plates form the ocean floors. Edges of plates often meet at cracks and gaps.

2Those cracks and gaps are called faults. A diagram of tectonic plates uses dotted lines. The lines mark the plate edges. Below the plates, Earth is hot enough to melt rock. Melted rock is a liquid called magma. Plates float on that liquid magma. Magma keeps moving. The moving magma drags the plates along. Three different things can happen where moving plates meet. Figure 1 shows plates moving against each other. One plate then slides over the other, or under it. Figure 2 shows plates moving past each other. Their edges grind together. Figure 3 shows plates moving apart. A gap opens, and magma seeps out. Buildings can be damaged by an earthquake. An earthquake can even destroy them. Terrible trembling. Most of the time, plates creep along very slowly. Sometimes big pieces of the plates snag on each other. The plates still try to move. Big chunks of rock stop the plates. Pressure builds. Energy builds. Then the rocks give way all at once. The stored pressure and energy rush out. The plates jerk ahead. The ground shakes. People standing far above feel an earthquake. A small quake shakes the ground only a little.

3Hanging objects may swing. Tree branches sway, as they would in a soft breeze. Some quakes are so small that nobody notices. Other quakes shake with terrible strength. Buildings crumble. Bridges fall. Wide cracks open in the ground across large areas. One picture shows ground ripped open by a quake. Strength is measured with a seismograph, said SIZE-moh-graf. Each quake gets a number from the Richter scale, said RICK-ter. The number depends on strength. A chart on the next page tells how each strength feels. On this scale, each whole number is ten times the strength of the number before it. A quake of 6.0 has ten times the strength of a quake of 5.0. A 6.0 quake has one hundred times the strength of a 4.0 quake. The biggest quake ever recorded reached 9.5. It struck the coast of Chile. Chile is a country in South America. These dangerous events are still being studied. Someday they may warn people before a quake begins. One picture shows a seismograph. The Richter scale. 1. People feel nothing. Only sensitive tools can detect the quake. 2. Most people feel nothing.

4A few people in tall buildings may notice a slight sway. 3. Many people near the starting place notice the shaking. No damage happens. 4. People at the starting place clearly feel it. Hanging objects swing. Pool water sloshes. A weak building may be harmed. 5. A wider area feels it. Weak buildings at the center, and nearby, often take heavy damage. Strong buildings may take some damage. 6. Weak buildings take heavy damage. Strong buildings take some damage. Harm can reach across 160 kilometers, or 100 miles. 7. This is a very major quake. At the center, most buildings fall. Cracks form in the earth. Pipes under the ground break. Large landslides can start. 8. Buildings are destroyed. Bridges are destroyed. Big cracks show in the ground. Landslides are large. 9. The ground seems to roll in waves. A whole river may shift. Items can fly upward. Buildings and other structures are totally destroyed. The Richter scale has no top number. The strongest quake on record was still a 9.5. Volcanoes and their huge blasts. A volcano happens when magma from deep inside comes out. A surface crack is the path it takes.

5Volcanoes usually form near plate edges. Many cracks and thin spots are there. Magma can leak through those spots. Magma that pours onto the surface hardens. It often piles up into a mountain. Sometimes the liquid rock flows out in a peaceful way. Many volcanoes that are still active in Hawaii behave this way. On one Hawaiian volcano, runny liquid rock flows smoothly. It looks like a calm river. Some volcanoes get plugged, as the left picture shows. They can then explode with violence, as the right picture shows. Hot magma may cool before it reaches the surface. It hardens into solid rock. That rock plugs the crack or the hole. A corked bottle is a good comparison. Magma keeps pushing upward. Gases trapped in the magma push against that plug. Pressure grows and grows. The volcano explodes without warning. Huge chunks of rock burst out. A whole mountainside can be torn away. Hot poisonous gas shoots into the sky. Ash shoots up. Melted rock shoots up. The blast has power that is hard to believe. An eruption brings many dangers. An exploding mountain sends tons of rock down its sides.

6Those falling rocks are called landslides. A landslide can bury a whole city. A landslide can block a river. A blocked river can cause a flood. Ash and dust climb in clouds while a volcano erupts. Ash falls like snow. The ash is heavy enough to collapse a house roof. An ash cloud can travel all the way around the world. A large ash cloud can block sunlight. After a large eruption, the temperature of the whole Earth can drop. Mount St. Helens stands in Washington State. One picture shows it before the eruption in 1980. Another picture shows it after that eruption. Trees in one picture were blown over by the blast. A volcano can blast out gas that is extremely hot. The gas is poisonous. The blast can be strong enough to knock down a whole forest. It can be hot enough to start fires that destroy things. Some large volcanoes wear snow and ice on top. Hot gases can melt all of that snow and ice at one time. Water then races down the mountain. The flood is full of dirt. People call it a mudslide. A mudslide is a landslide that flows like liquid.

7Scientists still cannot say when an earthquake will happen. They have become very good at spotting a large volcano that is about to erupt. In 1991, scientists warned people near a volcano in the Philippines. Their warning said an eruption was close. Many people left and reached safety before the huge blast. One picture shows scientists gathering information from a volcanic fissure. Tsunamis are the biggest waves on Earth. Wild waves. Earthquakes can cause tsunamis. Volcanoes can cause them too. People used to say tidal waves. Tidal refers to the ocean's normal tides. Tides do not cause tsunamis. The height of a tsunami can match the length of a football field. No other waves in the world are larger. Plate edges, where quakes and volcanoes often happen, usually lie near ocean edges. Shaking from a quake can set off large landslides. A volcano's blast can do the same. Whole hills can collapse. Sometimes the hill falls into the sea and builds a huge wave. Quakes also happen under the sea. Volcanoes happen under the sea too. An underwater quake makes the sea floor move with violence. An undersea volcano explodes beneath the water.

8Both events build huge waves. The waves spread over the ocean surface. A seaside volcano can send up steam. A tsunami races outward from its source. It goes in every direction. It can cross a whole ocean. Speed can reach 320 kilometers in one hour. That speed is 200 miles each hour. Out in the open ocean, the wave may not stand very high. It is very deep, though. Near land, the wave enters shallow water. The higher ocean floor pushes the wave up. Speed falls to about 70 kilometers each hour, or 45 miles each hour. The floor keeps lifting the wave. At the shore, the height can smash nearly every object ahead of it. When the wave meets the shore, it breaks as a giant wave. One picture shows damage from a tsunami. The largest tsunami on record began with a quake. The quake caused a landslide on the coast of Alaska. The wave sat in a very small bay. That bay sat near the slide that caused the wave. At the shore, most of these waves reach a height of one to ten stories. That height may not sound huge.

9The power of so much moving water is hard to picture. Most towns and villages on the coast sit well below 30 meters above the sea. Thirty meters is 100 feet. Even a small tsunami can wreck houses. It can wreck streets. It can wreck a whole town. A volcano in Indonesia set off a tsunami in 1883. That wave killed 36,000 people. Like volcanoes, tsunamis are getting easier to predict. They usually reach shore minutes to hours after the quake or volcano. Scientists have tools that detect volcanoes. The tools also detect earthquakes. After a large event, scientists can tell people a tsunami might follow. The warning cannot come in time to save homes. It cannot save belongings. Lives can still be saved in that time. Saving lives matters most. Many people describe these waves as walls of water. One picture shows a building ruined by an earthquake. Closing. Our quiet planet sometimes turns violent. In a large quake, the ground shakes and wrecks homes. Property is wrecked too. In a violent eruption, liquid rock can bury a city. Poisonous gases can bury it. Ash can bury it. Landslides can bury it.

10Coastal harm from tsunamis can cost billions of dollars. Scientists study these violent events as fully as they can. More knowledge lets them predict volcanoes. It lets them predict earthquakes. It lets them predict tsunamis. The goal is to save thousands of lives. Word list. Earthquake, a noun on page 4, means the crust of Earth shakes because of vibrations below. Faults, a noun on page 5, are cracks in Earth's crust where movement happens. Landslides, a noun on page 13, are soil and rock moving downhill in a mass. Magma, a noun on page 6, is melted rock in liquid form under Earth's surface. Mudslide, a noun on page 14, is a flood of water packed with dirt and debris. Plates, a noun on page 5, are big rock sheets forming the crust of Earth. Richter scale, a noun on page 9, is the scale that measures how strong an earthquake is. Tsunamis, a noun on page 4, are enormous ocean waves set off by underwater quakes, by landslides, or by volcanoes. Volcanoes, a noun on page 4, are spots where gas, ash, and lava pour onto the surface.

85. Women Justices of the High Court

1Washington, D.C., is where the Supreme Court building stands. Equal treatment of men and women is the idea this book follows. A path out of Arizona comes next. The Court moves from one woman to two. An American success story is told. A debt of gratitude is remembered. A professor of the year appears. One generation of change is shown. The first six women on the Court close the book. The year 1971 mattered deeply to Ruth Bader Ginsburg. A case headed to the U.S. Supreme Court had her as a lawyer. Rights are protected when the Court decides if laws follow the U.S. Constitution. Her case asked a question. Were women equal with men in the eyes of the law? Reed v. Reed was the name of that case. The two opposing sides give a case its name. Versus is what the letter v. stands for, and versus means against. A husband and a wife faced each other in court in Reed v. Reed. Both opponents share one name for that reason. Sex discrimination had already touched Ginsburg's own life. A young daughter was the reason she lost a chance to clerk at the Supreme Court. Pregnancy made her pay lower at a later job.

2Having children did not cost men their jobs. It did not cost them pay either. Equal treatment in school was what Ginsburg wanted for women and men. Equal treatment at work was what she wanted. Equal treatment under the law was what she wanted. The right to vote was a struggle for women a century ago. Outside the White House, one woman holds a banner. The Fourteenth Amendment was part of what Ginsburg and others explained in Reed v. Reed. The U.S. Civil War came first. After it, people added that amendment to the Constitution. Protection for people who had been enslaved was its aim. Women's rights drew little care from people in that era. Ginsburg argued that women were also shielded from discrimination by the Fourteenth Amendment. Men were favored over women by a law in Idaho. Illegal is what she said that state law was. The Supreme Court took her side. Women's rights were protected by the Fourteenth Amendment, the Court said, and this was the first time. After that ruling, people fought more laws. Those laws had treated women unfairly. Those laws were changed. Schooling could no longer be refused to women because they were women. Jobs could no longer be refused for that reason.

3An Equal Rights Amendment, or ERA, was what women rallied for in 1980. The same pay as men, for the same work, was their demand. Every justice had been a man from the Court's start in 1790. The first woman justice was Sandra Day O'Connor, ten years after Reed v. Reed. Horseback on an Arizona ranch was how Sandra grew up. School years also put her in Texas with her grandmother. A woman lawyer was someone nobody would hire after law school. A law office of her own was what O'Connor opened. Easter 1940 shows her with her family at that Arizona ranch. Texas in 1930 is where she was born. The year 2023 is when she died. Stanford in 1952 is where she finished law school. The years 1981 to 2006 are when she served as a justice. Young women all across the country found many doors opened, she said, when a woman was placed on the Court. Shared ideas with the president were why he nominated her in 1981. A mind of her own was still hers. Nine justices included her. A split of four votes against four sometimes happened among the others. Women's rights were helped by her tie-breaking votes.

4The right to privacy was helped by them too. New York in 1933 is where Ruth Bader Ginsburg was born. The year 2020 is when she died. Columbia in 1959 is where she finished law school. The years 1993 to 2020 are when she served as a justice. More than one woman on the Court is crucial, she said. People might think every woman thinks and works the same way if only one serves. A dozen years passed with O'Connor as the only woman there. The president nominated Ginsburg in 1993. Protection for the rights of women stayed her work, as it had been in Reed v. Reed. Other groups facing discrimination were protected by her too. A key point was explained in writing by her for the Court. Entrance to any public school cannot be denied to women. A childhood photo shows Sonia Sotomayor beside her mother, Celina. The year 2006 is when O'Connor retired. A woman was who she hoped would take the seat, but a man did. The year 2009 is when Sonia Sotomayor was nominated. Puerto Rico is where Sonia's parents came from. Nine is how old she was when her father died. A lawyer is what she decided to become at age ten.

5She had been watching a television show about a defense lawyer. The show was Perry Mason. New York in 1954 is where Sonia Sotomayor was born. Yale in 1979 is where she finished law school. From 2009 to the present is how long she has served as a justice. Modest and challenging circumstances were how she grew up, she said. Rich beyond measure is how she counts her life even so. A good education for Sonia was why her mother worked hard. Scholarships for college came because Sonia worked hard at school. A lawyer is what she became after law school. A judge is what she became later. A judge's task was the subject she spoke about after the president nominated her. Making the law is not that task. Applying the law is the task. The third woman on the Court is what she became in 2009. The first Latina justice is what she became too. High school classmates appear with Elena Kagan in a 1977 photo. The gavel is in her hands, and she sits in the bottom row. The next justice is what the president nominated Kagan to be in 2010. Kagan had clerked for a justice. No Black justice had served on the Court before that man.

6Cases brought to the Supreme Court later made her the top lawyer for the U.S. government. That role had never been held by a woman before her. New York in 1960 is where Elena Kagan was born. Harvard in 1986 is where she finished law school. From 2010 to the present is how long she has served as a justice. She said she owes a debt of gratitude. One woman she named is Sandra Day O'Connor. The other is Ruth Bader Ginsburg. Those two opened the way for Kagan and for many women of her generation. A judge is something Kagan had never been. Judges were also missing from the past of many of the 111 people who served before her. The law was something she knew very well. That knowledge helped her earn a place as woman number four among the Court's justices. A great writer is what people know her to be. Comic books are a big interest of hers too. An opinion for the Court is where she joined those two interests. Spiderman is who she talked about in that opinion. New Orleans in 1972 is where Amy Coney Barrett was born. Notre Dame in 1997 is where she finished law school.

7From 2020 to the present is how long she has served as a justice. Personal preferences must be set aside, she says, and that is one of a judge's most important duties. Personal beliefs must be set aside too. Seven children was the size of the family in which she was the oldest. Seven children is also the family she later mothered. Notre Dame Law School is where she studied, and she later came back to teach. Professor of the year is a vote she won three times. Ginsburg's death in 2020 led the president to nominate Barrett as the replacement. The fifth woman on the Court is what Barrett became, ten years after Kagan. A reading of the Constitution that changes as the country changes is what Ginsburg believed judges must give. Barrett's belief is different. The authors' meaning at the time of writing is how she thinks judges should read the words. Many important decisions come from the Supreme Court. The other two branches of government in the United States choose who sits there. Nine is the number of justices Congress requires. Life is the length of a justice's appointment. A nomination comes from the president only after a justice has retired or died.

8Approval of the president's choice belongs to the Senate, a part of Congress. Senators number 100. Each state sends two. A majority must confirm the choice, and confirm means they approve it. Only then may that person join the Court. Someone else must be chosen by the president if the majority disagrees. High school students talk with Ketanji Brown Jackson in 2019. They meet in Washington, D.C. The court there is the Court of Appeals. The nation's highest court has her as its first Black woman. Preschool is when she grew interested in a legal career. Law school is where her father went after leaving his teaching job. Coloring beside him was what she did while he studied legal texts. Two daughters of her own are hers today. Washington, D.C., in 1970 is where Ketanji Brown Jackson was born. Harvard in 1996 is where she finished law school. From 2022 to the present is how long she has served as a justice. One generation was enough, she said, for her family to travel from segregated life all the way to the highest court. Speech was her contest in high school. A national champion is what she became.

9Harvard was still a school her guidance counselor tried to talk her out of. She sent the application anyway. College there came later. Law school there came too. Several jobs followed law school. People who could not pay a lawyer were defended in one of those jobs. Stephen Breyer had her as a law clerk. The justice she replaced in 2022 was him. The earliest six women on the Supreme Court did not share one ethnic group. Different life stories were theirs too. Children were part of life for some of them. Children were not part of life for others. Lawyers, judges, or teachers were the first jobs of some. Different jobs were held by others. Lonely is how Ginsburg often said she felt after O'Connor retired. Each was grateful for the other. Very different from each other is what they also were. An interesting pair came from those contrasts. Writing about the two of them has been plentiful. Sisters in Law is a book that was published in 2016. A two-woman play with that same title opened in 2019. Arizona, the state O'Connor called home, saw the musical Justice open in 2022. A 2001 portrait at the Capitol in Washington, D.C., shows O'Connor and Ginsburg posing.

10Statues of men stand around them. The number of women on the Supreme Court is higher now than in any past year. Agreement on the law is not something they always share. Help from women in shaping the nation's laws is something they do share as a belief. In 2010, Elena Kagan laughs with Ruth Bader Ginsburg. Sonia Sotomayor and Sandra Day O'Connor laugh with them. A change or an addition to a document or a law is an amendment. A disagreement that a court of law settles is a case. The Constitution is the set of written rules for how the United States is governed. Unfair treatment of a person or a group is discrimination. The basis can be gender. It can be race. It can be age. It can be religion. It can be some other difference. To explain or to understand what something means is to interpret. Judges are what justices are. A woman from a Latin American country, or a woman whose ancestors came from one, is who Latina describes. Named for a job, an award, or an honor is what nominated means. Rights are freedoms people can fairly claim. They are powers people can fairly claim too.

86. Yo-Yo Ma and the Cello

1Chicago, Illinois, in 2012, saw Yo-Yo Ma play with a high-school student choir. Music had shaped him long before that day. Birth came in 1955 in Paris, France. A musical family was waiting. His mother sang. His father played violin and taught music as a professor. China was his mother's birthplace. China was his father's birthplace too. A move to France let his father teach at a music school. New York City came next, when Yo-Yo was seven. His father then founded the Children's Orchestra Society. An orchestra is a large set of musicians playing as one group. Stringed instruments and classical music are common for them. Classical can mean European music of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries. Symphonies and operas are examples of that older music from the West. Children learned that music through the society. Music was the plan for Yo-Yo from his earliest years.

2A violin arrived when he was only three. Terrible is how Ma says he sounded. No talent, he thought, was in him. Yeou-Cheng is his older sister. Her name sounds like YOO-CHANG. More than one violin, she says, was smashed in his frustration. He quit quickly. Music was all around him as he grew. Distance from it did not last. A Paris concert came a year later, with his parents. A double bass caught his eye. That was the instrument he decided to play. About 6 feet is its height. That is 1.8 meters. Too large for a small child, his parents decided. The next-largest stringed instrument was the compromise. That instrument is the cello. The word sounds like CHEL-oh. Yeou-Cheng already played violin with real skill when the cello began. Hard music was pushed on both children. A method came from their father, Yeou-Cheng said.

3Any hard task could be split into smaller, easier parts. Their mother, Yo-Yo says, taught the notes until thinking about them was unnecessary. Feeling behind the music could then be the focus. A 1961 photo catches a practice break with the Denver Symphony Orchestra. Yo-Yo was six. Yeou-Cheng was ten. Leonard Bernstein shared a performance with seven-year-old Yo-Yo in 1962. Bernstein was the most famous conductor in America. A conductor leads an orchestra, a choir, or another ensemble. An ensemble is people performing together, such as musicians or actors. John F. Kennedy, the president, sat in the audience. The next year put Yo-Yo and Yeou-Cheng on television together. Millions watched. Many children at seven are just meeting first notes on a recorder. Ma was already among the world's most famous cellists. His sister was a gifted violin player.

4Her own music career stopped once he was clearly the family star. Medicine is her work now. She is a doctor. She now leads the Children's Orchestra Society. Her father was the person who began it. High school ended early, at age fifteen. Juilliard was his college pick. It ranks among the world's best music schools. He left before long. Harvard University was the switch. Harvard stands with the strongest colleges inside the United States. Music is not its only focus. A life of nothing but cello was not what he wanted. A summer music festival came before Harvard. Jill Hornor was there, and he fell in love. She was a college student running the festival. Every remaining college summer brought him back. Playing and time with Jill filled those visits. Marriage came later. Two children came later. Emily is one.

5Nicholas is the other. Sweden in 2012 included his wife and children. A Polar Music Prize was given for his work. Back pain troubled Yo-Yo Ma while he was a young man. Cello playing had to stop as soon as he graduated. Surgery was done on his back. The goal was to fix a spine that curves the wrong way. Risk was high. A failed surgery might have ended cello playing forever. Very straight sitting was required for good playing. A life of back pain was the threat if he skipped surgery. He judged the risk worth taking. The operation succeeded. Six months in a body cast followed. Moving around was impossible. Playing was out of the question. Frustration filled that time. Demand for him as a cellist surged once the cast was gone. Performances spread across the world. Concerts were often booked years ahead.

6Album recording started at age twenty-three. More than seventy-five albums were out by his early sixties. Fifteen Grammy Awards are his. Living classical artists do not outsell many people ahead of him. His usual cello was made in 1733. Its worth is $2.5 million. A taxicab got that cello by accident in 1999. He had left it behind. The driver brought it back. Ma then recorded a reminder. Every taxi in New York played it. Riders were told not to forget items at the end of a trip. Performing again after the successful surgery made him happy. Best-in-the-world is now a common view of his playing. Historic events have featured him. One was the hundredth birthday of the Statue of Liberty. Another performance marked a yearly remembrance of the 9/11 attacks. One was the opening of the Olympic Games in 2002.

7One was the inauguration of President Obama. An inauguration is the ceremony that swears someone into office. Classical music treats him as a central figure. American culture does too. Culture is the set of ideas and habits that belong to one group of people. It can also mean a society with its own ways. The Presidential Medal of Freedom came from President Barack Obama in 2011. No civilian honor in the United States ranks higher. That same year he played with the Silk Road Ensemble. Dancer Charles Lil Buck Riley joined them. Three backgrounds matter deeply to Ma. One is Chinese heritage, a way of life passed down through generations. One is a French childhood. One is an American adulthood. Music of his was shaped by all three. European classical music was the start. Other kinds came soon. Bluegrass is one.

8Jazz is one. South American tango is one. Traditional music of China is one. Traditional music of Japan is one. Traditional music of Brazil is one. Openness to new cultures, he says, matters. Music is understood better that way. People are understood better that way. Classical music fills most of his albums. Many other styles have been part of his playing too. Songs from Cole Porter's Broadway musicals of the 1930s got his own versions. Themes by John Williams for Star Wars were recorded. Themes for E.T. were recorded. Themes for Jurassic Park were recorded. Several movie soundtracks of his own were recorded too. Bluegrass musicians have recorded with him. Jazz musicians have recorded with him. Orchestras around the world have recorded with him. One photo shows him with the pop musician Sting. The event was the opening of the 2002 Winter Olympic Games.

9Salt Lake City, Utah, was the place. Another photo shows the Silk Road Ensemble with him. Central Park in New York City was the place, in 2011. Mixed backgrounds always mattered to his music, so he founded that ensemble in 1998. Musicians and composers from twenty countries make up the group. A composer is a person who writes music. They collaborate, which means they work together on a project. Traditions from all over the world feed the music. The Silk Road itself was a trade route. It crossed Asia hundreds of years ago. People across the whole continent were linked by it. The name is not only a band. It is also an organization. Music and art are its tools for linking people from different places. When he is not playing with them, he helps plan programs. Teaching music is part of those programs.

10Playing music is part of them too. Germany in 2001 is the setting of a photo as he prepares to play. Hard work is what many famous musicians credit. Natural talent is what others credit. He says the reason for his worldwide popularity is a mystery to him. An accident is what he once called himself. Many things together, he says, made the success. Help came from his parents. Help came from his teachers. Help came from his sister. Help came from his wife. Help came from his kids. Paris shaped him. New York shaped him. Performing while very young shaped him. Change any single one of those, he feels, and world fame might not have come. A four-year-old's look at that giant double bass was one such piece. Without that look, the whole life would have gone another way.
